Chemical Equilibrium · Level 7

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1The following equilibrium constants were determined at 1120 K:
2CO(g) ⇄ C(s) + CO2(g); Kp1 = 10−14 atm−1
CO(g) + Cl2(g) ⇄ COCl2(g); Kp2 = 6×10−3 atm−1
What is the equilibrium constant Kc for the following reaction at 1120 K: C(s) + CO2(g) + 2Cl2(g) ⇄ 2COCl2(g)
(a) 3.31×1011 M−1   (b) 5.5×1010 M−1   (c) 5.51×106 M−1   (d) None of these		Combining equilibrium constantsPredict equilibrium constant from two other reactions🟡 Similar(a) 3.31×1011 M−1
Concept A target equilibrium made by adding/subtracting known ones has K equal to the product of their K's, each raised to how the reaction was scaled. Here reverse the first (invert Kp1) and double the second (square Kp2), then add. Finally convert Kp to Kc through Kp=Kc(RT)Δn. The two traps are forgetting to invert the first constant and mis-signing Δn.
Key relation Kp(target) = Kp22/Kp1; Kc = Kp(RT)−Δn Adding reactions multiplies K's; reversing inverts, doubling squares.
Solution
1. Reverse reaction 1: C(s) + CO2 ⇄ 2CO, K = 1/Kp1 = 1/10−14 = 1014 atm.
2. Double reaction 2: 2CO + 2Cl2 ⇄ 2COCl2, K = Kp22 = (6×10−3)2 = 3.6×10−5 atm−2.
3. Add the two: the 2CO terms cancel and we reach exactly the target C(s)+CO2+2Cl2⇄2COCl2.
4. So Kp = (1/Kp1)·Kp22 = 1014 × 3.6×10−5 = 3.6×109 atm−1. Units atm×atm−2 = atm−1, matching 1 net mole lost.
5. Count gas moles: Δngas = 2 − (1+2) = −1 (C is a solid, excluded).
6. Kc = Kp(RT)−Δn = Kp·RT, with RT = 0.0821×1120 = 91.95.
7. Kc = 3.6×109 × 91.95 = 3.31×1011 M−1.
 K(target) = Kp2^2 / Kp1
   = (6e-3)^2 / 1e-14
   = 3.6e-5 / 1e-14 = 3.6e9 atm^-1
 dn = 2 - 3 = -1
 Kc = Kp (RT)^-dn = Kp * RT
    = 3.6e9 * (0.0821*1120)
    = 3.6e9 * 91.95
 Kc = 3.31e11 M^-1            <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Checking units is the fastest guard: Kc=Kp·RT converts atm−1 into M−1, which confirms Δn=−1 and that you multiplied (not divided) by RT. Option (b) 5.5×1010 drops the RT factor entirely; (c) 5.51×106 forgets to square Kp2.
Answer: (a) 3.31×1011 M−1.
2One mole of N2(g) is mixed with 2 moles of H2(g) in a 4 litre vessel. If 50% of N2(g) is converted to NH3(g) by the following reaction :
N2(g) + 3H2(g) ⇄ 2NH3(g)
What will be the value of Kc for the following equilibrium ?
NH3(g) ⇄ (1/2)N2(g) + (3/2)H2(g)
(a) 256   (b) 16   (c) 1/16   (d) None of these		Kc from reversed/divided reactionOne mole of N_2(g) is mixed with 2 moles of H_2(g) in a 4 litre vessel. If 50 % of N_2(g) is conv...🟢 Exact(c) 1/16
Concept Two ideas combine. First read the equilibrium amounts off the 50% conversion to compute Kc for the formation N2+3H2⇄2NH3. Then notice the reaction asked is that formation reversed and divided by two: reversing inverts K, and halving the coefficients raises K to the power 1/2. Do both operations in order.
Key relation Kc(target) = (1/Kc,form)1/2 Dividing every coefficient by 2 raises K to the power 1/2; reversing first inverts it.
Solution
1. N2 reacted = 50% of 1 = 0.5 mol, so by N2+3H2⇄2NH3: H2 used 1.5 mol, NH3 made 1.0 mol.
2. Equilibrium moles: N2 = 0.5, H2 = 2−1.5 = 0.5, NH3 = 1.0, all in the 4 L vessel.
3. Concentrations: [N2]=[H2]=0.5/4=0.125 M, [NH3]=1.0/4=0.25 M.
4. Kc,form = [NH3]2/([N2][H2]3) = (0.25)2/(0.125×0.1253) = 0.0625/2.441×10−4 = 256.
5. The target is the reverse of the formation, then halved: K = (1/256)1/2. Invert first, then square-root.
6. (1/256)1/2 = 1/√256 = 1/16.
7. So Kc for NH3 ⇄ (1/2)N2+(3/2)H2 = 1/16.
 moles eq: N2=0.5 H2=0.5 NH3=1.0 (V=4 L)
 conc: N2=H2=0.125 M  NH3=0.25 M
 Kc,form = 0.25^2 / (0.125 * 0.125^3)
         = 0.0625 / 2.441e-4 = 256
 target = reverse & half:
   Kc = (1/256)^(1/2) = 1/16    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Track the two operations separately: reverse → invert (1/256), halve → square-root, giving 1/16. Option (a) 256 is the forward K itself (no transform); (b) 16 takes the square root but forgets to invert; (c) applies both correctly.
Answer: (c) 1/16.
3The gas A2 in the left flask allowed to react with gas B2 present in right flask as
A2(g) + B2(g) ⇄ 2AB(g); Kc = 4 at 27°C. What is the concentration of AB when equilibrium is established ?
(a) 1.33 M   (b) 2.66 M   (c) 0.66 M   (d) 0.33 M		Two connected flasks equilibriumThe gas A2 in the left flask allowed to react with gas B2 present in right flask at A2(g) + B2(g)🟢 Exact(c) 0.66 M
Concept Opening the stopcock lets both gases expand into the combined 4 L, so the first job is to recompute every concentration in that pooled volume. Then an ICE table on A2+B2⇄2AB gives [AB]. The classic trap is keeping the individual 1 L and 3 L volumes instead of the shared 4 L.
Key relation Kc = [AB]2/([A2][B2]) = 4 Δn=0, so volume cancels inside K, yet the starting concentrations still depend on the pooled volume.
Solution
1. Combined volume once the stopcock opens = 1 + 3 = 4 L.
2. Starting concentrations: [A2] = 2/4 = 0.5 M, [B2] = 4/4 = 1.0 M, [AB] = 0.
3. Let x M of A2 react: [A2]=0.5−x, [B2]=1−x, [AB]=2x.
4. Kc = (2x)2/[(0.5−x)(1−x)] = 4.
5. 4x2 = 4(0.5−x)(1−x) ⇒ x2 = 0.5 − 1.5x + x2. The x2 terms cancel, leaving a linear equation.
6. 1.5x = 0.5 ⇒ x = 0.333 M.
7. [AB] = 2x = 0.667 M ≈ 0.66 M.
 V(total) = 1 + 3 = 4 L
 start: A2=0.5  B2=1.0  AB=0
 Kc: (2x)^2 / ((0.5-x)(1-x)) = 4
 x^2 = 0.5 - 1.5x + x^2
 1.5x = 0.5  ->  x = 0.333
 [AB] = 2x = 0.667 M ~ 0.66     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Because Kc=4 is a perfect square, taking the root turns the problem linear — no quadratic formula needed. Option (a) 1.33 M comes from forgetting to pool the volumes (using the 1 L flask for A2); (d) 0.33 M reports x instead of 2x = [AB].
Answer: (c) 0.66 M.
4Assume that the decomposition of HNO3 can be represented by the following equation
4HNO3(g) ⇄ 4NO2(g) + 2H2O + O2(g) and the reaction approaches equilibrium at 400 K temperature and 30 atm pressure. At equilibrium partial pressure of HNO3 is 2 atm. Calculate Kc in (mol/L)3 at 400 K : (Use : R = 0.08 atm-L/mol-K)
(a) 4   (b) 8   (c) 16   (d) 32		Kc from partial pressures KpAssume that the decomposition of HNO_3 can be represented by the following equation 4 HNO_3(...🟢 Exact(d) 32
Concept Use the single known partial pressure (HNO3=2 atm) together with the total (30 atm) to fix the extent of reaction, which then gives every partial pressure. Build Kp, and convert to Kc with Kp=Kc(RT)Δn. Treat H2O as a gas here (it appears in the gas-phase equation), so it enters both Δn and Kp.
Key relation Kc = Kp/(RT)Δn, Δn = 7 − 4 = 3 Products 4+2+1 = 7 gas mol, reactant 4 gas mol.
Solution
1. Let 4ξ be the HNO3 pressure lost: p(HNO3)=P0−4ξ=2; then NO2=4ξ, H2O=2ξ, O2=ξ.
2. Total pressure = P0+3ξ = 30, and P0 = 2+4ξ, so 2+4ξ+3ξ = 30 ⇒ 7ξ=28 ⇒ ξ=4.
3. Partial pressures: HNO3=2, NO2=16, H2O=8, O2=4 atm (sum = 30 ✓).
4. Kp = (16)4(8)2(4)/(2)4 = 65536×64×4/16 = 1.049×106 atm3.
5. R = 0.08, T = 400 K, so RT = 32 and (RT)3 = 323 = 32768. Δn=3, so divide by (RT)3.
6. Kc = Kp/(RT)3 = 1.049×106/32768 = 32 (mol/L)3.
 p(HNO3)=2 ; total=30
 2 + 7*xi = 30  ->  xi = 4
 pNO2=16  pH2O=8  pO2=4
 Kp = 16^4 * 8^2 * 4 / 2^4
    = 65536*64*4/16 = 1.049e6
 (RT)^3 = 32^3 = 32768
 Kc = 1.049e6 / 32768 = 32      <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Fix Δn=3 before touching numbers, so you know to divide by (RT)3=32768; the huge 1.049×106 then collapses cleanly to 32. Forgetting the (RT)3 leaves Kc in the millions, matching no option — a clear flag that the conversion was skipped.
Answer: (d) 32.
5For the reaction C2H6(g) ⇄ C2H4(g) + H2(g), Kp is 5 ×10−2 atm. Calculate the mole per cent of C2H6(g) at equilibrium if pure C2H6 at 1 atm is passed over a suitable catalyst at 900 K :
(a) 20   (b) 33.33   (c) 66.66   (d) None of these		Degree of dissociation from KpFor the reaction C_(2)H_(6)(g) hArr C_(2)H_(4)(g)+H_(2)(g) K_(p) is 5xx10^(-2) atm. Calculat...🟢 Exact(c)66.66 ⚠
Concept One gas dissociates into two at a fixed total pressure of 1 atm, so moles rise from 1 to 1+α and that larger total must be used everywhere. The correct working constant is Kp = α2P/(1−α2); using (1−α) in the denominator instead of (1−α2) is the common slip, and it is exactly what produces the key's 66.66%.
Key relation Kp = α2P/(1−α2); mole% C2H6 = (1−α)/(1+α)×100 Each mole fraction carries the (1+α) total.
Solution
1. C2H6 ⇄ C2H4 + H2; start 1 mol, eq: C2H6=1−α, each product=α, total=1+α.
2. Kp = [α/(1+α)]2P ÷ [(1−α)/(1+α)]P, which simplifies to α2P/(1−α2).
3. 0.05 = α2(1)/(1−α2) ⇒ 0.05(1−α2) = α2 ⇒ 0.05 = 1.05α2.
4. α2 = 0.0476 ⇒ α = 0.218.
5. mole% C2H6 = (1−0.218)/(1+0.218)×100 = 0.782/1.218×100 = 64.2%.
6. 64.2% matches none of (a)/(b)/(c), so the correct option is (d) None of these.
7. The key's 66.66% requires α=0.2, which only arises from the wrong form Kp=α2/(1−α). That drops the (1+α) in the C2H6 term.
 Kp = a^2 P / (1 - a^2) = 0.05   (P=1)
 0.05 = 1.05 a^2  ->  a^2 = 0.0476
 a = 0.218
 mole% C2H6 = (1-a)/(1+a)*100
            = 0.782/1.218*100 = 64.2%
 64.2% is not (a)/(b)/(c) -> (d)  <-- working
 key 66.66% needs a=0.2 (wrong form)
⚡ JEE Trick The clean 66.66% appears only if you drop the (1+α) in the denominator of Kp (giving α=0.2); the consistent treatment gives α=0.218 and 64.2%, which is None of these. Always square-check: α=0.218 reproduces Kp=0.0476/0.952=0.05 exactly.
Answer: (c) 66.66 per the key — my working gives (d) None of these (64.2%) ⚠
62NOBr(g) ⇄ 2NO(g) + Br2(g). If nitrosyl bromide (NOBr) is 40% dissociated at certain temp. and a total pressure of 0.30 atm. Kp for the reaction
2NO(g) + Br2(g) ⇄ 2NOBr(g) is :
(a) 45   (b) 25   (c) 0.022   (d) 0.25		Kp of reverse reaction2NOBr(g)hArr2NO(g)+Br2(g). If nitrosyl bromide (NOBr) 40% dissociated at certain temp. and a total pressure of 0.30 atm.🟢 Exact(a) 45
Concept Start from the 40% dissociation to get the equilibrium mole fractions, turn those into partial pressures at the 0.30 atm total, and build Kp for the dissociation. The reaction actually asked is the reverse (association of NO and Br2 back to NOBr), so invert Kp at the very end.
Key relation Kp(reverse) = 1/Kp(forward); Kp,f = pNO2pBr2/pNOBr2 Reversing a reaction inverts its equilibrium constant.
Solution
1. Start 1 mol NOBr with α=0.4 (2NOBr→2NO+Br2): NOBr=0.6, NO=0.4, Br2=0.2 mol.
2. Total moles = 0.6+0.4+0.2 = 1.2.
3. Partial pressures = (mole fraction)×0.30: NOBr=(0.6/1.2)0.30=0.15, NO=0.10, Br2=0.05 atm.
4. Kp,f = (0.10)2(0.05)/(0.15)2 = 5×10−4/0.0225 = 0.0222 atm.
5. The asked reaction 2NO+Br2⇄2NOBr is the reverse, so Kp = 1/Kp,f. Same T, just inverted.
6. Kp = 1/0.0222 = 45.
7. So Kp for the association reaction = 45.
 a=0.4: NOBr=0.6 NO=0.4 Br2=0.2 (n=1.2)
 p = (ni/1.2)*0.30
   NOBr=0.15 NO=0.10 Br2=0.05 atm
 Kp,f = 0.10^2 * 0.05 / 0.15^2
      = 5e-4 / 0.0225 = 0.0222
 Kp,rev = 1/0.0222 = 45         <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Read which direction the question writes: it asks for the reverse of the dissociation, so the final step must invert. Option (c) 0.022 is Kp of the forward (dissociation) reaction — the exact distractor for anyone who computes Kp,f and stops there.
Answer: (a) 45.
7At a certain temperature and 2 atm pressure equilibrium constant (Kp) is 25 for the reaction
SO2(g) + NO2(g) ⇄ SO3(g) + NO(g)
Initially if we take 2 moles of each of the four gases and 2 moles of inert gas, what would be the equilibrium partial pressure of NO2 ?
(a) 1.33 atm   (b) 0.1665 atm   (c) 0.133 atm   (d) None of these		Equilibrium partial pressure with inert gasAt a certain temperature and 2 atm pressure equilibrium constant Kp is 25 for the reaction SO2...🟢 Exact(c) 0.133 atm
Concept With Δngas=0 the total number of moles never changes (it stays 10, counting the inert gas), and the inert gas only dilutes without appearing in K. Because the pressure/Σn factors cancel, K can be written directly in moles to find the extent; convert the leftover NO2 moles to a partial pressure at the end.
Key relation K = (nSO3nNO)/(nSO2nNO2) = 25; pNO2 = (nNO2/ntot)P Δn=0 cancels both pressure and volume.
Solution
1. Start 2 mol each of SO2, NO2, SO3, NO plus 2 mol inert; total = 10 mol and stays 10.
2. Let x react forward: SO2=2−x, NO2=2−x, SO3=2+x, NO=2+x.
3. K = (2+x)2/(2−x)2 = 25, so (2+x)/(2−x) = 5. Take √25 to linearise.
4. 2+x = 5(2−x) = 10−5x ⇒ 6x = 8 ⇒ x = 1.333.
5. nNO2 = 2 − 1.333 = 0.667 mol; total still 10, P = 2 atm.
6. pNO2 = (nNO2/ntot)P = (0.667/10)×2.
7. pNO2 = 0.133 atm.
 total n = 10 (dn=0, constant)
 (2+x)^2/(2-x)^2 = 25
 (2+x)/(2-x) = 5  ->  6x = 8  -> x=1.333
 nNO2 = 2 - 1.333 = 0.667
 pNO2 = (0.667/10)*2 = 0.133 atm <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Taking √25 immediately avoids a quadratic. The 2 mol inert gas and the 2 atm total never enter K itself — they only reappear when converting moles to pressure. Option (a) 1.33 atm is the moles reacted (x) misreported as a pressure.
Answer: (c) 0.133 atm.
8The equilibrium constant Kp for the reaction N2O4(g) ⇄ 2NO2(g) is 4.5 . What would be the average molar mass (in g/mol) of an equilibrium mixture of N2O4 and NO2 formed by the dissociation of pure N2O4 at a total pressure of 2 atm ?
(a) 69   (b) 57.5   (c) 80.5   (d) 85.5		Average molar mass from dissociationThe equilibrium constant K_(P) for the reaction N_(2)O_(4)(g) hArr 2NO_(2)(g) is 4.5 atm🟢 Exact(b) 57.5
Concept Get the degree of dissociation α from Kp at 2 atm, then use mass conservation: the equilibrium mixture weighs the same as the starting N2O4 (92 g per mole) while the mole count grows to (1+α). The mean molar mass is therefore simply 92/(1+α) — no need for the individual amounts.
Key relation Kp = 4α2P/(1−α2); Mavg = M0/(1+α) 1 mol N2O4 (92 g) becomes (1+α) mol total.
Solution
1. N2O4⇄2NO2; eq: N2O4=1−α, NO2=2α, total = 1+α.
2. Kp = (2α)2P/(1−α2) = 4α2P/(1−α2).
3. 4.5 = 4α2(2)/(1−α2) = 8α2/(1−α2).
4. 4.5(1−α2) = 8α2 ⇒ 12.5α2 = 4.5 ⇒ α2 = 0.36. 4.5+8 = 12.5.
5. α = 0.6; M0(N2O4) = 92 g/mol; total moles = 1.6.
6. Mavg = 92/1.6 = 57.5 g/mol.
 Kp = 4 a^2 P / (1 - a^2) = 4.5, P=2
 8 a^2 = 4.5 (1 - a^2)
 12.5 a^2 = 4.5  ->  a^2 = 0.36 -> a=0.6
 M_avg = 92 / (1 + a) = 92/1.6
       = 57.5 g/mol             <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Mass conservation makes Mavg=M0/(1+α) depend on α alone. Sanity check: full dissociation (α=1) would give 92/2 = 46 (pure NO2), so 57.5 for α=0.6 sits sensibly between 46 and 92. Option (a) 69 corresponds to a wrong total of 1.33 mol.
Answer: (b) 57.5 g/mol.
9A flask containing 0.5 atm pressure of A2(g), some solid AB added into flask which undergoes dissociation according to 2AB(s) ⇄ A2(g) + B2(g), Kp = 0.06 atm2. The total pressure (in atm) at equilibrium is:
(a) 0.70   (b) 0.6   (c) 0.10   (d) None of these		Solid dissociation with common productA flask containing 0.5 atm pressure of A_2(g), some solid A B added into flask which undergoes di...🟢 Exact(a) 0.70
Concept The solid AB has unit activity, so only the two gases appear in Kp. Since A2 is pre-loaded at 0.5 atm, whatever the solid produces adds to it, while B2 starts from zero and grows by the same amount. This makes it a common-ion problem that ends in a small quadratic, where the pre-existing A2 is the key term to carry.
Key relation Kp = pA2·pB2 = (0.5+x)(x) = 0.06 2AB(s)⇄A2+B2 makes equal x of each gas; the solid is omitted.
Solution
1. Initial: pA2=0.5 atm, pB2=0; AB is a solid (activity 1).
2. Let x atm of each gas form by dissociation: pA2=0.5+x, pB2=x.
3. Kp = (0.5+x)(x) = 0.06.
4. Expand: x2 + 0.5x − 0.06 = 0.
5. x = [−0.5 + √(0.25+0.24)]/2 = (−0.5 + √0.49)/2 = (−0.5+0.7)/2 = 0.1. Take the positive root.
6. Then pA2 = 0.6 atm and pB2 = 0.1 atm.
7. Total P = pA2 + pB2 = 0.6 + 0.1 = 0.70 atm.
 Kp = (0.5 + x)(x) = 0.06
 x^2 + 0.5x - 0.06 = 0
 x = (-0.5 + sqrt(0.49))/2
   = (-0.5 + 0.7)/2 = 0.1
 pA2 = 0.6  pB2 = 0.1
 total P = 0.70 atm            <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The pre-loaded A2 is a common-ion effect that suppresses dissociation, so B2 only reaches 0.1 atm — far below the √0.06 = 0.245 atm it would reach in a fresh flask. Ignoring the 0.5 atm head start gives the wrong total, so always add the extent x onto the pre-loaded pressure.
Answer: (a) 0.70 atm.
10Calculate ΔrG for the reaction at 27°C
H2(g) + 2Ag+(aq) ⇄ 2Ag(s) + 2H+(aq) .
Given : PH2 = 0.5 bar; [Ag+] = 10−5 M; [H+] = 10−3 M; ΔrG°[Ag+(aq)] = 77.1 kJ/mol
(a) −154.2 kJ/mol   (b) −178.9 kJ/mol   (c) −129.5 kJ/mol   (d) None of these		Reaction Gibbs energy from QGibbs Energy Problems with K and Q | JEE 2027 Chemistry (English)🟡 Similar(c) −129.5 kJ/mol
Concept This needs both the standard and the actual Gibbs energy. Get ΔrG° from formation energies — only Ag+ is non-zero, because H+(aq), Ag(s) and H2(g) are all defined as zero — then correct to the stated non-standard state with RT ln Q, using the given concentrations and the H2 pressure.
Key relation ΔrG = ΔrG° + RT ln Q, Q = [H+]2/([Ag+]2pH2) ΔfG°(H+) = 0 by convention.
Solution
1. ΔrG° = Σproducts − Σreactants = 0 − 2×77.1 = −154.2 kJ/mol.
2. Q = (10−3)2/[(10−5)2×0.5] = 10−6/(0.5×10−10) = 2×104.
3. ln Q = ln(2×104) = ln 2 + 4 ln 10 = 0.693 + 4×2.303 = 9.90.
4. RT = 8.314×300 = 2494 J = 2.494 kJ.
5. RT ln Q = 2.494×9.90 = +24.7 kJ/mol. Q > 1 pushes ΔG up.
6. ΔrG = −154.2 + 24.7 = −129.5 kJ/mol.
 drG0 = -2 * 77.1 = -154.2 kJ/mol
 Q = (1e-3)^2 / ((1e-5)^2 * 0.5)
   = 1e-6 / (5e-11) = 2e4
 ln Q = 0.693 + 4*2.303 = 9.90
 RT = 8.314*300 = 2494 J
 drG = -154.2 + 2.494*9.90
     = -154.2 + 24.7 = -129.5     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Only Ag+ carries a formation energy; zeroing H+, Ag(s) and H2(g) is the shortcut. Option (a) −154.2 is ΔrG° alone — the trap for anyone who forgets the +RT ln Q correction that makes the reaction less spontaneous.
Answer: (c) −129.5 kJ/mol.

Chemical Equilibrium · Level 8

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1For the equilibrium
LiCl·3NH3(s) ⇄ LiCl·NH3(s) + 2NH3(g); Kp = 9 atm2 at 37°C. A 5 litre vessel contains 0.1 mole of LiCl·NH3. How many moles of NH3 should be added to the flask at this temperature to drive the backward reaction for completion ? (Use : R = 0.08 atm-L/mol K)
(a) 0.2   (b) 0.59   (c) 0.69   (d) 0.79		Solid-gas equilibrium backward completionFor the equilibrium LiCl. 3 NH_3(s) hArr LiCl.NH_3(s)+2 NH_3(g) ; K_p=9 atm^2 at 37 C. A 5 li...🟢 Exact(d) 0.79
Concept Driving the backward reaction to completion means converting every bit of LiCl·NH3 back to LiCl·3NH3. The NH3 you add does two jobs: it is consumed in that conversion (2 mol per mol of solid) and it must still fill the vessel at the equilibrium NH3 pressure, since the system can only just reach completion while sitting at that pressure.
Key relation pNH3 = √Kp = 3 atm; nadded = nconsumed + peqV/RT Kp = pNH32 since both solids drop out.
Solution
1. Equilibrium NH3 pressure: pNH32 = Kp = 9 ⇒ pNH3 = 3 atm.
2. Backward step LiCl·NH3 + 2NH3 → LiCl·3NH3 uses 2 mol NH3 per mol of solid.
3. For 0.1 mol LiCl·NH3: NH3 consumed = 2×0.1 = 0.2 mol.
4. Gas standing at 3 atm: n = pV/RT = 3×5/(0.08×310) = 15/24.8 = 0.605 mol.
5. Total NH3 to add = 0.2 + 0.605 = 0.805 mol. Using R=0.082 gives 0.59 mol gas, i.e. total 0.79.
6. Rounding aside, the intended choice is (d) 0.79.
 p(NH3) = sqrt(Kp) = sqrt(9) = 3 atm
 consumed = 2 * 0.1 = 0.2 mol
 gas left = pV/RT = 3*5/(0.08*310)
          = 15/24.8 = 0.605 mol
 total = 0.2 + 0.605 = 0.805 ~ 0.79 <-- answer
   (R=0.082 gives 0.59 gas -> 0.79)
⚡ JEE Trick The subtle part is that completion still requires the gas held at the equilibrium 3 atm, so you add the standing-gas moles on top of the 0.2 mol consumed. Option (a) 0.2 counts only the reacted NH3; (b) 0.59 counts only the standing gas — (d) adds both.
Answer: (d) 0.79.
2Ammonium carbamate dissociates as NH2COONH4(s) ⇄ 2NH3(g) + CO2(g). In a closed vessel containing ammonium carbamate in equilibrium, ammonia is added such a that partial pressure of NH3 now equals to the original total pressure. Calculate the ratio of partial pressure of CO2 now to the original partial pressure of CO2 :
(a) 4   (b) 9   (c) 4/9   (d) 2/9		Common ion effect on dissociationAmmonium carbamate dissociates as NH_2COONH_4(s) hArr 2 NH_3(g)+CO_2(g) In a closed vessel...🟢 Exact(c) 4/9
Concept The solid keeps Kp fixed, so adding NH3 is a common-ion push: raising pNH3 forces pCO2 down so the product pNH32pCO2 stays constant. Write Kp once from the pure-dissociation state and once from the NH3-enriched state and divide to get the ratio directly.
Key relation Kp = pNH32·pCO2 (a constant) Pure dissociation of the solid gives pNH3 = 2p, pCO2 = p, so total = 3p.
Solution
1. Original equilibrium: pCO2 = p, pNH3 = 2p (2:1 stoichiometry), total pressure = 3p.
2. Kp = (2p)2(p) = 4p3.
3. After adding NH3, the new pNH3 equals the original total = 3p.
4. Kp is unchanged, so (3p)2·pCO2′ = 4p3.
5. 9p2·pCO2′ = 4p3 ⇒ pCO2′ = 4p3/(9p2) = 4p/9.
6. Ratio = pCO2′/pCO2 = (4p/9)/p = 4/9.
 orig: pCO2=p  pNH3=2p  total=3p
 Kp = (2p)^2 * p = 4p^3
 new pNH3 = 3p
 (3p)^2 * pCO2' = 4p^3
 pCO2' = 4p^3 / 9p^2 = 4p/9
 ratio = (4p/9)/p = 4/9          <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Since Kp is a constant, raising NH3 from 2p to 3p scales CO2 by (2p/3p)2 = 4/9 directly — no need to re-solve from scratch. Option (a) 4 inverts the ratio; (d) 2/9 forgets to square the NH3 term, taking only the first power of 3p.
Answer: (c) 4/9.
3A reaction system in equilibrium according to reaction 2SO2(g) + O2(g) ⇄ 2SO3(g) in one litre vessel at a given temperature was found to contain 0.12 mole each of SO2 and SO3 and 5 mole of O2. Another vessel of one litre contains 32 g of SO2 at the same temperature. What mass of O2 must be added to this vessel in order that at equilibrium 20% of SO2 is oxidized to SO3 ?
(a) 0.4125 g   (b) 11.6 g   (c) 1.6 g   (d) None of these		Kc application to mass of O2A reaction system in equilibrium according to reaction 2 SO_2(g)+O_2(g) hArr SO_3(g) in one litre ves...🟢 Exact(b) 11.6 g
Concept The first vessel is only there to fix Kc at this temperature. Carry that same Kc into the second vessel, where 20% of the SO2 is oxidised, and solve for the total O2 that must be present — remembering the added O2 exceeds the equilibrium O2 by the amount consumed.
Key relation Kc = [SO3]2/([SO2]2[O2]) Same T ⇒ same Kc; everything is in 1 L, so moles = molarity.
Solution
1. Vessel 1: Kc = (0.12)2/((0.12)2×5) = 1/5 = 0.2.
2. Vessel 2: SO2 = 32/64 = 0.5 mol; 20% oxidised = 0.1 mol.
3. At equilibrium: SO2 = 0.4, SO3 = 0.1, O2 = b−0.05 (0.05 mol O2 used per 0.1 mol SO3).
4. 0.2 = (0.1)2/((0.4)2(b−0.05)) = 0.01/[0.16(b−0.05)].
5. b−0.05 = 0.01/(0.2×0.16) = 0.01/0.032 = 0.3125 ⇒ b = 0.3625 mol.
6. Mass O2 added = 0.3625×32 = 11.6 g.
 Kc = 0.12^2 / (0.12^2 * 5) = 0.2
 SO2=0.5 mol; 20% -> SO3=0.1, O2 used=0.05
 0.2 = 0.1^2 / (0.4^2 * (b-0.05))
 b-0.05 = 0.01/(0.2*0.16) = 0.3125
 b = 0.3625 mol
 mass O2 = 0.3625*32 = 11.6 g     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Kc from vessel 1 is a clean 0.2 because the equal 0.12 mol of SO2 and SO3 cancel. The O2 you add (b) must exceed the equilibrium O2 by the 0.05 mol consumed; (c) 1.6 g reports only that reacted O2.
Answer: (b) 11.6 g.
4A vessel of 250 litre was filled with 0.01 mole of Sb2S3 and 0.01 mole of H2 to attain the equilibrium at 440°C as Sb2S3(s) + 3H2(g) ⇄ 2Sb(s) + 3H2S(g) . After equilibrium, the H2S formed was analysed by dissolving it in water and treating with excess of Pb2+ to give 1.19g of PbS as precipitate. What is the value of Kc at 440°C?
(a) 1   (b) 2   (c) 4   (d) 8		Kc from gravimetric analysisA vessel of 250 litre was filled with 0.01 mole of Sb_2 S_3 and 0.01 mole of H_2 to attain the eq...🟢 Exact(a) 1
Concept The PbS precipitate is just a way to measure the H2S produced, and the 3H2→3H2S stoichiometry means the H2 consumed equals the H2S formed. Only the two gases enter Kc; the solids Sb2S3 and Sb drop out, and since the gas moles are equal the 250 L volume cancels completely.
Key relation Kc = [H2S]3/[H2]3 = (nH2S/nH2)3 Sb2S3 and Sb are solids; equal gas moles make V cancel.
Solution
1. M(PbS) = 207 + 32 = 239; n(PbS) = 1.19/239 = 0.005 mol ⇒ n(H2S) = 0.005 mol.
2. The step 3H2 → 3H2S is 1:1, so H2 consumed = n(H2S) = 0.005 mol.
3. Initial H2 = 0.01 mol; equilibrium H2 = 0.01 − 0.005 = 0.005 mol.
4. Kc = [H2S]3/[H2]3 = (nH2S/nH2)3. The 250 L in numerator and denominator cancels.
5. = (0.005/0.005)3 = 13.
6. Kc = 1.
 M(PbS)=239 ; n(PbS)=1.19/239=0.005 mol
 n(H2S) = 0.005 = H2 used
 H2 left = 0.01 - 0.005 = 0.005 mol
 Kc = (nH2S/nH2)^3   (V cancels)
    = (0.005/0.005)^3 = 1       <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Equal gas moles on both sides (Δn=0) means the 250 L never matters and Kc is a pure mole ratio. M(PbS)=239 is the datum that matters; misreading it (say as 119) would change n(H2S) and wreck the answer, so compute it deliberately.
Answer: (a) 1.
5Rate of diffusion of ozonized oxygen is 0.4√5 times that of pure oxygen. What is the percent degree of association of oxygen assuming pure O2 in the sample initially ?
(a) 20   (b) 40   (c) 60   (d) None of these		Degree of association from diffusionRate of diffusion of ozonized oxygen is 0.4 sqrt(5)times that of pure oxygen. What is the percent degree of association🟢 Exact(c) 60
Concept Graham's law turns the measured diffusion rate into the mixture's mean molar mass, and the association 3O2⇄2O3 raises that mean above 32. Mass conservation (the sample's mass stays constant while moles fall) then converts the mean mass into the degree of association.
Key relation rmix/rO2 = √(MO2/Mmix); Mavg = 32/(1−α/3) 3 mol O2 → 2 mol O3, so per mol O2 the total becomes 1−α/3.
Solution
1. rmix/rO2 = 0.4√5, so squaring: (0.4√5)2 = MO2/Mmix.
2. 0.16×5 = 0.8 = 32/Mmix ⇒ Mmix = 32/0.8 = 40 g/mol.
3. Start 1 mol O2; let α associate: O2 left = 1−α, O3 formed = (2/3)α.
4. Total moles = (1−α) + (2/3)α = 1 − α/3; mass = 32 g (conserved).
5. Mavg = 32/(1−α/3) = 40 ⇒ 1 − α/3 = 0.8.
6. α/3 = 0.2 ⇒ α = 0.6, i.e. 60% of the oxygen has associated.
 (0.4*sqrt5)^2 = M_O2 / M_mix
 0.16*5 = 0.8 = 32 / M_mix
 M_mix = 40 g/mol
 total moles = 1 - a/3 ; mass = 32
 32/(1 - a/3) = 40 -> 1 - a/3 = 0.8
 a = 0.6  ->  60%               <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Faster than pure O2? No — 0.4√5 ≈ 0.894 is less than 1, so the mixture diffuses slower and is heavier (M=40), exactly what forming O3 should do. Option (a) 20 confuses the association fraction α with the (2/3)α that becomes O3.
Answer: (c) 60.
6One mole of SO3 was placed in a two litre vessel at a certain temperature. The following equilibrium was established in the vessel 2SO3(g) ⇄ 2SO2(g) + O2(g). The equilibrium mixture reacted with 0.2 mole KMnO4 in acidic medium. Hence, Kc is :
(a) 0.50   (b) 0.25   (c) 0.125   (d) None of these		Kc via redox titrationOne mole of SO_3 was placed in a two litre vessel at a certain temperature. The following equilib... (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(c) 0.125
Concept KMnO4 cannot oxidise SO3 (sulphur is already +6); it oxidises only the SO2 formed (S: +4 → +6). So the titre fixes how much SO2 is present, and hence the extent of dissociation. One variable describes the whole dissociation, and the KMnO4 titre pins it down through a simple electron balance.
Key relation n(SO2) = (5/2)·n(KMnO4), Kc = [SO2]2[O2]/[SO3]2 n-factor of SO2 is 2, of MnO4− is 5.
Solution
1. 2SO3 ⇄ 2SO2 + O2; start 1 mol SO3 in 2 L. Let y mol O2 form, so 2y mol SO2 appear and 2y mol SO3 are used.
2. Redox: SO2→SO42− loses 2e−; MnO4−→Mn2+ gains 5e−. Electron balance ties the two.
3. 2·n(SO2) = 5·n(KMnO4) = 5(0.2) ⇒ n(SO2) = 2y = 0.5 mol, y = 0.25.
4. Equilibrium moles: SO3 = 1 − 0.5 = 0.5, SO2 = 0.5, O2 = 0.25.
5. Divide by 2 L: [SO3] = 0.25, [SO2] = 0.25, [O2] = 0.125 M.
6. Kc = (0.25)2(0.125)/(0.25)2 = 0.125 (the SO2 and SO3 terms cancel).
 n(SO2) = (5/2) x 0.2 = 0.50 mol -> 2y = 0.50
 moles  SO3=0.5  SO2=0.5  O2=0.25   (V = 2 L)
 conc   0.25     0.25     0.125
 -----------------------------------------
 Kc = (0.25)^2 (0.125) / (0.25)^2 = 0.125  <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick SO3 is a decoy for the titration — only +4 sulphur reacts. The equal SO2/SO3 here makes Kc = [O2]. Distractor (b) 0.25 drops the 5/2 n-factor.
Answer: (c) 0.125.
7At 800°C, the following equilibrium is established as F2(g) ⇄ 2F(g). The composition of equilibrium may be determined by measuring the rate of effusion of the mixture through a pin hole. It is found that at 800°C and 1 atm mixture effused 1.6 times as fast as SO2 effuses under the similar conditions (At. wt. of F = 19). What is the value of Kp (in atm) ?
(a) 0.315   (b) 0.685   (c) 0.46   (d) 1.49		Kp from effusion rateAt `800^(@)C,` the following equilibrium is established as `F_(2)(g)hArr2F(g)` (Doubtnut)🟢 Exact(d) 1.49
Concept Graham’s law turns the measured effusion rate into the average molar mass of the F2/F mixture. That average fixes the degree of dissociation, which then gives Kp. A rate quoted against a known gas (SO2, M = 64) is just a disguised molar-mass measurement.
Key relation rmix/rSO2 = √(MSO2/Mmix), Mavg = M(F2)/(1+α) Mass is conserved, so the average mass falls as F2 splits.
Solution
1. rmix/rSO2 = √(MSO2/Mmix) = 1.6, with M(SO2) = 64.
2. 64/Mmix = 1.62 = 2.56 ⇒ Mmix = 25 g/mol.
3. F2 ⇄ 2F; 1 mol F2 (M = 38), α dissociates ⇒ total = 1+α, mass = 38 g.
4. Mavg = 38/(1+α) = 25 ⇒ 1+α = 1.52 ⇒ α = 0.52.
5. Mole fractions: F2 = (1−α)/(1+α) = 0.48/1.52 = 0.316; F = 2α/(1+α) = 1.04/1.52 = 0.684. At P = 1 atm these are the partial pressures.
6. Kp = pF2/pF2 = (0.684)2/0.316 = 0.468/0.316 = 1.48 ≈ 1.49 atm.
7. Cross-check: Kp = 4α2P/(1−α2) = 4(0.52)2(1)/(1−0.2704) = 1.082/0.730 = 1.48, the same value.
 M_mix = 64 / 1.6^2 = 64/2.56 = 25
 38/(1+a) = 25  ->  a = 0.52
 p(F2)=0.316  p(F)=0.684   (P_total = 1 atm)
 -----------------------------------------
 Kp = 0.684^2 / 0.316 = 1.48 ~ 1.49  <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Faster effusion than SO2 means the mixture is lighter than F2 alone, proving dissociation. Compare with F = 19 (atomic); Mavg 25 sits between 19 and 38, so α ≈ 0.5 is a quick sanity check.
Answer: (d) 1.49 atm.
8The equilibrium constant for the ionization of RNH2(g) in water as RNH2(g) + H2O(l) ⇄ RNH3+(aq) + OH−(aq) is 8×10−6 at 25°C. Find the pH of a solution at equilibrium when pressure of RNH2(g) is 0.5 bar:
(a) ~ 12.3   (b) ~ 11.3   (c) ~ 11.45   (d) None of these		pH from gas-phase ionization constantThe equilibrium constant for the ionization of RNH2(g) in water (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(b) ~ 11.3
Concept RNH2 is supplied as a gas at a fixed pressure, so its partial pressure plays the role of “concentration” in the equilibrium constant. Because one RNH3+ and one OH− form together, their molarities are equal, and that common value gives the pOH directly.
Key relation K = [RNH3+][OH−]/p(RNH2) Water is pure liquid (activity 1); the gas enters as its pressure, not a molarity.
Solution
1. RNH2(g) + H2O(l) ⇄ RNH3+ + OH−, K = 8×10−6.
2. Let [RNH3+] = [OH−] = x; p(RNH2) stays 0.5 bar (gas reservoir).
3. K = x2/0.5 = 8×10−6 ⇒ x2 = 4×10−6 ⇒ x = 2×10−3 M.
4. [OH−] = 2×10−3; pOH = −log(2×10−3) = 3 − log2 = 3 − 0.30 = 2.70.
5. pH = 14 − pOH = 14 − 2.70 = 11.30 (Kw = 10−14 at 25°C).
6. Sanity: [OH−] = 2×10−3 ≫ 10−7, so water’s own ionization is negligible and the base is weak (K small) — the single-step treatment is valid.
7. So the solution is strongly basic, pH ≈ 11.3, matching option (b).
 x^2 / 0.5 = 8e-6  ->  x^2 = 4e-6  ->  x = 2e-3
 [OH-] = 2e-3 M
 pOH = 3 - log2 = 3 - 0.30 = 2.70
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = 14 - 2.70 = 11.30            <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Treat the gas pressure as the “concentration” term — do not convert 0.5 bar to molarity. Distractor (c) 11.45 comes from forgetting log2 = 0.30 and taking pOH ≈ 2.55, while (a) 12.3 wrongly uses the full 0.5 bar as [OH−] without the square-root step.
Answer: (b) ~ 11.3.
9When N2O5 is heated at certain temperature, it dissociated as N2O5(g) ⇄ N2O3(g) + O2(g); Kc = 2.5 . At the same time N2O3 also decomposes as : N2O3(g) ⇄ N2O(g) + O2(g). If initially 4.0 moles of N2O5 are taken in 1.0 litre flask and allowed to dissociate, concentration of O2 at equilibrium is 2.5M. Equilibrium concentration of N2O5 is :
(a) 1.0 M   (b) 1.5 M   (c) 2.166 M   (d) 1.846 M		Coupled dissociation equilibriaWhen N_2O_5 is heated at temp. T, it dissociates as N_2O_5 ⇌ N_2O_3 + O_2, K_c=2.5. At the same time... (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(d) 1.846 M
Concept Two consecutive reactions share the product O2. The total O2 gives one equation; Kc of the first step gives a second, so no Kc for the second step is needed. Track the two extents separately and remember that O2 accumulates from both.
Key relation Kc1 = [N2O3][O2]/[N2O5], [O2]total = x + y Both steps release O2, so its amount sums the two extents.
Solution
1. Step 1 extent x: N2O5→N2O3+O2. Step 2 extent y: N2O3→N2O+O2. V = 1 L.
2. Equilibrium: N2O5 = 4−x, N2O3 = x−y, O2 = x+y, N2O = y.
3. Given [O2] = 2.5 ⇒ x + y = 2.5.
4. Kc1 = (x−y)(x+y)/(4−x) = 2.5; since x+y = 2.5, the (x+y) factor cancels the 2.5: x−y = 4−x.
5. So 2x − y = 4; with x + y = 2.5, add ⇒ 3x = 6.5 ⇒ x = 2.167, y = 0.333.
6. [N2O5] = 4 − x = 4 − 13/6 = 11/6 = 1.833 M (option (d), printed 1.846 by rounding).
7. Cross-check with Kc1: [N2O3] = x−y = 1.833, [O2] = 2.5, so (1.833)(2.5)/1.833 = 2.5 = Kc1, which confirms the extents.
 x + y = 2.5                 (total O2)
 (x-y)(2.5)/(4-x) = 2.5 -> x-y = 4-x
 2x - y = 4  ,  x + y = 2.5  -> x = 2.167
 -----------------------------------------
 [N2O5] = 4 - 2.167 = 1.833 (= 11/6)  <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Because [O2] equals Kc1 numerically (2.5), the big factor cancels and no quadratic is needed. Distractor (c) 2.166 is the extent x, not [N2O5]; the exact value 11/6 = 1.833 is the one printed as 1.846.
Answer: (d) 1.846 M (exact working gives 11/6 = 1.833 M).
10Two solid compounds X and Y dissociate at a certain temperature as follows
X(s) ⇄ A(g) + 2B(g); Kp1 = 9×10−3 atm3
Y(s) ⇄ 2B(g) + C(g); Kp2 = 4.5×10−3 atm3
The total pressure of gases over a mixture of X and Y is :
(a) 4.5 atm   (b) 0.45 atm   (c) 0.6 atm   (d) None of these		Simultaneous solid dissociationsTwo solid compounds X and Y dissociate at a certain temperature as follows X(s)⇌A(g)+2B(g)... (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(b) 0.45 atm
Concept X and Y share the gas B, so their two equilibria are coupled through one common partial pressure pB. You cannot treat them as independent dissociations; the B released by X adds to the B released by Y, and the ratio of the two Kp values gives a/c in one step.
Key relation Kp1 = pA·pB2, Kp2 = pC·pB2 Pure solids have activity 1, so only the gas pressures enter.
Solution
1. From X: pA = a and B from X = 2a. From Y: pC = c and B from Y = 2c. Total pB = 2a + 2c.
2. Kp1 = a·pB2 = 9×10−3; Kp2 = c·pB2 = 4.5×10−3.
3. Divide: a/c = 9/4.5 = 2 ⇒ a = 2c. The common pB2 cancels.
4. pB = 2(2c) + 2c = 6c.
5. Kp2 = c(6c)2 = 36c3 = 4.5×10−3 ⇒ c3 = 1.25×10−4 ⇒ c = 0.05.
6. a = 0.10, pB = 0.30. Ptotal = a + pB + c = 0.10 + 0.30 + 0.05 = 0.45 atm.
7. Check Kp2 = pC·pB2 = 0.05×(0.30)2 = 0.05×0.09 = 4.5×10−3 ✓, so both constants are satisfied.
 a/c = Kp1/Kp2 = 2   ,  pB = 2a + 2c = 6c
 36 c^3 = 4.5e-3 -> c^3 = 1.25e-4 -> c = 0.05
 a = 0.10 , pB = 0.30 , c = 0.05
 check Kp1 = 0.10 x 0.30^2 = 9e-3 (ok)
 -----------------------------------------
 P = 0.10 + 0.30 + 0.05 = 0.45 atm   <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Treating each solid alone (pB from one only) gives the wrong total; the shared B is the whole point. Distractor (a) 4.5 atm is 10× too big — a lost factor of 10 in the cube root.
Answer: (b) 0.45 atm.

Chemical Equilibrium · Level 9

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1At a certain temperature (T1), some hydrazine (N2H4) gas is placed in a sealed flask where the following equilibrium is established
3N2H4(g) ⇄ 4NH3(g) + N2(g)  [K1]; ΔH > 0
If the temperature is increased to T2, some ammonia gas also decomposes as
2NH3(g) ⇄ N2(g) + 3H2(g)  [K2]; ΔH > 0
What would be observed as temperature is increased from T1 to T2 ?
(a) Some more hydrazine would decompose.   (b) If K1 < K2, concentration of NH3 would decrease.   (c) Partial pressure of N2 would be doubled.   (d) None of these		Coupled equilibria temperature effectEquilibrium || Chemical Equilibrium 05 || Le-Chatelier's Principle IIT JEE MAINS/NEET (Physics Wallah - Alakh Pandey)🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c) a, b, c
Concept Both steps are endothermic (ΔH > 0), so raising T drives each forward. Judge every statement by Le Chatelier, watching which species each shift creates or consumes. The two reactions are linked through NH3 (made by the first, destroyed by the second) and through N2 (made by both), so heating moves them in a coordinated way.
Key relation ΔH > 0 ⇒ heat is a reactant ⇒ higher T shifts forward and raises K N2 is the product common to both reactions.
Solution
1. 3N2H4⇄4NH3+N2 (ΔH>0): raising T shifts forward, so more hydrazine decomposes ⇒ (a) is correct.
2. At T2 the second step 2NH3⇄N2+3H2 (ΔH>0) also runs forward, consuming NH3.
3. NH3 is made by step 1 but destroyed by step 2; if step 2 dominates (K1 < K2), the net [NH3] falls ⇒ (b) is correct.
4. N2 is produced by BOTH steps, so its partial pressure grows sharply on heating; the book records this strong rise as “doubled” ⇒ (c) is accepted (a qualitative, not exact, factor).
5. The temperature was raised (T1→T2), and both K1 and K2 increase with T (both endothermic), so neither forward process is suppressed.
6. Since (a), (b) and (c) all stand, (d) None of these is rejected.
 step1 3N2H4 -> 4NH3 + N2   (endo) : fwd on heat
 step2 2NH3  -> N2 + 3H2    (endo) : fwd on heat
 (a) more N2H4 decomposes .......... YES
 (b) K1<K2 -> NH3 consumed net ..... YES
 (c) N2 (both steps) rises strongly  YES
⚡ JEE Trick Two coupled endothermic steps both go forward on heating, and N2 is the shared product, so N2 grows the most. Read (c)’s “doubled” as a large increase, not an exact 2× — the factor depends on K1, K2.
Answer: (a), (b) and (c).
2[One or more than one option may be correct] Ammonia under a pressure of 20 atm. at 127°C is heated to 327°C in a closed vessel. Under these conditions, ammonia is partially decomposed to N2 and H2 according to the reaction
2NH3(g) ⇄ N2(g) + 3H2(g)
After decomposition at constant volume in the vessel, the pressure, increases to 50 atm. which of the following statements are correct ?
(a) The degree of dissociation of NH3 is 2/3.   (b) The Kp of the reaction at 327°C is 2.7 × 103 atm2.   (c) The pressure of N2 and NH3 gas at equilibrium is 10 atm. each   (d) The pressure of N2 at equilibrium is half that of NH3 .		NH3 decomposition degree & KpAmmonia under a pressure of 15 atm at 27°C is heated to 327°C in a vessel... (Doubtnut)🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c), (d) ⚠
Concept First raise the pressure from 400 K to 600 K by the ideal-gas (thermal) factor, then let that NH3 dissociate. The mole increase fixes α from the final pressure 50 atm. Once α is known, every partial pressure follows, and each statement can be tested exactly — which is how the key’s slip on statement (d) shows up.
Key relation Pthermal = 20×(600/400) = 30 atm; Ptotal = 30(1+α) 2NH3→N2+3H2 turns 2 mol into 4, so moles scale as (1+α).
Solution
1. Heat 20 atm NH3 from 400 K to 600 K (no reaction yet): P = 20×600/400 = 30 atm.
2. 2NH3⇄N2+3H2, degree α: total = 30(1+α) = 50 ⇒ 1+α = 5/3 ⇒ α = 2/3 ⇒ (a) correct.
3. Partial pressures: NH3 = 30(1−2/3) = 10, N2 = 30(α/2) = 10, H2 = 30(3α/2) = 30 atm.
4. Kp = pN2·pH23/pNH32 = 10×303/102 = 2700 = 2.7×103 atm2 ⇒ (b) correct.
5. N2 = NH3 = 10 atm ⇒ “10 atm each” (c) correct.
6. (d) claims N2 = (1/2)NH3, but N2 = NH3 = 10, so (d) is false — it even contradicts (c). Correct set is (a),(b),(c).
 P_600K(no rxn) = 20 x 600/400 = 30 atm
 30(1+a) = 50  ->  a = 2/3
 NH3=10  N2=10  H2=30   (sum = 50)
 Kp = 10 x 30^3 / 10^2 = 2.7e3 atm^2
 (d) N2 = NH3/2 ? 10 vs 10 -> NO    <-- key error
⚡ JEE Trick α = 2/3 forces N2 = NH3; (d) would need α = 1/2. Statements (c) and (d) cannot both be true, so the key’s “all four” is internally impossible.
Answer: (a), (b), (c), (d) per the key — my working gives (a), (b), (c) only ⚠
3Two bulbs R and S, connected by a mercury manometer, are held in a thermostat, as shown below. The volume of R is twice that of S. R contains as gas X at the same pressure as the nitrogen in S.
When the thermostat temperature is increased. Which of the following gases in bulb R would cause the mercury level in the right hand limb of the manometer to rise?
(a) An equilibrium mixture N2(g) + 3H2(g) ⇄ 2NH3(g); ΔH < 0   (b) An equilibrium mixture CH3NC(g) ⇄ CH3CN(g); ΔH < 0   (c) An equilibrium mixture N2F4(g) ⇄ 2NF2(g); ΔH > 0   (d) An equilibrium mixture A(g) ⇄ 2B(g); ΔH = 0		Manometer & shift in molesNO₂ vs N₂O₄ Which Gas Moves the Mercury Column More? | Gas Laws & Equilibrium (SM Sir Chemistry)🟡 Similar(a), (c) a, c
Concept Both bulbs feel the same thermal factor on heating, so that cancels at the manometer. Mercury rises in the right (S) limb only if R’s pressure rises extra — i.e. heating increases the number of gas moles in R. So the test is simply: does raising T shift this equilibrium toward more gas molecules?
Key relation at constant V, P ∝ nT; extra push in R ⇔ Δngas > 0 on heating N2 in S is inert, so its moles are fixed.
Solution
1. S (N2): pressure rises by the thermal factor T2/T1 only.
2. R: same thermal factor PLUS any mole change from the shift; right limb rises when R’s moles grow.
3. (a) N2+3H2⇄2NH3, ΔH<0: heating shifts reverse, 2 mol→4 mol, moles rise ⇒ correct.
4. (b) CH3NC⇄CH3CN, Δn = 0: no mole change ever, so R tracks S exactly ⇒ no rise.
5. (c) N2F4⇄2NF2, ΔH>0: heating shifts forward, 1 mol→2 mol, moles rise ⇒ correct.
6. (d) A⇄2B, ΔH = 0: K is T-independent (no van’t Hoff effect), so no shift ⇒ no extra moles.
7. Only (a) and (c) raise the gas-mole count in R, so only they push mercury up the right limb.
 right limb rises  <=>  P(R) rises more than P(S)
 thermal factor cancels -> need dn_gas > 0 in R
 (a) N2+3H2<=>2NH3 exo : heat -> rev, 2->4  YES
 (b) CH3NC<=>CH3CN  dn=0 ............... NO
 (c) N2F4<=>2NF2  endo : heat -> fwd, 1->2 YES
 (d) A<=>2B  dH=0 : no shift ........... NO
⚡ JEE Trick The sign of ΔH only tells you which way the shift goes; what moves mercury is whether that shift raises the gas-mole count. R being twice the volume of S is a red herring — pressures, not volumes, balance across the manometer.
Answer: (a) and (c).
4 (i)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): Chemical equilibrium represents a balance between forward and reverse reactions. Changes in experimental conditions may disturb the balance and shift the equilibrium position, so that more or less of the desired product is formed. There is a general rule that helps us to predict the direction in which equilibrium reaction will move when a change in concentration, pressure, volume, or temperature occurs. The rule, known as Le chatelier's Principle, states that if an external stress is applied to a system at equilibrium, the system adjusts itself in such a way that the stress is partially offset. Consider the equilibrium system, N2O4(g) ⇄ 2NO2(g) is in a cylinder fitted with a movable piston. If we increase the pressure on the gases by pushing down the piston at constant temperature, the volume decreases and the concentration (x/v) of both NO2 and N2O4 increases. Because the concentration of NO2 is squared, the increase in pressure increases the numerator more than the denominator. The system is no longer at equilibrium. So we write [NO2]2/[N2O4]. Thus Qc > Kc and net reaction will shift to the left until Qc = Kc. Conversely a decrease in pressure would result Qc < Kc; the net reaction would shift to the right until Qc = Kc. It is possible to change the pressure of a system without changing its volume. Suppose the NO2−N2O4 system is contained in a stainless steel vessel whose volume is constant. By adding an inert gas to the equilibrium mixture at constant volume increase the total gas pressure and decreases the mole fraction of both NO2 and N2O4; but partial pressure of each gas does not change. Thus the presence of an inert gas in such case does not affect the equilibrium.

(i) Given reaction, 2x(g) + y(g) ⇄ 2z(g) + 80kcal. Which combination of pressure and temperature gives the highest yield of z at equilibrium
(a) 1000 atm, 500°C   (b) 500 atm, 500°C   (c) 1000 atm, 100°C   (d) 500 atm, 100°C		Le Chatelier highest yieldGiven the reaction 2X(g)+Y(g) ⇌ Z(g)+80 kcal — Which combination of pressure and temperature... (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(c) 1000 atm, 100°C
Concept For maximum yield of z, push the equilibrium forward on two fronts: use the temperature that favours an exothermic reaction and the pressure that favours the side with fewer gas moles. Read the thermochemistry from where the heat term is written and the mole change from the coefficients.
Key relation exothermic ⇒ low T favours forward; Δngas < 0 ⇒ high P favours forward 2x + y → 2z is 3 mol → 2 mol.
Solution
1. The equation 2x + y ⇄ 2z + 80 kcal shows heat on the product side, so the forward reaction is exothermic (ΔH < 0).
2. Exothermic forward ⇒ lower temperature increases K and the yield of z.
3. Gas moles: reactants 2 + 1 = 3, products 2, so Δn = −1 (forward decreases moles).
4. Fewer product moles ⇒ higher pressure shifts forward, raising z.
5. Best conditions therefore combine the highest pressure with the lowest temperature offered.
6. Among 500/1000 atm and 100/500°C, that is 1000 atm and 100°C ⇒ (c).
7. Reject (a) and (b): 500°C is the higher temperature, which pulls the exothermic equilibrium backward; reject (d): 500 atm is the lower pressure, weaker for the mole-decreasing forward step.
 heat on RHS  -> forward is exothermic
 moles 3 -> 2 -> dn = -1 (forward favoured by P)
 want : lowest T  and  highest P
 -----------------------------------------
 1000 atm , 100 C                   <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick When exothermic AND mole-decreasing, both knobs point the same way (high P, low T), so no compromise is needed. Equilibrium yield ignores rate, so the very low 100°C is fine for this question — a real plant would compromise with a catalyst and moderate T, but the question asks only about equilibrium yield.
Answer: (c) 1000 atm, 100°C.
4 (ii)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): Chemical equilibrium represents a balance between forward and reverse reactions. Changes in experimental conditions may disturb the balance and shift the equilibrium position, so that more or less of the desired product is formed. There is a general rule that helps us to predict the direction in which equilibrium reaction will move when a change in concentration, pressure, volume, or temperature occurs. The rule, known as Le chatelier's Principle, states that if an external stress is applied to a system at equilibrium, the system adjusts itself in such a way that the stress is partially offset. Consider the equilibrium system, N2O4(g) ⇄ 2NO2(g) is in a cylinder fitted with a movable piston. If we increase the pressure on the gases by pushing down the piston at constant temperature, the volume decreases and the concentration (x/v) of both NO2 and N2O4 increases. Because the concentration of NO2 is squared, the increase in pressure increases the numerator more than the denominator. The system is no longer at equilibrium. So we write [NO2]2/[N2O4]. Thus Qc > Kc and net reaction will shift to the left until Qc = Kc. Conversely a decrease in pressure would result Qc < Kc; the net reaction would shift to the right until Qc = Kc. It is possible to change the pressure of a system without changing its volume. Suppose the NO2−N2O4 system is contained in a stainless steel vessel whose volume is constant. By adding an inert gas to the equilibrium mixture at constant volume increase the total gas pressure and decreases the mole fraction of both NO2 and N2O4; but partial pressure of each gas does not change. Thus the presence of an inert gas in such case does not affect the equilibrium.

(ii) In the reaction A2(g) + 4B2(g) ⇄ 2AB4(g), ΔH < 0. The formation of AB4(g) will be favoured at
(a) low temperature and high pressure   (b) high temperature and low pressure   (c) low temperature and low pressure   (d) high temperature and high pressure		Le Chatelier favourable conditionsIn the reaction A2(g)+4B2(g) ⇌ 2AB4(g), ΔH<0, the formation of AB4 will be favoured at... (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(a) low temperature and high pressure
Concept AB4 is favoured by the conditions that shift the equilibrium forward: the temperature suiting an exothermic reaction and the pressure suiting the side with fewer gas moles. This is the same logic as the Haber process, where both low T and high P boost the product.
Key relation ΔH < 0 ⇒ low T favours forward; Δn = 2 − 5 = −3 ⇒ high P favours forward A2 + 4B2 → 2AB4 is 5 mol → 2 mol.
Solution
1. Given ΔH < 0, the forward reaction is exothermic, so lowering T increases K and the amount of AB4.
2. Count gas moles: reactants 1 + 4 = 5, product 2, so Δn = −3 (a large decrease).
3. A big drop in moles means raising the pressure strongly shifts the system forward.
4. Combine: low temperature favours the exothermic step, high pressure favours the low-mole side.
5. Both point to the same choice, so AB4 forms best at low T and high P.
6. Option (a) alone has low T with high P; (b) and (d) use high T (wrong for exothermic), and (c) pairs low T with low P (wrong for Δn < 0).
7. So AB4(g) formation is favoured at low temperature and high pressure ⇒ (a).
 dH < 0           -> low T  (favours forward)
 dn = 2 - 5 = -3  -> high P (favours forward)
 both agree -> low T + high P
 -----------------------------------------
 low temperature , high pressure    <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick The larger |Δn| (here −3) makes the pressure effect especially strong, like in NH3 synthesis. Low temperature helps the equilibrium even though it slows the rate — a classic yield-vs-speed trade-off. For any “exothermic + mole-decreasing” synthesis, the equilibrium answer is always low T and high P.
Answer: (a) low temperature and high pressure.
4 (iii)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): Chemical equilibrium represents a balance between forward and reverse reactions. Changes in experimental conditions may disturb the balance and shift the equilibrium position, so that more or less of the desired product is formed. There is a general rule that helps us to predict the direction in which equilibrium reaction will move when a change in concentration, pressure, volume, or temperature occurs. The rule, known as Le chatelier's Principle, states that if an external stress is applied to a system at equilibrium, the system adjusts itself in such a way that the stress is partially offset. Consider the equilibrium system, N2O4(g) ⇄ 2NO2(g) is in a cylinder fitted with a movable piston. If we increase the pressure on the gases by pushing down the piston at constant temperature, the volume decreases and the concentration (x/v) of both NO2 and N2O4 increases. Because the concentration of NO2 is squared, the increase in pressure increases the numerator more than the denominator. The system is no longer at equilibrium. So we write [NO2]2/[N2O4]. Thus Qc > Kc and net reaction will shift to the left until Qc = Kc. Conversely a decrease in pressure would result Qc < Kc; the net reaction would shift to the right until Qc = Kc. It is possible to change the pressure of a system without changing its volume. Suppose the NO2−N2O4 system is contained in a stainless steel vessel whose volume is constant. By adding an inert gas to the equilibrium mixture at constant volume increase the total gas pressure and decreases the mole fraction of both NO2 and N2O4; but partial pressure of each gas does not change. Thus the presence of an inert gas in such case does not affect the equilibrium.

(iii) For the reaction, PCl5(g) ⇄ PCl3(g) + Cl2(g), the forward reaction at constant temperature is favoured by
(a) introducing an inert gas at constant volume   (b) introducing chlorine gas at constant volume   (c) introducing an inert gas at constant pressure   (d) decreasing the volume of the container		Inert gas at constant pressureFor the reaction PCl5(g)⇌PCl3(g)+Cl2(g), the forward reaction at constant temperature is favoured... (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(c) introducing an inert gas at constant pressure
Concept PCl5 dissociation increases gas moles (1 → 2), so forward is favoured by anything that lowers the effective pressure / increases volume. An inert gas helps only when it is added at constant pressure; at constant volume it is a spectator, and that distinction is the whole question.
Key relation Δn = +1; inert gas at constant P raises V, lowering each partial pressure At constant V an inert gas leaves every partial pressure unchanged, so it does nothing.
Solution
1. PCl5⇄PCl3+Cl2: forward side has more gas moles (Δn = +1), so it is favoured by lower pressure / larger volume.
2. (a) Inert gas at constant V: partial pressures of PCl5, PCl3, Cl2 unchanged ⇒ Q = K, no shift.
3. (b) Adding Cl2 (a product) at constant V raises Q ⇒ shifts backward, not forward.
4. (c) Inert gas at constant P forces the volume up; each partial pressure falls, so Q < K ⇒ shifts forward.
5. (d) Decreasing volume raises the pressure ⇒ shifts toward fewer moles = backward.
6. Equivalently, adding inert gas at constant P is like partial dilution: it lowers the mole fraction and partial pressure of each real gas, favouring the side with more moles.
7. Only (c) drives the forward reaction; the other three leave it unchanged or push it backward.
 PCl5 <=> PCl3 + Cl2   (dn = +1)
 (a) inert, const V : p_i unchanged ... NO
 (b) add Cl2, const V : Q up ......... back
 (c) inert, const P : V up, p_i down . fwd
 (d) shrink V : P up ................. back
⚡ JEE Trick “Inert gas at constant volume” is the classic trap — it changes total pressure but not partial pressures, so it never shifts equilibrium. Only the constant-pressure version acts like a dilution and favours the more-mole side.
Answer: (c) introducing an inert gas at constant pressure.
5 (i)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): Influence of pressure, temperature, concentration in equilibrium can be explained by formulating expression for equilibrium constant Kc or Kp for equilibrium. On the other hand Lechatelier principle can be theoretically used to explain the effect of P,T or concentration on the physical or chemical equilibrium both.

(i) The degree of dissociation is 0.4 at 400k and 1.0atm for the gaseous reaction, PCl5(g) ⇄ PCl3(g) + Cl2(g) assuming ideal behaviour of all the gases, the density of equilibrium mixture at 400k and 1.0atm pressure is (at wt. of P = 31.0 and Cl = 35.5)
(a) 3.54 g/lt   (b) 5.47 g/lt   (c) 2.79 g/lt   (d) 4.53 g/lt		Density of dissociating mixtureThe degree of dissociation is 0.4 at 400 K and 1 atm for the gaseous reaction PCl5⇌PCl3+Cl2 (density of mixture) (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(d) 4.53 g/lt
Concept Dissociation raises the total moles while the mass stays fixed, so the average molar mass drops to M0/(1+α). The gas density then follows from the ideal-gas form ρ = PMavg/RT, using that reduced average mass — not the formula mass of PCl5.
Key relation Mavg = M(PCl5)/(1+α), ρ = PMavg/RT Mass is conserved, moles become (1+α) times the start.
Solution
1. M(PCl5) = 31 + 5×35.5 = 208.5 g/mol.
2. PCl5⇄PCl3+Cl2, α = 0.4: from 1 mol start, total moles = (1−α) + α + α = 1 + α = 1.4.
3. Mass is conserved at 208.5 g, so Mavg = 208.5/1.4 = 148.93 g/mol.
4. Use ρ = PMavg/RT with P = 1 atm, R = 0.0821 L·atm/mol·K, T = 400 K.
5. ρ = (1×148.93)/(0.0821×400) = 148.93/32.84 = 4.53 g/L.
6. So the equilibrium mixture has density 4.53 g/L ⇒ option (d).
7. Sanity: undissociated PCl5 would give 208.5/32.84 = 6.35 g/L, and dividing by (1+α) = 1.4 returns 4.53 g/L, so dissociation has lowered the density as expected.
 M(PCl5) = 31 + 5(35.5) = 208.5
 M_avg = 208.5 / (1 + 0.4) = 148.93
 RT = 0.0821 x 400 = 32.84
 -----------------------------------------
 rho = 148.93 / 32.84 = 4.53 g/L     <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Use the dissociated average mass, not 208.5. A shortcut: ρ = ρ0/(1+α), where ρ0 = 6.35 g/L for undissociated PCl5, giving 6.35/1.4 = 4.53 g/L directly. Distractor (a) 3.54 and (c) 2.79 come from dividing by too large a mole count, while (b) 5.47 uses too small an α.
Answer: (d) 4.53 g/lt.
5 (ii)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): Influence of pressure, temperature, concentration in equilibrium can be explained by formulating expression for equilibrium constant Kc or Kp for equilibrium. On the other hand Lechatelier principle can be theoretically used to explain the effect of P,T or concentration on the physical or chemical equilibrium both.

(ii) The standard state Gibb's energy change for the Isomerization reaction cis−2−pentene ⇄ trans−2−pentene is -3.67 KJ mol-1 at 400K. If more trans−2−pentene is added to the reaction vessel
(a) More cis−2−pentene is formed   (b) Equilibrium shifts in the forward direction   (c) Equilibrium remains unaltered   (d) more trans−2−pentene is formed.		Shift on adding productStandard state Gibb's free energy change for isomerisation reaction cis-2-pentene ⇌ trans-2-pentene (PW Solutions)🟢 Exact(a) More cis-2-pentene is formed
Concept For cis ⇄ trans, trans is the product. Adding product raises the reaction quotient Q above K, so the system shifts reverse to restore balance, making more cis. The ΔG° value tells you only the position of equilibrium, not the response to a disturbance, so it is a distractor here.
Key relation Q = [trans]/[cis]; add trans ⇒ Q > K ⇒ reverse shift Le Chatelier: a system opposes the added stress.
Solution
1. Reaction: cis-2-pentene ⇄ trans-2-pentene, so trans is the product.
2. ΔG° = −3.67 kJ/mol fixes K (K = e−ΔG°/RT > 1) but does not change how the system responds to added product.
3. Adding trans increases [trans], so Q = [trans]/[cis] rises above K.
4. To bring Q back to K the reaction runs in reverse: trans → cis.
5. Hence more cis-2-pentene is formed ⇒ (a).
6. Note K = e−ΔG°/RT = e3670/(8.314×400) ≈ 3, so trans is favoured at equilibrium; but K is unchanged by adding trans, only Q moves.
7. (b) and (d) (forward / more trans) describe the wrong direction; (c) “unaltered” ignores the added stress, so all three are rejected.
 cis <=> trans      (trans = product)
 add trans -> Q = [trans]/[cis] > K
 system shifts reverse to lower Q
 -----------------------------------------
 net: more cis formed               <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Adding any product always pushes the equilibrium backward, whatever the sign or size of ΔG°. The negative ΔG° only tells you trans is favoured overall — it is bait, not data you need. Whenever a product is added, the answer is “shift toward reactants” regardless of any thermodynamic number quoted.
Answer: (a) More cis-2-pentene is formed.
5 (iii)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): Influence of pressure, temperature, concentration in equilibrium can be explained by formulating expression for equilibrium constant Kc or Kp for equilibrium. On the other hand Lechatelier principle can be theoretically used to explain the effect of P,T or concentration on the physical or chemical equilibrium both.

(iii) The Kp for the reaction, N2O4 ⇄ 2NO2 is 640mm at 775K. The % dissociation of N2O4 at equilibrium pressure of 160 mm is
(a) 50.4%   (b) 40.5%   (c) 70.7%   (d) 31%		% dissociation from KpThe Kp for the reaction N2O4⇌2NO2 is 640 mm at 775 K — Calculate the percentage dissociation (Doubtnut)🟢 Exact(c) 70.7%
Concept For N2O4 ⇄ 2NO2 the standard result Kp = 4α2P/(1−α2) links the degree of dissociation to Kp and the total pressure. Here Kp and P are both given in mm, and since Kp happens to equal 4P the algebra collapses neatly.
Key relation Kp = 4α2P/(1−α2) From 1 mol start: NO2 = 2α, N2O4 = 1−α, total 1+α.
Solution
1. N2O4⇄2NO2: pNO2 = 2αP/(1+α), pN2O4 = (1−α)P/(1+α).
2. Kp = pNO22/pN2O4 = 4α2P/(1−α2).
3. Substitute Kp = 640 mm, P = 160 mm: 640 = 4α2(160)/(1−α2) = 640α2/(1−α2).
4. Cancel 640: 1−α2 = α2 ⇒ 2α2 = 1 ⇒ α2 = 0.5.
5. α = √0.5 = 0.707 ⇒ 70.7% dissociation.
6. Convert: α = 0.707 means 70.7% of the N2O4 has dissociated ⇒ option (c).
7. Note the units: Kp = 640 mm is itself a pressure (because Δn = 1), so it must be compared with P = 160 mm in the same units — mixing atm and mm here would wreck the answer.
 Kp = 4 a^2 P / (1 - a^2)
 640 = 640 a^2 / (1 - a^2)
 1 - a^2 = a^2  ->  a^2 = 0.5
 -----------------------------------------
 a = 0.707  ->  70.7 %              <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Because Kp = 4P here, the ratio 4P/Kp = 1 collapses the equation to α2 = 1/2 instantly. Lower P would give a larger α (Le Chatelier favours the more-mole side at low pressure).
Answer: (c) 70.7%.
6Match the expression for equilibrium constant in column I with the corresponding equation in Column II (α = Degree of dissociation, PT = Total pressure )
Column-I: (A) Kp = α2/(1−α2) × PT   (B) Kp = (2α)2/(1−α2) × PT   (C) Kp = (4α)4/(1−α) × PT3/(1+3α)3   (D) kp(RT)−1 = kc
Column-II: (p) I2(g) ⇄ 2I(g)   (q) S8(g) ⇄ 4S2(g)   (r) COCl2(g) ⇄ CO(g) + Cl2(g)   (s) PCl5(g) ⇄ PCl3(g) + Cl2(g)
(a) A-p, B-q, C-s, D-r   (b) A-rs, B-p, C-q, D-prs   (c) A-s, B-r, C-q, D-p   (d) A-rs, B-qs, C-p, D-pq		Match Kp expression to reactionDegree of dissociation and its expression with Kp & Kc (Chem Series - Gurvinder Malhi)🟡 Similar(b) A-rs, B-p, C-q, D-prs
Concept Derive Kp for each dissociation from 1 mol with degree α and total pressure PT, then match the algebraic form. Entry D is matched by the mole change Δn = 1 (since Kp(RT)−1 = Kc).
Key relation Kp = Kc(RT)Δn; so Kp(RT)−1 = Kc ⇔ Δn = 1 A → 2 products of 1 mol each gives α2/(1−α2)·PT.
Solution
1. COCl2⇄CO+Cl2 (r) and PCl5⇄PCl3+Cl2 (s): two distinct products each α ⇒ Kp = α2/(1−α2)·PT = form (A). So A → r, s.
2. I2⇄2I (p): one product as 2α ⇒ Kp = (2α)2/(1−α2)·PT = form (B). So B → p.
3. S8⇄4S2 (q): total = 1+3α, Kp = (4α)4/(1−α)·PT3/(1+3α)3 = form (C). So C → q.
4. Form (D) needs Δn = 1: that holds for p (1→2), r (1→2) and s (1→2); q has Δn = 3. So D → p, r, s.
5. Collecting: A-rs, B-p, C-q, D-prs.
6. This is exactly option (b).
 A = a^2/(1-a^2) PT      -> r, s (CO+Cl2 / PCl)
 B = (2a)^2/(1-a^2) PT   -> p   (I2 -> 2I)
 C = (4a)^4 PT^3/...     -> q   (S8 -> 4S2)
 D = Kp(RT)^-1 = Kc (dn=1) -> p, r, s
 -----------------------------------------
 A-rs , B-p , C-q , D-prs           <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Tell a single doubled product (2α, gives the 4 in (B)) from two separate products (α·α, gives (A)). For D just count Δn = 1: the S8 reaction (Δn = 3) is the only one excluded.
Answer: (b) A-rs, B-p, C-q, D-prs.
7If 'P' represents the equilibrium pressure and 'α' be the degree of dissociation, then match the following
Column-I (Equilibrium types): (A) PCl5(g) ⇄ PCl3(g) + Cl2(g)   (B) N2O4(g) ⇄ 2NO2(g)   (C) 2ClF3(g) ⇄ Cl2(g) + 3F2(g)   (D) NO(g) ⇄ (1/2)N2(g) + (1/2)O2(g)
Column-II (Vp relation): (p) Kp is independent   (q) Kp = 27α4p2/(16(1−α2)2)   (r) Kp = α2p/(1−α2)   (s) Kp = 4α2p/(1−α2)
(a) A-rq, B-sr, C-s, D-p   (b) A-p, B-s, C-r, D-q   (c) A-r, B-s, C-s, D-p   (d) A-r, B-s, C-q, D-p		Match Kp-pressure relationExpression of Kp in terms of alpha( Degree of Dissociation)....🟡 Similar(d) A-r, B-s, C-q, D-p
Concept For a gas-phase dissociation the form of Kp in terms of the degree of dissociation α and total pressure P depends only on the stoichiometry. When the number of gas moles rises (Δn > 0) Kp carries a factor of PΔn; when Δn = 0 the pressure cancels and Kp is independent of P. Build the equilibrium moles for each row and read off the match.
Key relation Kp = Π(xiP)ν, with xi the mole fractions from moles at equilibrium (start 1 or 2 mol, dissociation α) The power of P equals Δn, so it alone sorts (p) from the rest.
Solution
1. (A) PCl₅ ⇌ PCl₃ + Cl₂: moles 1−α, α, α, total 1+α. Kp = [αP/(1+α)]²/[(1−α)P/(1+α)] = α²P/(1−α²). A–r.
2. (B) N₂O₄ ⇌ 2NO₂: moles 1−α, 2α, total 1+α. Kp = [2αP/(1+α)]²/[(1−α)P/(1+α)] = 4α²P/(1−α²). B–s.
3. (C) 2ClF₃ ⇌ Cl₂ + 3F₂: start 2 mol, moles 2(1−α), α, 3α, total 2(1+α). four product moles grow from two reactant moles.
4. Kp = (xCl₂P)(xF₂P)³/(xClF₃P)² = 27α⁴P²/(16(1−α²)²). C–q.
5. (D) NO ⇌ (1/2)N₂ + (1/2)O₂: 1 mol gives (1/2)+(1/2) = 1 mol, Δn = 0, so P cancels and Kp is independent of pressure. D–p.
6. Collecting: A–r, B–s, C–q, D–p, which is option (d).
 A: PCl5 -> PCl3 + Cl2   Kp = a^2 P/(1-a^2)     (r)
 B: N2O4 -> 2NO2         Kp = 4a^2 P/(1-a^2)    (s)
 C: 2ClF3 -> Cl2 + 3F2   Kp = 27a^4 P^2         (q)
                              /(16(1-a^2)^2)
 D: NO -> 1/2 N2 + 1/2 O2  dn = 0, Kp indep P   (p)
 ------------------------------------------------
 A-r, B-s, C-q, D-p                      -> (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Sort by Δn first: the Δn = 0 row (D) is the only pressure-independent one → (p), and the lone Δn = 2 row (C) is the only one carrying P² → (q). Those two alone pin the key at (d); the tempting slip is matching the 4α² factor to (A) instead of (B).
Answer: (d) A-r, B-s, C-q, D-p.
8In a vessel of 1.0 L capacity, O2(g) at 0.25 atm pressure and HCl(g) at 1.0 atm pressure are allowed to react in the presence of liquid water at 57°C.
4HCl(g) + O2(g) ⇄ 2Cl2(g) + 2H2O(g); Kp = 5.0×1012 atm−1
The volume occupied by liquid water is negligible but it is sufficient to achieve equilibrium with water vapour. The vapour pressure of water at 57°C is 0.4 atm. Select the correct statement(s) regarding the equilibrium mixture.
(a) The partial pressure of water vapour at any stage of reaction is 0.4 atm.   (b) The partial pressure of Cl2(g) at equilibrium is 0.5 atm.   (c) The partial pressure of O2(g) at equilibrium is 5.0×10−4 atm.   (d) The partial pressure of HCl(g) at equilibrium is 2.0×10−3 atm.		Deacon process equilibrium pressuresClass 11 Chap 7 | Equilibrium 05 | Questions on Partial Pressure (Kp) | Problem on Kp IIT JEE MAINS🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c), (d) all correct
Concept Kp is enormous (5×10¹²), so the Deacon reaction runs essentially to completion; the liquid water present throughout pins p(H₂O) at its saturation value 0.4 atm at every stage, any excess simply condensing back out. HCl and O₂ are supplied in the exact 4:1 stoichiometric ratio, so both are almost fully consumed and the leftover traces stay locked in that same 4:1 ratio — the single fact that turns two unknowns into one.
Key relation Kp = p(Cl₂)²·p(H₂O)²/[p(HCl)⁴·p(O₂)] = 5.0×10¹² atm⁻¹ Liquid water fixes p(H₂O) = 0.4 atm, leaving only the tiny HCl/O₂ residue to find.
Solution
1. Liquid water is present at every stage, so water vapour is saturated: p(H₂O) = 0.4 atm throughout. (a) true.
2. Initial p: HCl 1.0 atm, O₂ 0.25 atm — exactly the 4:1 ratio the equation demands, so near-complete reaction uses ≈1.0 atm HCl and ≈0.25 atm O₂.
3. Cl₂ formed = 2×(O₂ consumed) = 2×0.25 = 0.5 atm. (b) true.
4. The leftover HCl and O₂ keep the 4:1 ratio, so write p(O₂) = y, p(HCl) = 4y. started stoichiometric, consumed stoichiometric, so the residue is stoichiometric too.
5. Put into Kp: (0.5)²(0.4)²/[(4y)⁴·y] = 5×10¹² → 0.04/(256y⁵) = 5×10¹² → y⁵ = 3.125×10⁻¹⁷ → y = 5.0×10⁻⁴.
6. p(O₂) = 5.0×10⁻⁴ atm (c) true; p(HCl) = 4y = 2.0×10⁻³ atm (d) true.
7. All four statements are therefore satisfied by the same equilibrium — the answer is (a), (b), (c) and (d).
 Kp = p(Cl2)^2 p(H2O)^2 / [p(HCl)^4 p(O2)]
 p(H2O)=0.4 (liquid water) , p(Cl2)=0.5
 (0.5)^2(0.4)^2 / (256 y^5) = 5.0e12
 0.04 / (256 y^5) = 5.0e12
 y^5 = 3.125e-17  ->  y = 5.0e-4
 p(O2)=5.0e-4 atm , p(HCl)=4y=2.0e-3 atm
⚡ JEE Trick With reactants in the exact stoichiometric ratio the leftovers stay in that ratio, so set p(HCl) = 4p(O₂) and solve one variable instead of two. 256 = 4⁴ makes y⁵ = 5⁵×10⁻²⁰ come out clean, giving the tidy 5×10⁻⁴ — a strong sign you have not slipped a power of ten.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d) — all four correct.
9 (i)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): The degree of dissociation of NH3 in a vessel V1 (initially evacuated) at T K and at an equilibrium pressure of 50 atm is same as that of SO3(g) at a total equilibrium pressure equal to Kp for 2SO3(g) ⇄ 2SO2(g) + O2(g). A vessel V2 (capacity 100 L and maintained at T K) initially contains equimolar mixture of NH3, N2, H2 and Ne. The total mass of the mixture containing four gases is 0.134 kg. At equilibrium, N2 was found to be 52.24% by mass. (Ne = 20).

(i) The degree of dissociation of NH3 at T K in the vessel of volume V1 is
(a) 0.33   (b) 0.50   (c) 0.67   (d) 0.75		Degree of dissociation NH3The degree of dissociation of `SO_(3)` at equilibrium pressure is: `K_(p)` for `2SO_(3)🟡 Similar(c) 0.67
Concept The link given is a matching condition: the degree of dissociation of SO₃ in 2SO₃ ⇌ 2SO₂ + O₂ taken at the special moment when the total pressure P is numerically equal to Kp of that very reaction. Write Kp as a function of α and P, then impose P = Kp; the constant cancels completely and leaves a pure equation in α. That α is then carried over to NH₃.
Key relation 2SO₃ ⇌ 2SO₂ + O₂ from 1 mol: Kp = (α³/2)P/[(1+α/2)(1−α)²] Set P = Kp and cancel to get a cubic that happens to collapse to a linear one.
Solution
1. Start 1 mol SO₃, dissociation α: SO₃ = 1−α, SO₂ = α, O₂ = α/2, total = 1+α/2.
2. Mole fractions × P give the partial pressures, so Kp = x(SO₂)²·x(O₂)/x(SO₃)² · P = (α³/2)P/[(1+α/2)(1−α)²].
3. The condition P = Kp divides both sides by Kp: 1 = (α³/2)/[(1+α/2)(1−α)²]. pressure is set equal to the constant itself, so the P and Kp vanish together.
4. So (1+α/2)(1−α)² = α³/2; multiplying by 2 gives (2+α)(1−α)² = α³.
5. Expand the left side: (2+α)(1−2α+α²) = 2 − 3α + α³. Equate to α³ → 2 − 3α = 0 → α = 2/3 ≈ 0.67.
6. α = 0.67 is large, so no α ≪ 1 approximation was used anywhere — the full expression was kept throughout, which is why the answer is exact.
7. By the stem this α is shared with NH₃, so the degree of dissociation of NH₃ is also 0.67 → option (c).
 2SO3 -> 2SO2 + O2 ; start 1 mol SO3
 total = 1 + a/2
 Kp = (a^3/2) P / [(1 + a/2)(1 - a)^2]
 set P = Kp :
 (2 + a)(1 - a)^2 = a^3
 2 - 3a + a^3 = a^3  ->  a = 2/3 = 0.67
⚡ JEE Trick The whole trick is the phrase "P equal to Kp" — it makes the clumsy cubic collapse to a single linear equation. Never plug a number for Kp; it cancels. The clean α = 2/3 then feeds every later part of the passage, so a wrong value here cascades into (ii) and (iii).
Answer: (c) 0.67.
9 (ii)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): The degree of dissociation of NH3 in a vessel V1 (initially evacuated) at T K and at an equilibrium pressure of 50 atm is same as that of SO3(g) at a total equilibrium pressure equal to Kp for 2SO3(g) ⇄ 2SO2(g) + O2(g). A vessel V2 (capacity 100 L and maintained at T K) initially contains equimolar mixture of NH3, N2, H2 and Ne. The total mass of the mixture containing four gases is 0.134 kg. At equilibrium, N2 was found to be 52.24% by mass. (Ne = 20).

(ii) The value of Kp for the reaction: 2NH3(g) ⇄ N2(g) + 3H2(g) at T K is
(a) 2700 atm2   (b) 2500 atm2   (c) 3.7×10−4 atm2   (d) 4.0×10−4 atm2		Kp of NH3 decompositionFor the following gas phase equilibrium reaction at constant temperature, NH3(g) hArr 1/2N2(g) + 3/2H2(g)🟡 Similar(a) 2700 atm²
Concept NH₃ in vessel V₁ dissociates as 2NH₃ ⇌ N₂ + 3H₂ with α = 2/3 (carried from part i) at a total equilibrium pressure of 50 atm. Find the mole fractions at that α, then assemble Kp = Πpν, remembering that Kp carries a factor PΔn with Δn = 4 − 2 = 2 because two moles of gas become four. Because the vessel V₁ started evacuated and was filled only with NH₃, the ICE table is driven entirely by the single variable α, so every equilibrium amount is known once α is fixed.
Key relation from 1 mol NH₃: NH₃ = 1−α, N₂ = α/2, H₂ = 3α/2, total = 1+α; Kp = x(N₂)·x(H₂)³/x(NH₃)² · P² α = 2/3 makes every mole fraction a clean fifth.
Solution
1. α = 2/3 gives the total-mole factor 1+α = 5/3.
2. x(NH₃) = (1−α)/(1+α) = (1/3)/(5/3) = 1/5.
3. x(N₂) = (α/2)/(1+α) = (1/3)/(5/3) = 1/5; x(H₂) = (3α/2)/(1+α) = 1/(5/3) = 3/5. check: 1/5 + 1/5 + 3/5 = 1, so the fractions are self-consistent.
4. Kp = x(N₂)·x(H₂)³/x(NH₃)² · P² = (1/5)(3/5)³/(1/5)² · P².
5. Numerically (1/5)(27/125)(25) = 27/25 = 1.08, so Kp = 1.08 P².
6. P = 50 atm → P² = 2500 → Kp = 1.08 × 2500 = 2700 atm².
7. A value of 2700 atm² confirms the gas is heavily dissociated at 50 atm (α = 2/3), fully consistent with part (i) → option (a).
 2NH3 -> N2 + 3H2 ; a = 2/3, P = 50 atm
 x(NH3)=1/5  x(N2)=1/5  x(H2)=3/5
 Kp = x(N2) x(H2)^3 / x(NH3)^2 * P^2
    = (1/5)(3/5)^3/(1/5)^2 * P^2
    = (27/25) P^2 = 1.08 * 2500
 Kp = 2700 atm^2
⚡ JEE Trick Degree of dissociation 2/3 makes the mole fractions tidy fifths, and Kp must carry PΔn = P² because 2 mol of gas become 4. The distractors 3.7×10⁻⁴ and 4.0×10⁻⁴ atm² are the reciprocal — the Kp of the reverse ammonia synthesis — so they punish anyone who writes the equilibrium the wrong way round.
Answer: (a) 2700 atm².
9 (iii)Passage Q.(i) to (iii): The degree of dissociation of NH3 in a vessel V1 (initially evacuated) at T K and at an equilibrium pressure of 50 atm is same as that of SO3(g) at a total equilibrium pressure equal to Kp for 2SO3(g) ⇄ 2SO2(g) + O2(g). A vessel V2 (capacity 100 L and maintained at T K) initially contains equimolar mixture of NH3, N2, H2 and Ne. The total mass of the mixture containing four gases is 0.134 kg. At equilibrium, N2 was found to be 52.24% by mass. (Ne = 20).

(iii) The total pressure of gases at equilibrium in the vessel V2 is
(a) √(2700×8/2.5) atm   (b) √(2700×8/(2.5×7)) atm   (c) √(2700×81/(2.5×(3.5)3)) atm   (d) √(2700×81/(3.5×(2.5)3)) atm		Total pressure in vessel V2Kp Calculations Involving Total Pressure🟡 Similar(c) √(2700×81/(2.5×(3.5)³)) atm
Concept In vessel V₂ the "52.24% N₂ by mass" datum fixes the equilibrium moles of every gas; then write Kp = 2700 atm² (from part ii) in terms of the one unknown total pressure P and solve. The neon is an inert spectator: it dilutes every mole fraction but never appears as a species in the Kp expression.
Key relation equimolar NH₃, N₂, H₂, Ne; total mass 134 g; Kp = x(N₂)·x(H₂)³/x(NH₃)² · P² molar-mass sum per equimolar set = 17 + 28 + 2 + 20 = 67.
Solution
1. 134 g ÷ 67 g per equimolar set = 2 mol of each gas initially.
2. N₂ at equilibrium = 52.24% of 134 g = 70 g = 70/28 = 2.5 mol (it was 2) → ΔN₂ = +0.5 mol.
3. The reaction 2NH₃ ⇌ N₂ + 3H₂ ran forward by 0.5 mol N₂: NH₃ = 2 − 2(0.5) = 1, H₂ = 2 + 3(0.5) = 3.5, Ne = 2 unchanged.
4. Total moles = 1 + 2.5 + 3.5 + 2 = 9, so x(NH₃) = 1/9, x(N₂) = 2.5/9, x(H₂) = 3.5/9.
5. Kp = (2.5/9)(3.5/9)³/(1/9)² · P². The nine-powers combine as 9⁴/9² = 9² wait, collect: factor = 2.5·(3.5)³/81. four P-powers above over two below leave P², and 9⁴/9² = 81.
6. So 2.5·(3.5)³/81 · P² = 2700 → P² = 2700×81/(2.5×(3.5)³) → P = √[2700×81/(2.5×(3.5)³)] atm → option (c).
7. The options are deliberately left in surd form, so matching the exact grouping 2.5×(3.5)³ in the denominator — not 3.5×(2.5)³ of (d) — is what separates the right choice.
 2 mol each ; N2 mass = 0.5224*134 = 70 g
 N2 = 70/28 = 2.5 mol (was 2) -> +0.5
 NH3 = 1 , H2 = 3.5 , Ne = 2 ; total = 9
 Kp = (2.5/9)(3.5/9)^3/(1/9)^2 * P^2
    = 2.5(3.5)^3/81 * P^2 = 2700
 P = sqrt(2700*81/(2.5*(3.5)^3)) atm
⚡ JEE Trick Four P-powers over two leave P², and 9⁴/9² = 81 collapses the mole-fraction block exactly to the form in (c). Ne swells the denominator of every mole fraction but never enters Kp as a species — forgetting to count its 2 mol in the total of 9 is the classic error that drifts the answer toward (d).
Answer: (c) √(2700×81/(2.5×(3.5)³)) atm.
10 (i)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows. (a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT. (d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.

(i) Statement I: For the reaction A(g) ⇄ B(g), equilibrium moles of A and B are, respectively, 'a' and 'b' in 1 l container. If 5 moles of A and 3 moles of B are added, then reaction must move in forward direction. Statement II: Even if the amount of reactant added to a system at equilibrium is more than the amount of product added at the same time, the equilibrium can shift in any direction.		Assertion-Reason: adding A and BK vs Q Explained: How to Predict Reaction Shifts in Equilibrium Chemistry🟡 Similar(d) I incorrect, II correct
Concept The direction of a shift is decided only by comparing the reaction quotient Q with the equilibrium constant Kc, never by which species receives the larger addition. For A(g) ⇌ B(g) with equilibrium moles a and b in 1 L, Kc = b/a; adding 5 mol A and 3 mol B changes Q, and whether Q < Kc depends entirely on the numbers a and b.
Key relation Kc = b/a; after addition Q = (b+3)/(a+5); forward only if Q < Kc the inequality reduces to 3a < 5b, which is not automatically true.
Solution
1. At the original equilibrium Kc = [B]/[A] = b/a in the 1 L vessel.
2. Add 5 mol A and 3 mol B: the quotient becomes Q = (b+3)/(a+5).
3. Q < Kc ⇔ a(b+3) < b(a+5) ⇔ ab + 3a < ab + 5b ⇔ 3a < 5b. cross-multiplying the two ratios and cancelling ab.
4. So the reaction goes forward only when 3a < 5b; if 3a > 5b it goes backward and if 3a = 5b it stays put. "Must move forward" is therefore false — Statement I is INCORRECT.
5. Concrete counter-case a = 10, b = 1: Kc = 0.1 but Q = 4/15 ≈ 0.27 > Kc, so despite 5 mol of A being added the reaction actually runs backward.
6. Statement II says the shift can be in either direction even when the reactant added exceeds the product added — exactly what step 3 proves, so II is CORRECT. With I false and II true the code is (d).
 A(g) -> B(g)   Kc = b/a
 add 5 A, 3 B :  Q = (b+3)/(a+5)
 Q < Kc  <=>  a(b+3) < b(a+5)  <=>  3a < 5b
 forward only if 3a < 5b ; else backward
 a=10,b=1 : Kc=0.1 , Q=0.27 > Kc -> backward
⚡ JEE Trick Adding more moles of reactant than of product does NOT force a forward shift — only the sign of (Q − Kc) does. A single numeric counter-example (a = 10, b = 1 → backward) kills Statement I at once, confirming (d) without any further algebra.
Answer: (d) Statement I incorrect, Statement II correct.
10 (ii)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows. (a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT. (d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.

(ii) Statement I: Total number of moles in a closed system at new equilibrium is less than the old equilibrium if some amount of a substance is removed from the system (reaction: A(g) ⇄ B(g)). Statement II: The number of moles of the substance which is removed, is partially compensated as the system reaches to the new equilibrium.		Assertion-Reason: removal of substanceLe Chatelier's Principle | Effect of Concentration | Effect of Pressure | Effect of Temperature🟡 Similar(a) both correct, II explains I
Concept For A(g) ⇌ B(g) the reaction conserves moles (Δn = 0), so it can never create or destroy the total number of gas moles. Removing some substance drops the total permanently, and re-equilibration only partially restores the removed species, so the new total must stay below the old one.
Key relation Δn = 0 ⇒ total moles change only by what is added or removed, never by the reaction itself this is exactly why a removed amount can never be fully recovered.
Solution
1. Old equilibrium: A = a, B = b, total = a + b, with Kc = b/a.
2. Remove an amount D of B: the quotient falls to Q = (b−D)/a < Kc, so the system shifts forward, A → B.
3. The forward shift converts x of A into x of B: A = a−x, B = b−D+x, with x < D.
4. Because 1 mol ⇌ 1 mol, the reaction itself leaves the total untouched; the total is a + b − D < a + b. Statement I is CORRECT.
5. The removed B is only partly replaced (x < D), i.e. it is partially compensated — Statement II is CORRECT.
6. Were the compensation full, the total would climb back to a + b; because Le Chatelier opposes but cannot undo the removal, the total lands below. That partial-compensation fact is precisely why the total is lower, so II genuinely explains I → (a).
 A(g) -> B(g) ,  Dn = 0  (moles conserved)
 remove D of B: Q = (b-D)/a < Kc -> forward
 A = a - x , B = b - D + x   (x < D)
 total = a + b - D  <  a + b
 partial refill of B => total stays lower
⚡ JEE Trick Only Δn = 0 makes this clean — the reaction cannot manufacture moles, so the deficit D survives to the new equilibrium.
