If compensation were full the total would return to a + b; because it is only partial, the total stays below, and that causal link is what makes II the correct explanation rather than just a true co-statement, so (a) not (b).
Answer: (a) both correct, II explains I.
10 (iii)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows. (a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT. (d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.

(iii) Statement I: A net reaction can occur only if a system is not at equilibrium. Statement II: All reactions occur to reach a state of equilibrium.		Assertion-Reason: net reactionIntroduction to reaction quotient Qc | Chemical equilibrium | Chemistry | Khan Academy🟡 Similar(a) both correct, II explains I
Concept At equilibrium the forward and reverse rates are equal, so there is no net change. A net reaction therefore needs a non-zero driving force — a system displaced from equilibrium with Q ≠ K. And every reaction proceeds in the direction that carries it toward that equilibrium state, which is the deeper reason the first statement holds.
Key relation net rate = rf − rr; = 0 at equilibrium, ≠ 0 only when Q ≠ K the driving force vanishes exactly at equilibrium, where ΔG = 0.
Solution
1. Statement I: a net reaction can occur only if the system is NOT at equilibrium — correct, because at equilibrium rf = rr and the net rate is zero.
2. Statement II: all reactions occur to reach a state of equilibrium — correct; the quotient Q always drifts toward K.
3. Why is I true? Precisely because every reaction is driven toward equilibrium (II); once it arrives, the driving force disappears and no net reaction remains.
4. So II supplies the reason behind I — it is the cause, not merely a separate true fact that happens to sit alongside it.
5. Thermodynamically, at equilibrium Q = K and ΔG = 0, so there is no push in either direction and the macroscopic composition is frozen.
6. Both statements are true and II is the correct explanation of I → (a).
 net rate = r_f - r_r
 at equilibrium : r_f = r_r -> net = 0
 off equilibrium : Q != K -> net reaction
 reactions drive Q toward K   (II)
 => net reaction only away from eq  (I)
⚡ JEE Trick These two lines are the definition of equilibrium and its driving force — cause and effect, not two unrelated truths. The trap is code (b): once you see that "reactions run toward equilibrium" is the reason "no net reaction occurs at equilibrium", the answer has to be (a).
Answer: (a) both correct, II explains I.
10 (iv)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows. (a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT. (d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.

(iv) Statement I: A catalyst does not influence the value of equilibrium constant. Statement II: Catalyst influence the rates of both forward and backward reactions by the same factor.		Assertion-Reason: catalystLe-Chatelier's Principle: Adding a catalyst | Equilibrium | Chemistry | Khan Academy🟡 Similar(a) both correct, II explains I
Concept A catalyst provides an alternative path with a lower activation barrier, and it lowers that barrier for the forward and reverse steps by the same amount. This multiplies both rate constants by the same factor, so the equilibrium constant — which is their ratio — is left completely unchanged.
Key relation K = kf/kr; catalyst: kf → f·kf, kr → f·kr the common factor f cancels exactly in the ratio.
Solution
1. Statement I: a catalyst does not change the value of the equilibrium constant — correct.
2. Statement II: a catalyst speeds the forward and backward reactions by the same factor — correct, since it lowers the single shared barrier for both directions.
3. Write K = kf/kr. If both scale by f, then K′ = (f·kf)/(f·kr) = kf/kr = K, unchanged.
4. So the equal-factor speed-up of both directions (II) is exactly why K is untouched (I) — the statements are mechanism and consequence.
5. Physically a catalyst only shortens the time taken to reach equilibrium; it never moves the equilibrium position or the yield.
6. The same conclusion follows from thermodynamics: K depends only on ΔG° = −RT ln K, a state function untouched by the reaction path, so a catalyst (which only changes the path) cannot alter K.
7. Both statements are true and II is the correct explanation of I → (a).
 K = kf / kr
 catalyst : kf -> f*kf , kr -> f*kr
 K' = (f*kf)/(f*kr) = kf/kr = K  (unchanged)
 only the time to reach eq is shortened
 => I correct, II is its reason
⚡ JEE Trick A catalyst lowers the same barrier for both directions, so it cannot shift where equilibrium sits — only how fast you get there. Because the equal rate boost is the very mechanism behind "K unchanged", II is the correct explanation and the answer is (a), not (b).
Answer: (a) both correct, II explains I.
10 (v)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows. (a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I. (c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT. (d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.

(v) Statement I: Le Chatelier's principle predicts that an increase in temperature favours an endothermic process. Statement II: An endothermic process is one that absorbs heat and hence, tends to minimize the temperature increase.		Assertion-Reason: endothermicLe Chatelier’s principle: Changing temperature | Equilibrium | AP Chemistry | Khan Academy🟡 Similar(a) both correct, II explains I
Concept Raising the temperature adds heat, which is the applied stress. By Le Chatelier's principle the system shifts so as to consume that extra heat, i.e. in the endothermic direction, because an endothermic step absorbs heat and so partly offsets the very temperature rise that was imposed.
Key relation endothermic: ΔH > 0, heat absorbed; heating shifts equilibrium toward heat absorption and K rises with T treat heat as a reactant for an endothermic change.
Solution
1. Statement I: an increase in temperature favours the endothermic direction — correct.
2. Statement II: an endothermic process absorbs heat and so tends to minimize the temperature increase — correct.
3. Le Chatelier: a stress (here, added heat) is opposed, so the system runs the reaction that removes that heat from the surroundings.
4. The endothermic direction is exactly the heat-absorbing one (II), which is precisely why heating favours it (I) — statement and reason line up.
5. Quantitatively the van't Hoff relation ln(K₂/K₁) = −(ΔH/R)(1/T₂ − 1/T₁) gives K increasing with T whenever ΔH > 0, fully consistent with Statement I.
6. For an exothermic reaction the same logic runs in reverse — heating would favour the backward direction — so the endothermic qualifier in Statement I is essential and correct.
7. Both statements are true and II is the correct explanation of I → (a).
 raise T  = add heat (the stress)
 endothermic direction absorbs heat  (II)
 system shifts that way to oppose stress
 => heating favours endothermic  (I)
 van't Hoff : K up with T if dH > 0
⚡ JEE Trick Picture heat as a "reactant" for an endothermic reaction — adding it drives the reaction forward. Statement II states the heat-absorbing nature that makes Statement I work, so it is the correct explanation and the code is (a), not (b).
Answer: (a) both correct, II explains I.

1 (i)Passage : Q.(i) to (iii): Equilibrium constants are given for the following reactions at 27°C.
SrCl2·6H2O(s) ⇄ SrCl2·2H2O(s) + 4H2O(g); Kp = 2.56×10−10 atm4
Na2HPO4·12H2O(S) ⇄ Na2HPO4·7H2O(S) + 5H2O(g); Kp = 2.43×10−13 atm5
Na2SO4·10H2O(s) ⇄ Na2SO4(s) + 10 H2O(g); Kp = 1.024×10−27 atm10
The vapour pressure of water at 27°C is 0.04 atm.

(i) Which is the most effective drying agent at 27°C?
(a) SrCl2.2H2O   (b) Na2HPO4.7H2O   (c) Na2SO4   (d) All, equally		Most effective drying agent10 Vapour Pressure | Relative Humidity | Deliquescent Substance | IIT Advanced | JEE Main Class 11🟡 Similar(c) Na₂SO₄
Concept Each hydrate ⇌ lower hydrate + nH₂O(g) sets a fixed water-vapour "dissociation pressure" p = Kp1/n. The dried solid keeps pulling moisture from the air until p(H₂O) falls to that value, so the hydrate with the smallest dissociation pressure leaves the driest air and is the most effective drying agent. The raw Kp numbers cannot be compared directly — they carry different units.
Key relation Kp = (pH₂O)ⁿ ⇒ pH₂O = Kp1/n; lowest p ⇒ best desiccant units are atm⁴, atm⁵, atm¹⁰, so each Kp must be rooted to a plain atm first.
Solution
1. SrCl₂·6H₂O ⇌ SrCl₂·2H₂O + 4H₂O: p = (2.56×10⁻¹⁰)1/4. Since 2561/4 = 4, p = 4×10⁻³ atm.
2. Na₂HPO₄·12H₂O ⇌ Na₂HPO₄·7H₂O + 5H₂O: p = (2.43×10⁻¹³)1/5. Since 2431/5 = 3, p = 3×10⁻³ atm.
3. Na₂SO₄·10H₂O ⇌ Na₂SO₄ + 10H₂O: p = (1.024×10⁻²⁷)1/10. Since 10241/10 = 2, p = 2×10⁻³ atm.
4. Each lower hydrate or anhydrous solid absorbs water from the air until the surrounding p(H₂O) is pulled down to its own dissociation pressure.
5. The smallest such pressure belongs to Na₂SO₄ at 2×10⁻³ atm, so it dries the air the furthest.
6. Hence anhydrous Na₂SO₄ is the most effective drying agent → option (c).
 Kp = (pH2O)^n  ->  pH2O = Kp^(1/n)
 SrCl2.2H2O : (2.56e-10)^(1/4) = 4e-3 atm
 Na2HPO4.7H2O:(2.43e-13)^(1/5) = 3e-3 atm
 Na2SO4      :(1.024e-27)^(1/10)=2e-3 atm
 lowest pH2O = best drier -> Na2SO4
⚡ JEE Trick Never compare the raw Kp values — their units differ (atm⁴ vs atm⁵ vs atm¹⁰). Take the nth root to put every equilibrium on a common "atm" scale; the clean roots (4, 3, 2)×10⁻³ confirm the arithmetic, and the smallest pressure wins. A bigger Kp does not mean a better drier.
Answer: (c) Na₂SO₄.
1 (ii)Passage : Q.(i) to (iii): Equilibrium constants are given for the following reactions at 27°C.
SrCl2·6H2O(s) ⇄ SrCl2·2H2O(s) + 4H2O(g); Kp = 2.56×10−10 atm4
Na2HPO4·12H2O(S) ⇄ Na2HPO4·7H2O(S) + 5H2O(g); Kp = 2.43×10−13 atm5
Na2SO4·10H2O(s) ⇄ Na2SO4(s) + 10 H2O(g); Kp = 1.024×10−27 atm10
The vapour pressure of water at 27°C is 0.04 atm.

(ii) At what relative humidity will Na2SO4.10H2O s be efflorescent when exposed to the air at 27°C?
(a) Below 50%   (b) Above 50%   (c) Above 5%   (d) Below 5%		Efflorescence relative humidityEfflorescence is the spontaneous loss of water | Chemical Equilibrium | JEE Chemistry | Chem Mantra🟡 Similar(d) Below 5%
Concept Na₂SO₄·10H₂O effloresces — loses its water of crystallisation to the air — when the surrounding water-vapour pressure drops below the hydrate's own dissociation pressure. Converting that threshold pressure to relative humidity needs the saturation vapour pressure of water, 0.04 atm at 27 °C, as the 100% reference.
Key relation efflorescence if p(air) < pdiss = Kp1/10 = 2×10⁻³ atm; RH = p(air)/0.04 drying out requires air drier than the hydrate's vapour pressure.
Solution
1. The relevant equilibrium is Na₂SO₄·10H₂O ⇌ Na₂SO₄ + 10H₂O(g), with Kp = 1.024×10⁻²⁷ atm¹⁰.
2. Dissociation pressure pdiss = Kp1/10 = (1024×10⁻³⁰)1/10 = 2×10⁻³ atm.
3. If the air's p(H₂O) is below 2×10⁻³ atm, then Q < Kp and the forward reaction dominates: the solid releases water — efflorescence. a sub-saturated environment pulls the water off.
4. Relative humidity RH = p(H₂O)/psat = p/0.04.
5. The threshold is RH = (2×10⁻³)/0.04 = 0.05 = 5%.
6. So the decahydrate effloresces whenever the relative humidity is below 5%; above 5% the air is moist enough to hold the water in the crystal → option (d).
 Na2SO4.10H2O -> Na2SO4 + 10 H2O(g)
 p_diss = (1.024e-27)^(1/10) = 2e-3 atm
 efflorescence if p(air) < p_diss
 RH = p / 0.04
 threshold = 2e-3 / 0.04 = 0.05 = 5%
 => effloresces below 5% RH
⚡ JEE Trick Efflorescence means drying out, so the air must be drier than the hydrate's own vapour pressure — below the threshold, not above. The 0.04 atm saturation pressure is the 100% RH reference; dividing by it turns 2×10⁻³ atm into 5%, so (d), never the "above 50%" distractors.
Answer: (d) Below 5%.
1 (iii)Passage : Q.(i) to (iii): Equilibrium constants are given for the following reactions at 27°C.
SrCl2·6H2O(s) ⇄ SrCl2·2H2O(s) + 4H2O(g); Kp = 2.56×10−10 atm4
Na2HPO4·12H2O(S) ⇄ Na2HPO4·7H2O(S) + 5H2O(g); Kp = 2.43×10−13 atm5
Na2SO4·10H2O(s) ⇄ Na2SO4(s) + 10 H2O(g); Kp = 1.024×10−27 atm10
The vapour pressure of water at 27°C is 0.04 atm.

(iii) At what relative humidity will Na2HPO4.7H2O(s) deliquescent when exposed to the air at 27°C?
(a) Below 75%   (b) Above 75%   (c) Above 7.5%   (d) Below 7.5%		Deliquescence relative humidity10 Vapour Pressure | Relative Humidity | Deliquescent Substance | IIT Advanced | JEE Main Class 11🟡 Similar(c) Above 7.5%
Concept Na₂HPO₄·7H₂O takes up water (deliquesces toward the higher 12-hydrate) when the air's water-vapour pressure exceeds the 12 ⇌ 7 dissociation pressure. This is the exact mirror of efflorescence, so the condition flips to "above the threshold"; convert that pressure to relative humidity against the 0.04 atm saturation value.
Key relation water uptake if p(air) > pdiss = Kp1/5 = 3×10⁻³ atm; RH = p(air)/0.04 Q > Kp drives the reverse reaction that reabsorbs water.
Solution
1. The governing equilibrium is Na₂HPO₄·12H₂O ⇌ Na₂HPO₄·7H₂O + 5H₂O(g), with Kp = 2.43×10⁻¹³ atm⁵.
2. pdiss = Kp1/5 = (243×10⁻¹⁵)1/5 = 3×10⁻³ atm.
3. If the air's p(H₂O) exceeds 3×10⁻³ atm, then Q > Kp and the reverse reaction (Na₂HPO₄·7H₂O + 5H₂O → Na₂HPO₄·12H₂O) is favoured, so the solid absorbs water.
4. Relative humidity RH = p(H₂O)/psat = p/0.04.
5. The threshold is RH = (3×10⁻³)/0.04 = 0.075 = 7.5%.
6. So the heptahydrate deliquesces (picks up water) whenever the relative humidity is above 7.5%; below 7.5% the air is too dry and the 7-hydrate is instead stable → option (c).
 Na2HPO4.12H2O -> Na2HPO4.7H2O + 5 H2O(g)
 p_diss = (2.43e-13)^(1/5) = 3e-3 atm
 water uptake if p(air) > p_diss
 RH = p / 0.04
 threshold = 3e-3 / 0.04 = 0.075 = 7.5%
 => absorbs water above 7.5% RH
⚡ JEE Trick Water uptake is the exact mirror of efflorescence, so the inequality flips to "above the threshold". The 5th root (five waters) gives 3×10⁻³ atm, and dividing by the 0.04 atm saturation pressure yields 7.5%, so (c) — the "75%" traps are for those who forget that final division.
Answer: (c) Above 7.5%.
2The degree of dissociation of HI at a particular temperature of 0.8. Calculate the volume (in litre) of 1.6 M-Na2S2O3 solution required to neutralize the iodine present in an equilibrium mixture of a reaction when 2 moles each of H2 and I2 are heated in a closed vessel of 2 L capacity.Iodine titration from HI equilibriumThe degree of dissociation of `HI` at a particualr temperature is `0.8`. Calculate the volume of...🟢 Exact2
Concept The degree of dissociation of HI fixes the equilibrium constant for 2HI ⇌ H₂ + I₂, and therefore its reciprocal for the synthesis H₂ + I₂ ⇌ 2HI. Use that constant to find how much I₂ survives when 2 mol H₂ and 2 mol I₂ are heated together, then titrate the residual iodine with standard thiosulphate.
Key relation for 2HI ⇌ H₂ + I₂: Kc = (α/2)²/(1−α)²; and I₂ + 2S₂O₃²⁻ → 2I⁻ + S₄O₆²⁻ Δn = 0 for this reaction, so the vessel volume cancels — work purely in moles.
Solution
1. With α = 0.8 for 2HI ⇌ H₂ + I₂ starting from 1 mol HI: Kc = [(α/2)/(1−α)]² = (0.4/0.2)² = 4.
2. For the synthesis H₂ + I₂ ⇌ 2HI the constant is K′ = 1/Kc = 1/4 = 0.25.
3. Start 2 mol H₂ + 2 mol I₂ in 2 L; let x mol of each react forward: HI = 2x, H₂ = I₂ = 2−x.
4. K′ = [HI]²/([H₂][I₂]) = (2x/2)²/[((2−x)/2)²] = (2x/(2−x))² = 0.25. the 2 L divides out of every concentration.
5. Take the root: 2x/(2−x) = 0.5 → 2x = 1 − 0.5x → x = 0.4, so I₂ left = 2 − 0.4 = 1.6 mol.
6. Titration stoichiometry: 1 mol I₂ needs 2 mol S₂O₃²⁻ → 2 × 1.6 = 3.2 mol thiosulphate.
7. Volume of 1.6 M Na₂S₂O₃ = 3.2 mol ÷ 1.6 M = 2.0 L. The same Kc = 4 would hold whatever the starting amounts, since it is a property of the temperature only.
 2HI -> H2 + I2 , a=0.8 : Kc=(0.4/0.2)^2=4
 H2 + I2 -> 2HI : K' = 1/4 = 0.25
 start 2,2 in 2 L ; react x each
 (2x/(2-x))^2 = 0.25 -> 2x/(2-x) = 0.5
 x = 0.4 -> I2 left = 1.6 mol
 S2O3 = 2 * I2 = 3.2 mol
 V = 3.2 / 1.6 = 2.0 L
⚡ JEE Trick Volume cancels inside K′ because Δn = 0 for H₂ + I₂ ⇌ 2HI, so there is no need to carry the 2 L into the equilibrium algebra at all. The factor 2 in the iodine–thiosulphate stoichiometry is the usual trap — dropping it halves the answer to 1 L.
Answer: 2 L.
3To 500 ml of 0.9 M-AgNO3 solution was added 500 ml of 1.0M-Fe2+ solution and the reaction is allowed to achieve equilibrium at 25°C.
Ag+(aq) + Fe2+(aq) ⇄ Fe3+(aq) + Ag(s)
For 30 ml of the solution, 25 ml of 0.06 M−KMnO4 was required for oxidation of Fe2+ present. The equilibrium constant (in M−1) for the reaction at 25°C is		Kc from redox titrationRedox Titration between MnO4- and Fe2+🟡 Similar5
Concept Permanganate titrates only the Fe²⁺ that survives at equilibrium; that concentration reveals how far Ag⁺ + Fe²⁺ ⇌ Fe³⁺ + Ag(s) has proceeded. From the single Fe²⁺ figure every other equilibrium concentration follows by stoichiometry, and Kc is then built with the solid silver excluded, which is why the constant carries units of M⁻¹.
Key relation MnO₄⁻ + 5Fe²⁺ + 8H⁺ → Mn²⁺ + 5Fe³⁺ + 4H₂O, so n(Fe²⁺) = 5·n(MnO₄⁻); Kc = [Fe³⁺]/([Ag⁺][Fe²⁺]) Ag is a pure solid and drops out of the expression.
Solution
1. Mixing 500 mL 0.9 M AgNO₃ with 500 mL 1.0 M Fe²⁺ gives 1 L total: initial Ag⁺ = 0.45 M, Fe²⁺ = 0.50 M.
2. Titration of the 30 mL aliquot: n(MnO₄⁻) = 0.025 L × 0.06 M = 1.5×10⁻³ mol.
3. By the 5:1 stoichiometry n(Fe²⁺) = 5 × 1.5×10⁻³ = 7.5×10⁻³ mol, so [Fe²⁺] = 7.5×10⁻³/0.030 = 0.25 M at equilibrium.
4. Fe²⁺ consumed = 0.50 − 0.25 = 0.25 M, and by the 1:1:1 stoichiometry that much Ag⁺ reacted and that much Fe³⁺ formed.
5. Hence [Ag⁺] = 0.45 − 0.25 = 0.20 M and [Fe³⁺] = 0.25 M at equilibrium.
6. Kc = [Fe³⁺]/([Ag⁺][Fe²⁺]) = 0.25/(0.20 × 0.25) = 5 M⁻¹.
7. A sanity check: Ag⁺ was the limiting side (0.45 vs 0.50), and the modest Kc = 5 leaves 0.20 M Ag⁺ unreacted, so the reaction is far from complete — consistent with a small two-figure constant.
 1 L: Ag+ = 0.45 M , Fe2+ = 0.50 M (initial)
 MnO4- = 0.025*0.06 = 1.5e-3 mol / 30 mL
 Fe2+ = 5*1.5e-3 = 7.5e-3 mol -> 0.25 M
 reacted = 0.50 - 0.25 = 0.25 M
 Ag+ = 0.20 , Fe3+ = 0.25 M
 Kc = 0.25/(0.20*0.25) = 5 M^-1
⚡ JEE Trick The permanganate reads only the leftover Fe²⁺, and its n-factor 5 (Mn⁷⁺ → Mn²⁺) is essential — miss it and [Fe²⁺] comes out five-fold wrong. Solid Ag stays out of Kc, which is exactly why the constant carries the M⁻¹ unit the question asks for.
Answer: 5 M⁻¹.
4Fixed SPM, which allows only He(g) to cross it. The entire system is at equilibrium at 300 K. The volume of each chamber is 82.1 L. The total pressure in left chamber is 4 atm and in right chamber, 2 atm. NH3(g) and H2S(g) are obtained only from the dissociation of NH4HS(s). The value of Kp (in atm2) for the reaction NH4HS(s) ⇄ NH3(g) + H2S(g) isKp of NH4HS with semipermeable membraneVinay Sir - Chemical Equilibrium - Dissociation of NH4HS and effect of change in pressure and volume🟡 Similar1 atm²
Concept A semipermeable membrane that passes only He lets He reach the same partial pressure on both sides at equilibrium. The trap is to use the raw left-chamber total of 4 atm in Kp; you must first strip out the shared He pressure.
Key relation Kp = p(NH3)·p(H2S), with p(NH3)+p(H2S) = Pleft − p(He) the right chamber is pure He, so its total pressure IS the common He partial pressure.
Solution
1. Right chamber holds only He, so there p(He) = 2 atm (its total pressure).
2. He crosses the SPM freely, so at equilibrium its partial pressure is equal on both sides: p(He) = 2 atm in the left chamber as well. equal chemical potential → equal p for an ideal gas at one T.
3. Left total 4 atm = p(He)+p(NH3)+p(H2S) → p(NH3)+p(H2S) = 2 atm.
4. NH3 and H2S arise only from NH4HS(s), formed in a 1:1 ratio, so p(NH3) = p(H2S) = 1 atm each.
5. Kp = p(NH3)·p(H2S) = 1 × 1 = 1 atm².
6. The 82.1 L volume and 300 K are red herrings: Kp needs only partial pressures, and the pure solid never enters it.
 right chamber = He only  -> p(He) = 2 atm
 He crosses SPM  -> p(He) = 2 atm on left too
 left total 4 = 2 + p(NH3) + p(H2S)
   -> p(NH3) + p(H2S) = 2 atm
 1:1 dissociation -> each = 1 atm
 Kp = p(NH3) p(H2S) = 1 x 1 = 1    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick A pure-gas chamber on one side of the membrane is a gift: its total pressure is that gas's partial pressure directly. Skip the He subtraction and you would wrongly feed 4 atm into Kp; the solid is omitted throughout.
Answer: Kp = 1 atm².
5The standard reaction enthalpy of the reaction Zn(s) + H2O(g) ⇄ ZnO(s) + H2(g) is +223 kJ/mol and the standard reaction Gibb's functions is +33 kJ mol−1 at 1520 K. Assuming that both ΔH° and ΔS° remain constant, estimate the minimum temperature (in Kelvin) above which the equilibrium constant becomes greater than one.Minimum T for K greater than 1Gibbs Energy and Equilibrium Constant | JEE 2027 Chemistry (English)🟡 Similar1784 K
Concept The bridge between the equilibrium constant and the Gibbs function is ΔG° = −RT ln K, so the condition K > 1 is exactly ΔG° < 0. This reaction is strongly endothermic (ΔH° = +223 kJ) and only the entropy term can make it go, so we treat ΔH° and ΔS° as temperature-independent, pull ΔS° out of the single 1520 K data point, and then locate the temperature at which ΔG° changes sign. The commonest trap is mixing kJ with J when handling ΔS°.
Key relation ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS° = −RT ln K; at the crossover K = 1, ΔG° = 0, so T = ΔH°/ΔS° above this T the −TΔS° term overtakes ΔH° and K climbs past 1.
Solution
1. Write the given state: at T = 1520 K, ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS° → 33 = 223 − 1520·ΔS° (all in kJ).
2. Solve for the entropy: 1520·ΔS° = 223 − 33 = 190 kJ → ΔS° = 0.125 kJ mol⁻¹ K⁻¹ = 125 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹.
3. Translate the target: K > 1 ⇔ ln K > 0 ⇔ ΔG° < 0. from ΔG° = −RT ln K with T > 0.
4. Impose ΔG° < 0: ΔH° − TΔS° < 0 ⇔ T > ΔH°/ΔS°, and since ΔS° > 0 the inequality does not flip.
5. Substitute the numbers: T > 223 / 0.125 = 1784 K, so the minimum temperature is ≈ 1784 K.
6. Sanity check at 1520 K: TΔS° = 1520×0.125 = 190 < 223, so ΔG° = +33 > 0 and K < 1 there — consistent with needing a higher temperature, and ΔG° stays positive right up until 1784 K.
 at 1520 K:  33 = 223 - 1520 dS   (kJ)
 1520 dS = 223 - 33 = 190 kJ
   -> dS = 0.125 kJ/mol/K = 125 J/mol/K
 K > 1  =>  dG < 0  =>  T > dH/dS
 T > 223 / 0.125 = 1784 K          <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick For an endothermic reaction with positive ΔS°, the temperature where K passes through 1 is simply ΔH°/ΔS° — the whole 1520 K line exists only to hand you ΔS°. Keep ΔH° and ΔS° in the same energy unit (both kJ), or the ratio is wrong by a factor of 1000 and you would report an absurd 1.784 K.
Answer: minimum T ≈ 1784 K.

1 (i)Problem 1 - Q.(i) to (vi): Vinegar is an acid with its key ingredient, acetic acid. It is widely used for food preservation and cooking. The acetic acid concentration for table vinegar is typically 5% whereas higher concentration upto 18% is used as preservative. The word "vinegar" is derived from vin aigre, meaning "sour wine". A table vinegar sample contains 5% (mass/mass) of acetic acid.

(i) To what volume 10 mL of the above sample should be diluted to prepare 0.10 M acetic acid solution. (density 1.05 g mL−1, Molar mass of CH3COOH = 60.0 g mol−1)		Dilution volume of vinegar0.1 molar acetic acid | acetic acid 0.1 molar | 0.1 m acetic acid🟡 SimilarV = 87.5 mL
Concept Convert the 5% (w/w) stock to molarity using the density, then apply the dilution law. The trap: 5% is by mass, so density is needed to turn it into moles per litre.
Key relation M = 10·(%w/w)·d / Mw; M1V1 = M2V2 moles of solute are conserved on dilution.
Solution
1. In 1 L of stock: mass = 1000 mL × 1.05 g mL⁻¹ = 1050 g.
2. Acetic acid = 5% of 1050 = 52.5 g.
3. Moles = 52.5 / 60.0 = 0.875 → stock = 0.875 M (= 10×5×1.05/60).
4. Dilute 10 mL of 0.875 M to 0.10 M: 0.875 × 10 = 0.10 × V2.
5. V2 = 8.75 / 0.10 = 87.5 mL.
 stock M = 10 * 5 * 1.05 / 60 = 0.875 M
 M1 V1 = M2 V2
 0.875 * 10 = 0.10 * V2
 V2 = 8.75 / 0.10 = 87.5 mL        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick M = 10·%·d / Mw gets the stock molarity in one line; omit the density and you lose the factor that makes mass% into molarity.
Answer: dilute the 10 mL to V = 87.5 mL.
1 (ii)Problem 1 - Q.(i) to (vi): Vinegar is an acid with its key ingredient, acetic acid. It is widely used for food preservation and cooking. The acetic acid concentration for table vinegar is typically 5% whereas higher concentration upto 18% is used as preservative. The word "vinegar" is derived from vin aigre, meaning "sour wine". A table vinegar sample contains 5% (mass/mass) of acetic acid.

(ii) Write balanced equation for the dissociation of acetic acid [CH3COOH] in the above solution and give an appropriate expression for equilibrium constant Ka.		Dissociation equation and KaKa and pKa | Acidic Strength | JEE | NEET | Acid Dissociation Constant🟡 SimilarKa = [H₃O⁺][CH₃COO⁻]/[CH₃COOH]
Concept Acetic acid is a weak monoprotic acid; in water it donates its carboxyl proton to a water molecule. The equilibrium constant Ka is written with water omitted, since the pure solvent has unit activity.
Key relation CH3COOH + H2O ⇌ CH3COO⁻ + H3O⁺ one proton transferred to the solvent.
Solution
1. The weak acid partially ionises, transferring H⁺ to water.
2. Balanced equilibrium: CH3COOH + H2O ⇌ CH3COO⁻ + H3O⁺.
3. Apply the law of mass action: Ka = [products]/[reactants].
4. Water is the solvent (activity 1), so it is left out of the expression.
5. Ka = [H3O⁺][CH3COO⁻] / [CH3COOH] (= 1.75×10⁻⁵ at 25 °C).
 CH3COOH + H2O <=> CH3COO- + H3O+
 Ka = [H3O+][CH3COO-] / [CH3COOH]
 (H2O omitted: pure-solvent activity = 1)
⚡ JEE Trick Never place [H2O] in Ka for a dilute aqueous equilibrium; its concentration (≈55 M) is essentially constant and is folded into Ka.
Answer: Ka = [H3O⁺][CH3COO⁻] / [CH3COOH].
1 (iii)Problem 1 - Q.(i) to (vi): Vinegar is an acid with its key ingredient, acetic acid. It is widely used for food preservation and cooking. The acetic acid concentration for table vinegar is typically 5% whereas higher concentration upto 18% is used as preservative. The word "vinegar" is derived from vin aigre, meaning "sour wine". A table vinegar sample contains 5% (mass/mass) of acetic acid.

(iii) For the diluted solution, write the appropriate expression for total concentration of acetic acid expressed as CT. Derive the exact expression for [H3O+] in terms of Ka and CT.		Exact [H3O+] expressionDerive equation for a weak acid🟡 Similar[H₃O⁺] = (−Ka + √(Ka² + 4Ka·C_T))/2
Concept Let CT be the total (formal) acetic-acid concentration. Combining the mass balance with the proton condition gives a quadratic in h = [H3O⁺]. The trap is dropping the −h term too early — this part asks for the exact form.
Key relation CT = [CH3COOH] + [CH3COO⁻]; Ka = h²/(CT − h) in acid solution the charge balance gives [CH3COO⁻] = h once OH⁻ is neglected.
Solution
1. Mass balance: CT = [CH3COOH] + [CH3COO⁻].
2. Charge balance h = [CH3COO⁻] + [OH⁻]; in acid OH⁻ is tiny, so [CH3COO⁻] ≈ h.
3. Then [CH3COOH] = CT − h.
4. Ka = h·h/(CT − h) → h² + Kah − KaCT = 0.
5. Positive root: h = (−Ka + √(Ka² + 4KaCT))/2.
 CT = [HA] + [A-];  h = [A-]  (OH- small)
 [HA] = CT - h
 Ka = h^2 / (CT - h)
 h^2 + Ka h - Ka CT = 0
 h = (-Ka + sqrt(Ka^2 + 4 Ka CT))/2   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Only the positive root is physical. The familiar h = √(KaCT) is just the CT ≫ h limit of this exact quadratic.
Answer: [H3O⁺] = (−Ka + √(Ka² + 4KaCT))/2.
1 (iv)Problem 1 - Q.(i) to (vi): Vinegar is an acid with its key ingredient, acetic acid. It is widely used for food preservation and cooking. The acetic acid concentration for table vinegar is typically 5% whereas higher concentration upto 18% is used as preservative. The word "vinegar" is derived from vin aigre, meaning "sour wine". A table vinegar sample contains 5% (mass/mass) of acetic acid.

(iv) Apply appropriate approximation to simplify the expression for [H3O+] written by you in 7.3 and calculate the pH of the diluted vinegar solution. (Ka = 1.75×10−5)		pH of diluted vinegarpH of Weak Acids and Bases - Percent Ionization - Ka & Kb🟡 SimilarpH = 2.88
Concept When the acid is weak and dilute (α ≪ 1), CT − h ≈ CT, so the quadratic collapses to h = √(KaCT). Always verify α afterward.
Key relation h ≈ √(KaCT); pH = −log h valid only while α = h/CT is small.
Solution
1. CT = 0.10 M (the diluted solution from part i), Ka = 1.75×10⁻⁵.
2. Assume CT ≫ h: h = √(KaCT) = √(1.75×10⁻⁵ × 0.10) = √(1.75×10⁻⁶).
3. h = 1.32×10⁻³ M.
4. pH = −log(1.32×10⁻³) = 3 − log 1.32 = 3 − 0.12 = 2.88.
5. Check α = h/CT = 1.32×10⁻³/0.10 = 0.013 ≪ 1, so the approximation holds.
 h = sqrt(Ka * CT)
   = sqrt(1.75e-5 * 0.10)
   = sqrt(1.75e-6) = 1.32e-3 M
 pH = -log(1.32e-3) = 2.88         <-- answer
 alpha = h/CT = 0.013  (<< 1, OK)
⚡ JEE Trick Compute √(KaC) then take −log; confirm α < 0.05 or the exact quadratic from part (iii) is required.
Answer: pH = 2.88.
1 (v)Problem 1 - Q.(i) to (vi): Vinegar is an acid with its key ingredient, acetic acid. It is widely used for food preservation and cooking. The acetic acid concentration for table vinegar is typically 5% whereas higher concentration upto 18% is used as preservative. The word "vinegar" is derived from vin aigre, meaning "sour wine". A table vinegar sample contains 5% (mass/mass) of acetic acid.

(v) 5.0 mL of 0.1 M NaOH solution is added to 50 mL of the 0.1 M acetic acid solution
(a) Write the equilibrium reactions for dissociated and undissociated acetic acid present in the above solution
(b) For the above system, write appropriate expressions for [CH3COOH]eq and [CH3COO−]eq. (hint: the expressions should have [H3O+],[OH−], CT and [CH3COONa] ).
(c) Apply appropriate approximation and get the simplified expression for [H3O+].
(d) Calculate the pH of the resulting acetic acid solution		Buffer region expressions and pHHenderson-Hasselbalch equation | Acids and bases | AP Chemistry | Khan Academy🟡 SimilarpH ≈ 3.80 (buffer)
Concept A small amount of NaOH converts part of the acid into acetate, giving a buffer. Set up [CH3COO⁻] and [CH3COOH] from the charge and mass balances, then the acid form gives [H3O⁺].
Key relation [CH3COO⁻] = [CH3COONa] + [H3O⁺] − [OH⁻]; [CH3COOH] = CT − [CH3COO⁻]; h = Ka[CH3COOH]/[CH3COO⁻] Na⁺ is a spectator set by the NaOH added.
Solution
1. mmol: acid = 50×0.1 = 5; NaOH = 5×0.1 = 0.5 → salt formed 0.5, acid left 4.5, V = 55 mL.
2. (a) CH3COOH + H2O ⇌ CH3COO⁻ + H3O⁺ and CH3COO⁻ + H2O ⇌ CH3COOH + OH⁻.
3. (b) [CH3COO⁻] = [CH3COONa] + [H3O⁺] − [OH⁻]; [CH3COOH] = CT − [CH3COONa] − [H3O⁺] + [OH⁻].
4. (c) Buffer: [H3O⁺], [OH⁻] ≪ concentrations → [CH3COO⁻] ≈ [salt], [CH3COOH] ≈ CT − [salt], so h = Ka(CT − [salt])/[salt].
5. (d) acid/salt = 4.5/0.5 = 9 → h = Ka×9 = 1.58×10⁻⁴ → pH = pKa + log(0.5/4.5) = 4.76 − 0.95 = 3.80.
 acid 5 mmol, NaOH 0.5 -> salt 0.5, acid 4.5
 [A-] ~ [salt], [HA] ~ CT - [salt]
 h = Ka [HA]/[A-] = 1.75e-5 * (4.5/0.5)
   = 1.58e-4
 pH = 4.76 + log(0.5/4.5) = 3.80    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The exact balances collapse to Henderson–Hasselbalch pH = pKa + log([salt]/[acid]) once the small-ion terms drop out.
Answer: pH ≈ 3.80 (buffer; [A⁻] ≈ [salt], [HA] ≈ CT − [salt]).
1 (vi)Problem 1 - Q.(i) to (vi): Vinegar is an acid with its key ingredient, acetic acid. It is widely used for food preservation and cooking. The acetic acid concentration for table vinegar is typically 5% whereas higher concentration upto 18% is used as preservative. The word "vinegar" is derived from vin aigre, meaning "sour wine". A table vinegar sample contains 5% (mass/mass) of acetic acid.

(vi) Calculate the pH when 50 mL of 0.1 M NaOH is added to 50 mL of 0.1 M acetic acid solution. (Kw = 1.00×10−14)		pH at equivalence pointpH at Equivalence Point of Weak Acid with Strong Base Titration🟡 SimilarpH = 8.73
Concept Equal moles of NaOH and acetic acid give pure sodium acetate, which is basic by hydrolysis. Use the conjugate Kb = Kw/Ka; the pH is not 7.
Key relation Kb = Kw/Ka; [OH⁻] = √(KbC); pH = 14 − pOH acetate is the conjugate base of a weak acid.
Solution
1. acid = 50×0.1 = 5 mmol, NaOH = 5 mmol → all converted to acetate, V = 100 mL → C = 0.05 M.
2. Kb = Kw/Ka = 10⁻¹⁴/1.75×10⁻⁵ = 5.71×10⁻¹⁰.
3. CH3COO⁻ + H2O ⇌ CH3COOH + OH⁻.
4. [OH⁻] = √(KbC) = √(5.71×10⁻¹⁰ × 0.05) = √(2.86×10⁻¹¹) = 5.35×10⁻⁶.
5. pOH = −log(5.35×10⁻⁶) = 5.27 → pH = 14 − 5.27 = 8.73.
 salt = 5 mmol in 100 mL -> C = 0.05 M
 Kb = Kw/Ka = 1e-14/1.75e-5 = 5.71e-10
 [OH-] = sqrt(Kb C) = sqrt(2.86e-11)
       = 5.35e-6 M
 pOH = 5.27, pH = 14 - 5.27 = 8.73  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick At the equivalence point of weak acid + strong base the salt is basic, so pH > 7; use the conjugate Kb, never assume neutral.
Answer: pH = 8.73.
2 (i)Problem 2 - Q.(i) to (iii): Chemical equilibria in quantitative analysis. A. Many metal ions can be precipitated from their aqueous solutions as sulphides using hydrogen sulphide, a weak dibasic acid. The concentration of the sulphide ions in the solution can be controlled by adjusting the pH of the solution and thus the separation of the metal ions from the mixture can be achieved. Relevant data needed for calculations in different subparts is presented below. Dissociation constants of H2S: K1 = 1×10−9 and K2 = 1.3×10−14. Solubility product (Ksp): CdS = 1.0×10−27, Tl2S = 6.0×10−22.

(i) Solid NaOH is added to 1L of 0.05M solution of H2S until the pH reached the value of 12.0. Calculate-
(a) Initial pH of H2S solution before the addition of solid NaOH.
(b) Concentration of all the species at pH = 12.0 (Use appropriate approximations).
(c) Total mass of solid NaOH added to the solution.		H2S pH and species concentrationsPolyprotic Acid Base Equilibria Problems, pH Calculations Given Ka1, Ka2 & Ka3 - Ice Tables🟡 Similar(a) 5.15; (c) 2.452 g
Concept For 0.05 M H2S the first ionisation fixes the initial pH. At the strongly basic pH 12, HS⁻ dominates; get H2S and S²⁻ from K1, K2, and the NaOH needed from a charge balance.
Key relation K1 = [H⁺][HS⁻]/[H2S], K2 = [H⁺][S²⁻]/[HS⁻]; [Na⁺] = [OH⁻] + [HS⁻] + 2[S²⁻] − [H⁺] charge balance gives the NaOH added.
Solution
1. (a) [H⁺] = √(K1C) = √(10⁻⁹×0.05) = √(5×10⁻¹¹) = 7.07×10⁻⁶ → pH = 5.15.
2. (b) At pH 12: [H⁺] = 10⁻¹², [OH⁻] = 10⁻². Ratios [HS⁻]/[H2S] = K1/[H⁺] = 10³ and [S²⁻]/[HS⁻] = K2/[H⁺] = 0.013, so HS⁻ ≈ C = 0.05 M.
3. [H2S] = [HS⁻][H⁺]/K1 = 0.05×10⁻¹²/10⁻⁹ = 5×10⁻⁵ M.
4. [S²⁻] = K2[HS⁻]/[H⁺] = 1.3×10⁻¹⁴×0.05/10⁻¹² = 6.5×10⁻⁴ M.
5. (c) [Na⁺] = 10⁻² + 0.05 + 2(6.5×10⁻⁴) = 0.0613 M → mass NaOH = 0.0613×40 = 2.452 g.
 (a) [H+]=sqrt(K1 C)=sqrt(5e-11)=7.07e-6
     pH = 5.15
 (b) pH12: HS- ~ 0.05, H2S = 5e-5
     S2- = 6.5e-4, OH- = 1e-2, H+ = 1e-12
 (c) Na+ = 0.01 + 0.05 + 2(6.5e-4)
         = 0.0613 M
     mass = 0.0613 * 40 = 2.452 g   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick On the polyprotic ladder only neighbouring species matter; because K2 is so small, S²⁻ stays minor even at pH 12 while HS⁻ carries almost all the sulphur.
Answer: (a) pH = 5.15; (b) [HS⁻] ≈ 0.05, [H2S] = 5×10⁻⁵, [S²⁻] = 6.5×10⁻⁴, [OH⁻] = 10⁻², [H⁺] = 10⁻¹² M; (c) NaOH = 2.452 g.
2 (ii)Problem 2 - Q.(i) to (iii): Chemical equilibria in quantitative analysis. A. Many metal ions can be precipitated from their aqueous solutions as sulphides using hydrogen sulphide, a weak dibasic acid. The concentration of the sulphide ions in the solution can be controlled by adjusting the pH of the solution and thus the separation of the metal ions from the mixture can be achieved. Relevant data needed for calculations in different subparts is presented below. Dissociation constants of H2S: K1 = 1×10−9 and K2 = 1.3×10−14. Solubility product (Ksp): CdS = 1.0×10−27, Tl2S = 6.0×10−22.

(ii) A sample solution contains 0.003M each of Cd+2 ions and Tl+1 ions. The mixture needs to be separated using H2S at appropriate pH. The separation is assumed to be quantitatively complete when the concentration of Cd+2 ions in the sample solution becomes 1×10−6 of the initial value. Calculate the optimal pH range in which Cd+2(aq) and Tl+1(aq) can be separated quantitatively. (The H2S concentration in this solution is 0.10 M).		Optimal pH range for sulphide separationJEE Main 2026 Jan PYQ | Equilibrium | Selective Precipitation | Solubility Product (Ksp & pH)🟡 SimilarpH 2.70 to 3.85
Concept [S²⁻] is controlled by pH through K1K2[H2S]/[H⁺]². The lower pH limit supplies just enough S²⁻ to drop Cd²⁺ to 10⁻⁶ of its value; the upper pH limit keeps Tl₂S from precipitating.
Key relation [S²⁻] = K1K2[H2S]/[H⁺]² = 1.3×10⁻²⁴/[H⁺]² two Ksp inequalities bracket the usable window.
Solution
1. [S²⁻] = (10⁻⁹)(1.3×10⁻¹⁴)(0.10)/[H⁺]² = 1.3×10⁻²⁴/[H⁺]².
2. Lower limit: Cd²⁺ → 10⁻⁶×0.003 = 3×10⁻⁹ M needs [S²⁻] ≥ Ksp/[Cd²⁺] = 10⁻²⁷/3×10⁻⁹ = 3.33×10⁻¹⁹.
3. 1.3×10⁻²⁴/[H⁺]² ≥ 3.33×10⁻¹⁹ → [H⁺] ≤ 1.98×10⁻³ → pH ≥ 2.70.
4. Upper limit (no Tl₂S): [Tl⁺]²[S²⁻] ≤ Ksp → [S²⁻] ≤ 6×10⁻²²/(0.003)² = 6.67×10⁻¹⁷.
5. 1.3×10⁻²⁴/[H⁺]² ≤ 6.67×10⁻¹⁷ → [H⁺] ≥ 1.40×10⁻⁴ → pH ≤ 3.85.
 [S2-] = K1 K2 [H2S]/[H+]^2 = 1.3e-24/[H+]^2
 Cd to 3e-9: [S2-]>=1e-27/3e-9=3.33e-19
   [H+]<=1.98e-3  -> pH >= 2.70
 no Tl2S: [S2-]<=6e-22/(3e-3)^2=6.67e-17
   [H+]>=1.40e-4  -> pH <= 3.85    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Because [S²⁻] ∝ 1/[H⁺]², pH is a powerful lever: one inequality pushes Cd²⁺ down, the other holds Tl⁺ in solution, and together they fix the 2.70–3.85 window.
Answer: optimal pH range ≈ 2.70 to 3.85.
2 (iii)Problem 2 - Q.(i) to (iii): Chemical equilibria in quantitative analysis. A. Many metal ions can be precipitated from their aqueous solutions as sulphides using hydrogen sulphide, a weak dibasic acid. The concentration of the sulphide ions in the solution can be controlled by adjusting the pH of the solution and thus the separation of the metal ions from the mixture can be achieved. Relevant data needed for calculations in different subparts is presented below. Dissociation constants of H2S: K1 = 1×10−9 and K2 = 1.3×10−14. Solubility product (Ksp): CdS = 1.0×10−27, Tl2S = 6.0×10−22.

(iii) B. Volhard method is a back titration method and involves excess addition of AgNO3 and its back titration with potassium thiocyanate using ferric alum as the indicator. The end point is the appearance of a reddish brown colour due to the formation of iron thiocyanate complex. 10g of pesticide sample containing arsenic was treated appropriately to convert its arsenic content to AsO43−. Then 25 mL of 0.045 M AgNO3 was added to precipitate AsO43− as Ag3AsO4. The precipitate was filtered and the unreacted AgNO3 present in the filtrate was then titrated with 0.05M potassium thiocyanate. The observed titre value was 5.0 mL. Calculate the percentage of arsenic as AsO43− in the pesticide sample.		Volhard back-titration % arsenicVolhard's method of end point detection in precipitation titrations #bepharmawise🟡 Similar0.405%
Concept Back titration: total Ag⁺ added minus the Ag⁺ found by SCN⁻ equals the Ag⁺ that precipitated Ag3AsO4. Three Ag⁺ bind one AsO4³⁻. "% as AsO4³⁻" uses the AsO4³⁻ molar mass (139), not As (75).
Key relation n(Ag reacted) = n(Ag total) − n(SCN⁻); n(AsO4³⁻) = n(Ag reacted)/3 Ag3AsO4 has a 3:1 Ag:AsO4 ratio.
Solution
1. n(Ag total) = 25 × 0.045 = 1.125 mmol.
2. n(SCN⁻) = n(Ag excess) = 5.0 × 0.05 = 0.25 mmol.
3. n(Ag reacted) = 1.125 − 0.25 = 0.875 mmol.
4. n(AsO4³⁻) = 0.875/3 = 0.2917 mmol.
5. M(AsO4³⁻) = 75 + 64 = 139 → mass = 0.2917×139 = 40.5 mg = 0.0405 g → % = 0.0405/10 × 100 = 0.405%.
 Ag total  = 25 * 0.045 = 1.125 mmol
 Ag excess = 5.0 * 0.05 = 0.25 mmol
 Ag reacted = 0.875 mmol
 AsO4 = 0.875 / 3 = 0.2917 mmol
 mass = 0.2917 * 139 = 40.5 mg
 % = 0.0405 / 10 * 100 = 0.405 %    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The 3:1 Ag:AsO4 stoichiometry is the usual slip; and "% as AsO4³⁻" wants the oxo-anion mass 139 g mol⁻¹, not atomic As.
Answer: % AsO4³⁻ = 0.405%.
