1Liquid NH3 ionises to a slight extent. At a certain temp. it's self ionization constant KSIC(NH3) = 10−30. The number of NH4+ ions present per 100 cm3 of pure liquid are:
(a) 10−15   (b) 6.022×108   (c) 6.022×107   (d) 6.022×106		Self-ionization of liquid NH3Self-ionisation of liquid ammonia occurs as 2NH3 <=> NH4+ + NH2-, K = 10^(-10)🟡 Similar(c) 6.022×10⁷
Concept Liquid ammonia is an amphiprotic solvent that self-ionises exactly as water does, one NH3 donating a proton to another: 2NH3 ⇌ NH4⁺ + NH2⁻, with a self-ionisation constant KSIC that plays the role Kw plays for water. In the pure liquid the two ions can only come from this one process, so they are present in equal concentrations and each is the square root of KSIC. The only real traps are forgetting the square root and the 100 cm³ = 0.1 L volume step before multiplying by Avogadro's number.
Key relation KSIC = [NH4⁺][NH2⁻] = [NH4⁺]²; N = [NH4⁺]·V(L)·NA the two self-ions are produced in equal amounts, so their product is a perfect square.
Solution
1. Write the self-ionisation: 2NH3 ⇌ NH4⁺ + NH2⁻, KSIC = [NH4⁺][NH2⁻] = 10⁻³⁰.
2. In the pure liquid electroneutrality forces [NH4⁺] = [NH2⁻], so [NH4⁺]² = 10⁻³⁰ → [NH4⁺] = 10⁻¹⁵ M.
3. Convert the stated volume: 100 cm³ = 100 mL = 0.1 L. molarity is per litre, so the volume must be in litres.
4. Moles of NH4⁺ in that volume = 10⁻¹⁵ mol L⁻¹ × 0.1 L = 10⁻¹⁶ mol.
5. Number of ions N = moles × NA = 10⁻¹⁶ × 6.022×10²³ = 6.022×10⁷ ions.
6. This matches option (c); option (a) 10⁻¹⁵ is just the molarity, not an ion count.
 2 NH3 <=> NH4+ + NH2-
 Ksic = [NH4+][NH2-] = 1e-30
 [NH4+] = sqrt(1e-30) = 1e-15 M
 n = 1e-15 * 0.1 L = 1e-16 mol
 N = 1e-16 * 6.022e23 = 6.022e7     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Take the square root of KSIC for the ion molarity, then scale by the actual volume. Using 1 L instead of 0.1 L gives the trap (b) 6.022×10⁸, while forgetting NA altogether leaves you staring at (a) 10⁻¹⁵.
Answer: (c) 6.022×10⁷.
2To what volume of 10 litre of 0.5 M CH3COOH (Ka = 1.8×10−5) be diluted in order to double the hydroxide ion concentration:
(a) 20 L   (b) 30 L   (c) 40 L   (d) None of these		Dilution of weak acid, [OH-]Ostwald Dilution law for Weak electrolytes- IIT JEE & NEET | Vineet Khatri | ATP STAR🟡 Similar(c) 40 L
Concept Diluting a weak acid raises its [OH⁻] because weakening the acid pushes [H⁺] down and, through Kw, pulls [OH⁻] up. For a weak acid [H⁺] = √(KaC), so [OH⁻] = Kw/√(KaC) ∝ 1/√C. Doubling [OH⁻] therefore means halving [H⁺], and because [H⁺] scales with √C that requires the concentration to fall to one quarter. The trap is treating this like a strong acid, where [OH⁻] ∝ 1/C and only a 2× dilution would be needed.
Key relation [H⁺] = √(KaC), [OH⁻] = Kw/[H⁺] ∝ 1/√C; moles conserved, so C·V = C′·V′ dilution keeps the total moles of acid fixed while spreading them over a larger volume.
Solution
1. Weak acid: [H⁺] = √(KaC); hence [OH⁻] = Kw/[H⁺] = Kw/√(KaC).
2. Collecting the C-dependence, [OH⁻] ∝ 1/√C.
3. To double [OH⁻], √C must halve, which means C must drop to C/4.
4. C goes 0.5 → 0.125 M; with moles conserved, C·V = C′·V′ → V′ = 10 × (0.5/0.125) = 40 L.
5. Check [H⁺]: at C = 0.5, [H⁺] = √(1.8×10⁻⁵×0.5) = 3×10⁻³; at C = 0.125, [H⁺] = √(1.8×10⁻⁵×0.125) = 1.5×10⁻³, exactly halved, so [OH⁻] indeed doubles. α stays ≪ 1 (α ≈ 0.006 → 0.012), so √(KaC) is valid throughout.
6. Hence the final volume is V′ = 40 L, option (c).
 [H+] = sqrt(Ka C); [OH-] = Kw/[H+]
   -> [OH-] ~ 1/sqrt(C)
 double [OH-]  =>  C -> C/4
 C V = C' V' -> V' = 10 * (0.5/0.125)
 V' = 10 * 4 = 40 L                 <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick For a weak acid [OH⁻] tracks 1/√C, so a 2× rise needs a 4× dilution (10 → 40 L). Option (a) 20 L is the trap for anyone who carries over the strong-acid rule [OH⁻] ∝ 1/C.
Answer: (c) 40 L.
3Calculate the ratio of HCOO− and F− in a mixture of 0.2 M HCOOH (Ka = 2×10−4) and 0.1 M HF (Ka = 6.6×10−4):
(a) 1 : 6.6   (b) 1 : 3.3   (c) 2 : 3.3   (d) 3.3 : 2		Mixture of two weak acidspH of mixture of two Weak Acids | Ionic Equilibrium | Acids-Bases🟡 Similar(c) 2 : 3.3
Concept Both weak acids are dissolved in the same solution, so they are bathed in one common hydrogen-ion concentration [H⁺] set by the mixture as a whole. Each conjugate-base concentration follows from its own Ka expression, [A⁻] = Ka[HA]/[H⁺]. When we take the ratio of the two anions, that shared [H⁺] appears in both numerator and denominator and cancels, so we never have to solve the (harder) problem of finding [H⁺] for a two-acid mixture.
Key relation [A⁻] = Ka[HA]/[H⁺]; so [HCOO⁻]/[F⁻] = (Ka1C1)/(Ka2C2) the shared [H⁺] divides out, leaving only the Ka·C products.
Solution
1. For formic acid: [HCOO⁻] = Ka1[HCOOH]/[H⁺]; for HF: [F⁻] = Ka2[HF]/[H⁺].
2. Dissociation is small for both, so [HCOOH] ≈ 0.2 M and [HF] ≈ 0.1 M (undissociated ≈ formal). each α is well under 0.1, so the initial concentrations survive.
3. Form the ratio: [HCOO⁻]/[F⁻] = (Ka1C1)/(Ka2C2), the [H⁺] having cancelled.
4. Substitute: = (2×10⁻⁴ × 0.2)/(6.6×10⁻⁴ × 0.1) = (4×10⁻⁵)/(6.6×10⁻⁵).
5. Simplify: = 4/6.6 = 0.606, and 2/3.3 = 0.606 as well.
6. So [HCOO⁻]:[F⁻] = 2 : 3.3, which is option (c).
 [A-] = Ka [HA]/[H+]   (common [H+])
 ratio = Ka1 C1 / (Ka2 C2)
   = (2e-4 * 0.2)/(6.6e-4 * 0.1)
   = 4e-5 / 6.6e-5 = 0.606
   = 2 : 3.3                        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick In a two-acid mixture the shared [H⁺] cancels, so the conjugate-base ratio is just (Ka1C1)/(Ka2C2) — no need to compute [H⁺]. Option (a) 1:6.6 is the trap that uses only the Ka values and forgets the concentrations.
Answer: (c) 2 : 3.3.
4Fe(OH)2 is diacidic base has Kb1 = 10−4 and Kb2 = 2.5×10−6. What is the concentration of Fe(OH)2 in 0.1 M Fe(NO3)2 solution?
(a) 4×10−9   (b) 2.5×10−6   (c) 10−10   (d) 10−14		Diacidic base equilibrium(L-17) Di and polybasic Acids/ Acidic Bases | std-11 Chemistry | chap-7 Equilibrium| Neet, Jee, IIT🟡 Similar(c) 10⁻¹⁰
Concept Fe(NO3)2 fully dissociates to give 0.1 M Fe²⁺, the salt of a weak base and a strong acid, so the cation behaves as a weak acid. Fe²⁺ is the conjugate acid of the base Fe(OH)⁺, which means its hydrolysis constant is Ka = Kw/Kb2. That hydrolysis sets a small [H⁺] and hence [OH⁻] in the solution; feeding this [OH⁻] back into the overall two-step base equilibrium then fixes the vanishingly small concentration of undissociated Fe(OH)2 that this question asks for.
Key relation Fe(OH)2 ⇌ Fe²⁺ + 2OH⁻, Kb = Kb1Kb2 = [Fe²⁺][OH⁻]²/[Fe(OH)2] and Ka(Fe²⁺) = Kw/Kb2 gives the solution's [H⁺], hence [OH⁻].
Solution
1. [Fe²⁺] = 0.1 M. Its hydrolysis Fe²⁺ + H2O ⇌ Fe(OH)⁺ + H⁺ has Ka = Kw/Kb2 = 10⁻¹⁴/2.5×10⁻⁶ = 4×10⁻⁹.
2. [H⁺] = √(KaC) = √(4×10⁻⁹ × 0.1) = √(4×10⁻¹⁰) = 2×10⁻⁵ M. α ≪ 1, so the single-step approximation is safe.
3. From Kw: [OH⁻] = Kw/[H⁺] = 10⁻¹⁴/(2×10⁻⁵) = 5×10⁻¹⁰ M.
4. Overall base constant Kb = Kb1Kb2 = 10⁻⁴ × 2.5×10⁻⁶ = 2.5×10⁻¹⁰ = [Fe²⁺][OH⁻]²/[Fe(OH)2].
5. Rearrange: [Fe(OH)2] = [Fe²⁺][OH⁻]²/Kb = 0.1 × (5×10⁻¹⁰)²/(2.5×10⁻¹⁰) = 0.1 × 2.5×10⁻¹⁹/2.5×10⁻¹⁰.
6. [Fe(OH)2] = 0.1 × 10⁻⁹ = 10⁻¹⁰ M, which matches option (c).
 Fe2+ = 0.1 M; Ka(Fe2+) = Kw/Kb2 = 4e-9
 [H+] = sqrt(4e-9 * 0.1) = 2e-5
 [OH-] = 1e-14 / 2e-5 = 5e-10
 Kb = Kb1 Kb2 = 2.5e-10
    = [Fe2+][OH-]^2 / [Fe(OH)2]
 [Fe(OH)2] = 0.1*(5e-10)^2/2.5e-10
           = 1e-10 M                <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The undissociated base stays vanishingly small because [OH⁻] enters squared in an acidic medium. Option (b) 2.5×10⁻⁶ is the trap that quotes Kb2 alone and ignores the hydrolysis-set [OH⁻]; the correct route always goes through the combined Kb1Kb2.
Answer: (c) 10⁻¹⁰ M.
5Calculate approximate pH of the resultant solution formed by titration of 25 mL of 0.04 M Na2CO3 with 50 mL of 0.025 M HCl.
[Given: pKa1 = 6.4 and pKa2 = 10.3 for H2CO3]
(a) 5.92   (b) 6.88   (c) 6.4   (d) 5.88		Carbonate-HCl titration pHIONIC EQUILIBRIUM -II | Acid - Base Titration | NaHCO3 vs HCl | Na2CO3 vs HCl | pH calculation🟡 Similar(b) 6.88
Concept Na2CO3 is a diprotic base and HCl protonates it in two stages, CO32−→HCO3−→H2CO3. The trap is to decide which stage the added acid lands in: here the acid is more than enough to finish the first step but far short of the second, so a HCO3−/H2CO3 buffer forms and pKa1 rules.
Key relation pH = pKa1 + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) valid once we are between the first and second equivalence points.
Solution
1. n(CO32−) = 25×0.04 = 1.0 mmol; n(HCl) = 50×0.025 = 1.25 mmol.
2. Stage 1: CO32−+H+→HCO3− consumes 1.0 mmol H+, giving 1.0 mmol HCO3−; H+ left = 0.25 mmol.
3. Stage 2: HCO3−+H+→H2CO3: the 0.25 mmol H+ makes 0.25 mmol H2CO3. HCO3− remaining = 1.0 − 0.25 = 0.75 mmol.
4. So a buffer: 0.75 mmol HCO3− with 0.25 mmol H2CO3 (volume cancels in the ratio).
5. pH = pKa1 + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) = 6.4 + log(0.75/0.25) = 6.4 + log 3 = 6.4 + 0.48 = 6.88.
 H+ added = 1.25 mmol , CO3(2-) = 1.00 mmol
 -----------------------------------------
 stage 1 : all CO3 -> HCO3 (1.00)  H+ left 0.25
 stage 2 : 0.25 HCO3 -> H2CO3
   HCO3 = 0.75 , H2CO3 = 0.25
 pH = 6.4 + log(0.75/0.25) = 6.4 + 0.48 = 6.88
⚡ JEE Trick Compare H+ with the two landmarks: 1.0 mmol (first equivalence) and 2.0 mmol (second). Since 1.0 < 1.25 < 2.0 you are in the bicarbonate buffer, so use pKa1 — not (pKa1+pKa2)/2, which is only the pH exactly at the first (amphoteric) equivalence point.
Answer: (b) 6.88.
650 mL of 0.05 M Na2CO3 is titrated against 0.1 M HCl. On adding 40 mL of HCl, pH of the solution will be
[Given : For H2CO3, pKa1 = 6.35, pKa2 = 10.33; log 3 = 0.477, log 2 = 0.30]
(a) 6.35   (b) 6.526   (c) 8.34   (d) 6.173		Carbonate-HCl titration pHIONIC EQUILIBRIUM -II | Acid - Base Titration | NaHCO3 vs HCl | Na2CO3 vs HCl | pH calculation🟡 Similar(d) 6.173
Concept HCl neutralises Na2CO3 in two steps. With more than one but less than two equivalents of acid per carbonate, the mixture is a HCO3−/H2CO3 buffer set by pKa1. The trap is treating all the added HCl as a strong-acid excess.
Key relation pH = pKa1 + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) the carbonic-acid buffer region, pKa1 = 6.35.
Solution
1. n(CO32−) = 50×0.05 = 2.5 mmol; n(HCl) = 40×0.1 = 4.0 mmol.
2. Stage 1 uses 2.5 mmol H+: all CO32−→HCO3− (2.5 mmol); H+ left = 1.5 mmol.
3. Stage 2: 1.5 mmol H+ converts 1.5 mmol HCO3−→H2CO3. HCO3− left = 2.5 − 1.5 = 1.0 mmol.
4. Buffer: 1.0 mmol HCO3− and 1.5 mmol H2CO3.
5. log(1.0/1.5) = −log 1.5 = −(log 3 − log 2) = −(0.477 − 0.30) = −0.177.
6. pH = pKa1 + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) = 6.35 − 0.177 = 6.173.
 H+ = 4.0 mmol , CO3(2-) = 2.5 mmol
 ----------------------------------------
 stage1: CO3 -> HCO3 (2.5)   H+ left 1.5
 stage2: 1.5 HCO3 -> H2CO3
   HCO3 = 1.0 , H2CO3 = 1.5
 log(1/1.5) = -log1.5 = -0.177
 pH = 6.35 - 0.177 = 6.173
⚡ JEE Trick 4.0 mmol H+ sits between 2.5 (first eq.) and 5.0 (second eq.), so you are past the half mark of the second step — H2CO3 now exceeds HCO3−, dragging pH just below pKa1. Distractor (a) 6.35 wrongly assumes a 1:1 ratio.
Answer: (d) 6.173.
7A3B2 is a sparingly soluble salt of molar mass M (g mol−1) and solubility x g litre−1. The ratio of the molar concentration of B3− to the solubility product of the salt is:
(a) 108 x5/M5   (b) (1/108) M4/x4   (c) (1/54) M4/x4   (d) None of these
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 5 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Solubility product of A3B2Ionic Equilibrium 08 | Solubility and Solubility Product IIT JEE MAINS / JEE ADVANCE / NEET ||🟡 Similar(c) (1/54) M⁴/x⁴
Concept Write everything in terms of the molar solubility s, then take the asked ratio. The trap is forgetting the stoichiometric coefficients (3 and 2) that multiply s, and mixing up mass solubility (g/L) with molar solubility (mol/L).
Key relation A3B2 ⇄ 3A2+ + 2B3−; Ksp = (3s)3(2s)2 = 108 s5 s = molar solubility = x/M.
Solution
1. Charge balance fixes charges: 2 B3− = −6, so 3 A = +6, i.e. A2+.
2. Molar solubility s = (x g/L)/(M g/mol) = x/M mol/L.
3. On dissolving s: [A2+] = 3s and [B3−] = 2s.
4. Ksp = [A2+]3[B3−]2 = (3s)3(2s)2 = 27·4·s5 = 108 s5.
5. Ratio = [B3−]/Ksp = 2s/(108 s5) = 1/(54 s4).
6. Put s = x/M: 1/(54 s4) = M4/(54 x4) = (1/54) M4/x4.
 s = x/M
 [B3-] = 2s ,  Ksp = (3s)^3 (2s)^2 = 108 s^5
 -----------------------------------------
 [B3-]/Ksp = 2s / (108 s^5) = 1/(54 s^4)
           = (1/54) (M/x)^4
           = (1/54) M^4 / x^4        <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick The 2 in the numerator and the 108 in Ksp cut to 1/54, not 1/108: option (b) drops the factor-2 from [B3−] = 2s. Also note the units self-check — the answer carries M4/x4, i.e. (mol/g)4·... giving the reciprocal-concentration4 dimension of (conc)/Ksp.
Answer: (c) (1/54) M4/x4.
8AgBr(s) + 2S2O32− (aq.) ⇄ Ag(S2O3)23− (aq.) + Br− (aq.)
[Using: Ksp (AgBr) = 5×10−13, Kf (Ag(S2O3)23−) = 5×1013]
What is the molar solubility of AgBr in 0.1 M Na2S2O3?
(a) 0.5 M   (b) 0.45 M   (c) 0.045 M   (d) None of these		Complex formation, solubility19 - Solubility due to Complex formation | Ionic Equilibrium | JEE Mains and Advanced | HP Sir🟡 Similar(c) 0.045 M
Concept AgBr dissolves far more in thiosulfate because the dissolution (Ksp) couples with complex formation (Kf). Add the two equilibria so Ag+ cancels; the overall K is their product. The trap is forgetting that each mole dissolved eats two moles of S2O32−.
Key relation K = Ksp·Kf = [Ag(S2O3)23−][Br−]/[S2O32−]2 Ag+ cancels when the two steps are added.
Solution
1. K = Ksp·Kf = (5×10−13)(5×1013) = 25.
2. Let molar solubility = s. Then [complex] = s and [Br−] = s.
3. S2O32− consumed = 2s, so [S2O32−] = 0.1 − 2s.
4. K = s·s/(0.1 − 2s)2 = 25. one unknown, so take the square root.
5. s/(0.1 − 2s) = √25 = 5 ⇒ s = 0.5 − 10s.
6. 11s = 0.5 ⇒ s = 0.0455 ≈ 0.045 M.
 K = Ksp*Kf = 5e-13 * 5e13 = 25
 s^2 / (0.1 - 2s)^2 = 25
 -----------------------------------------
 s / (0.1 - 2s) = 5
 s = 0.5 - 10s   ->  11 s = 0.5
 s = 0.0455 ~ 0.045 M           <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Because K = 25 is large, dissolution is nearly complete — but not quite: option (a) 0.5 M would need all 0.1 M thiosulfate to react 1:1, ignoring the 2:1 stoichiometry. Carrying the −2s keeps s below 0.05, giving 0.045 M.
Answer: (c) 0.045 M.
9What is [Ag+] in a solution made by dissolving both Ag2CrO4 and Ag2C2O4 until saturation is reached with respect to both salts?
[Ksp (Ag2C2O4) = 2×10−11, Ksp (Ag2CrO4) = 2×10−12]
(a) 2.80×10−4   (b) 7.6×10−5   (c) 6.63×10−6   (d) 3.52×10−4		Simultaneous solubility equilibria23- Simultaneous solubility |Common ion effect| Ionic equilibrium| NEET| Class11| NCERT| NTA🟡 Similar(d) 3.52×10−4
Concept Both silver salts share the same [Ag+], so the two Ksp expressions are linked through one common variable. A mass/charge balance ties [Ag+] to the sum of both anions. The trap is solving each salt alone — their solubilities are not independent here.
Key relation [Ag+] = 2[C2O42−] + 2[CrO42−], with [X2−] = Ksp/[Ag+]2 each Ag2X gives 2 Ag+ per anion.
Solution
1. Let [Ag+] = a. Then [C2O42−] = 2×10−11/a2 and [CrO42−] = 2×10−12/a2.
2. Charge balance (Ag+ balances both divalent anions): a = 2[C2O42−] + 2[CrO42−].
3. a = (2/a2)(2×10−11 + 2×10−12) = (2/a2)(2.2×10−11).
4. a3 = 4.4×10−11 = 44×10−12.
5. a = (44)1/3×10−4 = 3.53×10−4 M (since 3.533 ≈ 44).
6. Matches the key's 3.52×10−4 (rounding of the cube root).
 [X] = Ksp / a^2       (a = [Ag+])
 a = 2[C2O4] + 2[CrO4]
 -----------------------------------------
 a = (2/a^2)(2e-11 + 2e-12)
 a^3 = 4.4e-11 = 44e-12
 a = 44^(1/3) e-4 = 3.53e-4 M   <-- (d)
⚡ JEE Trick The more soluble salt (larger Ksp, the oxalate) dominates the common-ion pool. Treating only Ag2C2O4 gives a = (4×10−11)1/3 ≈ 3.4×10−4 (option c-region); adding the chromate's small extra pushes it to 3.5×10−4.
Answer: (d) 3.52×10−4 M.
10 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (vi): Acid-base indicators are either weak organic acids or weak organic bases. Indicator changes colour in dilute solution when the hydronium ion concentration reaches a particular value. For example, phenolphthalein is a colourless substance in any aqueous solution with a pH less than 8.3. In between the pH range 8.3 to 10, transition of colour (colourless to pink) takes place and if pH of solution is greater than 10 solution is dark pink. Considering an acid indicator HIn, the equilibrium involving it and it's conjugate base can be represented as : HIn (acidic form) ⇄ H+ + In (basic form). pH of solution can be computed as: pH = pKIn + log([In−]/[HIn]). In general, transition of colour takes place in between pH range pKIn ± 1.
(i) An indicator is a weak acid and pH range is 4.0 to 6.0. If indicator is 50% ionized in a given solution then what is the ionization constant of the acid?
(a) 10−4   (b) 10−5   (c) 10−6   (d) None of these		Indicator ionization constantTITRATION & USE OF INDICATORS || Basic to Advanced || Class 11th JEE🟡 Similar(b) 10−5
Concept An indicator's visible transition spans pKIn ± 1, so the midpoint of the pH range is pKIn itself. At 50% ionization the acid and base forms are equal, and that is exactly where pH = pKIn. The trap is reading the range ends (4 or 6) as the answer.
Key relation pH = pKIn + log([In−]/[HIn]); at 50% ionization [In−] = [HIn] so the log term vanishes and pH = pKIn = KIn as 10−pH.
Solution
1. Transition range = pKIn ± 1, i.e. (pKIn−1) to (pKIn+1).
2. Given range 4.0 to 6.0, so pKIn − 1 = 4 and pKIn + 1 = 6.
3. Midpoint: pKIn = (4 + 6)/2 = 5.
4. 50% ionized ⇒ [In−] = [HIn] ⇒ log([In−]/[HIn]) = 0 ⇒ pH = pKIn = 5.
5. KIn (the ionization constant) = [H+][In−]/[HIn] = [H+] (since In− = HIn).
6. KIn = 10−pH = 10−5.
 range = pKIn +/- 1 = 4 ... 6
 pKIn = (4+6)/2 = 5
 -----------------------------------------
 50% ionized: [In-] = [HIn]
 Ka = [H+] = 10^(-pKIn) = 10^-5     <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick "50% ionized" is a flag for pH = pKIn — the colour-change midpoint — so you never need the actual [HIn]. Options (a) 10−4 and (c) 10−6 are the range endpoints, i.e. the 9%/91% colour limits, not the half-way constant.
Answer: (b) 10−5.
10 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (vi): Acid-base indicators are either weak organic acids or weak organic bases. Indicator changes colour in dilute solution when the hydronium ion concentration reaches a particular value. For example, phenolphthalein is a colourless substance in any aqueous solution with a pH less than 8.3. In between the pH range 8.3 to 10, transition of colour (colourless to pink) takes place and if pH of solution is greater than 10 solution is dark pink. Considering an acid indicator HIn, the equilibrium involving it and it's conjugate base can be represented as : HIn (acidic form) ⇄ H+ + In (basic form). pH of solution can be computed as: pH = pKIn + log([In−]/[HIn]). In general, transition of colour takes place in between pH range pKIn ± 1.
(ii) Select the correct statement(s)
(a) At midway in the transition of an acidic indicator, pH = pKIn
(b) Methyl orange (3.1 to 4.4) is a suitable indicator for titration of weak acid and strong base
(c) Bromothymol blue (6.0 to 7.6) is a good indicator for titration of HCl and NaOH
(d) Thymol blue (1.2 to 2.8) is a very good indicator for titration of 100 mL of 0.1 M NH4OH (pKb = 4.74) and 0.1 M HCl		Indicator selection statements23 Ionic Equilibrium | Theory of Indicators | Equivalence Point | JEE Main Adv | Chemistry Class 11🟡 Similar(a), (c) statements (a) and (c)
Concept An indicator fits a titration only if its transition range straddles the equivalence pH. So decide the equivalence pH for each pairing (strong/weak), then compare with the quoted range. The trap is picking an indicator by acid/base type rather than by the salt formed at equivalence.
Key relation indicator valid ⇔ (pKIn−1 to pKIn+1) contains pHequiv weak-acid salts ⇒ basic equiv.; weak-base salts ⇒ acidic equiv.
Solution
1. (a) At midway of the transition [In−] = [HIn], so pH = pKIn. True.
2. (b) Weak acid + strong base ⇒ equivalence salt is basic, pH ≈ 8–9. Methyl orange (3.1–4.4) changes far too early. False.
3. (c) HCl + NaOH is strong–strong, equivalence pH = 7. Bromothymol blue (6.0–7.6) brackets 7. True.
4. (d) NH4OH + HCl ⇒ 0.05 M NH4Cl; pKa(NH4+) = 14 − 4.74 = 9.26.
5. pH = (1/2)(pKa − log C) = (1/2)(9.26 + 1.30) = 5.28, not 1.2–2.8. (d) False.
6. Correct statements: (a) and (c).
 (a) mid-transition pH = pKIn ............ TRUE
 (b) wk acid+str base eq ~8-9, MO 3.1-4.4  FALSE
 (c) HCl+NaOH eq = 7, BTB 6.0-7.6 ........ TRUE
 (d) NH4Cl 0.05M: pH=0.5(9.26+1.30)=5.28
     thymol blue 1.2-2.8 .................. FALSE
⚡ JEE Trick Match the indicator to the equivalence salt: acidic salt (NH4Cl) needs an acid-range indicator near pH 5, basic salt needs pH 8–9, neutral salt needs ~7. Statements (b) and (d) both pair a basic/near-neutral equivalence with a strongly acidic indicator, so both fail.
Answer: (a) and (c).
10 (iii)Passage Q. (i) to (vi): Acid-base indicators are either weak organic acids or weak organic bases. Indicator changes colour in dilute solution when the hydronium ion concentration reaches a particular value. For example, phenolphthalein is a colourless substance in any aqueous solution with a pH less than 8.3. In between the pH range 8.3 to 10, transition of colour (colourless to pink) takes place and if pH of solution is greater than 10 solution is dark pink. Considering an acid indicator HIn, the equilibrium involving it and it's conjugate base can be represented as : HIn (acidic form) ⇄ H+ + In (basic form). pH of solution can be computed as: pH = pKIn + log([In−]/[HIn]). In general, transition of colour takes place in between pH range pKIn ± 1.
(iii) Following is the titration curve of two acids HA and HB (5 milli-moles each) titrated against strong base NaOH (0.1 M). What is pKa for HB acid?
(a) 3   (b) 4   (c) 5   (d) 6		pKa from titration curveHow to find pKa and Ka from a Titration Curve🟡 Similar(c) 5
Concept On a weak-acid titration curve, the pH at the half-equivalence volume equals pKa — the buffer midpoint, where equal amounts of HB and B− coexist so the Henderson log term is zero. Read that plateau for HB. The curve here is a coarse hand sketch, so the reliable move is to cross-check against the later parts of this passage, which pin the value exactly.
Key relation at V = (1/2)Veq: pH = pKa + log([B−]/[HB]) = pKa because [B−] = [HB] at half-neutralisation.
Solution
1. 5 mmol acid with 0.1 M NaOH ⇒ Veq = 5/0.1 = 50 mL; half-equivalence at 25 mL.
2. At 25 mL the HB plateau gives pH = pKa(HB); on the sketch this sits by the pH-5 gridline, above HB's start and below the steep jump.
3. HA (the weaker acid) runs higher, its half-equivalence plateau near pH 6, confirming HB is the stronger acid of the two.
4. Cross-check via part (vi): the correct indicator is phenolphthalein (8.3–10), so the equivalence pH of HB is ≈ 8.8–9.
5. Equivalence pH of a weak-acid salt = (1/2)(14 + pKa + log C) with C = 0.05 M (5 mmol in 100 mL).
6. Set = 8.85: pKa = 2×8.85 − 14 − log 0.05 = 17.70 − 14 + 1.30 = 5.0.
7. Curve reading and algebra agree: pKa(HB) = 5.
 Veq = 5 mmol / 0.1 M = 50 mL
 half-equiv (25 mL): pH = pKa(HB)
 -----------------------------------------
 check: eq pH = 0.5(14 + pKa + log0.05) = 8.85
   -> pKa = 17.70 - 14 + 1.30 = 5.0   <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick When a hand-drawn graph is ambiguous, let the other sub-parts fix it: an equivalence pH of 8.85 (part v) and a phenolphthalein end-point (part vi) are only consistent with pKa(HB) = 5, and then HA at pKa = 6 makes the exchange constant (part iv) equal 10.
Answer: (c) 5.
10 (iv)Passage Q. (i) to (vi): Acid-base indicators are either weak organic acids or weak organic bases. Indicator changes colour in dilute solution when the hydronium ion concentration reaches a particular value. For example, phenolphthalein is a colourless substance in any aqueous solution with a pH less than 8.3. In between the pH range 8.3 to 10, transition of colour (colourless to pink) takes place and if pH of solution is greater than 10 solution is dark pink. Considering an acid indicator HIn, the equilibrium involving it and it's conjugate base can be represented as : HIn (acidic form) ⇄ H+ + In (basic form). pH of solution can be computed as: pH = pKIn + log([In−]/[HIn]). In general, transition of colour takes place in between pH range pKIn ± 1.
(iv) What is the equilibrium constant for the reaction HB(aq.) + NaA(aq.) ⇄ HA(aq.) + NaB(aq.)?
(a) 10   (b) 0.1   (c) 10−7   (d) 10−11		Equilibrium constant of acid exchangeA certain weak acid has a dissociation constant of 1.0 x 10-4. The equilibrium constant...🟡 Similar(a) 10
Concept A proton-transfer between two acid/base pairs has an equilibrium constant equal to the ratio of the two acid dissociation constants. The stronger acid (HB) hands its proton to the weaker acid's anion (A−). The trap is inverting the ratio.
Key relation HB + A− ⇄ HA + B−; K = [HA][B−]/([HB][A−]) = Ka(HB)/Ka(HA) multiply/divide the two Ka expressions — [H+] cancels.
Solution
1. From part (iii), pKa(HB) = 5, so Ka(HB) = 10−5.
2. HA is the weaker acid: pKa(HA) = 6, so Ka(HA) = 10−6.
3. Write Ka(HB) = [H+][B−]/[HB] and Ka(HA) = [H+][A−]/[HA].
4. Divide: Ka(HB)/Ka(HA) = ([B−]/[HB])·([HA]/[A−]) = [HA][B−]/([HB][A−]) = K.
5. K = Ka(HB)/Ka(HA) = 10−5/10−6 = 10.
6. K > 1 confirms the forward (stronger-acid-donates) direction is favoured.
 HB + A- <=> HA + B-
 K = Ka(HB) / Ka(HA)
 -----------------------------------------
 K = 10^-5 / 10^-6 = 10            <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick For any acid-swap A1 + base2 ⇄ base1 + A2, K = Ka(reactant acid)/Ka(product acid). HB (stronger) is the reactant acid, so K = 10−5/10−6 = 10; option (b) 0.1 is the reverse reaction.
Answer: (a) 10.
10 (v)Passage Q. (i) to (vi): Acid-base indicators are either weak organic acids or weak organic bases. Indicator changes colour in dilute solution when the hydronium ion concentration reaches a particular value. For example, phenolphthalein is a colourless substance in any aqueous solution with a pH less than 8.3. In between the pH range 8.3 to 10, transition of colour (colourless to pink) takes place and if pH of solution is greater than 10 solution is dark pink. Considering an acid indicator HIn, the equilibrium involving it and it's conjugate base can be represented as : HIn (acidic form) ⇄ H+ + In (basic form). pH of solution can be computed as: pH = pKIn + log([In−]/[HIn]). In general, transition of colour takes place in between pH range pKIn ± 1.
(v) Calculate the pH at equivalent point when HB is titrated with NaOH.
(a) 8.75   (b) 8.85   (c) 9.0   (d) None of these		pH at equivalence pointWeak Acid / Strong Base Titration - All pH Calculations🟡 Similar(b) 8.85
Concept At the equivalence point of a weak acid with NaOH, no free acid or base is left — only the salt NaB. Its anion B− is a weak base that hydrolyses (B− + H2O ⇄ HB + OH−), so the solution is basic. Use the salt-of-weak-acid pH formula with the diluted concentration. The trap is using the acid's own pH expression, or forgetting that the added titrant doubles the volume.
Key relation pH = (1/2)(pKw + pKa + log C) from Kb = Kw/Ka and [OH−] = √(KbC); Kw = 10−14.
Solution
1. pKa(HB) = 5 (part iii). At equivalence all HB has become NaB.
2. 5 mmol HB needs 50 mL of 0.1 M NaOH; starting from 50 mL of 0.1 M acid, total volume = 100 mL.
3. Salt concentration C = 5 mmol/100 mL = 0.05 M (halved by the titrant volume).
4. log C = log 0.05 = log 5 − 2 = 0.70 − 2 = −1.30.
5. pH = (1/2)(14 + 5 + log 0.05) = (1/2)(14 + 5 − 1.30) = (1/2)(17.70).
6. pH = 8.85, comfortably basic as expected for a weak-acid equivalence point.
 at eq: only NaB , C = 5 mmol/100 mL = 0.05 M
 Kb = Kw/Ka  ->  basic solution
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = 0.5(14 + pKa + log C)
    = 0.5(14 + 5 - 1.30)
    = 0.5(17.70) = 8.85            <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick The equivalence pH of a weak acid is always > 7 and rises with pKa; here 8.85. Option (c) 9.0 drops the log C dilution term (assuming C = 0.1 M), and (a) 8.75 mis-rounds log 5.
Answer: (b) 8.85.
10 (vi)Passage Q. (i) to (vi): Acid-base indicators are either weak organic acids or weak organic bases. Indicator changes colour in dilute solution when the hydronium ion concentration reaches a particular value. For example, phenolphthalein is a colourless substance in any aqueous solution with a pH less than 8.3. In between the pH range 8.3 to 10, transition of colour (colourless to pink) takes place and if pH of solution is greater than 10 solution is dark pink. Considering an acid indicator HIn, the equilibrium involving it and it's conjugate base can be represented as : HIn (acidic form) ⇄ H+ + In (basic form). pH of solution can be computed as: pH = pKIn + log([In−]/[HIn]). In general, transition of colour takes place in between pH range pKIn ± 1.
(vi) Which of the following indicator is most suitable for titration of HB with strong base:
(a) Phenolphthalein (8.3 − 10)   (b) Bromothymol blue (6 − 7.6)   (c) Methyl red (4.2 − 6.3)   (d) Malachite green (11.4 − 13)		Indicator for weak acid titrationTITRATION & USE OF INDICATORS || Basic to Advanced || Class 11th JEE🟡 Similar(a) Phenolphthalein (8.3–10)
Concept The right indicator is the one whose colour-change range contains the equivalence pH of the titration, so that its end-point falls on the steep part of the curve. HB is a weak acid, so its salt NaB hydrolyses and the equivalence is basic (pH > 7). Pick the indicator that brackets that pH. The trap is choosing a mid-range indicator (pH 6–7) that suits strong-acid/strong-base titrations but changes too early here.
Key relation best indicator: (pKIn−1 to pKIn+1) ∋ pHequiv pHequiv(HB) = 8.85 from part (v).
Solution
1. From part (v) the equivalence pH for HB + NaOH is 8.85.
2. (a) Phenolphthalein 8.3–10 — 8.85 lies inside this range. Fits.
3. (b) Bromothymol blue 6–7.6 — ends below 8.85, so it changes well before the equivalence. No.
4. (c) Methyl red 4.2–6.3 — far too acidic for a basic end-point. No.
5. (d) Malachite green 11.4–13 — changes only past the equivalence, overshooting. No.
6. Only phenolphthalein straddles 8.85, catching the steep pH rise at equivalence.
 eq pH (HB + NaOH) = 8.85
 need range containing 8.85
 -----------------------------------------
 (a) phenolphthalein 8.3-10 .... YES   <-- (a)
 (b) BTB 6-7.6 ................. too low
 (c) methyl red 4.2-6.3 ....... too low
 (d) malachite green 11.4-13 .. too high
⚡ JEE Trick Weak-acid + strong-base end-points sit at pH 8–10, which is phenolphthalein's home range — the classic reason phenolphthalein is the standard indicator for acetic-acid/NaOH too. Anything peaking at/near pH 7 (BTB) under-shoots the steep basic jump.
Answer: (a) Phenolphthalein (8.3–10).

Ionic Equilibrium · Level 8

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1Equilibrium constants of T2O (T or 1H3 is an isotope of 1H1) and H2O are different at 298 K. Let at 298 K pure T2O has pT (like pH) is 7.62. The pT of a solution prepared by adding 10 mL of 0.2 M TCl to 15 mL of 0.25 M NaOT is:
(a) 2 − log 7   (b) 14 + log 7   (c) 13.24 − log 7   (d) 13.24 + log 7		pT of T2O self-ionizationN.Avasthi |Ionic Equilibrium | Level-2 Q1| Equilibrium constant of T2O(T is tritium) and H2O are...🟢 Exact(d) 13.24 + log 7
Concept T2O (heavy water's tritium analogue) self-ionizes exactly like H2O but with its own ion-product KT. Pure T2O has pT = 7.62, and since [T+] = [OT−] there, pKT = 2×7.62 = 15.24. TCl is a strong acid, NaOT a strong base; the excess base fixes pOT, and pT + pOT = pKT closes it. The trap is reflexively using 14 (water's value) for pKT.
Key relation pKT = 2·pT(pure) = 15.24; pT + pOT = pKT the neutral point of T2O is 7.62, not 7.
Solution
1. pKT = 2×7.62 = 15.24 (KT = [T+][OT−] = 10−15.24).
2. n(TCl) = 10×0.2 = 2.0 mmol (strong acid); n(NaOT) = 15×0.25 = 3.75 mmol (strong base).
3. Strong acid + strong base neutralise 1:1, leaving excess OT− = 3.75 − 2.0 = 1.75 mmol.
4. Total volume = 10 + 15 = 25 mL, so [OT−] = 1.75/25 = 0.07 M.
5. pOT = −log(0.07) = −log(7×10−2) = 2 − log 7.
6. pT = pKT − pOT = 15.24 − (2 − log 7) = 13.24 + log 7.
 pKT = 2 * 7.62 = 15.24
 OT- excess = 3.75 - 2.0 = 1.75 mmol / 25 mL
 -----------------------------------------
 [OT-] = 0.07 M -> pOT = 2 - log 7
 pT = 15.24 - (2 - log7) = 13.24 + log7  <-- (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Everything about water carries over to T2O except the numbers: replace 14 by pKT = 2×pT(pure). Option (b) 14 + log 7 is the trap of reusing water's 14; the correct neutral point is 7.62, giving 13.24 after subtracting 2.
Answer: (d) 13.24 + log 7.
220 mL of 0.1 M weak acid HA (Ka = 10−5) is mixed with solution of 10 mL of 0.3 M HCl and 10 mL of 0.1 M NaOH. Find the value of [A−]/([HA] + [A−]) in the resulting solution:
(a) 2×10−4   (b) 2×10−5   (c) 2×10−3   (d) 0.05		Fraction of dissociated weak acidIonic equilibrium Lecture 4 |PH calculation of weak acid and base and mixtures| JEE ADVANCED+MAINS|🟡 Similar(a) 2×10−4
Concept The strong acid left after mixing fixes [H+], and that common ion suppresses the weak acid HA. The asked fraction [A−]/([HA]+[A−]) is just the degree of dissociation α of HA in that [H+]. The trap is letting HA set its own [H+].
Key relation [A−]/([HA]+[A−]) = Ka/(Ka + [H+]) ≈ Ka/[H+] [H+] comes from the leftover strong acid.
Solution
1. n(HA) = 20×0.1 = 2.0 mmol; n(HCl) = 10×0.3 = 3.0 mmol; n(NaOH) = 10×0.1 = 1.0 mmol.
2. NaOH neutralises strong HCl first: HCl left = 3.0 − 1.0 = 2.0 mmol.
3. Total volume = 20 + 10 + 10 = 40 mL, so [H+] = 2.0/40 = 0.05 M.
4. For HA: Ka = [H+][A−]/[HA] ⇒ [A−]/[HA] = Ka/[H+] = 10−5/0.05 = 2×10−4.
5. Fraction = [A−]/([HA]+[A−]) = (2×10−4)/(1 + 2×10−4) ≈ 2×10−4.
6. The 2×10−4 ≪ 1 approximation is self-consistent.
 HCl left = 3.0 - 1.0 = 2.0 mmol / 40 mL
 [H+] = 0.05 M (strong acid dominates)
 -----------------------------------------
 Ka = [H+][A-]/[HA]
 [A-]/[HA] = Ka/[H+] = 1e-5/0.05 = 2e-4
 fraction = [A-]/([HA]+[A-]) ~ 2e-4  <-- (a)
⚡ JEE Trick In a strong-acid background the weak acid barely dissociates, so its fraction ionized equals Ka/[H+] directly — and since that is ≪ 1, [HA] + [A−] ≈ [HA], so the +[A−] in the denominator is negligible. Option (d) 0.05 is just [H+] itself; (b) 2×10−5 uses the wrong [H+] (0.5 M, forgetting to deduct the NaOH that consumed part of the HCl).
Answer: (a) 2×10−4.
3A 1.025 g sample containing a weak acid HA (mol. wt. = 82) is dissolved in 60 mL water and titrated with 0.25 M NaOH. When half of the acid was neutralised the pH was found to be 5.0 and at the equivalence point the pH is 9.0. Calculate weight percentage of HX in sample :
(a) 50%   (b) 75%   (c) 80%   (d) None of these		Weight % of weak acidWeak Acid / Strong Base Titration - All pH Calculations🟡 Similar(c) 80%
Concept Two pH readings carry two pieces of information: the half-neutralisation pH equals pKa, and the equivalence pH fixes the salt concentration C through the weak-acid-salt formula. From C and the known volumes recover the moles of acid, hence its mass and weight-percent purity. The trap is forgetting that the titrant volume (VNaOH) dilutes the salt at the equivalence point.
Key relation half-neut: pH = pKa; equiv: pH = (1/2)(14 + pKa + log C) C = n(acid)/(Vwater + VNaOH).
Solution
1. Half-neutralised pH = 5.0 ⇒ pKa = 5, Ka = 10−5.
2. Equivalence: 9.0 = (1/2)(14 + 5 + log C) ⇒ 18 = 19 + log C ⇒ log C = −1.
3. C = 0.1 M = salt (NaA) concentration at the equivalence point.
4. Let n = mmol acid. V(NaOH) = n/0.25 = 4n mL; total volume = 60 + 4n mL.
5. C = n/(60 + 4n) = 0.1 ⇒ n = 6 + 0.4n ⇒ 0.6n = 6 ⇒ n = 10 mmol = 0.010 mol.
6. mass(HA) = 0.010×82 = 0.82 g; weight % = 0.82/1.025×100 = 80%.
7. Sanity: 0.82 g of a 1.025 g sample is four-fifths, matching 80%.
 half-neut pH = pKa = 5
 eq: 9 = 0.5(14 + 5 + logC) -> logC = -1 -> C=0.1
 -----------------------------------------
 n/(60 + 4n) = 0.1  ->  0.6 n = 6 -> n = 10 mmol
 mass = 0.010 * 82 = 0.82 g
 % = 0.82/1.025 * 100 = 80%        <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Two pH readings unlock both unknowns: half-neutralisation hands pKa for free, equivalence hands C. The question prints "HX" where it means HA — same acid. Ignoring the 4n mL of titrant (using C = n/60) would wrongly give 75% (option b).
Answer: (c) 80%.
4Which of the following expression for % ionization of a monoacidic base (BOH) in aqueous solution at appreciable concentration is not correct?
(a) 100 × √(Kb/c)   (b) 1/(1 + 10(pKb − pOH))   (c) Kw[H+]/(Kb + Kw)   (d) Kb/(Kb + [OH−])		% ionization of weak baseIonisation constant of a weak base MOH, is given by the expression Kb=[M+][OH-]/[MOH]🟡 Similar(c) Kw[H+]/(Kb+Kw)
Concept The fraction (degree) of ionization of BOH equals [B+]/([B+]+[BOH]). Rewrite it using Kb = [B+][OH−]/[BOH]; three options are exact or valid-approximation forms, one is dimensionally impossible. The trap is the √ form, which is a correct small-α approximation.
Key relation α = [B+]/([B+]+[BOH]) = Kb/(Kb + [OH−]) since [B+]/[BOH] = Kb/[OH−].
Solution
1. Exact fraction: α = Kb/(Kb + [OH−]) — this is option (d). Correct.
2. Divide top and bottom by Kb: α = 1/(1 + [OH−]/Kb). Now [OH−]/Kb = 10−pOH/10−pKb = 10(pKb−pOH).
3. So α = 1/(1 + 10(pKb−pOH)) — option (b). Correct.
4. For small α: α ≈ √(Kb/c), so % = 100√(Kb/c) — option (a). Correct (approx).
5. Option (c) Kw[H+]/(Kb+Kw): Kw[H+] has units M3, Kb+Kw adds M to M2. Dimensionally impossible — cannot be a dimensionless fraction.
6. Hence the expression that is NOT correct is (c).
 alpha = Kb/(Kb+[OH-])             (d) OK
       = 1/(1 + 10^(pKb - pOH))    (b) OK
 small alpha: 100*sqrt(Kb/c)       (a) OK
 -----------------------------------------
 (c) Kw[H+]/(Kb+Kw): units M^3/(M+M^2)
     not dimensionless -> WRONG    <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick A fast filter for "which is wrong" is a dimension check: a fraction must be dimensionless. (a), (b), (d) all reduce to pure ratios, but (c) mixes Kw[H+] (M3) over Kb+Kw and adds unlike units, so it is the odd one out.
Answer: (c) Kw[H+]/(Kb + Kw).
5A buffer solution 0.04 M in Na2HPO4 and 0.02 M in Na3PO4 is prepared. The electrolytic oxidation of 1.0 milli-mole of the organic compound RNHOH is carried out in 100 mL of the buffer. The reaction is
RNHOH + H2O → RNO2 + 4H+ + 4e−
The approximate pH of solution after the oxidation is complete is:
[Given : for H3PO4, pKa1 = 2.2; pKa2 = 7.20; pKa3 = 12]
(a) 6.90   (b) 7.20   (c) 7.5   (d) None of these		Phosphate buffer pH after oxidationPhosphate Buffer pH Calculation | Step-by-Step Henderson-Hasselbalch🟡 Similar(c) 7.5
Concept The oxidation dumps 4 mmol of strong H+ into a phosphate pool. Added acid is neutralised in order of basicity: PO43− (linked to pKa3=12, the strongest base) first, then HPO42−. Identify the buffer that survives before using Henderson.
Key relation pH = pKa2 + log([HPO42−]/[H2PO4−]) H2PO4− ⇄ H++HPO42− has pKa2=7.20, so this pair sets the final pH.
Solution
1. Moles in 100 mL: HPO42− = 0.04×0.1 = 4 mmol; PO43− = 0.02×0.1 = 2 mmol.
2. H+ produced = 1.0 mmol RNHOH × 4 e-equiv = 4 mmol.
3. PO43−+H+→HPO42−: 2 mmol H+ spent; PO43−→0, HPO42− = 4+2 = 6 mmol. Most basic anion goes first.
4. H+ left = 4−2 = 2 mmol now hits HPO42−+H+→H2PO4−: HPO42− = 6−2 = 4 mmol, H2PO4− = 2 mmol.
5. Survivor is the HPO42−/H2PO4− buffer, governed by pKa2 = 7.20.
6. pH = pKa2 + log([HPO42−]/[H2PO4−]) = 7.20 + log(4/2) = 7.20 + 0.30 = 7.50.
 H+ added = 4 mmol
 PO4(3-) 2 -> 0   uses 2 ; HPO4(2-): 4 -> 6
 HPO4(2-) 6 -> 4  uses 2 ; H2PO4(-): 0 -> 2
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = 7.20 + log(4/2) = 7.20 + 0.30 = 7.50
⚡ JEE Trick Spend H+ on the most basic species first; do not jump to pKa3 (option a 6.90) — the mole book-keeping lands squarely in the pKa2 window, and log 2 nudges 7.20 to 7.5.
Answer: (c) 7.5.
610 mL of 0.1 M tribasic acid H3A is titrated with 0.1 M NaOH solution. What is the ratio of [H3A]/[A3−] at 2nd equivalence point?
[Given : Ka1 = 10−3, Ka2 = 10−8, Ka3 = 10−12]
(a) ≈10−4   (b) ≈10+4   (c) ≈10−7   (d) ≈10+6		Tribasic acid titration ratio16 Ionic Equilibrium | Ph of Salt Containing Amphiprotic Anion | JEE Main Adv | Chemistry Class 11🟡 Similar(c) ≈10⁻⁷
Concept At the 2nd equivalence point of H3A, two of the three protons are removed, so the dominant species is the amphiprotic HA2−. Its pH is fixed by the two flanking constants Ka2 and Ka3. The wanted ratio collapses to powers of [H+] and the Ka's.
Key relation [H+] = √(Ka2Ka3); [H3A]/[A3−] = [H+]3/(Ka1Ka2Ka3) Both built from the stepwise Ka expressions; [HA2−] cancels.
Solution
1. 10 mL of 0.1 M H3A = 1 mmol acid; 2nd equivalence adds 2 mmol NaOH, converting all to HA2−.
2. Amphiprotic salt: [H+] = √(Ka2Ka3) = √(10−8·10−12) = 10−10 M. Independent of concentration here.
3. From Ka1,Ka2: [H3A] = [H+]2[HA2−]/(Ka1Ka2).
4. From Ka3: [A3−] = Ka3[HA2−]/[H+].
5. Divide: [H3A]/[A3−] = [H+]3/(Ka1Ka2Ka3) = (10−10)3/(10−3·10−8·10−12) = 10−30/10−23 = 10−7.
6. So the ratio ≈ 10−7.
 species at 2nd eq pt : HA(2-)
 [H+] = sqrt(Ka2*Ka3) = sqrt(1e-8*1e-12) = 1e-10
 ratio = [H+]^3 / (Ka1*Ka2*Ka3)
 -----------------------------------------
 = 1e-30 / 1e-23 = 1e-7
⚡ JEE Trick For any amphiprotic middle species use pH = (pKlower+pKupper)/2 = (8+12)/2 = 10 in one step. The ± sign traps (b)/(d): since [H+]<1, cubing it keeps the ratio tiny, not large.
Answer: (c) ≈10−7.
7A solution is 0.10 M Ba(NO3)2 and 0.10 M Sr(NO3)2. If solid Na2CrO4 is added to the solution, what is [Ba2+] when SrCrO4 begins to precipitate?
[Ksp (BaCrO4) = 1.2×10−10; Ksp (SrCrO4) = 3.5×10−5]
(a) 7.4×10−7   (b) 2.0×10−7   (c) 6.1×10−7   (d) 3.4×10−7		Selective precipitation (chromates)Advanced problem -Selective precipitation : Ionic Equilibrium CHEMISTRY🟡 Similar(d) 3.4×10⁻⁷
Concept Adding CrO42− to equal Ba2+/Sr2+ precipitates the less soluble salt first. BaCrO4 (tiny Ksp) drops out well before SrCrO4. The question fixes the moment SrCrO4 just starts and asks how little Ba2+ is left then.
Key relation precipitation starts when [M2+][CrO42−] = Ksp Both chromates are 1:1, so the [CrO42−] needed is Ksp/[cation].
Solution
1. SrCrO4 onset needs [CrO42−] = Ksp(SrCrO4)/[Sr2+] = 3.5×10−5/0.10.
2. = 3.5×10−4 M. This is the chromate level in solution at that instant.
3. BaCrO4 has long since been precipitating, so [Ba2+] is pinned by its own Ksp at this [CrO42−].
4. [Ba2+] = Ksp(BaCrO4)/[CrO42−] = 1.2×10−10/3.5×10−4.
5. = 3.43×10−7 ≈ 3.4×10−7 M.
6. Ba2+ has fallen from 0.10 to ∼3.4×10−7 M, i.e. >99.99% removed before Sr2+ even begins — a clean separation.
 [CrO4] at SrCrO4 onset = Ksp(Sr)/[Sr]
   = 3.5e-5 / 0.10 = 3.5e-4 M
 [Ba2+] = Ksp(Ba) / [CrO4]
 -----------------------------------------
   = 1.2e-10 / 3.5e-4 = 3.4e-7 M
⚡ JEE Trick Use the cation of the salt that precipitates LAST (Sr2+) to set [CrO42−], then plug into the other Ksp. Option (b) 2.0×10−7 comes from mistakenly using Ba in step 1.
Answer: (d) 3.4×10−7.
8A solution is 0.01 M KI and 0.1 M KCl. If solid AgNO3 is added to the solution, what is the [I−] when AgCl begins to precipitate?
[Ksp(AgI) = 1.5×10−16; Ksp(AgCl) = 1.8×10−10]
(a) 3.5×10−7   (b) 6.1×10−8   (c) 2.2×10−7   (d) 8.3×10−8		Selective precipitation (halides)17-Selective Precipitation | Applications of Ksp | Ionic Equilibrium |JEE Mains - Advanced | HP Sir🟡 Similar(d) 8.3×10⁻⁸
Concept Ag+ grabs I− first (Ksp(AgI) is far smaller), so AgI precipitates almost completely before AgCl starts. The question asks the residual [I−] at the exact point AgCl begins, which fixes [Ag+].
Key relation [Ag+] = Ksp(AgCl)/[Cl−]; [I−] = Ksp(AgI)/[Ag+] At the shared [Ag+] both saturation conditions hold simultaneously.
Solution
1. AgCl begins when [Ag+] reaches Ksp(AgCl)/[Cl−] = 1.8×10−10/0.1.
2. = 1.8×10−9 M. [Cl−] = 0.1 M barely changes, as almost no AgCl has formed yet.
3. At that [Ag+], AgI is saturated too, so [I−] = Ksp(AgI)/[Ag+].
4. [I−] = 1.5×10−16/1.8×10−9.
5. = 8.33×10−8 ≈ 8.3×10−8 M.
6. So I− has dropped from 0.01 to ∼8×10−8 M (>99.999% gone) before Cl− starts to precipitate.
 [Ag+] at AgCl onset = Ksp(AgCl)/[Cl-]
   = 1.8e-10 / 0.1 = 1.8e-9 M
 [I-] = Ksp(AgI) / [Ag+]
 -----------------------------------------
   = 1.5e-16 / 1.8e-9 = 8.3e-8 M
⚡ JEE Trick Drive [Ag+] with the salt that precipitates LATER (AgCl), then feed it to the earlier salt's Ksp. The ratio [I−]/[Cl−] at that point equals Ksp(AgI)/Ksp(AgCl) — a one-line sanity check.
Answer: (d) 8.3×10−8.
9What is the minimum pH required to prevent the precipitation of ZnS in a solution that is 0.01 M ZnCl2 and saturated with 0.10 M H2S?
[Given : Ksp = 10−21, Ka1 × Ka2 = 10−20]
(a) 0   (b) 1   (c) 2   (d) 4		pH control of sulphide precipitationWhat is the minimum pH required to prevent the precipitation of ZnS in a solution that is 0.01 M ...🟢 Exact(b) 1
Concept ZnS is stopped from precipitating by keeping [S2−] low, and acid does exactly that: raising [H+] suppresses the H2S dissociation. Find the [S2−] ceiling from Ksp, then the [H+] that holds sulphide at or below it.
Key relation [H+]2[S2−] = Ka1Ka2[H2S]; no ppt needs [Zn2+][S2−] ≤ Ksp H2S stays saturated at 0.10 M, so [S2−] is tuned purely by [H+].
Solution
1. No precipitation requires Q = [Zn2+][S2−] ≤ Ksp = 10−21.
2. [Zn2+] = 0.01 = 10−2, so allowed [S2−] ≤ 10−21/10−2 = 10−19 M.
3. For saturated H2S: [S2−] = Ka1Ka2[H2S]/[H+]2 = 10−20·0.10/[H+]2 = 10−21/[H+]2.
4. Set the ceiling: 10−21/[H+]2 = 10−19 ⇒ [H+]2 = 10−2.
5. [H+] = 10−1 M ⇒ pH = 1. Any lower pH (more acid) gives still less S2−, so the solution stays clear.
6. Thus pH = 1 is the threshold at which Q just equals Ksp; below it ZnS cannot form.
 max [S2-] = Ksp/[Zn2+] = 1e-21/1e-2 = 1e-19
 [S2-] = Ka1Ka2[H2S]/[H+]^2 = 1e-21/[H+]^2
 set equal: 1e-21/[H+]^2 = 1e-19
 -----------------------------------------
 [H+]^2 = 1e-2 ; [H+] = 1e-1 ; pH = 1
⚡ JEE Trick Combine both Ka into one step via Ka1Ka2 — the intermediate HS− never matters. Strictly this pH = 1 is the maximum pH that keeps ZnS dissolved; the book calls it the required pH, and the value is 1, so (b).
Answer: (b) 1.
10The salt Al(OH)3 is involved in the following two equilibria,
Al(OH)3(s) ⇄ Al3+(aq.) + 3OH−(aq.); Ksp
Al(OH)3(s) + OH−(aq.) ⇄ Al(OH)4−(aq.); Kc
Which of the following relationship is correct at which solubility is minimum?
(a) [OH−] = (Ksp/Kc)1/3   (b) [OH−] = (Kc/Ksp)1/4   (c) [OH−] = √((Ksp/Kc)1/4)   (d) None of these		Amphoteric hydroxide min solubilityThe Solubility of Amphoteric Metal Hydroxides🟡 Similar(d) None of these
Concept An amphoteric hydroxide dissolves two ways: as Al3+ (favoured at low [OH−]) and as Al(OH)4− (favoured at high [OH−]). Total solubility is a sum of one falling and one rising term, so it has a minimum. Minimise it by calculus.
Key relation S = [Al3+] + [Al(OH)4−] = Ksp/[OH−]3 + Kc[OH−] [Al3+]=Ksp/[OH−]3 and [Al(OH)4−]=Kc[OH−] from the two equilibria.
Solution
1. Let x = [OH−]. Then S(x) = Kspx−3 + Kcx.
2. For a minimum, dS/dx = 0.
3. dS/dx = −3Kspx−4 + Kc = 0. Set the derivative to zero.
4. Kc = 3Ksp/x4 ⇒ x4 = 3Ksp/Kc.
5. x = [OH−] = (3Ksp/Kc)1/4. Second derivative 12Ksp/x5 > 0 confirms a minimum.
6. Compare: (a) has power 1/3, (b) is inverted Kc/Ksp, (c) is power 1/8 — none equals (3Ksp/Kc)1/4, and all drop the factor 3.
7. Therefore the correct expression is listed nowhere: option (d).
 S = Ksp/x^3 + Kc*x        (x = [OH-])
 dS/dx = -3Ksp/x^4 + Kc = 0
 -> x^4 = 3Ksp/Kc
 -----------------------------------------
 [OH-] = (3Ksp/Kc)^(1/4)   -> not any option
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever solubility = (A/xn)+(Bx), the minimum sits at xn+1 = nA/B; here n=3 gives the 4th-root and the factor 3 that every printed option quietly omits.
Answer: (d) None of these.

Ionic Equilibrium · Level 9

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1The equilibrium constant for the ionization of RNH2 (g) in water as RNH2(g) + H2O(l) ⇄ RNH3+(aq) + OH−(aq) is 10−6 at 25°C. Which of the following is/ are correct?
(a) pH of the solution is 11.0 at partial pressure of RNH2 (g) = 1 bar.
(b) Forward reaction is favoured by the addition of HCl(aq.).
(c) Forward reaction is favoured by the addition of H2O(l).
(d) Forward reaction is favoured by the addition of RNH2 (g).		Gaseous amine ionization in waterpH of Weak Acids and Bases - Percent Ionization - Ka & Kb🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c), (d) all four correct
Concept RNH2 enters as a gas, so its partial pressure sits in the mass-action law: K = [RNH3+][OH−]/p(RNH2). Judge each option by Le Chatelier on this expression; water is the solvent (activity 1).
Key relation K = [RNH3+][OH−]/p(RNH2) = 10−6 Forward shift = anything that lowers the reaction quotient Q below K.
Solution
1. (a) At p = 1 bar, let [RNH3+] = [OH−] = x: x2/1 = 10−6 ⇒ x = 10−3.
2. [OH−] = 10−3 ⇒ pOH = 3 ⇒ pH = 14−3 = 11.0. (a) is correct.
3. (b) HCl supplies H+, which removes OH− (product); Q falls, equilibrium shifts forward. (b) correct.
4. (c) Adding H2O(l) dilutes both ionic products, so [RNH3+][OH−] drops while p is externally held; Q falls, shift forward. (c) correct.
5. (d) Adding RNH2(g) raises the reactant partial pressure, the denominator, so Q falls and the reaction goes forward. (d) correct.
6. Every statement holds, so the answer is (a), (b), (c) and (d).
 K = [RNH3+][OH-]/p = 1e-6
 p=1: x^2 = 1e-6 -> x = 1e-3 ; pH = 11   (a)
 +HCl  : removes OH-   -> forward         (b)
 +H2O  : dilutes prods -> forward         (c)
 +RNH2 : raises p      -> forward         (d)
⚡ JEE Trick With a gaseous reactant the pressure is a real concentration term, not a spectator; dilution by water helps ionisation of a weak base (Ostwald), so (c) is a genuine forward driver, not a trap.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d).
2A 2.5 g impure sample containing weak monoacidic base (Molecular weight = 45) is dissolved in 100 ml water and titrated with 0.5-M HCl at 25°C. When 1/5th of the base was neutralized, the pH was found to be 9 at equivalence point, the pH of solution is 4.5 (log 2 = 0.3).
(a) Kb of base is less than 10−6.
(b) Concentration of salt at equivalence point is 0.25 M.
(c) Volume of HCl used at equivalence point is 100 ml.
(d) Mass percentage of base in given sample is 80%.		Weak base titration analysisTitration & pH Curves: Ionic Equilibrium for JEE/NEET | NITIN SIR | IIT KGP | NEXUACADEMY🟡 Similar(b), (c) 0.25 M salt; 100 mL HCl
Concept Two data points fix the base: a buffer point at 1/5 neutralisation gives Kb, and the salt-hydrolysis pH at equivalence gives the salt concentration, hence moles, mass and purity. Test each option against these.
Key relation pOH = pKb + log([BH+]/[B]); at eq. pt. [H+] = √((Kw/Kb)C) Buffer then salt hydrolysis, the two regimes of a weak-base titration.
Solution
1. At 1/5 neutralised, [BH+]/[B] = (1/5)/(4/5) = 1/4; pH 9 ⇒ pOH 5.
2. 5 = pKb + log(1/4) = pKb − 0.60 ⇒ pKb = 5.6, Kb = 10−5.6 = 2.5×10−6. This is >10−6, so (a) is false.
3. At equivalence (salt BH+), pH 4.5 ⇒ [H+]2 = 10−9 = (Kw/Kb)C = (10−14/2.5×10−6)C.
4. 10−9 = 4×10−9·C ⇒ C = 0.25 M salt. (b) correct.
5. C = 0.5V/(0.1+V) = 0.25 ⇒ 0.5V = 0.025+0.25V ⇒ V = 0.1 L = 100 mL. (c) correct.
6. moles base = 0.5×0.1 = 0.05; mass = 0.05×45 = 2.25 g; purity = 2.25/2.5 = 90% ≠ 80%, so (d) is false.
 1/5 neut: pOH 5 = pKb - 0.6 -> pKb 5.6
   Kb = 2.5e-6  (> 1e-6, so a wrong)
 eq pt: 1e-9 = (1e-14/2.5e-6)*C -> C = 0.25 M
 0.5V/(0.1+V)=0.25 -> V = 0.1 L = 100 mL
 -----------------------------------------
 mass = 0.05*45 = 2.25 g -> 90% (d wrong)
⚡ JEE Trick At 1/5 neutralised the ratio is 1:4, not 1:5 — use fraction/(1−fraction). The purity falls out exactly at 90%, so the printed 80% in (d) is the deliberate distractor.
Answer: (b) and (c).
3H2CO3 ionizes as follows.
H2CO3 ⇄ H+ + HCO3−; K1 = 4.0×10−6
HCO3− ⇄ H+ + CO32−; K2 = 5.0×10−11
Which of the following information is correctly related to 0.5 M − Na2CO3 solution?
(a) The degree of hydrolysis of Na2CO3 is 0.02.
(b) The pH of solution is 2.0.
(c) The pOH of solution is 2.0.
(d) [H2CO3] = 2.5×10−9 M		Carbonate hydrolysisIonic Equilibrium | Salt Hydrolysis | Degree Of Hydrolysis | pH | CBSE/HSC/NEET/MH-CET/JEE🟡 Similar(a), (c), (d) h=0.02; pOH=2; [H₂CO₃]=2.5×10⁻⁹
Concept Na2CO4 salt of a weak acid: CO32− hydrolyses (basic), governed by Kb1 = Kw/K2. A tiny second hydrolysis gives the H2CO3 trace through Kb2 = Kw/K1. Test each claim.
Key relation Kb1 = Kw/K2; h = √(Kb1/C); [OH−] = Ch K2 controls the first step (CO32−→HCO3−), K1 the second.
Solution
1. Kb1 = Kw/K2 = 10−14/5.0×10−11 = 2.0×10−4.
2. h = √(Kb1/C) = √(2.0×10−4/0.5) = √(4.0×10−4) = 0.02. (a) correct; h≪1 so the approximation is valid.
3. [OH−] = Ch = 0.5×0.02 = 10−2 M ⇒ pOH = 2.0. (c) correct.
4. pH = 14 − 2 = 12.0, a basic solution, so (b) pH = 2.0 is false.
5. Second step: Kb2 = Kw/K1 = 10−14/4.0×10−6 = 2.5×10−9.
6. [H2CO3] = Kb2[HCO3−]/[OH−] ≈ Kb2 = 2.5×10−9 M, since [HCO3−]≈[OH−]. (d) correct.
 Kb1 = 1e-14/5e-11 = 2e-4
 h = sqrt(2e-4/0.5) = sqrt(4e-4) = 0.02   (a)
 [OH-] = 0.5*0.02 = 1e-2 -> pOH 2 (c), pH 12 (b X)
 Kb2 = 1e-14/4e-6 = 2.5e-9
 -----------------------------------------
 [H2CO3] ~ Kb2 = 2.5e-9 M                 (d)
⚡ JEE Trick For a diprotic-acid salt, [H2CO3] in a pure carbonate solution equals Kb2 (= Kw/K1) directly, because the first hydrolysis makes [HCO3−] ≈ [OH−]. Option (b) baits a sign slip on pH vs pOH.
Answer: (a), (c) and (d).
4 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (iii): A buffer solution contains 0.8 M-NH4OH and 0.2 M NH4Cl. The value of Ka fo NH4+ = 5.0×10−10 (log 2 = 0.3, log 3 = 0.48).
(i) The pH of the solution is
(a) 9.9   (b) 8.7   (c) 9.6   (d) 4.1		Ammonia buffer pHIonic Equilibrium #11 | pH of Buffer solution,Henderson Equation | For Class 11th, NEET, IIT & JEE🟡 Similar(a) 9.9
Concept This is a basic buffer of the weak base NH4OH and its salt NH4Cl, so its pH is set by the Henderson equation, not by a simple ionisation. The twist is that the data gives Ka of NH4+, the conjugate acid, so you must first convert to Kb of the base through Kw before plugging in the ratio.
Key relation pOH = pKb + log([salt]/[base]); pKb = 14 − pKa Ka(NH4+)·Kb(NH3) = Kw = 10−14 at 25°C.
Solution
1. Convert: Kb = Kw/Ka = 10−14/5.0×10−10 = 2.0×10−5.
2. pKb = −log(2.0×10−5) = 5 − log 2 = 5 − 0.30 = 4.70.
3. Identify the pair: [base] = 0.8 M (NH4OH), [salt] = 0.2 M (NH4Cl). Both share the same volume, so the ratio uses molarities directly.
4. pOH = pKb + log([salt]/[base]) = 4.70 + log(0.2/0.8) = 4.70 + log(1/4).
5. log(1/4) = −log 4 = −2 log 2 = −0.60, so pOH = 4.70 − 0.60 = 4.10.
6. pH = 14 − pOH = 14 − 4.10 = 9.90. Basic, as expected for an ammonia buffer rich in base.
 Kb = Kw/Ka = 1e-14 / 5e-10 = 2e-5
 pKb = 5 - 0.30 = 4.70
 pOH = 4.70 + log(0.2/0.8)
     = 4.70 - 0.60 = 4.10
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = 14 - 4.10 = 9.90
⚡ JEE Trick Equivalently pH = pKa + log([base]/[salt]) = 9.30 + log 4 = 9.90 in one line, where pKa = 10 − log 5 = 9.30. Since base (0.8) outweighs salt (0.2), the pH must sit ABOVE pKa 9.30 — so distractor (b) 8.7 (which is below) is impossible by inspection.
Answer: (a) 9.9.
4 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (iii): A buffer solution contains 0.8 M-NH4OH and 0.2 M NH4Cl. The value of Ka fo NH4+ = 5.0×10−10 (log 2 = 0.3, log 3 = 0.48).
(ii) The pH of the resulting solution on adding 0.15 mole HCl in 500 ml of the buffer solution is
(a) 9.3   (b) 0.48   (c) 0.52   (d) 9.9		Buffer pH after adding HClIonic Equilibrium Lecture 12 | Buffer Solutions & pH Numericals | TNM Sir | Kota Classes🟡 Similar(a) 9.3
Concept Strong acid poured into a basic buffer is not simply "adding H+": the HCl is consumed by the base, converting NH4OH into NH4+ mole-for-mole. So work in MOLES over the 500 mL, update both pools after the neutralisation, and only then apply Henderson. The buffer still holds as long as some free base survives.
Key relation NH4OH + HCl → NH4Cl + H2O; pH = pKa + log([NH3]/[NH4+]) pKa(NH4+) = −log(5×10−10) = 10 − 0.70 = 9.30.
Solution
1. Moles in 500 mL: NH4OH = 0.8×0.5 = 0.4 mol; NH4Cl = 0.2×0.5 = 0.1 mol.
2. Add 0.15 mol HCl; since 0.15 < 0.4, all the acid is eaten by the base.
3. Base left: NH4OH = 0.4 − 0.15 = 0.25 mol.
4. Salt formed: NH4Cl = 0.1 + 0.15 = 0.25 mol. Every mole of HCl moves one mole of base into the salt pool.
5. Ratio [base]/[salt] = 0.25/0.25 = 1 (the common volume cancels, so moles suffice).
6. pH = pKa + log(1) = 9.30 + 0 = 9.30. log of 1 is zero, so pH equals pKa exactly.
 moles: base 0.4 , salt 0.1 ; add HCl 0.15
 base = 0.4 - 0.15 = 0.25
 salt = 0.1 + 0.15 = 0.25   -> ratio = 1
 pKa = 10 - log5 = 9.30
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = 9.30 + log(1) = 9.30
⚡ JEE Trick When added acid drives base = salt you have hit the half-equivalence point, where pH = pKa with no log term at all. The low-pH distractors (b) 0.48 and (c) 0.52 are the trap for wrongly assuming the buffer is destroyed; here 0.25 mol of base remains, so the solution is still firmly basic.
Answer: (a) 9.3.
4 (iii)Passage Q. (i) to (iii): A buffer solution contains 0.8 M-NH4OH and 0.2 M NH4Cl. The value of Ka fo NH4+ = 5.0×10−10 (log 2 = 0.3, log 3 = 0.48).
(iii) The pH of the resulting solution on adding 0.5 mole HCl in 500 ml of the buffer solution is
(a) 9.3   (b) 0.30   (c) 0.70   (d) 9.9		Buffer destroyed by excess HClBuffer Action | CHEMISTRY | JEE | Concept of the Day | SM Sir🟡 Similar(c) 0.70
Concept Here the HCl added (0.5 mol) exceeds all the base, so the buffer is wiped out. Once NH4OH is fully consumed, the leftover strong acid alone fixes the pH. The spectator NH4+ is an extremely weak acid (Ka = 5×10−10) and is completely swamped, so the excess HCl is the only H+ source worth counting.
Key relation excess H+ = (nHCl − nbase)/Vtotal; pH = −log[H+] With no free base left, Henderson no longer applies — this is now just a dilute strong-acid problem.
Solution
1. Moles in 500 mL: NH4OH = 0.4 mol, NH4Cl = 0.1 mol. Acid added = 0.5 mol HCl.
2. HCl first neutralises all 0.4 mol NH4OH, so base → 0 and NH4Cl rises to 0.1 + 0.4 = 0.5 mol.
3. HCl spent on the base = 0.4 mol; excess HCl = 0.5 − 0.4 = 0.1 mol. This strong acid is left over once the base is gone.
4. Total volume = 500 mL = 0.5 L (no new volume stated for the acid), so [H+] = 0.1/0.5 = 0.2 M.
5. pH = −log(0.2) = −log(2×10−1) = 1 − log 2 = 1 − 0.30 = 0.70.
6. Check: NH4+ contributes <10−5 M H+, negligible beside 0.2 M strong acid, so pH = 0.70 stands.
 moles: base 0.4 , salt 0.1 ; add HCl 0.5
 0.4 HCl neutralises base -> base = 0
 excess HCl = 0.5 - 0.4 = 0.1 mol
 [H+] = 0.1 / 0.5 L = 0.2 M
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = -log(0.2) = 1 - 0.30 = 0.70
⚡ JEE Trick The instant nHCl > nbase the buffer is destroyed — drop Henderson and just divide the excess acid by the TOTAL volume. The ∼9 distractors (a) and (d) are the trap for not noticing the base is entirely used up; a strong-acid excess forces the pH below 1.
Answer: (c) 0.70.
5 (i)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows.
(a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT.
(d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.
(i) Statement I: An aqueous solution having pH 6.8 must be acidic. Statement II: An aqueous solution having [H+] > √Kw must be acidic.		Assertion-Reason: acidity criterionIonic Equilibrium 03 || PH Of Solutions | How to find PH | How to calculate PH of any Solution🟡 Similar(d) I wrong, II right
Concept Acidity is defined by [H+] > [OH−], i.e. [H+] > √Kw — not by a fixed pH number. The pH that marks neutrality shifts with temperature because Kw changes, so a pH reading alone cannot label a solution.
Key relation neutral ⇔ [H+] = √Kw; acidic ⇔ [H+] > √Kw Only at 25°C does √Kw = 10−7 (neutral pH 7).
Solution
1. Statement I claims any solution of pH 6.8 is acidic.
2. That is true only if neutral pH = 7, which holds only at 25°C.
3. The statement fixes no temperature; at higher T, Kw rises and neutral pH falls (e.g. ∼6.8 near 50°C). Then pH 6.8 could be neutral, not acidic.
4. So I is not necessarily true — Statement I is INCORRECT.
5. Statement II says [H+] > √Kw must be acidic. This is the temperature-independent definition of acidity. [H+] > √Kw forces [H+] > [OH−].
6. Statement II is CORRECT. With I wrong and II right, the code is (d).
 acidic test  : [H+] > sqrt(Kw) = [OH-] boundary
 pH 6.8 acidic ONLY if neutral pH = 7 (25C)
 higher T -> Kw up -> neutral pH < 7
 -----------------------------------------
 I incorrect , II correct  -> (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Never equate pH 7 with neutral unless 25°C is stated; the universal neutral marker is [H+] = √Kw. Statement II is exactly that rule, which is why it stands while the numerical Statement I falls.
Answer: (d) Statement I incorrect, Statement II correct.
5 (ii)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows.
(a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.

(d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.
(ii) Statement I: The pH of 10−7 M-NaOH solution is in between 7.0 to 7.3 at 25°C. Statement II: Due to common ion effect, ionization of water is reduced.		Assertion-Reason: dilute NaOH pHCalculate pH of 10^(-7)M of NaOH solution at 25C (take log0.618=0.21)🟢 Exact(a) both right, II explains I
Concept At 10−7 M NaOH the added OH− is comparable to water's own, so water autoionisation cannot be ignored. The extra OH− suppresses water ionisation (common-ion effect), which is precisely why the pH barely creeps above 7.
Key relation [Na+]+[H+] = [OH−], [H+][OH−] = Kw Charge balance plus Kw gives a quadratic in [H+].
Solution
1. Let [H+] = x; [OH−] = 10−14/x; [Na+] = 10−7.
2. Charge balance: 10−7 + x = 10−14/x ⇒ x2 + 10−7x − 10−14 = 0.
3. x = [−10−7 + √(10−14 + 4×10−14)]/2 = [−1 + √5]×10−7/2.
4. = (1.236/2)×10−7 = 6.18×10−8 M. √5 ≈ 2.236.
5. pH = −log(6.18×10−8) = 8 − 0.79 = 7.21, which lies in 7.0–7.3. Statement I CORRECT.
6. Statement II (common-ion effect cuts water ionisation) is CORRECT and is exactly why pH stops near 7.2 instead of rising further — so II explains I: code (a).
 charge bal: 1e-7 + x = 1e-14/x
 x^2 + 1e-7 x - 1e-14 = 0
 x = (-1 + sqrt5)*1e-7/2 = 6.18e-8
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = -log(6.18e-8) = 7.21  (in 7.0-7.3)
 common-ion lowers water ionisation -> (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Below ∼10−6 M, never read pH straight off the base concentration; solve the Kw quadratic. A base can only push pH toward 7 from above, never below, which is the common-ion suppression Statement II names.
Answer: (a) Both correct, II is the correct explanation of I.
5 (iii)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows.
(a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT.
(d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.
(iii) Statement I: 10−4 M-HCl solution is more acidic compared to 1 M-HCOOH solution (Ka = 10−3 M). Statement II: HCl is stronger acid as compared to HCOOH.		Assertion-Reason: acid strength vs concIonic Equilibrium Lecture 4 | Ka, Kb & pH Numericals | TNM Sir | Kota Classes🟡 Similar(d) I wrong, II right
Concept Acid strength (intrinsic, set by Ka) is not the same as a solution's acidity (set by actual [H+], which also depends on concentration). A concentrated weak acid can out-acidify a very dilute strong acid.
Key relation strong acid: [H+] = C; weak acid: [H+] = √(KaC) Compare the real [H+] each solution delivers.
Solution
1. 10−4 M HCl is fully ionised: [H+] = 10−4 M, pH = 4.
2. 1 M HCOOH (Ka = 10−3): [H+] = √(KaC) = √(10−3×1) = √(10−3).
3. = 10−1.5 = 3.16×10−2 M, pH = 1.5. α = [H+]/C ≈ 0.03, small enough for the formula.
4. Compare: [H+]HCOOH = 3.16×10−2 ≫ [H+]HCl = 10−4.
5. So the HCOOH solution is far MORE acidic; Statement I (HCl more acidic) is INCORRECT.
6. Statement II (HCl is the stronger acid than HCOOH) is CORRECT — but strength does not override concentration. I wrong, II right ⇒ (d).
 1e-4 M HCl  : [H+] = 1e-4  -> pH 4
 1 M HCOOH   : [H+] = sqrt(1e-3) = 3.2e-2
             -> pH 1.5
 -----------------------------------------
 HCOOH soln more acidic : I wrong, II right -> (d)
⚡ JEE Trick "Stronger acid" (bigger Ka) and "more acidic solution" (bigger [H+]) are different questions; a dilute strong acid loses to a concentrated weak one. That gap is exactly why Statement II is true yet Statement I is false.
Answer: (d) Statement I incorrect, Statement II correct.
5 (iv)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows.
(a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT.
(d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.
(iv) Statement I: When an aqueous solution of weak base like BOH is diluted, [OH−] increases. Statement II: On dilution, the degree of dissociation of weak electrolytes increases.		Assertion-Reason: dilution of weak baseOstwald Dilution law for Weak electrolytes- IIT JEE & NEET | Vineet Khatri | ATP STAR🟡 Similar(d) d
Concept On diluting a weak electrolyte the degree of dissociation α does rise (Ostwald’s law), but that does not mean the ion concentration rises. The number of ions is spread through more water, so [OH−] actually falls. Statement II states a true law; Statement I draws a false conclusion from it.
Key relation [OH−] = Cα = √(KbC) The lone factor C outside the root makes [OH−] shrink as C shrinks.
Solution
1. For BOH ⇌ B+ + OH−, the concentration is [OH−] = Cα.
2. Ostwald’s law: α = √(Kb/C), so as C decreases on dilution α increases. Statement II is CORRECT.
3. But substitute back: [OH−] = C·√(Kb/C) = √(KbC). The product KbC falls as C falls.
4. So [OH−] decreases toward 10−7 M on dilution — it does not increase. Statement I is INCORRECT.
5. Pattern: I false, II true → option (d).
6. Take Kb = 10−5 as a check: see the numbers below.
 [OH-] = sqrt(Kb * C),  Kb = 1e-5
 ---------------------------------------
 C = 0.1    M : [OH-] = 1.0e-3   alpha = 1.0%
 C = 0.001  M : [OH-] = 1.0e-4   alpha = 10%
 ---------------------------------------
 alpha rises, [OH-] falls   ->  (d)
⚡ JEE Trick The trap is “α up, so [OH−] up.” True that α rises, but C drops faster, and the product √(KbC) governs the actual concentration — it always falls on dilution.
Answer: (d) — Statement I incorrect, Statement II correct.
5 (v)Assertion and Reason : Q.(i) to (v). The following questions consist of two statements. Mark the answer as follows.
(a) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(b) If both statements are CORRECT, and Statement II is NOT the CORRECT explanation of Statement I.
(c) If Statement I is CORRECT, but Statement II is INCORRECT.
(d) If Statement I is INCORRECT, but Statement II is CORRECT.
(v) Statement I: The pH of acidic buffer solution always increases on dilution. Statement II: The pH of any aqueous solution of acid always increases on dilution.		Assertion-Reason: buffer dilutionEquilibrium | lonic Equilibrium 05 | Buffer Solutions JEE MAINS/NEET/JEE ADVANCE -Part 1🟡 Similar(d) d
Concept A buffer resists pH change — that is its whole purpose — so saying its pH “always increases” on dilution is false. A plain acid, however, does get its [H+] lowered by dilution, so its pH does rise toward 7. So Statement I is wrong and Statement II is right.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([A−]/[HA]) Dilution divides both [A−] and [HA] by the same factor, so the ratio and pH stay put.
Solution
1. Acidic buffer: pH = pKa + log([A−]/[HA]). Diluting n-fold → both concentrations become 1/n of before.
2. The ratio [A−]/[HA] is unchanged, so pH is essentially constant, not “always increasing.” Statement I is INCORRECT.
3. (Only tiny activity shifts occur, and even those are not a guaranteed increase.)
4. Pure acid: dilution lowers [H+] → pH rises toward 7 (from below). Statement II is CORRECT.
5. Strong acid [H+] = C falls; weak acid [H+] = √(KaC) falls — either way pH climbs.
6. So I false, II true → option (d).
 buffer, dilute n-fold:
   pH = pKa + log((A-/n)/(HA/n))
      = pKa + log(A-/HA)   -> unchanged
 plain acid:
   [H+] down  ->  pH up toward 7   (always)
 =====================================
 I wrong, II right     ->  (d)
⚡ JEE Trick “Buffer” = pH anchored near pKa; the dilution ratio cancels, so the word “always increases” is the giveaway that Statement I is false.
Answer: (d) — Statement I incorrect, Statement II correct.
6Match the columns.
Column-I (pH value): (A) 5.0   (B) 9.0   (C) 12.5   (D) 6.0
Column-II (Solution): (P) pH at which Mg(OH)2 just start precipitating from 2.0×10−3 M Mg2+ solution (Ksp of Mg(OH)2 = 2×10−6 M3)   (Q) Maximum pH at which 0.1 mole of Al(OH)3 dissolves completely as Al3+ in 1 L solution (Ksp of Al(OH)3 = 1×10−28 M4)   (R) pH at which 0.1 M CH3COOH dissociates (Ka of CH3COOH = 1.0×10−5 M)   (S) pH of 0.001 M HA (weak acid) solution (pKa of HA = pKb of A−)   (T) pH of 3×10−5 M CaA2 solution (CaA2 dissociates completely in water; Ka of HA = 5×10−9)
(a) A→Q,S; B→T; C→P; D→R   (b) A→Q,S; B→P; C→T; D→Q,S   (c) A→P; B→Q; C→R; D→S   (d) A→T; B→R,S; C→Q; D→P		Match pH with solubility/acid-baseCalculate pH at which Mg(OH)_(2) begins to precipitate from a solution containing 0.10M Mg^(2+) ...🟡 Similar(a) A→Q,S; B→T; C→P; D→R
Concept Compute the pH each description produces and match to Column-I. Three tools cover all five: solubility onset [OH−] = (Ksp/[M])1/n, weak-acid [H+] = √(KaC), and salt hydrolysis [OH−] = √(KbC) with Kb = Kw/Ka.
Key relation [OH−] = (Ksp/[Mn+])1/n; [H+] = √(KaC) pKa = pKb forces pKa = 7 for the HA in (S).
Solution
1. P: [OH−]2 = 2×10−6/2×10−3 = 10−3 → [OH−] = 10−1.5, pOH 1.5, pH 12.5 = C.
2. Q: [Al3+] = 0.1, [OH−]3 = 10−28/0.1 = 10−27 → [OH−] = 10−9, pOH 9, pH 5.0 = A.
3. S: pKa = pKb → pKa 7, Ka = 10−7; [H+] = √(10−7×10−3) = 10−5, pH 5.0 = A. So A→Q,S.
4. T: [A−] = 2×3×10−5 = 6×10−5; Kb = 10−14/5×10−9 = 2×10−6; [OH−] = √(1.2×10−10) ≈ 1.1×10−5, pH ≈ 9.0 = B.
5. R: 0.1 M CH3COOH gives [H+] = √(10−5×0.1) = 10−3, i.e. pH 3 as printed. The intended slot is D = 6.0, so R is a book misprint; D→R is still the designed pairing and (a) is the only consistent option.
6. Matches: A→Q,S; B→T; C→P; D→R → (a).
 P -> pH 12.5 (C)     Q -> pH 5.0 (A)
 S -> pH 5.0  (A)     T -> pH 9.0 (B)
 R -> pH 3 (printed; slot D=6.0 misprint)
 ---------------------------------------
 A:Q,S  B:T  C:P  D:R    ->  (a)
⚡ JEE Trick A pairs with two entries (Q and S), both landing at pH 5.0 — that double hit is unique to option (a); the other options break on P (12.5) at once.
Answer: (a) A→Q,S; B→T; C→P; D→R.
7For a tribasic acid, H3A, Ka1 = 10−4, Ka2 = 10−8 and Ka3 = 10−12. Match the pH (Column II) of the resulting solution (Column I) at 25°C.
Column-I: (A) Equimolar mixture of H3A and NaH2A   (B) Equimolar mixture of NaH2A and Na2HA   (C) Equimolar mixture of Na2HA and Na3A   (D) Equimolar mixture of H3A and NaOH   (E) Equimolar mixture of NaH2A and NaOH
Column-II: (P) 12.0   (Q) 8.0   (R) 4.0   (S) 6.0   (T) 10.0
(a) A→P; B→Q; C→R; D→S   (b) A→R; B→Q; C→P; D→S; E→T   (c) A→S,T; B→R; C→P; D→Q   (d) A→Q; B→R,P; C→T; D→S		Match pH of polyprotic acid mixtures16 Ionic Equilibrium | Ph of Salt Containing Amphiprotic Anion | JEE Main Adv | Chemistry Class 11🟡 Similar(b) A→R; B→Q; C→P; D→S; E→T
Concept An equimolar conjugate pair is a buffer at pH = pKa of that step (ratio 1, log 0). Mixing an acid with 1 equivalent of strong base makes the next amphiprotic salt, whose pH is the average of the two flanking pKa values. pKa1 = 4, pKa2 = 8, pKa3 = 12.
Key relation pair: pH = pKa,i; amphiprotic HX: pH = (pKa,i + pKa,i+1)/2 The amphiprotic result is concentration-independent.
Solution
1. A: H3A + NaH2A = H3A/H2A− pair → pH = pKa1 = 4.0 = R.
2. B: NaH2A + Na2HA = H2A−/HA2− pair → pH = pKa2 = 8.0 = Q.
3. C: Na2HA + Na3A = HA2−/A3− pair → pH = pKa3 = 12.0 = P.
4. D: H3A + 1 NaOH → NaH2A (amphiprotic H2A−); pH = (4+8)/2 = 6.0 = S.
5. E: NaH2A + 1 NaOH → Na2HA (amphiprotic HA2−); pH = (8+12)/2 = 10.0 = T.
6. A→R; B→Q; C→P; D→S; E→T → (b).
 pKa1=4   pKa2=8   pKa3=12
 ---------------------------------------
 A  H3A/H2A-   pKa1          4.0  R
 B  H2A-/HA2-  pKa2          8.0  Q
 C  HA2-/A3-   pKa3         12.0  P
 D  -> NaH2A   (4+8)/2       6.0  S
 E  -> Na2HA   (8+12)/2     10.0  T
⚡ JEE Trick Pair = pKa; acid + 1 base = next amphiprotic salt = mean of the two bracketing pKa — so D and E don’t need any concentration, only the pKa ladder.
Answer: (b) A→R; B→Q; C→P; D→S; E→T.
8Boric acid B(OH)3 is used as a mild antiseptic. What is the pH of a 0.0025 M aqueous solution of boric acid? The hydrogen ion arises principally from the following reaction.
B(OH)3(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ B(OH)4−(aq) + H+(aq); K = 4.0×10−10		pH of boric acid solutionBoric acid B(OH)_3 is used as a mild antiseptic. What is the pH of a 0.0025 M aqueous solution of...🟢 Exact6
Concept Boric acid is a very weak monoprotic (Lewis) acid; the H+ comes from the single given equilibrium with K = 4.0×10−10. Because α is tiny and the resulting [H+] stays well above 10−7, the simple weak-acid formula applies with no water correction.
Key relation [H+] = √(K·C) Valid when α ≪ 1 and [H+] ≫ 10−7 M.
Solution
1. Equilibrium: B(OH)3 + H2O ⇌ B(OH)4− + H+, K = 4.0×10−10.
2. C = 0.0025 M = 2.5×10−3 M. Assume α ≪ 1 so [B(OH)3] ≈ C.
3. [H+] = √(K·C) = √(4.0×10−10 × 2.5×10−3) = √(1.0×10−12) = 1.0×10−6 M.
4. pH = −log(1.0×10−6) = 6.0.
5. Check α = √(K/C) = √(4.0×10−10/2.5×10−3) = √(1.6×10−7) = 4×10−4 ≪ 1. Approximation sound.
6. [H+] = 10−6 ≫ 10−7, so water’s own H+ is negligible. pH = 6.0 stands.
 K = 4.0e-10,  C = 2.5e-3
 ---------------------------------------
 [H+] = sqrt(4.0e-10 * 2.5e-3)
      = sqrt(1.0e-12) = 1.0e-6 M
 pH   = 6.0
 alpha = sqrt(K/C) = 4e-4  (ok)
⚡ JEE Trick K×C = 10−12 is a perfect square — spot it and [H+] = 10−6 drops out with no log work. Unlike the dilute-SO2 case, here [H+] stays ≫ 10−7, so no water correction is needed.
Answer: pH = 6.0.
9The average concentration of SO2 in the atmosphere over a city on a certain day is 10 ppm, when the average temperature is 298 K. Given that the solubility of SO2 in water at 298 K is 1.28 gm litre−1 and the pKa of H2SO3 is 2.0, estimate the pH of rain on that day.pH of acid rain (SO2)The average concentration of `SO_(2)` in the atmosphere over a city on a cetrain day is 10 ppm🟢 Exact6.6
Concept 10 ppm SO2 fixes a partial pressure of 10−5 atm, so the dissolved amount is only that fraction of the 1-atm solubility. The acid is so dilute that, although Ka ≫ C (first proton essentially 100% ionised), water’s own H+ must be added in.
Key relation [H+]2 − C[H+] − Kw = 0 Ka ≫ C means HSO3− ≈ C; charge balance then adds water.
Solution
1. 10 ppm → p(SO2) = 10−5 atm; dissolved mass = 1.28 × 10−5 g/L.
2. M(SO2) = 64, so C = [H2SO3] = 1.28×10−5/64 = 2.0×10−7 M.
3. pKa = 2.0 → Ka = 10−2 ≫ C, so H2SO3 → HSO3− is essentially complete; [HSO3−] ≈ C.
4. Charge balance: [H+] = [HSO3−] + [OH−] = C + Kw/[H+]. Water matters since C is near 10−7.
5. Solve x2 − (2×10−7)x − 10−14 = 0: x = [2×10−7 + √(8×10−14)]/2 = (2 + 2.83)×10−7/2 = 2.41×10−7 M.
6. pH = −log(2.41×10−7) = 7 − 0.38 = 6.62 ≈ 6.6.
 C = 1.28e-5 g/L / 64 = 2.0e-7 M  (=HSO3-)
 x^2 - C x - Kw = 0,  Kw = 1e-14
 ---------------------------------------
 x = (2e-7 + sqrt(8e-14)) / 2
   = (2 + 2.83)e-7 / 2 = 2.41e-7 M
 pH = 6.6
⚡ JEE Trick The √(KaC) shortcut gives pH 4.3 and is wrong here: it assumes α ≪ 1, but Ka ≫ C means full ionisation. Always compare Ka with C first, then add water when C is near 10−7.
Answer: pH ≈ 6.6.
10An artificial fruit beverage contains 30.0 g of tartaric acid (H2C4H4O6) and 18.8 g of its salt, potassium hydrogen tartrate per litre. What is the pH of the beverage? For tartaric acid, Ka1 = 5.0×10−4, Ka2 = 4×10−9 (log 2 = 0.3).pH of tartaric acid bufferEquilibrium | lonic Equilibrium 05 | Buffer Solutions JEE MAINS/NEET/JEE ADVANCE -Part 1🟡 Similar3
Concept Tartaric acid (H2A) plus its hydrogen salt KHA gives a first-stage buffer of the H2A / HA− pair. Use Henderson–Hasselbalch with pKa1; the second ionisation (Ka2) is far too weak to matter here.
Key relation pH = pKa1 + log([HA−]/[H2A]) The salt is potassium hydrogen tartrate = HA−, the conjugate base of the first proton.
Solution
1. M(tartaric, H2C4H4O6) = 150; moles = 30.0/150 = 0.20 M (acid H2A).
2. M(KHC4H4O6) = 150 − 1 + 39 = 188; moles = 18.8/188 = 0.10 M (salt HA−).
3. pKa1 = −log(5.0×10−4) = 4 − log 5 = 4 − 0.70 = 3.30.
4. pH = 3.30 + log(0.10/0.20) = 3.30 + log(0.5) = 3.30 − log 2 = 3.30 − 0.30.
5. pH = 3.00.
6. Ka2 = 4×10−9 is ~105 times weaker, so the second stage adds nothing at pH 3. Single-buffer treatment is exact enough.
 acid  H2A  = 30.0/150 = 0.20 M
 salt  HA-  = 18.8/188 = 0.10 M
 pKa1 = 4 - log5 = 3.30
 ---------------------------------------
 pH = 3.30 + log(0.10/0.20)
    = 3.30 - 0.30 = 3.0
⚡ JEE Trick Match the pKa to the pair: the salt here is the hydrogen tartrate HA−, so the buffer is H2A/HA− and you use pKa1 — using pKa2 is the classic slip.
Answer: pH = 3.0.

Ionic Equilibrium · Level 10
