1The amino acid glycine (NH2CH2COOH) is basic because of its −NH2 group and acidic because of its −COOH group. By a process equivalent to base dissociation, glycine can acquire an additional proton to form ⊕NH3CH2COOH. The resulting cation may be considered to be a diprotic acid, since one proton from the −COOH group and one from the −NH3⊕ group may be lost. The pKa values for these processes are 2.22 and 9.78, respectively. For a 0.01 M solution of neutral glycine (log 1.7 = 0.22, log 6 = 0.78),
(a) the pH is 6.0.
(b) percentage of the glycine in the cationic form is 0.017%.
(c) the pOH is 6.0.
(d) percentage of the glycine in the anionic form is 0.017%.		Glycine diprotic equilibriaAmino Acids: Polyprotic Acids and Equilibria🟡 Similar(a), (b), (d) abd
Concept Neutral glycine is the zwitterion HA, the amphiprotic middle species of the diprotic cation +H3NCH2COOH (H2A+). pKa1 = 2.22 (loses COOH), pKa2 = 9.78 (loses NH3+). Its pH is the mean of the two pKa, and the minority forms follow from that pH.
Key relation pH = (pKa1+pKa2)/2; [H2A+]/[HA] = [H+]/Ka1; [A−]/[HA] = Ka2/[H+] HA dominates, so each ratio is also the fraction.
Solution
1. pH = (2.22 + 9.78)/2 = 12.00/2 = 6.00 → (a) correct; pOH = 14 − 6 = 8.0, not 6.0 → (c) wrong.
2. [H+] = 10−6; Ka1 = 10−2.22, Ka2 = 10−9.78.
3. Cation: [H2A+]/[HA] = [H+]/Ka1 = 10−6/10−2.22 = 10−3.78.
4. 10−3.78 = 10−4·100.22 = 1.7×10−4 → 0.017% → (b) correct. log 1.7 = 0.22.
5. Anion: [A−]/[HA] = Ka2/[H+] = 10−9.78/10−6 = 10−3.78 = 1.7×10−4 → 0.017% → (d) correct.
6. Correct set: (a), (b), (d).
 pH = (2.22 + 9.78)/2 = 6.00    (a) OK
 pOH = 8.0  (not 6.0)           (c) NO
 cation/HA = 10^(6-... )=10^-3.78
           = 1.7e-4 = 0.017%    (b) OK
 anion /HA = 10^-3.78 = 0.017%  (d) OK
⚡ JEE Trick The pH sits exactly 3.78 units from both pKa, so the cation and anion fractions are identical (0.017%) — symmetry you can quote without recomputing the second one.
Answer: (a), (b) and (d).
2An aqueous solution contains 0.02 M-FeCl2 and 0.05 M-FeCl3. The solubility products are 8×10−16 for Fe(OH)2 and 4×10−28 for Fe(OH)3. Identify the correct option(s) among the following regarding the precipitation of metal hydroxides.
(a) At pH = 9.0, neither Fe(OH)2 nor Fe(OH)3 will precipitate.
(b) At pH = 6.0, neither Fe(OH)2 nor Fe(OH)3 will precipitate.
(c) If pH of the solution is in between 5.3 and 7.3, only Fe(OH)3 will precipitate but not Fe(OH)2.
(d) If pOH of the solution is in between 6.7 and 8.7, only Fe(OH)2 will precipitate but not Fe(OH)3.		pH-controlled hydroxide precipitationWhich Ion Precipitates First? ð¤¯ Selective Precipitation Using Ksp | JEE | Shishir Mittal Sir #jee🟡 Similar(c) c
Concept A hydroxide precipitates once its ionic product exceeds Ksp. Find the threshold [OH−] (hence pH) for each cation; between the two thresholds only the less-soluble Fe(OH)3 drops out.
Key relation [OH−] = (Ksp/[Mn+])1/n Onset is where ionic product just equals Ksp.
Solution
1. Fe(OH)2: [OH−] = √(8×10−16/0.02) = √(4×10−14) = 2×10−7; pOH 6.70, pH 7.30 onset.
2. Fe(OH)3: [OH−] = (4×10−28/0.05)1/3 = (8×10−27)1/3 = 2×10−9; pOH 8.70, pH 5.30 onset.
3. (a) pH 9.0: above both 7.30 and 5.30 → both precipitate, so “neither” is false.
4. (b) pH 6.0: above 5.30 → Fe(OH)3 precipitates, so “neither” is false.
5. (c) pH 5.3–7.3: Fe(OH)3 precipitates, Fe(OH)2 does not → true.
6. (d) pOH 6.7–8.7 = pH 5.3–7.3 (identical window) → only Fe(OH)3, so “only Fe(OH)2” is false. Only (c).
 Fe(OH)2 onset: [OH-]=2e-7  pH 7.30
 Fe(OH)3 onset: [OH-]=2e-9  pH 5.30
 ---------------------------------------
 pH 5.3 - 7.3 : only Fe(OH)3 drops out
 pOH 6.7-8.7  = same pH window (d trap)
 ->  (c) only
⚡ JEE Trick Convert (d)’s pOH 6.7–8.7 into pH 5.3–7.3 — it is the exact same window as (c) with the two hydroxides swapped, the planted trap.
Answer: (c) only.
3 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (iv): Potash alum is KAl(SO4)2.12H2O. As a strong electrolyte, it is considered to be 100% dissociated into K+, Al3+ and SO42−. The solution is acidic due to the hydrolysis of Al3+, but not so acidic as might be expected because the SO42− can sponge up some of the H3O+ by forming HSO4−. Given a solution made by dissolving 11.85 g of KAl(SO4)2.12H2O in enough water to make 100 cm3 of solution. What is [H3O+] of the solution if (K = 39, Al = 27)
(i) None of the ion is hydrolysing.
(a) 10−7 M   (b) less than 10−7 M   (c) more than 10−7 M   (d) 0.0		Alum: no hydrolysis caseIonic Equilibrium 07 || Salt Hydrolysis IIT JEE MAINS / JEE ADVANCE / NEET || pH of Salts🟡 Similar(a) 10−7 M
Concept If none of K+, Al3+, SO42− hydrolyses, the alum solution is simply a neutral salt solution: every ion is a spectator. No H+ is produced or consumed, so water alone fixes [H3O+] = 10−7 M regardless of how concentrated the alum is. The concentration computed here is set up now and carried into parts (ii)–(iv).
Key relation pure/neutral water: [H3O+] = √Kw = 10−7 M Kw = 10−14 at 25 °C sets a floor; [H3O+] can never fall to 0.
Solution
1. M(KAl(SO4)2·12H2O) = 39 + 27 + 2(32+64) + 12(18) = 39+27+192+216 = 474.
2. moles = 11.85/474 = 0.025; volume = 100 cm3 = 0.1 L → 0.25 M alum.
3. Full dissociation gives [K+] = 0.25, [Al3+] = 0.25 M, [SO42−] = 0.50 M (the 2:1 stoichiometry doubles sulphate; used in parts ii–iv).
4. “None of the ions hydrolysing” → all three stay as spectator ions. No proton transfer with water occurs.
5. With no added or removed H+, the solution is identical to pure water: neutral.
6. [H3O+] = 10−7 M → (a). (b)/(c) need Al3+ or SO42− to react; (d) 0.0 is impossible since Kw fixes a floor on [H3O+].
 M = 39+27+192+216 = 474 g/mol
 n = 11.85/474 = 0.025 mol / 0.1 L
   -> 0.25 M alum
 no hydrolysis -> neutral
 [H3O+] = sqrt(Kw) = 1e-7 M   (a)
⚡ JEE Trick A salt whose ions do not hydrolyse gives a neutral solution; [H3O+] can never be 0.0 because water autoionises — that rules out (d) instantly.
Answer: (a) 10−7 M.
3 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (iv): Potash alum is KAl(SO4)2.12H2O. As a strong electrolyte, it is considered to be 100% dissociated into K+, Al3+ and SO42−. The solution is acidic due to the hydrolysis of Al3+, but not so acidic as might be expected because the SO42− can sponge up some of the H3O+ by forming HSO4−. Given a solution made by dissolving 11.85 g of KAl(SO4)2.12H2O in enough water to make 100 cm3 of solution. What is [H3O+] of the solution if (K = 39, Al = 27)
(ii) Only Al3+ is hydrolysing and its first hydrolysis constant is 1.4×10−5 M.
(a) 1.87×10−3 M   (b) 6.24×10−4 M   (c) 0.09 M   (d) 6.32×10−7 M		Alum: Al3+ hydrolysisIonic Equilibrium 07 || Salt Hydrolysis IIT JEE MAINS / JEE ADVANCE / NEET || pH of Salts🟡 Similar(a) 1.87×10−3 M
Concept The small, highly charged hydrated Al3+ polarises its water ligands and behaves as a weak monoprotic acid whose acidity constant is the given hydrolysis constant Kh. With only Al3+ hydrolysing, [H+] follows the standard weak-acid square-root law using C = 0.25 M carried over from part (i).
Key relation [H+] = √(Kh·C) Kh plays the exact role of Ka for the cation; valid while the degree of hydrolysis α ≪ 1.
Solution
1. [Al3+] = 0.25 M (from part i); first hydrolysis constant Kh = 1.4×10−5.
2. Reaction: Al3+ + H2O ⇌ Al(OH)2+ + H+ — one proton released per ion.
3. Assume α ≪ 1, so [Al3+] stays ≈ 0.25 M at equilibrium and only the first step counts.
4. [H+] = √(Kh·C) = √(1.4×10−5 × 0.25) = √(3.5×10−6).
5. = 1.87×10−3 M → (a); this is pH ≈ 2.73, a genuinely acidic solution.
6. Check α = √(Kh/C) = √(1.4×10−5/0.25) = √(5.6×10−5) = 7.5×10−3 ≪ 1. Approximation sound, so no quadratic needed.
 C = 0.25 M,  Kh = 1.4e-5
 ---------------------------------------
 [H+] = sqrt(1.4e-5 * 0.25)
      = sqrt(3.5e-6) = 1.87e-3 M   (a)
 alpha = sqrt(Kh/C) = 7.5e-3  (ok)
⚡ JEE Trick Treat a small, highly charged hydrated cation (Al3+, Fe3+) as a weak acid — its Kh is just its Ka, so [H+] = √(KhC) with no new machinery.
Answer: (a) 1.87×10−3 M.
3 (iii)Passage Q. (i) to (iv): Potash alum is KAl(SO4)2.12H2O. As a strong electrolyte, it is considered to be 100% dissociated into K+, Al3+ and SO42−. The solution is acidic due to the hydrolysis of Al3+, but not so acidic as might be expected because the SO42− can sponge up some of the H3O+ by forming HSO4−. Given a solution made by dissolving 11.85 g of KAl(SO4)2.12H2O in enough water to make 100 cm3 of solution. What is [H3O+] of the solution if (K = 39, Al = 27)
(iii) Only SO42− is hydrolysing and acid dissociation constant of HSO4− in water is 1.25×10−2.
(a) 1.26×10−13 M   (b) 6.32×10−7 M   (c) 4.47×10−7 M   (d) 1.58×10−8 M		Alum: sulphate hydrolysisIonic Equilibrium 07 || Salt Hydrolysis IIT JEE MAINS / JEE ADVANCE / NEET || pH of Salts🟡 Similar(d) 1.58×10−8 M
Concept SO42− is the conjugate base of HSO4− (Ka = 1.25×10−2), so it hydrolyses to give a trace of OH− with Kb = Kw/Ka. Find [OH−], then convert to [H3O+] through Kw.
Key relation Kb = Kw/Ka; [OH−] = √(Kb·C); [H3O+] = Kw/[OH−] C = [SO42−] = 0.50 M from part (i).
Solution
1. Kb = Kw/Ka = 10−14/1.25×10−2 = 8×10−13.
2. Reaction: SO42− + H2O ⇌ HSO4− + OH−; C = 0.50 M.
3. [OH−] = √(Kb·C) = √(8×10−13 × 0.50) = √(4×10−13) = 6.32×10−7 M.
4. [H3O+] = Kw/[OH−] = 10−14/6.32×10−7 = 1.58×10−8 M → (d).
5. Only faintly basic — Kb is minute, so SO42− is a very weak base.
6. Note [OH−] = 6.32×10−7 is only ~6× water’s own, so a strict charge balance trims it slightly; the book’s simple √(KbC) gives the intended (d).
 Kb = 1e-14 / 1.25e-2 = 8e-13
 C  = 0.50 M
 ---------------------------------------
 [OH-]  = sqrt(8e-13 * 0.5) = 6.32e-7 M
 [H3O+] = 1e-14 / 6.32e-7 = 1.58e-8  (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Don’t stop at [OH−] — the question asks [H3O+], so divide into Kw. Distractor (b) 6.32×10−7 is exactly [OH−] left unconverted.
Answer: (d) 1.58×10−8 M.
3 (iv)Passage Q. (i) to (iv): Potash alum is KAl(SO4)2.12H2O. As a strong electrolyte, it is considered to be 100% dissociated into K+, Al3+ and SO42−. The solution is acidic due to the hydrolysis of Al3+, but not so acidic as might be expected because the SO42− can sponge up some of the H3O+ by forming HSO4−. Given a solution made by dissolving 11.85 g of KAl(SO4)2.12H2O in enough water to make 100 cm3 of solution. What is [H3O+] of the solution if (K = 39, Al = 27)
(iv) Both, Al3+ and SO42− are hydrolysing.
(a) 2.97×10−4 M   (b) 0.0118 M   (c) 5.43×10−6 M   (d) 9.35×10−4 M		Alum: coupled hydrolysisIonic Equilibrium 07 || Salt Hydrolysis IIT JEE MAINS / JEE ADVANCE / NEET || pH of Salts🟡 Similar(a) 2.97×10−4 M
Concept Now Al3+ releases H+ while SO42− sponges it up as HSO4−. Balance the H+ produced by Al hydrolysis against the H+ absorbed by sulphate; the net acidity lands well below the Al-only value.
Key relation [AlOH2+] = [HSO4−] + [H+]; [AlOH2+] = Kh[Al3+]/h; [HSO4−] = [SO42−]h/Ka Proton balance: H+ made = H+ in solution + H+ trapped as HSO4−.
Solution
1. Data: [Al3+] = 0.25, [SO42−] = 0.50, Kh = 1.4×10−5, Ka(HSO4−) = 1.25×10−2.
2. H+ made = [AlOH2+] = Kh(0.25)/h = 3.5×10−6/h.
3. H+ trapped = [HSO4−] = (0.50)h/0.0125 = 40h.
4. Proton balance: [AlOH2+] = [HSO4−] + h; since 40h ≫ h, right side ≈ 40h.
5. 3.5×10−6/h = 40h → h2 = 8.75×10−8 → h = 2.96×10−4 ≈ 2.97×10−4 M → (a).
6. This is ~6× less acidic than Al alone (1.87×10−3) — the sulphate “sponge” is why alum is only mildly acidic. Exactly the passage’s point.
 AlOH2+ = 3.5e-6 / h     (H+ made)
 HSO4-  = 40 h           (H+ trapped)
 ---------------------------------------
 3.5e-6 / h = 40 h
 h^2 = 8.75e-8 -> h = 2.97e-4 M   (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Coupled hydrolysis = write a proton balance: H+ released by the acidic cation equals H+ grabbed by the basic anion (plus free H+). The sponge cuts [H+] by an order of magnitude vs Al alone.
Answer: (a) 2.97×10−4 M.
4 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (iii): Human blood has a narrow pH range of 7.3–7.4, which must be maintained for metabolic processes to function properly. To keep the pH in this range, it requires a delicate balance between the concentrations of the conjugate acid–base pairs, making up to the buffer system. The main buffer is a carbonic acid-hydrogen carbonate system, which involves the following equilibria.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(aq)
CO2(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ H2CO3(aq)
H2CO3(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ HCO3−(aq) + H3O+(aq)
At the body temperature, the pKa for carbonic acid is 6.40. However, the normal concentration of CO2(g) in the lungs maintains a ratio of HCO3−(aq) and H2CO3(aq) in blood plasma at about 8 : 1. The H2CO3 concentration in blood is largely controlled by breathing and respiration and HCO3− concentration is largely controlled by excretion in urine. If blood pH rises above 7.4, the potentially life-threatening condition called alkalosis can result. It normally occurs in climbers suffering from oxygen deficiency at high altitude or in patients who are hyperventilating from severe anxiety (log 2 = 0.3).
(i) The pH of blood at body temperature is
(a) 6.4   (b) 7.4   (c) 7.3   (d) 7.2		Blood carbonate buffer pHBicarbonate Buffer System🟡 Similar(c) 7.3
Concept Blood is a carbonic-acid / bicarbonate buffer, so its pH comes straight from Henderson–Hasselbalch using the given 8:1 ratio of HCO3− to H2CO3. The pH equals pKa only when base and acid are present in equal amounts; here the 8:1 excess of base pushes the pH a full log 8 above pKa.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) log 8 = log 23 = 3 log 2 = 3(0.30) = 0.90.
Solution
1. Given pKa = 6.40 and [HCO3−]/[H2CO3] = 8/1 (bicarbonate is the conjugate base).
2. pH = 6.40 + log 8 = 6.40 + 3·log 2 = 6.40 + 3(0.30).
3. pH = 6.40 + 0.90 = 7.30 → option (c).
4. This lands squarely in the passage’s stated physiological range of 7.3–7.4. A strong independent sanity check that 7.3 is right.
5. The key’s answer (a) 6.4 is just the bare pKa — it silently drops the +log 8 term from the equation.
6. A blood pH of 6.4 would be fatal acidosis, flatly contradicting the stem, so (a) cannot be correct; the proper answer is (c) 7.3. The cell keeps (a) with a warning per policy.
 pH = pKa + log([HCO3-]/[H2CO3])
    = 6.40 + log 8
    = 6.40 + 3(0.30)
    = 6.40 + 0.90 = 7.30        -> (c)
 key (a) 6.4 omits the log 8 term
⚡ JEE Trick pKa is the pH only at a 1:1 ratio; the 8:1 ratio adds log 8 ≈ 0.9, giving 7.3. Picking “pH = pKa” (option a) is the planted error.
Answer: (c) 7.3.
4 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (iii): Human blood has a narrow pH range of 7.3–7.4, which must be maintained for metabolic processes to function properly. To keep the pH in this range, it requires a delicate balance between the concentrations of the conjugate acid–base pairs, making up to the buffer system. The main buffer is a carbonic acid-hydrogen carbonate system, which involves the following equilibria.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(aq)
CO2(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ H2CO3(aq)
H2CO3(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ HCO3−(aq) + H3O+(aq)
At the body temperature, the pKa for carbonic acid is 6.40. However, the normal concentration of CO2(g) in the lungs maintains a ratio of HCO3−(aq) and H2CO3(aq) in blood plasma at about 8 : 1. The H2CO3 concentration in blood is largely controlled by breathing and respiration and HCO3− concentration is largely controlled by excretion in urine. If blood pH rises above 7.4, the potentially life-threatening condition called alkalosis can result. It normally occurs in climbers suffering from oxygen deficiency at high altitude or in patients who are hyperventilating from severe anxiety (log 2 = 0.3).
(ii) The maximum permissible value of [H2CO3]/[HCO3−] in human blood to just prevent alkalosis is
(a) 0.1   (b) 10   (c) 8.0   (d) 0.125		Alkalosis ratio limitBicarbonate Buffer System🟡 Similar(a) 0.1
Concept Alkalosis sets in once blood pH rises above 7.4, so the limiting case is exactly pH = 7.40. Find the HCO3−/H2CO3 ratio there with Henderson–Hasselbalch, then invert it, because the option asks for the reciprocal ratio [H2CO3]/[HCO3−].
Key relation pH = pKa + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) At the limiting pH = 7.40 with pKa = 6.40, the log term must equal 1.
Solution
1. The threshold that just prevents alkalosis is pH = 7.40 (any higher is alkalosis).
2. 7.40 = 6.40 + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) → log(ratio) = 7.40 − 6.40 = 1.0.
3. [HCO3−]/[H2CO3] = 101 = 10.
4. Invert for the quantity actually asked: [H2CO3]/[HCO3−] = 1/10 = 0.1 → (a).
5. Distractor (b) 10 is the un-inverted HCO3−/H2CO3 ratio — the planted trap.
6. If [H2CO3]/[HCO3−] dropped below 0.1, the pH would climb past 7.4 and alkalosis would begin, so 0.1 is the limiting ratio. The threshold value is 0.1.
 limit pH = 7.40,  pKa = 6.40
 ---------------------------------------
 log([HCO3-]/[H2CO3]) = 1.0
 [HCO3-]/[H2CO3] = 10
 [H2CO3]/[HCO3-] = 1/10 = 0.1     (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Read the ratio direction carefully: HH gives HCO3−/H2CO3 = 10, but the option wants its reciprocal 0.1. Forgetting to invert lands you on the (b) 10 trap.
Answer: (a) 0.1.
4 (iii)Passage Q. (i) to (iii): Human blood has a narrow pH range of 7.3–7.4, which must be maintained for metabolic processes to function properly. To keep the pH in this range, it requires a delicate balance between the concentrations of the conjugate acid–base pairs, making up to the buffer system. The main buffer is a carbonic acid-hydrogen carbonate system, which involves the following equilibria.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(aq)
CO2(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ H2CO3(aq)
H2CO3(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ HCO3−(aq) + H3O+(aq)
At the body temperature, the pKa for carbonic acid is 6.40. However, the normal concentration of CO2(g) in the lungs maintains a ratio of HCO3−(aq) and H2CO3(aq) in blood plasma at about 8 : 1. The H2CO3 concentration in blood is largely controlled by breathing and respiration and HCO3− concentration is largely controlled by excretion in urine. If blood pH rises above 7.4, the potentially life-threatening condition called alkalosis can result. It normally occurs in climbers suffering from oxygen deficiency at high altitude or in patients who are hyperventilating from severe anxiety (log 2 = 0.3).
(iii) Which of the following statement is correct?
(a) One way to treat alkalosis can be to get the patient to breathe more quickly so that the amount of CO2 exhaled increases and the blood becomes deficient in CO2.
(b) One way to treat alkalosis is to get the patient breathe into a bag so that the exhaled CO2 is re-inhaled.
(c) The pH of blood is independent of the concentration of CO2.
(d) Alkalosis cannot be controlled by breathing and respiration.		Treatment of alkalosisWhy do People Hyperventilate into Paper Bags? (Explained in 30 Seconds!)🟡 Similar(b) rebreathe the exhaled CO2 (breathe into a bag)
Concept Alkalosis means blood pH has risen above 7.4, i.e. there is too little H2CO3 (dissolved CO2) relative to HCO3−. The cure must push pH back DOWN, which means raising dissolved CO2, not expelling it. Any option that removes CO2 makes alkalosis worse.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) raising [H2CO3] drops the ratio and lowers pH toward 7.4.
Solution
1. In alkalosis pH is above 7.4, so treatment must DECREASE pH, i.e. increase dissolved CO2/H2CO3.
2. (a) Breathing faster expels MORE CO2, lowering H2CO3, so pH rises still further. This worsens alkalosis — wrong.
3. (b) Breathing into a bag re-inhales exhaled CO2, raising dissolved CO2 and H2CO3; the ratio falls and pH returns to normal. This is the standard clinical cure — correct.
4. (c) pH is NOT independent of CO2: CO2 fixes [H2CO3], one arm of the buffer. Wrong.
5. (d) Alkalosis CAN be controlled by breathing — option (b) does exactly that. Wrong.
6. The only correct statement is (b); the key prints (d).
 alkalosis means pH above 7.4  (too basic)
 cure = lower pH = raise H2CO3 = keep CO2
 (a) breathe fast : lose CO2, pH rises   worse
 (b) rebreathe bag: gain CO2, pH falls   CURE
 (c) pH independent of CO2               false
 (d) breathing cannot help               false
 ----------------------------------------
 correct statement = (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Respiratory alkalosis from hyperventilation is famously treated by rebreathing into a paper bag. Any option that removes CO2 must be wrong, so (a) and (d) fall at once.
Answer: (b) rebreathe the exhaled CO2 — breathe into a bag.
5 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (v): The first and second dissociation constants of H2CO3 are 4.0×10−6 and 2.5×10−11, respectively. In a particular titration, 50 ml of 1.0 M-Na2CO3 is taken in a flask and 1.0 M-HCl solution is added drop by drop. Determine the pH of the resulting solution on adding the following volume of HCl solution. Assume that volume is additive. (log 2 = 0.3, log 3 = 0.48).
(i) 0 ml?
(a) 1.7   (b) 12.3   (c) 3.7   (d) 10.3		Carbonate titration: 0 mLIEQ-53 50 mL of 0.05 M Na2CO3 is titrated against 0.1 M HCl on adding 40 mL of HCl what will be pH🟡 Similar(b) 12.3
Concept 1.0 M Na2CO3 is the salt of a strong base and the weak diprotic acid H2CO3. Before any HCl is added, CO32− hydrolyses and the basicity is fixed by Kb1 = Kw/Ka2. The second hydrolysis (HCO3−) is negligible.
Key relation Kb1 = Kw/Ka2; [OH−] = √(Kb1C); pH = 14 − pOH Kw = 10−14 at 25 °C.
Solution
1. CO32− + H2O ⇄ HCO3− + OH−, with Kb1 = Kw/Ka2 = 10−14/2.5×10−11 = 4×10−4.
2. At 0 mL HCl the carbonate is undiluted: C = 1.0 M.
3. [OH−] = √(Kb1C) = √(4×10−4 × 1) = 2×10−2 M.
4. α = [OH−]/C = 0.02 ≤ 1, so the approximation is valid. the 1−α correction is under 2 percent.
5. pOH = −log(2×10−2) = 2 − log2 = 2 − 0.30 = 1.70.
6. pH = 14 − 1.70 = 12.30.
7. Sanity: the next point (25 mL) is pH = pKa2 = 10.6; pure carbonate must be MORE basic, so pH must exceed 10.6 — only 12.3 fits, 10.3 is impossible.
 Kb1 = Kw/Ka2 = 1e-14 / 2.5e-11 = 4e-4
 [OH-] = sqrt(Kb1 * C) = sqrt(4e-4) = 0.02 M
 pOH = 2 - log2 = 2 - 0.30 = 1.70
 ----------------------------------------
 pH = 14 - 1.70 = 12.3        (option b)
⚡ JEE Trick A titration curve only falls as acid is added, so each listed pH must be lower than the one before. That monotonic check alone rules out 10.3 at the very start.
Answer: (b) 12.3.
5 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (v): The first and second dissociation constants of H2CO3 are 4.0×10−6 and 2.5×10−11, respectively. In a particular titration, 50 ml of 1.0 M-Na2CO3 is taken in a flask and 1.0 M-HCl solution is added drop by drop. Determine the pH of the resulting solution on adding the following volume of HCl solution. Assume that volume is additive. (log 2 = 0.3, log 3 = 0.48).
(ii) 25 ml?
(a) 10.6   (b) 10.3   (c) 10.9   (d) 3.4		Carbonate titration: 25 mLIEQ-53 50 mL of 0.05 M Na2CO3 is titrated against 0.1 M HCl on adding 40 mL of HCl what will be pH🟡 Similar(a) 10.6
Concept The first proton added converts CO32− to HCO3−. At 25 mL exactly half the carbonate is converted, giving an equimolar CO32−/HCO3− buffer — the half-equivalence point of the second ionisation.
Key relation pH = pKa2 + log([CO32−]/[HCO3−]) at half-equivalence the log term is 0, so pH = pKa2.
Solution
1. Initial CO32− = 50 mL × 1.0 M = 50 mmol.
2. HCl added = 25 mL × 1.0 M = 25 mmol H+.
3. CO32− + H+ → HCO3− consumes 25 mmol CO32−, making 25 mmol HCO3−.
4. Left: CO32− = 25 mmol, HCO3− = 25 mmol — a 1:1 buffer. the common volume cancels, so the mole ratio is 1.
5. log2.5 = log(5/2) = log5 − log2 = 0.70 − 0.30 = 0.40, so pKa2 = −log(2.5×10−11) = 11 − 0.40 = 10.60.
6. pH = pKa2 + log(25/25) = 10.60 + log 1 = 10.60 + 0 = 10.60.
7. The dilution (adding 25 mL) changes both amounts equally, so it cancels in the ratio and the pH stays exactly pKa2. this is why distractors (b) 10.3 and (c) 10.9 — which assume a shifted ratio — are wrong.
 n(CO3^2-) = 50 mmol ;  n(H+) = 25 mmol
 CO3^2- + H+ -> HCO3-
 left: CO3^2- = 25 , HCO3- = 25  (ratio 1)
 log2.5 = log5 - log2 = 0.70 - 0.30 = 0.40
 pKa2 = 11 - 0.40 = 10.60
 ----------------------------------------
 pH = pKa2 + log 1 = 10.6      (option a)
⚡ JEE Trick Spot the half-equivalence point (half the stoichiometric acid) and you write pH = pKa instantly — no concentration or dilution needed. For a diprotic acid the quarter-way point of the titration always sits at pKa2.
Answer: (a) 10.6.
5 (iii)Passage Q. (i) to (v): The first and second dissociation constants of H2CO3 are 4.0×10−6 and 2.5×10−11, respectively. In a particular titration, 50 ml of 1.0 M-Na2CO3 is taken in a flask and 1.0 M-HCl solution is added drop by drop. Determine the pH of the resulting solution on adding the following volume of HCl solution. Assume that volume is additive. (log 2 = 0.3, log 3 = 0.48).
(iii) 50 ml?
(a) 10.6   (b) 5.4   (c) 8.0   (d) 6.0		Carbonate titration: 50 mLIEQ-53 50 mL of 0.05 M Na2CO3 is titrated against 0.1 M HCl on adding 40 mL of HCl what will be pH🟡 Similar(c) 8.0
Concept 50 mmol H+ exactly converts all 50 mmol CO32− to HCO3− — the first equivalence point. The solution is now pure NaHCO3, and HCO3− is amphiprotic (it is both the acid of Ka2 and the base of Ka1).
Key relation pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2 for an amphiprotic ion the result is independent of its concentration.
Solution
1. H+ added = 50 mL × 1.0 M = 50 mmol, equal to the 50 mmol CO32−.
2. CO32− + H+ → HCO3−: all carbonate becomes 50 mmol HCO3−.
3. Solution is pure NaHCO3 (0.5 M in 100 mL), the amphiprotic case. no excess acid or base remains.
4. pKa1 = −log(4.0×10−6) = 6 − log4 = 6 − 0.60 = 5.40.
5. pKa2 = −log(2.5×10−11) = 11 − 0.40 = 10.60.
6. pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2 = (5.40 + 10.60)/2 = 16.0/2 = 8.00.
7. Check: 8.0 lies between the 25-mL value (10.6) and the 75-mL value (5.4), as a titration curve demands; 6.0 does not.
 H+ = 50 mmol  -> all CO3^2- -> HCO3- (50 mmol)
 amphiprotic HCO3- :
 pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2
 pKa1 = 6 - log4 = 5.40 ; pKa2 = 10.60
 ----------------------------------------
 pH = (5.40 + 10.60)/2 = 8.0   (option c)
⚡ JEE Trick For any amphiprotic salt (NaHCO3, NaHS, NaH2PO4) use pH = (pKa1+pKa2)/2 — no concentration needed. That it is concentration-free is the giveaway.
Answer: (c) 8.0.

Concept After 50 mL all carbonate is HCO3−; the next 25 mmol H+ converts half of that HCO3− to H2CO3. At 75 mL we are at the half-equivalence point of the FIRST ionisation, giving an equimolar HCO3−/H2CO3 buffer.
Key relation pH = pKa1 + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) equal amounts make the log term 0, so pH = pKa1.
Solution
1. Total H+ added = 75 mL × 1.0 M = 75 mmol.
2. First 50 mmol: CO32− → HCO3− (50 mmol HCO3− formed).
3. Remaining 25 mmol: HCO3− + H+ → H2CO3, converting 25 mmol.
4. Left: HCO3− = 50 − 25 = 25 mmol, H2CO3 = 25 mmol — a 1:1 buffer. volume is common, so the ratio is 1.
5. pKa1 = −log(4.0×10−6) = 6 − log4 = 6 − 0.60 = 5.40.
6. pH = pKa1 + log(25/25) = 5.40 + 0 = 5.40.
 H+ = 75 mmol = 50 (-> HCO3-) + 25 (-> H2CO3)
 left: HCO3- = 25 , H2CO3 = 25   (ratio 1)
 pKa1 = 6 - log4 = 6 - 0.60 = 5.40

⚡ JEE Trick The two half-equivalence points of a diprotic titration sit at pKa2 (quarter-way) and pKa1 (three-quarter-way). Here 75 mL out of the full 100 mL is three-quarters, so pH = pKa1.
Answer: (d) 5.4.
5 (v)Passage Q. (i) to (v): The first and second dissociation constants of H2CO3 are 4.0×10−6 and 2.5×10−11, respectively. Concept 100 mmol H+ converts all 50 mmol CO32− first to HCO3− and then fully to H2CO3 — the second equivalence point. The solution is now pure weak acid H2CO3, so Ostwald’s formula gives the pH.
Key relation [H+] = √(Ka1C); pH = −log[H+] only Ka1 matters since the second ionisation of H2CO3 is tiny.
Solution

6 (i)Passage Q. (i) to (ii): Amino acid glycine (NH2−CH2−COOH) exists as a zwitterion in aqueous solution. The Ka and Kb values of glycine are 1.6×10−10 (pKa = 9.8) and 2.5×10−12 (pKb = 11.6), respectively. The Ka and Kb values are for zwitterion of amino acid with the following structure [+NH3−CH2−COO−].
(i) What is the value of Kb for −NH2 group in glycine?
(a) 4×10−3   (b) 1.6×10−10   (c) 6.25×10−5   (d) 2.5×10−12		Glycine zwitterion KbZwitterion formation | Isoelectric point of Amino acid | IIT JEE & NEET | Vineet Khatri | ATP STAR🟡 Similar(c) 6.25×10⁻⁵
Concept The given Ka = 1.6×10−10 is the acidity of the −NH3+ group of the zwitterion (it loses H+ to give −NH2). The basicity asked for, Kb of the −NH2 group, belongs to the conjugate base of that very ionisation.
Key relation Ka(−NH3+) × Kb(−NH2) = Kw ⇒ Kb = Kw/Ka conjugate acid–base pair at 25 °C.
Solution
1. Acid ionisation: +NH3−CH2−COO− ⇄ NH2−CH2−COO− + H+, Ka = 1.6×10−10.
2. The base −NH2 is the conjugate base of −NH3+: NH2−CH2−COO− + H2O ⇄ +NH3−CH2−COO− + OH−.
3. For any conjugate pair, Ka×Kb = Kw = 10−14.
4. So Kb(−NH2) = Kw/Ka = 10−14/1.6×10−10.
5. = (1/1.6)×10−4 = 0.625×10−4 = 6.25×10−5.
6. This matches option (c). do NOT use the stated Kb = 2.5×10−12; that is the basicity of −COO−, a different group.
 Ka(-NH3+) = 1.6e-10   (given, acid of ammonium)
 -NH2 is its conjugate base
 Ka * Kb = Kw = 1e-14
 Kb(-NH2) = 1e-14 / 1.6e-10
 ----------------------------------------
 Kb = 6.25e-5                  (option c)
⚡ JEE Trick Pair the groups correctly: Ka of −NH3+ pairs with Kb of −NH2, and Kb of −COO− pairs with Ka of −COOH. Mixing them is the trap behind distractor (d).
Answer: (c) 6.25×10−5.
6 (ii)Passage Q. (i) to (ii): Amino acid glycine (NH2−CH2−COOH) exists as a zwitterion in aqueous solution. The Ka and Kb values of glycine are 1.6×10−10 (pKa = 9.8) and 2.5×10−12 (pKb = 11.6), respectively. The Ka and Kb values are for zwitterion of amino acid with the following structure [+NH3−CH2−COO−].
(ii) An aqueous solution of glycine has pH
(a) nearly 7   (b) nearly 7.9   (c) nearly 6.1   (d) nearly 11.5		pH of glycine solutionHow To Calculate The Isoelectric Point of Amino Acids and Zwitterions🟡 Similar(c) nearly 6.1
Concept Glycine in water is the amphiprotic zwitterion +NH3−CH2−COO−. Its pH is the isoelectric point, pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2, where pKa1 is the −COOH group and pKa2 the −NH3+ group of the fully protonated acid.
Key relation pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2; pKa1 = 14 − pKb(−COO−) the stated pKb = 11.6 is the basicity of −COO−, conjugate to −COOH.
Solution
1. Treat glycine as the diprotic acid +NH3−CH2−COOH with two pKa values.
2. pKa2 (loss of H+ from −NH3+) is given directly: pKa2 = 9.8.
3. The −COOH group is the conjugate acid of −COO− (pKb = 11.6), so pKa1 = 14 − 11.6 = 2.4.
4. The zwitterion is the intermediate (amphiprotic) form between these two.
5. pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2 = (2.4 + 9.8)/2.
6. = 12.2/2 = 6.1, independent of concentration — option (c). slightly acidic because the two pKa’s straddle 6.1, not 7.
 glycine (aq) = amphiprotic zwitterion
 pKa2 (-NH3+)  = 9.8        (given)
 pKa1 (-COOH)  = 14 - pKb = 14 - 11.6 = 2.4
 pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2 = (2.4 + 9.8)/2
 ----------------------------------------
 pH = 6.1                      (option c)
⚡ JEE Trick An amino-acid solution is not neutral: its pH is the isoelectric point, the mean of the two pKa’s. “Nearly 7” (a) is the tempting but wrong default.
Answer: (c) nearly 6.1.
7The pH of blood stream is maintained by a proper balance of H2CO3 and NaHCO3 concentrations. What volume of 5 M-NaHCO3 solution should be mixed with a 10 ml sample of blood which is 2 M in H2CO3 in order to maintain a pH of 7.4? The value of Ka for H2CO3 in blood is 8.0×10−7 (log 2 = 0.3). [Answer is a 4-digit integer-grid value; here in mL.]Volume of NaHCO3 for blood pHph of buffer solutions - Henderson Hassel Balch Equation (JEE/NEET)🟡 Similar0080
Concept Blood is an H2CO3/NaHCO3 buffer. Henderson–Hasselbalch fixes the required mole ratio of HCO3− to H2CO3; from that we get the moles of NaHCO3 and hence the volume of the 5 M stock.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]) in one solution the ratio of concentrations equals the ratio of moles.
Solution
1. pKa = −log(8.0×10−7) = 7 − log8 = 7 − 3log2 = 7 − 0.90 = 6.10.
2. 7.4 = 6.10 + log([HCO3−]/[H2CO3]), so log(ratio) = 1.30.
3. ratio = 101.3 = 10 × 100.3 = 10 × 2 = 20. 100.3 = 2 since log2 = 0.30.
4. moles H2CO3 = 10 mL × 2 M = 20 mmol.
5. moles HCO3− needed = 20 × (moles H2CO3) = 20 × 20 = 400 mmol. the ratio is of moles because both species share the same final volume.
6. The 5 M NaHCO3 supplies these: volume = 400 mmol / 5 M = 80 mL.
7. The added 80 mL dilutes everything, but H2CO3 and HCO3− are diluted identically, so the ratio — and therefore the pH 7.4 — is unaffected.
 pKa = 7 - log8 = 7 - 3 log2 = 7 - 0.90 = 6.10
 7.4 = 6.10 + log r  ->  log r = 1.30
 r = 10^1.3 = 10 x 10^0.3 = 10 x 2 = 20
 n(H2CO3) = 10 mL x 2 M = 20 mmol
 n(HCO3-) = 20 x 20 = 400 mmol
 V = 400 mmol / 5 M
 ----------------------------------------
 V = 80 mL                     (grid 0080)
⚡ JEE Trick log8 = 3 log2 = 0.90, so pKa is a clean 6.10 and the needed ratio is exactly 101.3 = 20. Working in moles (same volume) and noting dilution cancels save you the volume algebra entirely.
Answer: V = 80 mL (grid value 0080).
8The acid ionization of hydrated aluminium ion is
Al(H2O)63+(aq) + H2O(l) ⇄ Al(H2O)5OH2+(aq) + H3O+(aq); Ka = 1.0×10−5
How many milligrams of AlCl3 should be dissolved in sufficient water to get 400 ml of solution of pH 3.0? [Answer is a 4-digit integer-grid value; here in mg.]		Mass of AlCl3 for given pHIonic Equilibrium 07 || Salt Hydrolysis IIT JEE MAINS / JEE ADVANCE / NEET || pH of Salts🟡 Similar5340
Concept Al(H2O)63+ is a weak acid of known Ka. Fixing the pH fixes [H+] = [Al(H2O)5OH2+]; the Ka expression then gives the AlCl3 concentration needed, and mass follows from volume and molar mass.
Key relation Ka = [H+]2/C ⇒ C = [H+]2/Ka AlCl3 is a strong electrolyte, so [Al3+] = C of AlCl3.
Solution
1. pH 3.0 ⇒ [H+] = 1.0×10−3 M, and each H+ comes with one Al(H2O)5OH2+, so both are 10−3 M.
2. Ka = [H+][AlOH2+]/[Al3+] = (10−3)2/C = 1.0×10−5.
3. C = (10−3)2/10−5 = 10−6/10−5 = 0.10 M. [H+] is only 1 percent of C, so neglecting it in (C − x) is fine.
4. This is the required [AlCl3] since Al3+ = AlCl3 dissolved.
5. moles in 400 mL = 0.10 M × 0.400 L = 0.0400 mol.
6. M(AlCl3) = 27 + 3×35.5 = 133.5; mass = 0.0400 × 133.5 = 5.34 g = 5340 mg.
 [H+] = 10^-3 = [AlOH2+]
 Ka = [H+]^2 / C  ->  C = (1e-3)^2 / 1e-5 = 0.10 M
 n = 0.10 x 0.400 L = 0.0400 mol
 M(AlCl3) = 27 + 3(35.5) = 133.5 g/mol
 mass = 0.0400 x 133.5 = 5.34 g
 ----------------------------------------
 mass = 5340 mg               (grid 5340)
⚡ JEE Trick For a weak acid at a given pH, C = [H+]2/Ka is the one-step shortcut. Check [H+] < 5 percent of C to confirm the approximation; here it is 1 percent.
Answer: mass = 5340 mg (grid value 5340).
9A saturated solution of iodine in water contains 0.254 g of I2 in 1 L. More than this can dissolve in a KI solution because of the following equilibrium.
I2(aq) + I−(aq) ⇄ I3−(aq)
A 0.1 M-KI solution actually dissolved 12.7 g of iodine per litre, most of which is converted to I3−. Assuming that the concentration of I2 in all saturated solutions is the same, calculate the equilibrium constant for the above reaction (Take: 0.96×5.1 = 4.9, atomic mass of iodine = 127). [Answer is a 4-digit integer-grid value.]		Equilibrium constant of triiodideApplication of Thermodynamics: Lecture 11: Determination of Equillibrium Constant of KI+I2=KI3🟡 Similar0960
Concept Free [I2] is the same (saturation) in every solution, so it is fixed by the pure-water solubility. The extra iodine that dissolves in KI is the I3− formed; subtracting it from the KI gives the leftover I−.
Key relation K = [I3−]/([I2][I−]) M(I2) = 2×127 = 254 g/mol converts grams to molarity.
Solution
1. Saturated [I2] in water = 0.254 g·L−1 / 254 g·mol−1 = 1.0×10−3 M; same free [I2] in the KI solution.
2. Total iodine dissolved in KI = 12.7 / 254 = 5.0×10−2 M.
3. [I3−] = total − free I2 = 0.050 − 0.001 = 0.049 M.
4. Each I3− uses one I−, so leftover [I−] = 0.100 − 0.049 = 0.051 M.
5. K = [I3−]/([I2][I−]) = 0.049 / (1.0×10−3 × 0.051).
6. 0.049/0.051 = 0.96, so K = 0.96 / 10−3 = 960. uses the hint 0.96 × 5.1 = 4.9.
 [I2] sat  = 0.254/254 = 1.0e-3 M  (all solns)
 total I2 in KI = 12.7/254 = 0.050 M
 [I3-] = 0.050 - 0.001 = 0.049 M
 [I-]  = 0.100 - 0.049 = 0.051 M
 K = 0.049 / (1.0e-3 x 0.051) = 0.96/1e-3
 ----------------------------------------
 K = 960                       (grid 0960)
⚡ JEE Trick The “same saturated [I2]” clause is the whole trick: it pins free [I2] from the water value, so the added iodine in KI is essentially all I3−.
Answer: K = 960 (grid value 0960).
10The solubility product (Ksp) of Ca(OH)2 at 25°C is 3.2×10−5. A 500 ml of saturated solution of Ca(OH)2 is mixed with equal volume of 1.6 M-NaOH. How much Ca(OH)2 (in milligrams) is precipitated? [Answer is a 4-digit integer-grid value; here in mg.]Common-ion precipitation of Ca(OH)2The solubility product constant of Ca (OH)2 at 25^0c is 4.42 x 10-5. A 500 ml of saturated solution🟡 Similar0740
Concept A saturated Ca(OH)2 solution already holds dissolved Ca2+. Adding a large excess of OH− (common ion) forces almost all of that Ca2+ out as solid Ca(OH)2; the mass precipitated is essentially all the Ca2+ present.
Key relation Ksp = [Ca2+][OH−]2 = 4s3 after mixing, [OH−] is set by the added NaOH; [Ca2+] = Ksp/[OH−]2.
Solution
1. Saturated Ca(OH)2: 4s3 = 3.2×10−5, s3 = 8×10−6, s = 2.0×10−2 M.
2. In 500 mL, moles Ca2+ = 0.020 × 0.500 = 0.010 mol.
3. Mix with 500 mL of 1.6 M NaOH = 0.80 mol OH−; total volume 1.0 L.
4. [OH−] ≈ 0.80 M (the carbonate’s own OH− is tiny by comparison).
5. [Ca2+]eq = Ksp/[OH−]2 = 3.2×10−5/(0.80)2 = 5.0×10−5 M — negligible. so almost all Ca2+ precipitates.
6. Ca2+ precipitated ≈ 0.010 mol; M(Ca(OH)2) = 40 + 2×17 = 74.
7. mass = 0.010 × 74 = 0.74 g = 740 mg.
 sat: 4 s^3 = 3.2e-5 -> s = 0.020 M (Ca2+)
 n(Ca2+) = 0.020 x 0.500 = 0.010 mol
 added OH- = 1.6 x 0.500 = 0.80 mol ; V = 1.0 L
 [Ca2+]eq = Ksp/[OH-]^2 = 3.2e-5/0.64 ~ 5e-5 (tiny)
 ppt = 0.010 mol x 74 g/mol
 ----------------------------------------
 mass ~ 740 mg                (grid 0740)
⚡ JEE Trick With OH− flooded to ~0.8 M, [Ca2+] left is ~5×10−5 M, under 1 percent of the original, so you may take precipitation as complete and skip the exact subtraction.
Answer: mass precipitated ≈ 740 mg (grid value 0740).

1 (i)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(i) Calculate the value of overall equilibrium constant K'a, for the equilibrium between [H+](aq), [HCO3−](aq) and CO2 dissolved.
(In vivo, the (HCO3−/CO2) is an open system in which [CO2]dissolved is maintained constant and any excess CO2 produced by the reaction H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq) → CO2 + H2O is expelled out by the lungs. This reaction is responsible for maintaining the pH of blood.)		Overall equilibrium constant K'aBicarbonate Buffer System🟡 SimilarK'a = 7.9×10⁻⁷
Concept The target equilibrium, CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H+ + HCO3−, is the sum of the hydration step (ii) and the first ionisation (iii). When equilibria are added, their constants multiply.
Key relation K′a = Keq × Ka1 reactions (i) and (iv) are not part of this overall change.
Solution
1. (ii): CO2(aq) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3, Keq = 5×10−3.
2. (iii): H2CO3 ⇄ H+ + HCO3−, Ka1 = 1.58×10−4.
3. Adding (ii) + (iii) cancels H2CO3 and gives CO2(aq) + H2O ⇄ H+ + HCO3−.
4. K′a = Keq × Ka1 = (5×10−3)(1.58×10−4).
5. = 7.9×10−7 (pK′a = 6.10).
 target = (ii) + (iii)
 K'a = Keq x Ka1 = 5e-3 x 1.58e-4
 ----------------------------------------
 K'a = 7.9e-7     (pK'a = 6.10)
⚡ JEE Trick Add reactions ⇒ multiply K’s; reverse ⇒ reciprocal; scale by n ⇒ power n. Here only the two middle steps connect dissolved CO2 to H+ + HCO3−.
Answer: K′a = 7.9×10−7.
1 (ii)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(ii) (a) The pH of blood in the body is maintained at 7.4. What is the ratio of [HCO3−]/[CO2] and the concentration of each component present at pH 7.4?
(b) What would be the pH of the blood if 5×10−3 M H+ is added under conditions where the increased [CO2] cannot be exhaled out?
(c) What would be the pH if 5×10−3 M [H+] is added and the increased [CO2] is exhaled out? (thereby the original [CO2] does not change)		Blood buffer ratio and pH after acid loadBicarbonate Buffer System🟡 Similar(a) 20:1, HCO₃⁻=2.4×10⁻² M, CO₂=1.2×10⁻³ M; (b) pH=6.58; (c) pH=7.29
Concept Use the overall K′a = 7.9×10−7 (pK′a = 6.10) in Henderson–Hasselbalch form with dissolved CO2 as the acid. At pH 7.4 the ratio and the total pool fix both concentrations; adding strong acid then shifts them, differently for a closed vs an open (CO2-exhaled) system.
Key relation pH = pK′a + log([HCO3−]/[CO2]); [HCO3−] + [CO2] = 2.52×10−2 M H+ + HCO3− → CO2 + H2O moves one from each column.
Solution
1. (a) ratio = K′a/[H+]; [H+] = 10−7.4 = 3.98×10−8, so ratio = 7.9×10−7/3.98×10−8 = 20 (i.e. 20:1).
2. From total 2.52×10−2: [HCO3−] = (20/21)(2.52×10−2) = 2.4×10−2 M, [CO2] = (1/21)(2.52×10−2) = 1.2×10−3 M.
3. (b) Closed system: added 5×10−3 M H+ converts 5×10−3 M HCO3− to CO2.
4. [HCO3−] = 2.4×10−2 − 5×10−3 = 1.9×10−2; [CO2] = 1.2×10−3 + 5×10−3 = 6.2×10−3.
5. pH = 6.10 + log(1.9×10−2/6.2×10−3) = 6.10 + log 3.06 = 6.10 + 0.49 = 6.58. a big drop — the body cannot survive this, so the CO2 must be exhaled.
6. (c) Open system: CO2 is exhaled, so [CO2] stays at 1.2×10−3; only [HCO3−] falls to 1.9×10−2.
7. pH = 6.10 + log(1.9×10−2/1.2×10−3) = 6.10 + log 15.8 = 6.10 + 1.20 = 7.30 ≈ 7.29.
 (a) [H+]=10^-7.4=3.98e-8 ; ratio=K'a/[H+]=20
     HCO3- = 20/21 x 2.52e-2 = 2.4e-2 M
     CO2   =  1/21 x 2.52e-2 = 1.2e-3 M
 (b) closed: HCO3-=1.9e-2 , CO2=6.2e-3
     pH = 6.10 + log(3.06) = 6.58
 (c) open : HCO3-=1.9e-2 , CO2=1.2e-3 (fixed)
     pH = 6.10 + log(15.8) = 7.29
⚡ JEE Trick The open lung system is the lesson: exhaling the extra CO2 keeps the denominator fixed, so the same acid load drops pH by only 0.1 (to 7.29) instead of 0.8 (to 6.58).
Answer: (a) [HCO3−]/[CO2] = 20:1, [HCO3−] = 2.4×10−2 M, [CO2] = 1.2×10−3 M; (b) pH = 6.58; (c) pH = 7.29.
1 (iii)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(iii) If a person exhales 274 mL min−1 of CO2 at 37°C and 101.3 kPa pressure, calculate [CO2] and [HCO3−] in CO2 rich blood. (Blood flows through the lungs at a rate of 5.4 L min−1)
(In the body, haemoglobin binds with O2. The O2 binding curves for haemoglobin in presence and absence of CO2 is qualitatively sketched below. The curves indicate percent saturation of haemoglobin by O2 as a function of partial pressure of O2.)		CO2 and HCO3- in CO2-rich bloodBicarbonate Buffer System🟡 Similar[HCO₃⁻]=25.8×10⁻³ M, [CO₂]=1.39×10⁻³ M (PCO₂≈46 mmHg)
Concept The CO2 a person exhales per minute is the extra carbonate carried by the CO2-rich (venous) blood arriving at the lungs. Converting the exhaled gas to moles and dividing by the blood flow gives the extra carbonate per litre, which we add to the arterial pool and then split by the buffer.
Key relation n = PV/RT; ΔC = n / (flow); total = 2.52×10−2 + ΔC H+ from the added CO2 is taken up by haemoglobin, so pH stays near 7.4 and the ratio stays near 20.
Solution
1. Exhaled CO2: n = PV/RT = (101.3×103 Pa × 274×10−6 m3) / (8.314 × 310 K) = 1.08×10−2 mol·min−1.
2. Per litre of blood (flow 5.4 L·min−1): ΔC = 1.08×10−2 / 5.4 = 2.0×10−3 M.
3. This is the carbonate the venous blood loses in the lungs, so CO2-rich blood carries that much extra.
4. Total carbonate (venous) = 2.52×10−2 + 2.0×10−3 = 2.72×10−2 M.
5. Haemoglobin absorbs the released H+, so the pH stays close to 7.4 and most of the extra appears as HCO3−: [HCO3−] ≈ 25.8×10−3 M.
6. [CO2] = total − [HCO3−] = 2.72×10−2 − 2.58×10−2 = 1.39×10−3 M.
7. Check via step (i): PCO2 = [CO2]/K = 1.39×10−3/3.01×10−5 ≈ 46 mmHg, the normal venous value, and ratio 25.8/1.39 = 18.6 gives pH 7.37 — slightly acidic, as expected for CO2-rich blood.
 n = PV/RT = (101.3e3 x 274e-6)/(8.314 x 310)
   = 1.08e-2 mol/min
 dC = n / 5.4 L = 2.0e-3 M
 total(venous) = 2.52e-2 + 2.0e-3 = 2.72e-2 M
 HCO3- ~ 25.8e-3 M (pH stays ~7.4, Hb buffers H+)
 CO2 = 2.72e-2 - 2.58e-2 = 1.39e-3 M
 check: PCO2 = CO2/K = 1.39e-3/3.01e-5 ~ 46 mmHg
⚡ JEE Trick Use PV = nRT at body conditions (310 K, 101.3 kPa) and divide by the blood flow to turn a gas rate into a concentration. The haemoglobin curve shown in the problem is the reason pH barely moves while CO2 loads up.
Answer: [HCO3−] ≈ 25.8×10−3 M and [CO2] ≈ 1.39×10−3 M (PCO2 ≈ 46 mmHg).
1 (iv)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(iv) Which of the following statement/s is/are correct for the given curves?
(a) In presence of CO2, higher PO2 is needed for a given percent saturation
(b) In presence of CO2, lower PO2 is needed for a given percent saturation
(c) In absence of CO2, maximum saturation of haemoglobin occurs at lower PO2
(d) In absence of CO2 for any given percent saturation, higher PO2 is needed		Interpreting Hb O2-saturation curvesA-Level Biology - Haemoglobin - Oxygen Dissociation Curve | Bohr Effect (2027/28 exams)🟡 Similar(a) & (c)
Concept On a percent-saturation vs PO2 plot, a curve shifted to the right needs a higher O2 pressure to reach the same saturation, i.e. lower O2 affinity. CO2 (the Bohr effect) lowers haemoglobin’s affinity, so the “in presence of CO2” curve is the right/lower one and “absence” is the left/upper one.
Key relation right shift ⇒ higher PO2 for the same % saturation; left shift ⇒ lower PO2 Affinity is read off horizontally at a fixed saturation.
Solution
1. Fix the identities: left/upper curve = absence of CO2, right/lower curve = presence of CO2.
2. (a) In presence of CO2, higher PO2 is needed for a given % saturation — this is exactly the right-shifted curve. Correct.
3. (b) is the opposite of (a), so it is wrong.
4. (c) In absence of CO2, maximum saturation occurs at lower PO2 — the left curve plateaus (nears 100%) earlier. Correct.
5. (d) says absence needs a higher PO2 for any saturation, but the absence curve needs a lower one. Wrong.
6. Correct statements: (a) and (c).
 absence of CO2 : LEFT/UPPER  (high O2 affinity)
 presence of CO2: RIGHT/LOWER (low  O2 affinity)
 -----------------------------------------
 (a) presence -> higher PO2   OK
 (c) absence  -> plateau at lower PO2   OK
 (b),(d) reversed statements  NO
 answer = (a) and (c)              <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Bohr effect: CO2/H+ push the curve to the right so Hb unloads O2 where tissues are acidic. Right = needs more pressure; a rightward shift never lowers the pressure needed.
Answer: (a) and (c).
1 (v)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(Each Haemoglobin (Hb) molecule can take up four molecules of O2. In the figure oxygenated fraction of haemoglobin is plotted as a function of oxygen pressure in kPa. The curves in the figure are for different types of haemoglobin, namely, normal (curve 1) and abnormal (curves 2 and 3). (O2 pressure in lungs, and muscles is 15 kPa and 2 kPa respectively.))
(v) Using the figure given above, calculate the number of moles of O2 absorbed in muscle tissues when one mole of (Hb) travels from the lungs to the muscles and back again, in all three types of (Hb) shown in the figure.
(In intense physical activity, lactic acid is produced in the muscles, particularly in an anaerobic metabolism. High lactic acid/lactate ([HL]/[L−]) levels in muscles lead to high acidity and destruction of other metabolites. In blood, lactic acid (HL) (KHL = 1.4×10−4) is neutralised by the bicarbonate present in the blood.)
Moles of O2 released to musclesA-Level Biology - Haemoglobin - Oxygen Dissociation Curve | Bohr Effect (2027/28 exams)🟡 SimilarNormal Hb Curve 1 ≈ 3.2 mol; Abnormal Curve 2 ≈ 1.6 mol; Curve 3 ≈ 2.9 mol
Concept Each Hb binds 4 O2. On the round trip, Hb loads in the lungs (15 kPa) and unloads in the muscle (2 kPa). The O2 handed to muscle equals the drop in oxygenated fraction between those two pressures, times 4, times 1 mol Hb.
Key relation n(O2) = 4 × [ f(15 kPa) − f(2 kPa) ] f is the oxygenated fraction read off each curve.
Solution
1. Read each curve at P = 15 kPa (lungs) and P = 2 kPa (muscle).
2. Curve 1 (normal): f(15) ≈ 1.0, f(2) ≈ 0.20, so Δf ≈ 0.80.
3. Curve 2 (far left, very high affinity): f(15) ≈ 1.0, f(2) ≈ 0.60, so Δf ≈ 0.40.
4. Curve 3 (far right, low affinity): f(15) ≈ 0.80, f(2) ≈ 0.075, so Δf ≈ 0.72.
5. Multiply each Δf by 4: Curve 1 = 3.2, Curve 2 = 1.6, Curve 3 ≈ 2.9 mol O2.
6. Curve 2 clings to O2 (releases least); the normal Hb delivers the most of the three at these pressures.
 n(O2) = 4 x [ f(15 kPa) - f(2 kPa) ]
 -----------------------------------------
 Curve1: 4 x (1.00 - 0.20) = 3.2 mol
 Curve2: 4 x (1.00 - 0.60) = 1.6 mol
 Curve3: 4 x (0.80 - 0.075) = 2.9 mol   <-- answers
⚡ JEE Trick Delivery depends on the gap between loading and unloading, not on how high a curve sits. A too-tight curve (2) saturates early but barely unloads, so it delivers the least.
Answer: Curve 1 ≈ 3.2 mol, Curve 2 ≈ 1.6 mol, Curve 3 ≈ 2.9 mol.
1 (vi)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(In intense physical activity, lactic acid is produced in the muscles, particularly in an anaerobic metabolism. High lactic acid/lactate ([HL]/[L−]) levels in muscles lead to high acidity and destruction of other metabolites. In blood, lactic acid (HL) (KHL = 1.4×10−4) is neutralised by the bicarbonate present in the blood.)
(vi) Calculate the pH of blood in muscles when the lactic acid concentration is 2.70×10−3 M.		pH of blood in muscles (lactic acid)8 Ph Calculation of Monoprotic Weak Acid Or Weak Base | JEE Main Advanced | Chemistry Class 11🟡 SimilarpH = 3.16
Concept Lactic acid (HL) is a weak monoprotic acid, KHL = 1.4×10−4, at C = 2.70×10−3 M. Because C is small, check α before using the usual [H+] = √(KaC): here α is not tiny, so the (C − x) term must be kept.
Key relation Ka = x²/(C − x), x = [H+] Exact for a weak acid; drop the −x only when α < ~5%.
Solution
1. First-pass α = √(Ka/C) = √(1.4×10−4/2.70×10−3) = √0.0519 = 0.23 (23%), too big to approximate.
2. Quadratic: x² + Kax − KaC = 0, with KaC = 1.4×10−4×2.70×10−3 = 3.78×10−7.
3. Discriminant = (1.4×10−4)² + 4(3.78×10−7) = 1.96×10−8 + 1.512×10−6 = 1.532×10−6; √ = 1.238×10−3.
4. x = (−1.4×10−4 + 1.238×10−3)/2 = 5.49×10−4 M.
5. pH = −log(5.49×10−4) = 4 − log 5.49 = 4 − 0.74 = 3.26.
6. The simple √(KaC) gives [H+] = 6.15×10−4, pH = 3.21; the book’s printed 3.16 is a rounding/arithmetic slip.
 alpha = sqrt(Ka/C) = 0.23  -> keep (C-x)
 x^2 + Ka x - Ka C = 0,  Ka C = 3.78e-7
 x = 5.49e-4 M
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = -log(5.49e-4) = 3.26     <-- answer
 (sqrt(KaC) approx -> 3.21 ; book 3.16)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever α > ~5%, dropping the −x overstates [H+]; the exact root here lifts pH from 3.21 to 3.26. A dilute weak acid is the classic trap for the √(KaC) shortcut.
Answer: pH ≈ 3.26 (book prints 3.16; the simple √(KaC) formula gives 3.21).
1 (vii)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(In intense physical activity, lactic acid is produced in the muscles, particularly in an anaerobic metabolism. High lactic acid/lactate ([HL]/[L−]) levels in muscles lead to high acidity and destruction of other metabolites. In blood, lactic acid (HL) (KHL = 1.4×10−4) is neutralised by the bicarbonate present in the blood.)
(vii) Calculate the equilibrium constant for the reaction between lactic acid and bicarbonate. Write the equations for the appropriate equilibria involved. (For this calculation, assume the dissociation constants of H2CO3 as Ka1 = 4.5×10−7 and Ka2 = 4.7×10−11).		K for lactic acid + bicarbonateEQM CONSTANTS FOR WEAK ACID WEAK BASE REACTION🟡 SimilarK1 = 3.11×102
Concept Lactic acid is neutralised by bicarbonate. Build the net reaction by adding the dissociation of HL to the reverse of the first dissociation of H2CO3; adding equilibria multiplies their K values. Use Ka1(H2CO3) = 4.5×10−7 as instructed.
Key relation K = Ka(HL)/Ka1(H2CO3) The shared H+ cancels when the two equilibria are summed.
Solution
1. HL ⇄ H+ + L−; Ka(HL) = 1.4×10−4.
2. H+ + HCO3− ⇄ H2CO3; this is the reverse of Ka1, so its constant = 1/Ka1 = 1/(4.5×10−7).
3. Add them: HL + HCO3− ⇄ L− + H2CO3 (the H+ cancels).
4. K = Ka(HL) × (1/Ka1) = Ka(HL)/Ka1.
5. K = 1.4×10−4 / 4.5×10−7 = 3.11×102.
6. K >> 1 confirms bicarbonate drives the neutralisation essentially to completion.
 HL        -> H+ + L-        Ka   = 1.4e-4
 H+ + HCO3- -> H2CO3         1/Ka1, Ka1=4.5e-7
 -----------------------------------------
 HL + HCO3- -> L- + H2CO3
 K = Ka/Ka1 = 1.4e-4/4.5e-7 = 3.11e2  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Added reactions multiply K; a reversed step inverts its K. Here a weak acid (Ka 1.4×10−4) attacking a weaker one’s base gives K >> 1 — the stronger acid always wins the proton tug-of-war.
Answer: K = 3.11×102.
1 (viii)Problem 1 · Acid-Base Equilibria. The transport of gases through circulation is an important function of blood in the body. CO2 generated in our body dissolves in the blood and is in equilibrium with CO2 in gas phase. A complete representation of CO2 buffer system in the body is shown below.
CO2(g) ⇄ CO2(dissolved); K = 3.01×10−5 ----(i)
CO2(dissolved) + H2O ⇄ H2CO3(aq); Keq = 5×10−3 ----(ii)
H2CO3(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + HCO3−(aq); Ka1 = 1.58×10−4 ----(iii)
HCO3−(aq) ⇄ H+(aq) + CO32−(aq); Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 ----(iv)
When PCO2 is expressed in terms of mm Hg, the values of K, Ka1 and Ka2 given above are at 37°C. A total carbonate pool (essentially [HCO3−] + [CO2](dissolved)) in blood plasma is 2.52×10−2 M.
(viii) Blood also contains Ca (II) ions. Determine the maximum concentration of calcium ions that can be present in blood at pH = 7.4. ([HCO3−] = 0.022 M at this pH and for simplification assume that only Ca will precipitate as CaCO3, Ksp of CaCO3 = 4.9×10−9)		Max [Ca2+] in blood at pH 7.4Calculate the Solubility of Ag3PO4 and CaCO3 Hg2Cl2🟡 Similar[Ca2+]free = 1.9×10−4 M
Concept CaCO3 precipitates once [Ca2+][CO32−] reaches Ksp. The free carbonate comes from bicarbonate via the second dissociation, which is strongly pH-dependent, so find [CO32−] first, then the maximum [Ca2+].
Key relation [CO32−] = Ka2[HCO3−]/[H+], [Ca2+]max = Ksp/[CO32−] Ksp sets the solubility ceiling.
Solution
1. [H+] at pH 7.4 = 10−7.4 = 3.98×10−8 M.
2. Using the blood value Ka2 = 5.6×10−11 and [HCO3−] = 0.022 M.
3. [CO32−] = 5.6×10−11 × 0.022 / 3.98×10−8 = 1.232×10−12/3.98×10−8 = 3.10×10−5 M.
4. [Ca2+]max = Ksp/[CO32−] = 4.9×10−9/3.10×10−5 = 1.58×10−4 M.
5. The book’s 1.9×10−4 M comes from using Ka2 = 4.7×10−11 (the value supplied in part vii), which gives [CO32−] = 2.60×10−5 M.
6. With the chapter’s own Ka2 for blood the answer is 1.6×10−4 M.
 [H+] = 10^-7.4 = 3.98e-8
 [CO3^2-] = Ka2 [HCO3-]/[H+]
   = 5.6e-11 x 0.022 / 3.98e-8 = 3.10e-5
 [Ca2+] = Ksp/[CO3^2-]
   = 4.9e-9 / 3.10e-5 = 1.6e-4 M   <-- answer
 (Ka2=4.7e-11 -> 1.9e-4 M, the book)
⚡ JEE Trick Raising pH raises [CO32−], so less Ca2+ can stay dissolved — alkalosis risks calcification. Watch which Ka2 the sub-part intends; the two given values differ and change the number.
Answer: [Ca2+] ≈ 1.6×10−4 M (the book’s 1.9×10−4 M uses Ka2 = 4.7×10−11).
2 (i)Problem 2 · Acid Base chemistry.
A. (Vinegar is used in food preparations. The main ingredient of vinegar is acetic acid that gives it a pungent taste. A sample of vinegar has 5% v/v acetic acid. The density of acetic acid is 1.05 g mL−1.)
(i) (a) Calculate the molarity of acetic acid in vinegar solution.
(b) Calculate the pH of the above vinegar sample. (Ka for acetic acid = 1.75×10−5)		Molarity and pH of vinegarTitration Experiment & Calculate the Molarity of Acetic Acid in Vinegar🟡 Similar(a) Molarity = 0.875 M; (b) pH = 2.41
Concept 5% v/v means 5 mL of pure acetic acid (density 1.05 g mL−1) in every 100 mL of solution. Convert that to molarity, then treat the dilute acetic acid as a weak monoprotic acid for the pH.
Key relation M = (Vacid·d/Mw)/Vsoln; [H+] = √(KaC) Mw(CH3COOH) = 60 g mol−1.
Solution
1. Mass of acetic acid per 100 mL = 5 mL × 1.05 g mL−1 = 5.25 g.
2. Moles = 5.25/60 = 0.0875 mol.
3. Molarity = 0.0875 mol / 0.100 L = 0.875 M.
4. [H+] = √(KaC) = √(1.75×10−5 × 0.875) = √(1.53×10−5) = 3.91×10−3 M.
5. Check α = √(Ka/C) = √(2.0×10−5) = 4.5×10−3 << 1, so the approximation is valid.
6. pH = −log(3.91×10−3) = 3 − log 3.91 = 3 − 0.59 = 2.41.
 mass = 5 x 1.05 = 5.25 g / 100 mL
 M = (5.25/60)/0.100 = 0.875 M     <-- (a)
 -----------------------------------------
 [H+] = sqrt(1.75e-5 x 0.875) = 3.91e-3
 pH = -log(3.91e-3) = 2.41         <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick “5% v/v” uses the density of the pure acid, not of the solution — a common trap. Then pH of a weak acid is just (1/2)(pKa − log C).
Answer: (a) 0.875 M; (b) pH = 2.41.
2 (ii)Problem 2 · Acid Base chemistry.
A. (Vinegar is used in food preparations. The main ingredient of vinegar is acetic acid that gives it a pungent taste. A sample of vinegar has 5% v/v acetic acid. The density of acetic acid is 1.05 g mL−1.)
(ii) 100 mL of the above vinegar sample is diluted to 250 mL and then 25 mL of the diluted solution is titrated against NaOH solution (0.100 M).
(a) Calculate the pH of the solution when 10 mL of NaOH solution was added.
(b) Calculate the pH of the solution at the equivalence point (equivalence point is theoretical end point of the titration).		pH during vinegar-NaOH titrationWeak Acid / Strong Base Titration - All pH Calculations🟡 Similar(a) pH = 3.87; (b) pH = 8.32–8.82
Concept Dilute the vinegar (100 to 250 mL) to 0.35 M, then titrate 25 mL with 0.100 M NaOH. Partial neutralisation gives an acetate/acetic-acid buffer (Henderson); at the equivalence point only acetate remains and its hydrolysis sets a basic pH.
Key relation pH = pKa + log(nA−/nHA); at equiv. [OH−] = √(KbC) Kb = Kw/Ka, Kw = 10−14.
Solution
1. Diluted C = 0.875 × 100/250 = 0.35 M; in 25 mL, nHA = 0.35 × 25 = 8.75 mmol.
2. (a) n(NaOH) = 0.100 × 10 = 1.0 mmol → A− = 1.0 mmol, HA left = 7.75 mmol.
3. pKa = −log(1.75×10−5) = 4.76; pH = 4.76 + log(1.0/7.75) = 4.76 − 0.89 = 3.87.
4. (b) Equivalence needs 8.75 mmol NaOH = 87.5 mL, so total V = 112.5 mL; [A−] = 8.75/112.5 = 0.0778 M.
5. Kb = 10−14/1.75×10−5 = 5.71×10−10; [OH−] = √(5.71×10−10×0.0778) = 6.67×10−6 M.
6. pOH = 5.18, so pH = 14 − 5.18 = 8.82.
 (a) buffer: 1.0 mmol A-, 7.75 mmol HA
     pH = 4.76 + log(1.0/7.75) = 3.87   <-- (a)
 (b) equiv: [A-] = 8.75/112.5 = 0.0778 M
     Kb = 1e-14/1.75e-5 = 5.71e-10
     [OH-] = sqrt(Kb C) = 6.67e-6
     pH = 14 - 5.18 = 8.82              <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Don’t forget the volume added when finding the acetate concentration at equivalence; ignoring dilution is what spreads the book’s range (8.32–8.82). A weak-acid/strong-base equivalence point is always basic.
Answer: (a) pH = 3.87; (b) pH = 8.82 (book range 8.32–8.82).
2 (iii)Problem 2 · Acid Base chemistry.
A. (Vinegar is used in food preparations. The main ingredient of vinegar is acetic acid that gives it a pungent taste. A sample of vinegar has 5% v/v acetic acid. The density of acetic acid is 1.05 g mL−1.)
(iii) A pH meter is usually calibrated using standard buffer solution for which the pH is exactly known. A buffer solution consisting of sodium acetate and acetic acid with pH = 5 is to be used for calibration of a pH meter. How many moles of sodium acetate and acetic acid are required to prepare 250 mL of this buffer solution? (The total concentration of acetic acid in all forms in the solution is 0.8 M).		Moles for acetate buffer pH 5The pH of a buffer solution containing equal moles of acetic acid and sodium acetate is 4.8. (pKa...)🟡 Similarmoles salt = 0.128, moles acid = 0.0719
Concept An acetate buffer at pH 5 fixes the salt-to-acid ratio through Henderson; the stated total acetic-acid concentration (0.8 M, all forms) fixes their sum. Two equations, two unknowns, then scale to 250 mL.
Key relation [salt]/[acid] = 10(pH − pKa); [salt] + [acid] = 0.8 M pKa = −log(1.75×10−5) = 4.76.
Solution
1. Ratio r = [salt]/[acid] = 10(5 − 4.76) = 100.243 = 1.75.
2. With [salt] + [acid] = 0.8: [acid] = 0.8/(1 + 1.75) = 0.8/2.75 = 0.291 M.
3. [salt] = 0.8 − 0.291 = 0.509 M.
4. In 250 mL (0.25 L): moles acid = 0.291 × 0.25 = 0.0727 mol.
5. Moles salt = 0.509 × 0.25 = 0.127 mol.
6. Using the book’s rounded pKa = 4.75 (r = 1.78) gives salt = 0.128, acid = 0.0719 mol — same to rounding.
 r = 10^(5-4.76) = 1.75
 [acid] = 0.8/(1+1.75) = 0.291 M
 [salt] = 0.8 - 0.291  = 0.509 M
 -----------------------------------------
 n(acid) = 0.291 x 0.25 = 0.0727 mol  <-- answer
 n(salt) = 0.509 x 0.25 = 0.127  mol  <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick “Total in all forms” = [salt] + [acid]; combine it with the Henderson ratio rather than guessing. pH one unit above pKa means salt dominates (here ~1.75:1).
Answer: moles salt ≈ 0.127, moles acid ≈ 0.0727.
2 (iv)Problem 2 · Acid Base chemistry.
B. Two standard methods for estimation of amino acids are described below. In method I, a sample of valine is treated with nitrous acid and the volume of nitrogen gas released is measured. The reaction is as follows:
(CH3)2C(NH2)COOH + HNO2 → (CH3)2C(OH)COOH + N2 + H2O ----(Method I)
In method II, valine is treated with excess of perchloric acid in acetic acid (such a titration is called as a non aqueous titration where glacial acetic acid is used as solvent.) The reaction is indicated below:
CH3CH(NH2)COOH + HClO4 → CH3CH(NH3)+COOH + ClO4− ----(Method II)
After the reaction is complete, the unreacted HClO4 is determined by titrating it with standard solution of sodium acetate. 50.0 mL of a 0.150 M solution of HClO4 is added to a sample of valine in glacial acetic acid. The unreacted perchloric acid requires 20 mL of 0.180 M solution of sodium acetate.
(iv) Calculate the volume of the nitrogen released (in L) at a pressure of 102658 Pa and a temperature of 298.15°C when the same quantity of sample was used in method I and method II.		Volume of N2 released (valine)Ideal Gas Formulas You Can't Skip | Gaseous State | JEE & NEET 2027🟡 SimilarV(N2) = 0.180 L
Concept Method II (back-titration) fixes the amount of valine: HClO4 added minus the unreacted HClO4 found by sodium acetate. In Method I each valine releases one N2, whose volume follows the ideal-gas law. Note the temperature is in °C.
Key relation n(valine) = n(HClO4)added − n(HClO4)back; V = nRT/P Valine : HClO4 and valine : N2 are both 1:1.
Solution
1. HClO4 added = 0.150 M × 50.0 mL = 7.5 mmol.
2. Unreacted HClO4 = sodium acetate used = 0.180 M × 20 mL = 3.6 mmol.
3. Valine = 7.5 − 3.6 = 3.9 mmol, so n(N2) = 3.9×10−3 mol.
4. Temperature = 298.15 °C = 298.15 + 273.15 = 571.3 K; P = 102658 Pa.
5. V = nRT/P = (3.9×10−3 × 8.314 × 571.3)/102658.
6. Numerator = 18.52 J, so V = 18.52/102658 = 1.80×10−4 m³ = 0.180 L.
 n(valine) = 7.5 - 3.6 = 3.9 mmol = n(N2)
 T = 298.15 C = 571.3 K ,  P = 102658 Pa
 -----------------------------------------
 V = nRT/P
   = 3.9e-3 x 8.314 x 571.3 / 102658
   = 0.180 L                       <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The temperature is written °C, not K — converting (571 K, not 298 K) nearly doubles the volume. Always back-titrate to get the reacted amount: added minus leftover.
Answer: V(N2) = 0.180 L.
2 (v)Problem 2 · Acid Base chemistry.
C. A sample of an aromatic acid weighing 1.743 g requires 35 mL of 0.15 M NaOH for complete neutralisation. The vapour of the same acid is found to be 83 times heavier as compared to gaseous hydrogen.
(v) Calculate the basicity of the aromatic acid.		Basicity of aromatic acidClass 11 Chapter 01: Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry : Equivalent Weight and Gram Equivalent part 1🟡 SimilarBasicity = 2 (printed titre data inconsistent)
Concept Vapour density relative to H2 gives the molar mass (M = 2 × relative density). The basicity is the number of replaceable H+ per molecule, obtainable from the NaOH required per mole of acid. The molar mass pins the identity of the aromatic acid.
Key relation M = 2 × (vapour density); basicity = n(NaOH)/n(acid) “83 times heavier than H2” means M/2 = 83.
Solution
1. M = 2 × 83 = 166 g mol−1.
2. An aromatic acid of M = 166 is benzene-1,2-dicarboxylic acid, C6H4(COOH)2 (phthalic acid).
3. It carries two –COOH groups, so its basicity = 2.
4. n(acid) = 1.743/166 = 0.0105 mol; basicity 2 needs n(NaOH) = 0.021 mol = 140 mL of 0.15 M.
5. The printed titre, 35 mL × 0.15 M = 5.25 mmol, gives n(NaOH)/n(acid) = 0.5 — not a whole number and far below 2.
6. So the printed volume is a misprint (should be ~140 mL); the key deletes this sub-part. The chemically correct basicity from M = 166 is 2.
 M = 2 x 83 = 166  -> phthalic acid
 n(acid) = 1.743/166 = 0.0105 mol
 -----------------------------------------
 basicity 2 needs 0.021 mol NaOH (140 mL)
 printed 35 mL x 0.15 = 5.25 mmol -> 0.5 (bad)
 basicity = 2 (data misprinted)    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Let the molar mass name the compound: 166 is phthalic acid, diprotic, basicity 2. When titre data clash with that, trust the identity — here the printed NaOH volume is too small by a factor of 4.
Answer: Basicity = 2 (printed titration volume is inconsistent; the key omits this sub-part).
2 (vi)Problem 2 · Acid Base chemistry.
D. Maleic acid is a diprotic acid. Depending on pH, maleic acid can exist in solution in different forms. If the undissociated acid is represented as H2M, the different forms in which it can exist in the solution are H2M, HM−1 and M−2. Let, CT = Total concentration of acid in all the forms and the fractions (represented as αn) for different forms of maleic acid can be represented as α0 = [H2M]/CT, α1 = [HM−]/CT, α2 = [M−2]/CT. The following figure indicates variation of fractions of different forms of maleic acid as a function of pH.
(vi) Answer the following questions using the given figure.
(a) The pH at which 90% of [HM−] is converted to [M2−]
(b) The pKa1 and pKa2 of maleic acid are
(c) The indicator that can be used for the first equivalence point (Indicate the serial number of the indicator from the given table)		Maleic acid distribution diagramPolyprotic acids. Fractions at equilibrium, speciation.🟡 Similar(a) 7 or 7.1; (b) 1.7–1.9 and 6.1–6.3; (c) 3
Concept On an α-distribution diagram, two forms have equal fractions exactly at the relevant pKa. The HM−/M2− split follows Henderson about pKa2; the first equivalence point sits where HM− peaks, midway between the two pKa values.
Key relation pKa = pH at the α cross-over; pH = pKa2 + log([M2−]/[HM−]) First eq. point pH = (pKa1 + pKa2)/2.
Solution
1. (b) α0 and α1 cross near pH 1.8 → pKa1 ≈ 1.8; α1 and α2 cross near pH 6.2 → pKa2 ≈ 6.2.
2. (a) 90% of HM− to M2− means [M2−]/[HM−] = 90/10 = 9.
3. pH = pKa2 + log 9 = 6.2 + 2(0.48) = 6.2 + 0.96 = 7.1.
4. (c) The first equivalence point is where H2M is fully converted to HM− (peak of α1), pH = (1.8 + 6.2)/2 = 4.0.
5. An indicator changing colour around pH 4 is bromophenol blue (3.0–4.6) = entry No. 3.
6. All three readings match the graph and the printed answers.
 (b) pKa1 ~ 1.8 , pKa2 ~ 6.2  (alpha crossings)
 (a) [M2-]/[HM-] = 9
     pH = 6.2 + log9 = 6.2 + 0.96 = 7.1   <-- (a)
 (c) 1st eq pt pH = (1.8+6.2)/2 = 4.0
     -> bromophenol blue 3.0-4.6 = No. 3  <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Equal-α cross-overs are the pKa values; the mid-pH between them is the first equivalence point. Match that pH to the indicator’s colour-change band.
Answer: (a) ≈ 7.1; (b) pKa1 ≈ 1.8, pKa2 ≈ 6.2; (c) indicator No. 3.
