 1 mol O3, 50% diss:
   O3 left = 0.5  O2 = 0.75  total = 1.25
 x(O3) = 0.4   x(O2) = 0.6   (P = 1 atm)
 Kp = 0.6^3/0.4^2 = 0.216/0.16 = 1.35
 dG = -RT ln Kp = -8.3*300*0.3
    = -747 J/mol                  <-- 747
⚡ JEE Trick The supplied ln1.35=0.3 is a deliberate check: if your mole bookkeeping gives a Kp other than 1.35, revisit the 3:2 O2:O3 ratio. The stem asks for the magnitude after the minus sign, so the answer is 747.
Answer: 747 (ΔG° = −747 J mol−1).
344.0 moles of argon and 5.0 moles of PCl5 are introduced into an evacuated flask of 100 litre capacity at 610 K. The system is allowed to equilibrate. At equilibrium, the total pressure of mixture was found to be 6.0 atm. The Kp for the reaction is [Given : R = 0.082 L atm K−1 mol−1] (JEE Main 2022 (29 Jun Shift 2), Level-6)
(a) 2.25   (b) 6.24   (c) 12.13   (d) 15.24
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 29 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Kp of PCl5 with inert gas4.0 moles of argon and 5.0 moles of PCl5 are introduced into an evacuated flask🟢 Exact(a) 2.25
Concept Argon is an inert gas: it adds to the total moles and total pressure but never appears in Kp. Use the ideal-gas law with the measured total pressure to find the total moles at equilibrium, then subtract the known amounts to get how much PCl5 dissociated, and finally form Kp from the reacting species only.
Key relation ntotal = PtotalV/RT; PCl5⇄PCl3+Cl2, Kp = pPCl₃pCl₂/pPCl₅ Ar enters ntotal only, not Kp.
Solution
1. ntotal = PtotalV/RT = 6.0×100/(0.082×610) = 600/50.02 = 12 mol.
2. Let x mol PCl5 dissociate: ntotal = Ar + (5−x) + x + x = 9 + x.
3. 9 + x = 12 ⇒ x = 3 mol dissociated. one extra mole gas per PCl5 broken
4. Equilibrium moles: PCl5 = 2, PCl3 = 3, Cl2 = 3, Ar = 4 (total 12).
5. Partial P = mole fraction × 6: pPCl₅ = (2/12)6 = 1.0, pPCl₃ = (3/12)6 = 1.5, pCl₂ = 1.5 atm.
6. Kp = pPCl₃·pCl₂/pPCl₅ = 1.5×1.5/1.0 = 2.25, matching option (a).
 n_tot = PV/RT = 6*100/(0.082*610)
       = 600/50.02 = 12 mol
 12 = 4(Ar) + (5-x) + x + x -> x = 3
 pPCl5=1.0  pPCl3=1.5  pCl2=1.5 atm
 Kp = 1.5*1.5/1.0 = 2.25          (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Inert argon changes ntotal and Ptotal but is absent from Kp — feed it only into the gas-law step, never into the equilibrium expression. Getting x=3 from ntotal=12 is the pivot of the whole problem.
Answer: (a) 2.25.
3540% of HI undergoes decomposition to H2 and I2 at 300 K. ΔG⊖ for this decomposition reaction at one atmosphere pressure is ____ Jmol−1 -[nearest integer] (Use R = 8.31 JK−1 mol−1; log2 = 0.3010, ln10 = 2.3, log3 = 0.477) (JEE Main 2022 (26 Jun Shift 2), Level-5)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Standard free energy of HI decomposition40% of HI undergoes decomposition to H2 and I2 at 300 K. ΔG⊖ for this decomposition reaction🟢 Exact2735
Concept “40% of HI decomposes” is quoted per one mole of HI, so the reaction is written on the one-mole basis HI ⇌ (1/2)H₂ + (1/2)I₂, not as 2HI ⇌ H₂ + I₂. The basis matters: the second form has K equal to the square of the first, so its ΔG° would be exactly twice as large. Because the equilibrium constant comes out less than 1, ΔG° is positive, meaning decomposition is non-spontaneous under standard conditions.
Key relation ΔG° = −RT ln Kp, with Kp = (xH₂1/2·xI₂1/2)/xHI Δngas = 0 for this reaction, so Kp = Kx = Kc and total pressure drops out entirely.
Solution
1. Start with 1 mol HI. 40% reacts, so 0.4 mol HI is consumed on the 2HI scale, producing 0.2 mol H₂ and 0.2 mol I₂.
2. Equilibrium moles: HI = 0.6, H₂ = 0.2, I₂ = 0.2; total = 1.0 mol. The total is unchanged because the decomposition conserves the number of gas molecules.
3. With total = 1 mol, each mole fraction equals the mole number, and Δn = 0 lets Kp use them directly.
4. Kp = (0.21/2·0.21/2)/0.6 = 0.2/0.6 = 1/3.
5. ΔG° = −RT ln(1/3) = +RT ln 3 = 8.31×300×(ln10·log3) = 8.31×300×2.3×0.477.
6. ln 3 = 2.3 × 0.477 = 1.0971, and RT = 8.31 × 300 = 2493 J/mol.
7. ΔG° = 2493 × 1.0971 = 2735 J mol⁻¹.
 HI <-> 1/2 H2 + 1/2 I2   (per mole of HI)
 start: 1        0       0
 eq  : 0.6     0.2     0.2    total = 1.0
 Kp = (0.2^.5 * 0.2^.5)/0.6 = 0.2/0.6 = 1/3
 dG = -RT ln(1/3) = RT ln3
    = 8.31 * 300 * 2.3 * 0.477
    = 2493 * 1.0971
    = 2735 J/mol              <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Had you used 2HI ⇌ H₂ + I₂ (K = 1/9) you would get RT ln 9 = 5470 J, exactly double. The phrase “per mole of HI” fixes the one-mole half-reaction basis. Quick sanity check: K < 1 forces ΔG° > 0, so a negative sign anywhere would flag an error.
Answer: ΔG° ≈ 2735 J mol⁻¹.
3656.0 L of nitrogen gas is mixed with excess of hydrogen gas and it is found that 20 L of ammonia gas is produced, The volume of unused nitrogen gas is found to be ____ L. (JEE main-2022-25Jul-Shift-2, Level-5)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 25 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Volume of unused N256.0 L of nitrogen gas is mixed with excess of hydrogen gas and it is found that 20 L of ammonia gas🟢 Exact46
Concept For gases at the same temperature and pressure, volumes combine in exactly the same ratio as moles (Gay-Lussac's law of combining volumes, a consequence of Avogadro). So the full ideal-gas equation is never needed here; you just read the balanced coefficients as volume ratios. Hydrogen is in excess, so nitrogen is the species to track, and the question asks only how much of it survives.
Key relation N₂ + 3H₂ ⇌ 2NH₃ gives V(N₂ used) : V(NH₃) = 1 : 2 The stoichiometric mole ratio is also the volume ratio at fixed T, P.
Solution
1. Balanced equation: 1 volume N₂ + 3 volumes H₂ → 2 volumes NH₃.
2. Ammonia produced = 20 L, so nitrogen consumed = 20 × (1/2) = 10 L.
3. Hydrogen is explicitly in excess, so N₂ is the limiting-tracked reactant; its starting volume is 56.0 L.
4. Unused N₂ = initial − consumed = 56.0 − 10 = 46.0 L.
5. Cross-check the hydrogen: H₂ needed = 20 × (3/2) = 30 L, which is available because H₂ is in excess — fully consistent.
6. There is no change in temperature or pressure, so the volume bookkeeping needs no correction factor.
7. Rounded to the nearest integer, the unused nitrogen volume is 46 L.
 N2 + 3H2 <-> 2NH3   (volumes ~ moles)
 2 vol NH3  <-- 1 vol N2
 NH3 formed = 20 L
 N2 used = 20 * (1/2) = 10 L
 H2 used = 20 * (3/2) = 30 L  (excess OK)
 N2 left = 56.0 - 10 = 46 L     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Treat gas volumes like moles and the whole problem is one subtraction. The classic trap is subtracting 20 L (the NH₃ volume) directly from 56 L to get 36; you must subtract the N₂ that made it, which is only half of 20, i.e. 10 L.
Answer: 46 L of nitrogen remain unused.
37At 298 K, the equilibrium constant is 2×1015 for the reaction: Cu(s) + 2Ag+(aq) ⇄ Cu2+(aq) + 2Ag(s)
The equilibrium constant for the reaction 1⁄2Cu2+(aq) + Ag(s) ⇄ 1⁄2Cu(s) + Ag+(aq) is x×10−8.
The value of x is ____ . (Round off the answer to the nearest integer) (JEE Main 2022-26 Jul-Shift-1, Level-5)		Manipulating K (reverse, half)At 298 K, the equilibrium constant is 2×10^15 for the reaction Cu(s)+2Ag+ ⇌ Cu2+ +2Ag(s)🟢 Exact2
Concept Two rules govern how K transforms. Reversing a reaction inverts its K; multiplying every coefficient by a factor n raises K to the power n. The target here is the given reaction both reversed and scaled by (1/2), so the two operations compose into a single exponent of −(1/2) applied to the original K.
Key relation reverse → 1/K; scale by n → Kn, hence K′ = (2×1015)−1/2 Half of the reversed reaction means the exponent is −(1/2).
Solution
1. Given: Cu + 2Ag⁺ ⇌ Cu²⁺ + 2Ag, K = 2×1015.
2. Target: (1/2)Cu²⁺ + Ag ⇌ (1/2)Cu + Ag⁺ — the reverse of the given reaction with all coefficients halved.
3. Reverse first: Krev = 1/(2×1015) = 0.5×10−15.
4. Then halve every coefficient: K′ = (Krev)1/2 = (2×1015)−1/2.
5. Compute (2×1015)1/2 = √(20×1014) = √20 × 107 = 4.47×107.
6. K′ = 1/(4.47×107) = 0.224×10−7 = 2.24×10−8.
7. With K′ = x×10−8, x = 2.24, which rounds to 2.
 target = reverse(given), coeffs halved
 Krev = 1 / (2e15)
 K' = (2e15)^(-1/2)
    = 1 / sqrt(20e14)
    = 1 / (4.47e7)
    = 2.24e-8  ->  x = 2.24 ~ 2   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Fold the two operations into one exponent: reversing plus halving is simply K−1/2, done in a single step. The usual traps are forgetting the square root (which gives 5×10−16) or forgetting to reverse (which leaves a 107-order number instead of 10−8).
Answer: x = 2 (K′ = 2.24×10−8, rounded to nearest integer).
38PCl5 dissociates as PCl5(g) ⇄ PCl3(g) + Cl2(g) 5 moles of PCl5 are placed in a 200 litre vessel which contains 2 moles of N2 and is maintained at 600 K. The equilibrium pressure is 2.46 atm. The equilibrium constant Kp for the dissociation of PCl5 is ______ ×10−3. (nearest integer) (Given: R = 0.082 L atm K−1 mol−1; Assume ideal gas behaviour) (JEE Main 2022 (24 Jun Shift 2), Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 24 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Kp of PCl5 dissociationPCl5 dissociates as PCl5(g) ⇌ PCl3(g)+Cl2(g); 5 moles in 200 L vessel with 2 mol N2 at 600 K, Kp🟢 Exact1107
Concept The total moles at equilibrium come straight from PV = nRT; the inert N₂ counts toward that total but never appears in Kp. The number of moles beyond the 7 initially present (5 mol PCl₅ + 2 mol N₂) measures exactly how far PCl₅ has dissociated, since each PCl₅ that breaks adds one net mole of gas.
Key relation ntotal = PV/RT, then Kp = pPCl₃·pCl₂/pPCl₅ Each partial pressure equals (ni/ntotal)·P, with P the measured total pressure.
Solution
1. ntotal = PV/RT = (2.46×200)/(0.082×600) = 492/49.2 = 10 mol.
2. Let x mol PCl₅ dissociate. Moles: N₂ = 2, PCl₅ = (5−x), PCl₃ = x, Cl₂ = x; total = 7 + x.
3. 7 + x = 10 ⇒ x = 3. So PCl₅ = 2, PCl₃ = 3, Cl₂ = 3, N₂ = 2.
4. Partial pressures (P = 2.46): pPCl₃ = pCl₂ = (3/10)×2.46 = 0.738; pPCl₅ = (2/10)×2.46 = 0.492.
5. Kp = (pPCl₃·pCl₂)/pPCl₅ = (0.738×0.738)/0.492 = 0.5446/0.492.
6. Kp = 1.107 atm.
7. Written as (integer)×10−3: 1.107 = 1107×10−3, so the answer is 1107.
 n_tot = PV/RT = 492/49.2 = 10 mol
 7 + x = 10  ->  x = 3
 PCl5=2  PCl3=3  Cl2=3  N2=2  (tot 10)
 p(PCl3)=p(Cl2)=0.738 , p(PCl5)=0.492
 Kp = (0.738*0.738)/0.492
    = 1.107 atm = 1107e-3        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The inert N₂ only inflates ntotal; it lowers every mole fraction equally but is absent from Kp itself. Forgetting to include the 2 mol N₂ in ntotal is the usual slip and would wrongly hand you x = 5 (complete dissociation).
Answer: Kp ≈ 1107×10−3 atm.
39For a reaction at equilibrium A(g) ⇄ B(g) + 1⁄2C(g) the relation between dissociation constant (K), degree of dissociation (α) and equilibrium pressure (p) is given by : (JEE Main 2022 (24 Jun Shift 1), Level-2)
(a) K = α2p2 / [(2+α)1/2(1−α)]   (b) K = α2p2 / [(1+3⁄2α)1/2(1−α)]   (c) K = (αp)3/2 / [(1+3⁄2α)1/2(1−α)]   (d) K = (αp)3/2 / [(1+α)(1−α)1/2]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 24 Jun · MorningSolution ↗
K vs degree of dissociationA(g) ⇌ B(g) + C/2(g) The correct relationship between Kp, α and equilibrium pressure P is🟢 Exact(a) K = α^(3/2)·p^(1/2)/[(2+α)^(1/2)(1−α)]
Concept For A ⇌ B + (1/2)C the total moles grow because of the extra half-mole of C, giving ntotal = 1 + α/2 = (2+α)/2. Write each partial pressure from its mole fraction, then assemble Kp with the half-integer exponent on C. The (2+α) factor in the denominator is the fingerprint of this particular stoichiometry and is what distinguishes option (a) from the others.
Key relation Kp = pB·pC1/2/pA, with pi = (ni/ntotal)·p The exponents in Kp follow the coefficients 1 for B, 1/2 for C, and −1 for A.
Solution
1. Start 1 mol A. At degree α: A = 1−α, B = α, C = α/2; ntotal = 1 + α/2 = (2+α)/2. Call this T.
2. pA = (1−α)p/T, pB = αp/T, pC = (α/2)p/T.
3. Kp = [αp/T]·[(α/2)p/T]1/2 ÷ [(1−α)p/T].
4. Collect the pressure powers: p·p1/2/p = p1/2.
5. Collect α: α·(α/2)1/2 = α3/2/√2, and (1−α) stays in the denominator.
6. The T factors combine to T−1/2 = √2/(2+α)1/2, whose √2 cancels the √2 from step 5.
7. Kp = α3/2p1/2/[(2+α)1/2(1−α)] — exactly option (a).
 A <-> B + 1/2 C   start 1
 eq: 1-a , a , a/2   n_tot = (2+a)/2 = T
 Kp = pB * pC^.5 / pA
 p-part  = p^(1/2)
 a-part  = a^(3/2)/sqrt2
 T-part  = sqrt2/(2+a)^(1/2)
 Kp = a^(3/2) p^(1/2)/[(2+a)^.5 (1-a)]  (a)
⚡ JEE Trick The power of p is Σ(product coeffs) − Σ(reactant coeffs) = (1 + 1/2) − 1 = 1/2, so p1/2 alone eliminates every option carrying p3/2. Among what remains, only (a) has the correct (2+α) denominator rather than (1 + (3/2)α).
Answer: (a) K = α3/2p1/2/[(2+α)1/2(1−α)].
40The standard free energy change ΔG° for 50% dissociation of N2O4 into NO2 at 27°C and 1 atm pressure is −x J mol−1. The value of x is −.... J. (Nearest Integer) [Given : R = 8.31 J K−1 mol−1, log1.33 = 0.1239 ln10 = 2.3] (JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 1), Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 25 Jun · MorningSolution ↗
Standard free energy of N2O4 dissociationThe standard free energy change (∆G°) for 50% dissociation of N2O4 into NO2 at 27°C and 1 atm🟢 Exact710
Concept N₂O₄ ⇌ 2NO₂ at 50% dissociation under 1 atm gives Kp > 1, so ΔG° is negative. The stem writes ΔG° = −x, so x is the positive magnitude of a negative free-energy change. Use the standard result Kp = 4α²p/(1−α²) for an A ⇌ 2B dissociation, where total moles are 1 + α. The whole task is one Kp evaluation followed by one logarithm, with the given log and ln values doing the arithmetic.
Key relation Kp = 4α²p/(1−α²) and ΔG° = −RT ln Kp The 4α² comes from NO₂ appearing squared; the (1−α²) from (1−α)(1+α) in the mole fractions, since the total is (1+α) mol.
Solution
1. α = 0.5 (50% dissociation), p = 1 atm, T = 27°C = 300 K.
2. Moles at equilibrium per 1 mol N₂O₄: N₂O₄ = 1−α = 0.5, NO₂ = 2α = 1.0; total = 1+α = 1.5.
3. Kp = 4(0.5)²(1)/(1−0.5²) = 4×0.25/(1−0.25) = 1.0/0.75 = 1.33.
4. ΔG° = −RT ln Kp = −RT ln(1.33).
5. ln(1.33) = log(1.33)·ln10 = 0.1239×2.3 = 0.2850.
6. RT = 8.31 × 300 = 2493 J/mol, so ΔG° = −2493×0.2850 = −710.5 J mol⁻¹.
7. Since ΔG° = −x, x = 710 to the nearest integer.
 N2O4 <-> 2NO2   a=0.5 , p=1 atm
 Kp = 4a^2 p/(1-a^2) = 1/0.75 = 1.33
 ln1.33 = 0.1239 * 2.3 = 0.2850
 RT = 8.31*300 = 2493
 dG = -2493 * 0.2850
    = -710 J/mol  ->  x = 710       <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Kp > 1 immediately signals ΔG° < 0, which matches the “−x” form; a positive result would be wrong by sign. Memorising Kp = 4α²p/(1−α²) for the A ⇌ 2B type saves rebuilding the whole ICE table each time. Note the convenient coincidence 1/0.75 = 1.33, which is exactly the number the given log 1.33 was planted to evaluate.
Answer: x = 710 (ΔG° ≈ −710 J mol⁻¹).
41At 600 K, 2 mol of NO are mixed with 1 mol of O2.
2NO(g) + O2(g) ⇄ 2NO2(g)
The reaction occurring as above comes to equilibrium under a total pressure of 1 atm. Analysis of the system shows that 0.6 mol of oxygen are present at equilibrium. The equilibrium constant for the reaction is ______ . (JEE Main 2022-28 July-Shift-2, Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 28 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Kp of NO oxidationAt 600 K, 2 mol of NO are mixed with 1 mol of O2; 2NO+O2⇌2NO2, equilibrium constant🟢 Exact2
Concept The amount of O₂ remaining pins down how much reaction has occurred; stoichiometry then delivers every equilibrium mole. With the total pressure fixed at 1 atm, each partial pressure equals its mole fraction (times 1 atm), so Kp follows directly from the mole table without any extra constant.
Key relation 2NO + O₂ ⇌ 2NO₂, Kp = pNO₂²/(pNO²·pO₂) pi = (ni/ntotal)·P with P = 1 atm; Δn = −1 so one ntotal factor survives.
Solution
1. Start: NO = 2 mol, O₂ = 1 mol. At equilibrium O₂ = 0.6 mol, so O₂ consumed = 0.4 mol.
2. By stoichiometry (2NO and 2NO₂ per O₂): NO consumed = 2×0.4 = 0.8; NO₂ formed = 0.8.
3. Equilibrium moles: NO = 1.2, O₂ = 0.6, NO₂ = 0.8; total = 2.6 mol.
4. Partial pressures (P = 1 atm): pNO = 1.2/2.6, pO₂ = 0.6/2.6, pNO₂ = 0.8/2.6.
5. Kp = (0.8/2.6)²/[(1.2/2.6)²(0.6/2.6)]; the 2.6² in numerator and denominator cancel, leaving one factor of 2.6 up top.
6. Kp = (0.8²×2.6)/(1.2²×0.6) = (0.64×2.6)/(1.44×0.6).
7. = 1.664/0.864 = 1.93 ≈ 2.
 2NO + O2 <-> 2NO2   start 2 , 1 , 0
 O2 used = 1 - 0.6 = 0.4
 eq: NO=1.2  O2=0.6  NO2=0.8  tot=2.6
 Kp = (0.8^2 * 2.6)/(1.2^2 * 0.6)
    = 1.664/0.864 = 1.93 ~ 2        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Because Δn = −1, the ntotal = 2.6 does not fully cancel — exactly one power of (ntotal/P) stays in the numerator, which is why 2.6 appears there. At P = 1 atm it is harmless, but dropping it would wrongly give Kp ≈ 0.74.
Answer: Kp ≈ 2.
42Consider the reaction A ⇄ B at 1000 K. At time t' the temperature of the system was increased to 2000 K and the system was allowed to reach equilibrium. Throughout this experiment the partial pressure of A was maintained at 1 bar. Given below is the plot of the partial pressure of B with time. What is the ratio of the standard Gibbs energy of the reaction at 1000 K to that at 2000 K ? (JEE Adv. 2020 (Paper-1), Level-6)
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2020 · Paper 1Solution ↗
Ratio of standard Gibbs energy at two TConsider the reaction A⇌B at 1000 K. At time t', the temperature was increased to 2000 K (JEE Adv 2020)🟢 Exact0.25
Concept Because pA is held at 1 bar throughout, K = pB/pA equals pB numerically at each temperature. Read the two plateau values of pB from the plot, build ΔG° = −RT ln K at each T, and take the ratio; the gas constant and most of the logarithm cancel, leaving a clean fraction.
Key relation ΔG° = −RT ln K, with K = pB/pA pA = 1 bar, so K is simply the plateau value of pB in bar.
Solution
1. Before t′ the system is at 1000 K and the curve plateaus at pB = 10 bar, so K1000 = 10/1 = 10.
2. After t′ the system is at 2000 K and plateaus at pB = 100 bar, so K2000 = 100/1 = 100. The brief spike to 115 is transient overshoot, not the equilibrium value.
3. ΔG°1000 = −R(1000) ln 10.
4. ΔG°2000 = −R(2000) ln 100 = −R(2000)(2 ln 10).
5. Ratio = ΔG°1000/ΔG°2000 = [R·1000·ln10]/[R·2000·2 ln10].
6. R and ln 10 cancel top and bottom.
7. Ratio = 1000/(2000×2) = 1000/4000 = 0.25.
 K = pB/pA ,  pA = 1 bar
 1000 K: pB=10  -> K1 = 10
 2000 K: pB=100 -> K2 = 100
 dG1 = -R(1000) ln10
 dG2 = -R(2000) ln100 = -R(2000)(2 ln10)
 dG1/dG2 = 1000/(2000*2) = 0.25     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick ln 100 = 2 ln 10, so the logs cancel and only the temperature ratio (1000/2000) times the log ratio (1/2) survives: (1/2)×(1/2) = 0.25. The trap is reading the 115 spike as the second equilibrium value instead of the 100 plateau.
Answer: ΔG°1000/ΔG°2000 = 0.25.

Ionic Equilibrium · Previous Year Questions

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1Consider two Group IV metal ions X2+ and Y2+.
A solution containing 0.01 M X2+ and 0.01 M Y2+ is saturated with H2S. The pH at which the metal sulphide YS will form as a precipitate is _______ (Nearest integer)
(Given : Ksp(XS) = 1×10−22 at 25°C, Ksp(YS) = 4×10−16 at 25°C, [H2S] = 0.1M in solution, Ka1 × Ka2 (H2S) = 1.0×10−21, log 2 = 0.30, log 3 = 0.48, log 5 = 0.70) (JEE Main 2026-24 Jan-Shift-1, Level-7)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 24 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Selective sulphide precipitation, KspConsider two Group IV metal ions X2+ and Y2+. A solution containing 0.01M X2+ and 0.01M Y2+ saturated H2S🟢 Exact4
Concept In a solution kept saturated with H₂S, the sulphide-ion concentration is fixed entirely by pH through [S²⁻] = Ka1Ka2[H₂S]/[H⁺]². The metal sulphide YS just begins to precipitate when the ionic product [Y²⁺][S²⁻] first equals its Ksp. Finding that threshold [S²⁻] and back-solving for [H⁺] gives the minimum pH.
Key relation [S²⁻] = Ka1Ka2[H₂S]/[H⁺]², precipitation when [Y²⁺][S²⁻] = Ksp Lower [H⁺] (higher pH) raises [S²⁻], so there is a minimum pH that triggers YS.
Solution
1. [S²⁻] required to start YS = Ksp(YS)/[Y²⁺] = 4×10⁻¹⁶/0.01 = 4×10⁻¹⁴ M.
2. Express [S²⁻] via pH: [S²⁻] = Ka1Ka2[H₂S]/[H⁺]² = (10⁻²¹×0.1)/[H⁺]² = 10⁻²²/[H⁺]².
3. Set the two equal: 10⁻²²/[H⁺]² = 4×10⁻¹⁴.
4. [H⁺]² = 10⁻²²/(4×10⁻¹⁴) = 0.25×10⁻⁸ = 2.5×10⁻⁹.
5. [H⁺] = √(2.5×10⁻⁹) = √(25×10⁻¹⁰) = 5×10⁻⁵ M.
6. pH = −log(5×10⁻⁵) = 5 − log 5 = 5 − 0.70 = 4.30.
7. Nearest integer: pH = 4.
 [S2-] needed = Ksp/[Y2+] = 4e-16/0.01 = 4e-14
 [S2-] = Ka1Ka2[H2S]/[H+]^2 = 1e-22/[H+]^2
 1e-22/[H+]^2 = 4e-14
 [H+]^2 = 2.5e-9 -> [H+] = 5e-5
 pH = 5 - log5 = 5 - 0.70 = 4.3 ~ 4   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Only the product Ka1Ka2 matters, because [S²⁻] depends on [H⁺]² — the individual K values are never needed. The much smaller Ksp(XS) = 10⁻²² means XS would start precipitating at a far lower pH (around pH 1), which is exactly why acidifying lets you drop XS while keeping YS in solution — the basis of qualitative group-IV cation separation. Watch the square root in step 5: [H⁺]² = 2.5×10⁻⁹ must be rewritten as 25×10⁻¹⁰ so the root is clean.
Answer: pH ≈ 4.
2Consider a weak base 'B' of pKb = 5.699. 'x' mL of 0.02 M HCl and 'y' mL of 0.02 M weak base 'B' are mixed to make 100 mL of a buffer of pH 9 at 25°C. The values of 'x' and 'y' respectively are:
[Given: log 2 = 0.3010, log 3 = 0.4771, log 5 = 0.699] (JEE Main 2026-28 Jan-Shift-1, Level-6)
(a) x = 11.1, y = 88.9   (b) x = 42.7, y = 57.3   (c) x = 14.3, y = 85.7   (d) x = 85.7, y = 14.3
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 28 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Weak base buffer, HCl titrationConsider a weak base B of pKb=5.699. x mL of 0.02 M HCl and y mL of 0.02 M weak base B mixed to 100 mL buffer pH 9🟢 Exact(c) x = 14.3, y = 85.7
Concept Mixing a weak base B with strong acid HCl produces a basic buffer: the HCl converts part of B into its conjugate acid BH⁺, and the leftover B plus that BH⁺ form the buffer. A target pH of 9 means pOH = 5, and the Henderson–Hasselbalch equation for bases fixes the ratio of salt to base; the fixed total volume supplies the second equation.
Key relation pOH = pKb + log([BH⁺]/[B]), with [BH⁺] ∝ x, [B] ∝ (y−x) x mL of HCl makes x-equivalents of BH⁺ and leaves (y−x)-equivalents of free B (equal 0.02 M cancels).
Solution
1. pH = 9 ⇒ pOH = 14 − 9 = 5, and pKb = 5.699.
2. Millimoles (×0.02): HCl = 0.02x forms BH⁺ = 0.02x; base remaining = 0.02(y−x).
3. 5 = 5.699 + log[x/(y−x)] ⇒ log[x/(y−x)] = −0.699.
4. Since 0.699 = log 5, this gives x/(y−x) = 1/5.
5. 5x = y − x ⇒ y = 6x.
6. Total volume: x + y = 100 ⇒ x + 6x = 100 ⇒ 7x = 100 ⇒ x = 14.3 mL.
7. y = 100 − 14.3 = 85.7 mL — matching option (c).
 pOH = pKb + log([BH+]/[B])
 5 = 5.699 + log(x/(y-x))
 log(x/(y-x)) = -0.699 = -log5
 x/(y-x) = 1/5  ->  y = 6x
 x + y = 100  ->  7x = 100
 x = 14.3 , y = 85.7              <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick HCl must be the smaller volume: it only has to convert a fraction of B into BH⁺, leaving excess base for the buffer, so x < y immediately kills (d) and (b). The tidy 1/5 ratio is simply 10−0.699, and the deliberately quoted log 5 = 0.699 is the clue the data planted. A quick validity check: with x = 14.3 the buffer holds BH⁺ = 0.286 mmol and free B = 1.43 mmol, both present in reasonable amounts, confirming a genuine buffer rather than past the equivalence point.
Answer: (c) x = 14.3 mL, y = 85.7 mL.
3Consider the dissociation equilibrium of the following weak acid HA ⇄ H+(aq) + A−(aq).
If the pKa of the acid is 4, then the pH of 10 mM. HA solution is _______. (Nearest integer)
[Given : The degree of dissociation can be neglected with respect to unity] (JEE Main 2026-28 Jan-Shift-1, Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2026 · 28 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
pH of weak acid from pKaConsider the dissociation equilibrium of weak acid HA⇌H+(aq)+A-(aq); pKa 4, pH of 10 mM HA🟢 Exact3
Concept For a weak acid whose dissociation is negligible against unity, Ostwald's dilution law gives [H⁺] = √(KaC). Here both the Ka (from pKa = 4) and the concentration are exact powers of ten, so the pH comes out as a whole number without any log-table work. The only real pitfall is the unit: 10 mM is a millimolar figure that must be converted to molarity before substituting.
Key relation [H⁺] = √(Ka·C), equivalently pH = (1/2)(pKa − log C) Valid because α ≪ 1, which the question explicitly permits.
Solution
1. pKa = 4 ⇒ Ka = 10⁻⁴.
2. C = 10 mM = 0.01 M = 10⁻² M. Milli means 10⁻³, so 10 mM is 10×10⁻³ = 10⁻² M.
3. [H⁺] = √(KaC) = √(10⁻⁴×10⁻²) = √(10⁻⁶).
4. [H⁺] = 10⁻³ M.
5. pH = −log(10⁻³) = 3.
6. Cross-check with the formula: pH = (1/2)(pKa − log C) = (1/2)(4 − (−2)) = (1/2)(6) = 3.
7. Verify the approximation: α = [H⁺]/C = 10⁻³/10⁻² = 0.1, small enough for Ostwald's law.
 Ka = 10^-4 , C = 10 mM = 10^-2 M
 [H+] = sqrt(Ka C) = sqrt(10^-6)
      = 10^-3 M
 pH = -log(10^-3) = 3            <-- answer
 check: alpha = 10^-3/10^-2 = 0.1 (small)
⚡ JEE Trick pH = (1/2)(pKa − log C) collapses the whole calculation to a single line. Watch the milli-prefix: 10 mM is 10⁻² M, not 10⁻³ M — misreading it would give pH 3.5 and the wrong nearest integer. Also resist the urge to invoke the Kw correction used for ultra-weak acids: here KaC = 10⁻⁶ dwarfs Kw = 10⁻¹⁴ by eight orders of magnitude, so water's contribution is utterly negligible and the plain Ostwald formula is exact.
Answer: pH = 3.
4At 25°C, the concentration of H+ ions in 1.00×10−3 M aqueous solution of a weak monobasic acid having acid dissociation constant (Ka) of 4.00×10−11 is X×10−7 M. The value of X is _____.
Use: ionic product of water (Kw) = 1.00×10−14 at 25°C (JEE Adv. 2025 (Paper-1), Level-5)
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2025 · Paper 1Solution ↗
[H+] of weak monobasic acidJEE Advanced 2025 Paper-1 Ionic Equilibrium: At 25°C, concentration of H+ in 1.00×10^-3 M weak monobasic acid Ka 4.00×10^-11🟢 Exact2.24
Concept Ka is extremely small (4×10⁻¹¹), so the acid is so weak that water's own self-ionisation (Kw) supplies a comparable share of the hydrogen ions. The usual [H⁺] = √(KaC) underestimates the result and must be upgraded to include the Kw term.
Key relation [H⁺]² = KaC + Kw From the charge balance [H⁺] = [A⁻] + [OH⁻], with [A⁻] ≈ KaC/[H⁺] (acid barely dissociated) and [OH⁻] = Kw/[H⁺].
Solution
1. Naive estimate: √(KaC) = √(4×10⁻¹¹×10⁻³) = √(4×10⁻¹⁴) = 2×10⁻⁷ M — right at the √Kw scale, so water cannot be ignored.
2. Charge balance: [H⁺] = [A⁻] + [OH⁻].
3. Because the acid barely dissociates, [HA] ≈ C, so [A⁻] = KaC/[H⁺]; also [OH⁻] = Kw/[H⁺].
4. Multiply through by [H⁺]: [H⁺]² = KaC + Kw.
5. KaC = 4×10⁻¹⁴ and Kw = 1×10⁻¹⁴.
6. [H⁺]² = 4×10⁻¹⁴ + 1×10⁻¹⁴ = 5×10⁻¹⁴.
7. [H⁺] = √(5×10⁻¹⁴) = √5×10⁻⁷ = 2.236×10⁻⁷ M, so X = 2.24.
 Ka C = 4e-11 * 1e-3 = 4e-14
 Kw = 1e-14  (comparable -> keep it)
 [H+]^2 = KaC + Kw = 4e-14 + 1e-14
        = 5e-14
 [H+] = sqrt(5) e-7 = 2.24e-7      <-- X=2.24
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever √(KaC) lands within roughly a decade of 10⁻⁷ M, add Kw under the root. Ignoring it here would give X = 2.00; the water contribution is precisely what nudges the answer up to 2.24. The tell is that Ka is far below the usual 10⁻⁵-ish range of ordinary weak acids, so the acid can barely out-ionise water and the √5 factor (≈ 2.236) is the signature of the combined KaC + Kw = 5×10⁻¹⁴.
Answer: X = 2.24 ([H⁺] = 2.24×10⁻⁷ M).
5Arrange the following in increasing order of solubility product : Ca(OH)2, AgBr, PbS, HgS (JEE Main 2025 (28 Jan. Shift 2), Level-4)
(a) HgS < AgBr < PbS < Ca(OH)2   (b) Ca(OH)2 < AgBr < HgS < PbS   (c) PbS < HgS < Ca(OH)2 < AgBr   (d) HgS < PbS < AgBr < Ca(OH)2
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 28 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Order of solubility productsArrange the following in increasing order of solubility product: Ca(OH)2, AgBr, PbS, HgS🟢 Exact(d) HgS < PbS < AgBr < Ca(OH)₂
Concept A larger solubility product means a more soluble salt, so arranging in increasing Ksp is the same as arranging from least to most soluble. Heavy-metal sulphides (HgS, PbS) are the most insoluble substances in common tables; the silver halide AgBr is intermediate; and a group-2 hydroxide such as Ca(OH)₂ is comparatively quite soluble. Only the orders of magnitude are needed.
Key relation smaller Ksp → less soluble Approximate Ksp: HgS ≈ 10⁻⁵², PbS ≈ 10⁻²⁸, AgBr ≈ 5×10⁻¹³, Ca(OH)₂ ≈ 5×10⁻⁶.
Solution
1. HgS is famously the least soluble common salt, Ksp ≈ 10⁻⁵² — the smallest value here.
2. PbS comes next, Ksp ≈ 10⁻²⁸; still a heavy-metal sulphide but many orders above HgS.
3. AgBr, Ksp ≈ 5×10⁻¹³, is a sparingly soluble silver halide — larger than both sulphides.
4. Ca(OH)₂, Ksp ≈ 5×10⁻⁶, is only slightly soluble yet by far the largest of the four.
5. Order the four exponents: −52 < −28 < −12.3 < −5.3.
6. Increasing Ksp: HgS < PbS < AgBr < Ca(OH)₂.
7. This is option (d).
 Ksp (approx):
 HgS      ~ 1e-52   (smallest)
 PbS      ~ 1e-28
 AgBr     ~ 5e-13
 Ca(OH)2  ~ 5e-6    (largest)
 order: HgS < PbS < AgBr < Ca(OH)2   (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Rank by cation softness: soft Hg²⁺ with soft S²⁻ gives the tiniest Ksp, while hard Ca²⁺ with OH⁻ gives the largest. You almost never need the exact figures — just the correct ladder of magnitudes, which places the two sulphides lowest.
Answer: (d) HgS < PbS < AgBr < Ca(OH)₂.
6The equilibrium Cr2O72− ⇄ 2CrO42− is shifted to the right in : (JEE Main 2025 (8 April Shift 2), Level-4)
(a) an acidic medium   (b) a basic medium   (c) a neutral medium   (d) a weakly acidic medium		Dichromate-chromate equilibriumThe equilibrium Cr2O7 2- ⇌ 2CrO4 2- is shifted to the right in (acidic/basic medium)🟢 Exact(b) a basic medium
Concept The dichromate–chromate interconversion is entirely pH-controlled. Orange Cr₂O₇²⁻ and yellow CrO₄²⁻ are joined by an equilibrium that releases H⁺ when dichromate converts to chromate. Adding base (OH⁻) removes that H⁺ and pulls the equilibrium toward chromate; adding acid does the reverse.
Key relation Cr₂O₇²⁻ + H₂O ⇌ 2CrO₄²⁻ + 2H⁺ By Le Chatelier, removing the product H⁺ with base shifts the equilibrium to the right.
Solution
1. The complete aqueous equilibrium is Cr₂O₇²⁻ + H₂O ⇌ 2CrO₄²⁻ + 2H⁺.
2. The stem's shorthand Cr₂O₇²⁻ ⇌ 2CrO₄²⁻ is this same reaction with H⁺ implied on the product side.
3. A basic medium supplies OH⁻, which neutralises the H⁺ being produced.
4. Removing a product (H⁺) shifts the equilibrium to the right, generating more CrO₄²⁻.
5. An acidic medium adds H⁺, pushing the equilibrium left toward Cr₂O₇²⁻ — so (a) is wrong.
6. A weakly acidic medium does the same thing less strongly, so (d) is also wrong; a neutral medium (c) gives no net shift.
7. Only a basic medium drives it right, so the answer is (b).
 Cr2O7^2- + H2O <-> 2CrO4^2- + 2H+
 add OH- (base): H+ removed
   -> shifts RIGHT -> more CrO4^2-
 add H+ (acid): shifts LEFT -> Cr2O7^2-
 answer: basic medium               (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Colour cue: orange dichromate rules in acid, yellow chromate in base. “Shifted right” here means forming more chromate, which demands a basic medium. Any option that supplies H⁺ — acidic or weakly acidic — must be wrong by inspection, and “neutral” gives no driving force at all. One trap is reading the arrow direction loosely: it is the removal of the product H⁺, not the addition of OH⁻ as a reactant, that formally pulls the equilibrium toward chromate.
Answer: (b) a basic medium.
7The molar solubility(s) of zirconium phosphate with molecular formula (Zr4+)3(PO43−)4 is given by relation (JEE Main 2025 (22 Jan. Shift 2), Level-3)
(a) (Ksp/9612)1/3   (b) (Ksp/6912)1/7   (c) (Ksp/8435)1/7   (d) (Ksp/5348)1/6
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 22 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Molar solubility expressionThe molar solubility(s) of zirconium phosphate (22 Jan 2025 Shift-2 JEE Main PYQ)🟢 Exact(b) (K_sp/6912)^(1/7)
Concept For an ionic solid AmBn of molar solubility s, each dissolved formula unit releases m cations and n anions, so the ions appear at ms and ns and Ksp = (ms)m(ns)n = mmnn·s(m+n). Zirconium phosphate is (Zr⁴⁺)₃(PO₄³⁻)₄, giving m = 3 cations and n = 4 anions per formula unit, so the total ion count is 7 and the solubility sits under a seventh root. Note the 4+ and 3− charges are balanced by the 3:4 ratio, confirming the formula.
Key relation Ksp = (3s)³(4s)⁴ = 3³·4⁴·s⁷ Three Zr⁴⁺ at 3s and four PO₄³⁻ at 4s per dissolved formula unit.
Solution
1. Dissolution: Zr₃(PO₄)₄ → 3Zr⁴⁺ + 4PO₄³⁻.
2. If the molar solubility is s, then [Zr⁴⁺] = 3s and [PO₄³⁻] = 4s.
3. Ksp = [Zr⁴⁺]³[PO₄³⁻]⁴ = (3s)³(4s)⁴.
4. (3s)³ = 27s³ and (4s)⁴ = 256s⁴.
5. Ksp = 27s³ × 256s⁴ = (27×256) s⁷ = 6912 s⁷.
6. s⁷ = Ksp/6912.
7. s = (Ksp/6912)1/7 — option (b).
 Zr3(PO4)4 -> 3 Zr4+  +  4 PO4^3-
 [Zr4+]=3s   [PO4^3-]=4s
 Ksp = (3s)^3 (4s)^4
     = 27 s^3 * 256 s^4
     = 6912 s^7
 s = (Ksp/6912)^(1/7)              <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick The exponent on s is always (number of cations + anions) = 3 + 4 = 7, so the root is a seventh root — that alone eliminates the 1/3 and 1/6 options and leaves only (b) and (c). Between those, compute the prefactor mmnn = 3³×4⁴ = 27×256 = 6912, not the look-alike 8435 distractor in (c). A handy shortcut: the coefficient is always mmnn, so you can read it straight off the subscripts without expanding the product.
Answer: (b) s = (Ksp/6912)1/7.
8Which of the following happens when NH4OH is added gradually to the solution containing 1M A2+ and 1M B3+ ions? Given : Ksp A(OH)2 = 9×10−10 and Ksp B(OH)3 = 27×10−18 at 298 K. (JEE Main 2025 (23 Jan. Shift 1), Level-3)
(a) Both A(OH)2 and B(OH)3 do not show   (b) A(OH)2 will precipitate before B(OH)3 precipitation with NH4OH   (c) B(OH)3 will precipitate before A(OH)2   (d) A(OH)2 and B(OH)3 will precipitate together
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 23 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Selective hydroxide precipitationWhich of the following happens when NH4OH is added gradually to the solution containing 1M A2+ and 1🟢 Exact(c) B(OH)₃ precipitates before A(OH)₂
Concept NH4OH feeds OH- into the solution slowly. A hydroxide starts to precipitate the moment its ionic product reaches its Ksp. The ion that needs the smaller [OH-] to hit its Ksp precipitates first. The trap: you cannot compare the two Ksp values directly, because the power on [OH-] differs (square for A, cube for B).
Key relation for M(OH)n, [OH-]needed = (Ksp/[Mn+])1/n precipitation begins when [Mn+][OH-]n = Ksp.
Solution
1. For A(OH)2: [OH-] = √(Ksp/[A2+]) = √(9×10-10/1) = 3×10-5 M.
2. For B(OH)3: [OH-] = (Ksp/[B3+])1/3 = (27×10-18/1)1/3 = 3×10-6 M.
3. Compare the two thresholds: B(OH)3 needs only 3×10-6 M, ten times less than the 3×10-5 M that A(OH)2 demands. the cube root pulls the number down hard.
4. As NH4OH is added, [OH-] climbs from near zero, so the lower threshold is crossed first.
5. Hence B(OH)3 precipitates before A(OH)2 — option (c).
 A(OH)2: [OH-] = sqrt(9e-10 / 1)    = 3e-5 M
 B(OH)3: [OH-] = (27e-18 / 1)^(1/3) = 3e-6 M
 -------------------------------------------
 3e-6 < 3e-5  ->  B(OH)3 goes down first
⚡ JEE Trick Do not assume the "smaller Ksp" salt precipitates first when the cation charges differ — convert each Ksp into the [OH-] it needs and compare those. Here B3+ wins because the cube root of 27×10-18 is tiny.
Answer: (c) B(OH)3 will precipitate before A(OH)2.
9If 1 mM solution of ethylamine produces pH = 9, then the ionization constant (Kb) of ethylamine is 10−x. The value of x is _____ (nearest integer). [The degree of ionization of ethylamine can be neglected with respect to unity.] (JEE Main 2025 (23 Jan. Shift 1), Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 23 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Kb of weak base from pHIf 1 mM solution of ethylamine produces pH = 9,then the ionization constant (Kb)of ethylamine is10-x🟢 Exact7
Concept Ethylamine is a weak base. From the pH we get [OH-], and because its degree of ionization is neglected the equilibrium concentration of the base equals its formal concentration C. Ostwald's dilution law then hands Kb directly. Trap: use pOH (not pH) to get [OH-] for a base.
Key relation Kb = [BH+][OH-]/[B] ≈ [OH-]2/C [BH+] = [OH-] and [B] ≈ C when α ≪ 1.
Solution
1. pH = 9, so pOH = 14 − 9 = 5 and [OH-] = 10-5 M (Kw = 10-14 at 25 °C).
2. Formal concentration C = 1 mM = 10-3 M.
3. Each ionized base gives one BH+ and one OH-, so [BH+] = [OH-] = 10-5 M.
4. Ionization is neglected, so [B] ≈ C = 10-3 M.
5. Kb = [OH-]2/C = (10-5)2/10-3 = 10-10/10-3 = 10-7.
6. Matching 10-x = 10-7 gives x = 7. α = [OH-]/C = 10-2 = 1 %, so α ≪ 1 holds.
 pOH = 14 - 9 = 5     ->  [OH-] = 1e-5 M
 C   = 1 mM = 1e-3 M
 Kb  = (1e-5)^2 / 1e-3 = 1e-10/1e-3 = 1e-7
 ------------------------------------------
 10^-x = 10^-7   ->   x = 7
⚡ JEE Trick For a weak base read [OH-] from pOH first; plugging pH straight in is the classic slip. The one-liner Kb = [OH-]2/C works only while ionization is small, which the question grants explicitly.
Answer: x = 7.
10Ksp for Cr(OH)3 is 1.6×10−30. What is the molar solubility of this salt in water? (JEE Main 2025 (24 Jan. Shift 1), Level-2)
(a) (1.8×10−30)/27   (b) 5√(1.8×10−30)   (c) 4√((1.6×10−30)/27)   (d) 2√(1.6×10−30)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 24 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Molar solubility of Cr(OH)3JEE Mains-PYQ-2025 Ksp for Cr(OH)3 is 1.6x10-30. What is the molar solubility of this salt in water?🟢 Exact(c) ⁴√((1.6×10⁻³⁰)/27)
Concept For a salt M(OH)3 the stoichiometry is one cation to three hydroxide, so the Ksp expression carries a factor of 27 and a fourth power of the solubility. The answer must be the fourth root of Ksp/27, not any lower root. Trap: forgetting the 33 = 27 coefficient.
Key relation Ksp = [Cr3+][OH-]3 = s(3s)3 = 27 s4 in pure water each formula unit gives s of Cr3+ and 3s of OH-.
Solution
1. Dissolution: Cr(OH)3 ⇌ Cr3+ + 3 OH-, with [Cr3+] = s and [OH-] = 3s.
2. Ksp = s·(3s)3 = s·27s3 = 27 s4.
3. Solve for s: s4 = Ksp/27, so s = (Ksp/27)1/4.
4. Substitute: s = ((1.6×10-30)/27)1/4 = 4√((1.6×10-30)/27). this is option (c).
5. Reject (b): a fifth root with 1.8×10-30 implies four hydroxides, not three.
6. Reject (d): a square root fits a 1:1 salt like MX, not M(OH)3.
 Cr(OH)3 -> Cr3+ + 3 OH-     [s] , [3s]
 Ksp = s*(3s)^3 = 27 s^4
 s^4 = Ksp/27
 ------------------------------------------
 s = (1.6e-30 / 27)^(1/4)          (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For M(OH)n the pattern is Ksp = nn s(n+1); here n = 3 gives 27 s4, so the root is a fourth root over Ksp/27. Spot the coefficient and the root order in one glance to kill the distractors.
Answer: (c) 4√((1.6×10-30)/27).
11A weak acid HA has degree of dissociation x. Which option gives the correct expression of (pH − pKa)? (JEE Main 2025 (28 Jan. Shift 1), Level-2)
(a) 0   (b) log(1+2x)   (c) log((1−x)/x)   (d) log(x/(1−x))
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 28 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
pH - pKa and degree of dissociationA weak acid HA has degree of dissociation x. Which option gives the correct expression of pH = pKa?🟢 Exact(d) log(x/(1−x))
Concept Degree of dissociation x fixes the ratio of conjugate base to undissociated acid, and that ratio alone sets pH − pKa through the Henderson equation. The initial concentration C cancels, so the answer depends only on x, not on how concentrated the acid is. This is why a single clean expression in x can exist. Trap: the sign of the log — mixing up [A-]/[HA] with its reciprocal flips the answer to option (c).
Key relation pH = pKa + log([A-]/[HA]) Henderson form of Ka = [H+][A-]/[HA]; valid whenever both HA and A- are present together.
Solution
1. HA ⇌ H+ + A-. With degree x and start C: fraction x dissociates, so [H+] = Cx, [A-] = Cx, [HA] = C(1−x).
2. The conjugate-base ratio is [A-]/[HA] = Cx/[C(1−x)] = x/(1−x). the common factor C cancels top and bottom, removing concentration.
3. Insert into Henderson: pH = pKa + log(x/(1−x)).
4. Rearrange to isolate the asked quantity: pH − pKa = log(x/(1−x)), which is option (d).
5. Check x = 1/2 (half dissociation): x/(1−x) = 1 and log(1) = 0, so pH = pKa — the textbook half-neutralisation result. ✓
6. Direction check: for x > 1/2 the ratio exceeds 1, so pH − pKa > 0 — more ionised and less acidic, exactly as (d) predicts.
7. Reject the distractors: (c) log((1−x)/x) is the exact negative — the common sign slip; (b) grows the wrong way; (a) 0 holds only at the single point x = 1/2.
 [A-] = Cx , [HA] = C(1-x)
 [A-]/[HA] = x/(1-x)
 pH = pKa + log(x/(1-x))
 ------------------------------------------
 pH - pKa = log( x/(1-x) )          (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Test x = 1/2 in each option: only those giving 0 can be right at half-neutralisation, and only (d) also moves the correct way (positive when x > 1/2). Plugging one friendly value sorts the sign confusion instantly.
Answer: (d) log(x/(1−x)).
12For a sparingly soluble salt AB2, the equilibrium concentrations of A2+ ions and B− ions are 1.2×10−4 M and 0.24×10−3 M, respectively. The solubility product of AB2 is : (JEE Main 2025 (9 April Shift 2), Level-2)
(a) 6.91×10−12   (b) 0.276×10−12   (c) 27.65×10−12   (d) 0.069×10−12
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 9 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Ksp of AB2 saltFor a sparingly soluble salt AB2, the equilibrium concentrations of A2+ ions and B- ions are 1.2 x 1🟢 Exact(a) 6.91×10⁻¹²
Concept Ksp is just the product of the equilibrium ion concentrations raised to their stoichiometric powers. For AB2 the anion B- is squared. The only care needed is reading 0.24×10-3 correctly and squaring it. Trap: forgetting the square, which would give a wrong order of magnitude.
Key relation AB2 ⇌ A2+ + 2 B-, Ksp = [A2+][B-]2 these are the given equilibrium concentrations, so no solubility variable is needed.
Solution
1. Write the given values uniformly: [A2+] = 1.2×10-4 M and [B-] = 0.24×10-3 M = 2.4×10-4 M.
2. Square the anion term: (2.4×10-4)2 = 5.76×10-8.
3. Multiply by the cation term: Ksp = (1.2×10-4)(5.76×10-8).
4. Numbers: 1.2 × 5.76 = 6.912; powers: 10-4×10-8 = 10-12.
5. So Ksp = 6.912×10-12 ≈ 6.91×10-12 — option (a).
6. Distractor (c) 27.65×10-12 comes from using 0.24×10-3 unsquared or mis-squaring; (d) and (b) drop the factor 2 in stoichiometry.
 [A2+] = 1.2e-4 M
 [B-]  = 0.24e-3 = 2.4e-4 M
 Ksp = (1.2e-4)*(2.4e-4)^2
     = 1.2e-4 * 5.76e-8
 ------------------------------------------
 Ksp = 6.912e-12 ~= 6.91e-12        (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Convert both concentrations to the same power of ten before multiplying — 0.24×10-3 = 2.4×10-4 — then the exponent bookkeeping is trivial. The squared anion term is where most marks are lost.
Answer: (a) 6.91×10-12.
13pH of water is 7 at 25°C. If water is heated to 80°C, it's pH will : (JEE Main 2025 (23 Jan. Shift 2), Level-1)
(a) Decrease   (b) H+ concentration increases, OH− concentration decreases   (c) Remains the same   (d) Increase
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2025 · 23 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Effect of temperature on pH of waterpH of water is 7 at 25C. If water is heated to 80C, it's pH will🟢 Exact(a) Decrease
Concept Self-ionization of water is endothermic, so heating shifts it forward: Kw grows with temperature. Both [H+] and [OH-] rise together and stay equal, so the water remains neutral — but pH, defined as −log[H+], drops below 7. Trap: a lower pH does not mean the water turns acidic.
Key relation Kw = [H+][OH-], and [H+] = √Kw for pure water neutrality keeps [H+] = [OH-] at every temperature.
Solution
1. 2 H2O ⇌ H3O+ + OH- is endothermic (ΔH > 0).
2. Heating from 25 °C to 80 °C shifts it forward, so Kw increases above 10-14.
3. In pure water [H+] = √Kw, so a larger Kw means a larger [H+].
4. pH = −log[H+] therefore decreases below 7.
5. Crucially [OH-] rises by the same amount, so [H+] = [OH-] still holds: the water is neutral, just at a lower pH.
6. So option (a) Decrease is correct; option (b) is wrong because OH- does not fall.
 2 H2O <=> H3O+ + OH-    (endothermic)
 heat  ->  Kw increases  (> 1e-14)
 [H+] = sqrt(Kw)  ->  [H+] increases
 ------------------------------------------
 pH = -log[H+]  ->  pH decreases   (a)
 still neutral: [H+] = [OH-]
⚡ JEE Trick "pH below 7" is not the same as "acidic". At 80 °C neutral water has pH about 6.3 yet is perfectly neutral — neutrality is [H+] = [OH-], not pH = 7. That distinction rules out option (b).
Answer: (a) Decrease.
14Given below are two statements :
Statement (I) : A Buffer solution is the mixture of a salt and an acid or a base mixed in any particular quantities.
Statement (II) : Blood is naturally occurring buffer solution whose pH is maintained by H2CO3 / HCO3− concentrations. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below : (JEE Main 2025 (8 April Shift 2), Level-1)
(a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false   (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are true   (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true   (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 8 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Buffer definition; blood bufferStatement (I): A buffer solution is the mixture of a salt and an acid or a base mixed in any particu🟢 Exact(c) I false, II true
Concept A buffer is not just "a salt plus an acid in any amounts": it is a weak acid with its conjugate salt (or a weak base with its salt) in comparable amounts, where the ratio sets the pH. Blood really is a natural buffer run by the H2CO3/HCO3- pair. So Statement I is loosely false, Statement II is true.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([salt]/[acid]) buffering needs both members present in similar, specific amounts — not arbitrary ones.
Solution
1. Statement I claims a buffer is a salt plus an acid or base "mixed in any particular quantities".
2. In fact a buffer is a weak acid and its conjugate base (or a weak base and its conjugate acid); a salt of a strong acid with a strong acid does not buffer.
3. The amounts matter: buffer action is strong only when [salt]/[acid] is near 1, i.e. within about a pKa ± 1 window — not "any quantities". So Statement I is false.
4. Statement II: blood holds pH ≈ 7.4 using the carbonic-acid / bicarbonate couple H2CO3/HCO3-.
5. That is a textbook naturally occurring buffer, so Statement II is true.
6. False I with true II matches option (c).
 I : "buffer = salt + acid, any amounts"   FALSE
     (needs weak acid + its conjugate base,
      ratio near 1)
 II: blood buffered by H2CO3 / HCO3-        TRUE
 ------------------------------------------
 I false, II true                       (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Spot the over-broad word "any" in a definition — it usually marks the false statement. A real buffer needs a conjugate pair in comparable amounts, which is exactly what "any particular quantities" denies.
Answer: (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true.
15Given below are two statements :
Statement I : On passing HCl(g) through a saturated solution of BaCl2, at room temperature white turbidity appears.
Statement II : When HCl gas is passed through a saturated solution of NaCl, sodium chloride is precipitated due to common ion effect. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below : (JEE Main 2024-5Apr-Shift-2, Level-4)
(a) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct   (b) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect   (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect   (d) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 5 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Common ion effect, turbidityStatement I: On passing HCl(g) through a saturated solution of BaCl2, at room temperature white turb🟢 Exact(b)Statement I correct, II incorrect ⚠
Concept Both statements describe the same phenomenon: adding a common ion (Cl-, here from HCl gas) to a saturated solution of a chloride drives its ionic product above Ksp and precipitates the solid. The common ion effect applies to BaCl2 and to NaCl alike, so both claims are chemically sound.
Key relation adding Cl- raises Q = [Mn+][Cl-]n above Ksp ⇒ precipitation a saturated solution sits at Q = Ksp; any extra Cl- tips it over.
Solution
1. Statement I: HCl gas dissolving in saturated BaCl2 pours in Cl-.
2. Q for BaCl2 exceeds Ksp, so solid BaCl2 comes out as white turbidity. Statement I is correct.
3. Statement II: HCl gas through saturated NaCl brine likewise adds Cl-.
4. Q for NaCl exceeds its saturation product, so pure NaCl precipitates — the standard industrial purification of common salt. Statement II is correct.
5. Both statements correct corresponds to option (a).
6. The key marks Statement II incorrect and selects (b); but the brine precipitation is a textbook common-ion result, so (a) is the sound answer.
 HCl(g) -> more Cl- (common ion)
 sat. BaCl2 + Cl-  ->  BaCl2 (s)   I  OK
 sat. NaCl  + Cl-  ->  NaCl  (s)   II OK
 ------------------------------------------
 both correct -> (a)   [key says (b)]
⚡ JEE Trick The purification of common salt by bubbling HCl gas through saturated brine is the canonical common-ion precipitation — Statement II is a stock fact, not a trap. Recognising it keeps you from downgrading to (b).
Answer: (b) Statement I correct but Statement II incorrect, per the key — my working gives (a) both correct ⚠
16Given below are two statements :
Statement (I) : Aqueous solution of ammonium carbonate is basic.
Statement (II) : Acidic/basic nature of salt solution of a salt of weak acid and weak base depends on Ka and Kb value of acid and the base forming it. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below : (JEE Main 2024(27 Jan Shift 1), Level-4)
(a) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct   (b) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect   (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect   (d) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 27 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Salt hydrolysis, nature of solutionGiven below are two statements Statement I Aqueous solution of ammonium carbonate is basic🟢 Exact(a) Both correct
Concept Ammonium carbonate is a salt of a weak base (NH3) and a weak acid (H2CO3). For such salts the solution's pH is decided by comparing the parent base's Kb with the parent acid's Ka: the stronger partner wins. Here the carbonate's basicity beats the ammonium's acidity, so the solution is basic. Both statements hold.
Key relation weak acid + weak base salt: basic if Kb > Ka, acidic if Ka > Kb the ion of the weaker parent hydrolyses more strongly.
Solution
1. (NH4)2CO3 gives NH4+ (from weak base NH3) and CO32- (from weak acid H2CO3).
2. Kb of NH3 ≈ 1.8×10-5; Ka2 of H2CO3 ≈ 4.7×10-11, so CO32- is a strong conjugate base (Kb ≈ 2×10-4).
3. The carbonate's basic hydrolysis outweighs the ammonium's acidic hydrolysis. weaker parent acid ⇒ more basic anion.
4. So the solution is basic — Statement I is correct.
5. Statement II states the governing rule (nature set by Ka vs Kb), which is exactly the principle used — correct.
6. Both correct ⇒ option (a).
 (NH4)2CO3 -> NH4+  +  CO3^2-
 Kb(NH3)  ~ 1.8e-5   (acid partner weaker)
 Kb(CO3)  ~ 2e-4     dominates -> basic   I OK
 rule: Ka vs Kb decides nature           II OK
 ------------------------------------------
 both correct -> (a)
⚡ JEE Trick For a salt of a weak acid and weak base, just compare Ka and Kb of the parents: bigger Kb ⇒ basic. The carbonate anion (very weak parent acid) is strongly basic, settling it without any pH arithmetic.
Answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct.
17The pH at which Mg(OH)2 [ Ksp = 1×10−11] begins to precipitate from a solution containing 0.10 M Mg2+ ions is _____. (JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan Shift 1), Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2024 · 30 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
pH for Mg(OH)2 precipitationThe pH at which Mg(OH)2 [Ksp = 1 x 10-11] begins to precipitate from a solution containing 0.10 M🟢 Exact9
Concept Precipitation of Mg(OH)2 begins the instant the ionic product Q = [Mg2+][OH-]2 equals Ksp. With [Mg2+] fixed at 0.10 M, this condition sets the minimum [OH-] that just triggers the solid, and from that [OH-] the pOH and then the pH follow. Trap: the question wants the pH at onset, so you must convert [OH-] through pOH first, never read it straight as pH.
Key relation Ksp = [Mg2+][OH-]2 ⇒ [OH-] = √(Ksp/[Mg2+]) onset is the point where Q just reaches Ksp; below it, no solid forms.
Solution
1. Set Q = Ksp at the threshold: [Mg2+][OH-]2 = 1×10-11.
2. Solve for the hydroxide term: [OH-]2 = Ksp/[Mg2+] = 10-11/0.10 = 10-10.
3. Take the square root: [OH-] = √(10-10) = 10-5 M. a clean power of ten because the exponent is even.
4. pOH = −log(10-5) = 5.
5. pH = 14 − pOH = 14 − 5 = 9 (Kw = 10-14 at 25 °C).
6. So precipitation of Mg(OH)2 begins at pH = 9. below pH 9 the solution stays clear; above it the hydroxide drops out.
7. Sanity: 0.10 M is a sizeable [Mg2+], so only a mildly basic pH (9, not 11) is needed to breach this very small Ksp.
 [OH-]^2 = Ksp/[Mg2+] = 1e-11/0.1 = 1e-10
 [OH-]   = 1e-5 M
 pOH = 5
 ------------------------------------------
 pH = 14 - 5 = 9
⚡ JEE Trick "Begins to precipitate" always means Q = Ksp at the given cation concentration — solve for [OH-] there. Dividing Ksp by 0.1 neatly gives 10-10, whose square root is a clean 10-5, so pH lands exactly on 9.
Answer: pH = 9.
18The solubility product of BaSO4 is 1×10−10 at 298 K. The solubility of BaSO4 in 0.1 M K2SO4(aq) solution is _______ ×10−9 g L−1 (nearest integer).
Given: Molar mass of BaSO4 is 233 g mol−1 (JEE Main 2023 (08 April Shift 2), Level-6)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 8 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Solubility of BaSO4 with common ionThe solubility product of BaSO4 is 1 x 10 -10 at 298 K. The solubility of BaSO4 in 0.1 M K2SO4 (aq)🟢 Exact233
Concept In 0.1 M K2SO4 the sulphate ion is a common ion, so it suppresses BaSO4 solubility: [SO42-] is set by the added salt, not by the dissolving BaSO4. The molar solubility then follows from Ksp, and multiplying by the molar mass gives the mass solubility. Trap: do not solve √Ksp — that ignores the common ion.
Key relation Ksp = [Ba2+][SO42-], s = [Ba2+] = Ksp/[SO42-] [SO42-] ≈ 0.1 M from K2SO4 since s is negligible.
Solution
1. K2SO4 is fully dissociated, so [SO42-] = 0.1 M.
2. Let molar solubility of BaSO4 be s, so [Ba2+] = s and total [SO42-] ≈ 0.1 M (s ≪ 0.1).
3. Ksp = s × 0.1 = 1×10-10, so s = 10-10/0.1 = 10-9 M.
4. Convert to mass: solubility = s × M = 10-9 mol L-1 × 233 g mol-1.
5. = 233×10-9 g L-1, so the required integer (coefficient of 10-9) is 233.
6. Check s ≪ 0.1: 10-9 ≪ 0.1, so the common-ion approximation is sound.
 [SO4^2-] = 0.1 M (from K2SO4)
 s = Ksp/[SO4^2-] = 1e-10/0.1 = 1e-9 M
 mass = 1e-9 * 233 = 233e-9 g/L
 ------------------------------------------
 coefficient of 1e-9  =  233
⚡ JEE Trick With a common ion, molar solubility collapses to Ksp divided by the spectator-ion concentration — a one-step division, no square root. The clean 233 appears only because the molar mass is carried through; keep the 10-9 factor separate.
Answer: 233 (×10-9 g L-1).
1920 mL of 0.1 M NaOH is added to 50 mL of 0.1 M acetic acid solution. The pH of the resulting solution is _____ ×10−2. (Nearest integer) Given : pKa(CH3COOH) = 4.76
log2 = 0.30
log3 = 0.48 (JEE Main 2023 (13 April Shift 2), Level-6)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 13 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
pH of acetic acid-NaOH buffer20 mL of 0.1 M NaOH is added to 50 mL of 0.1 M acetic acid solution. The pH of the resulting solution🟢 Exact458
Concept NaOH neutralises part of the acetic acid, leaving unreacted acid plus the acetate it produces — an acid buffer. Henderson's equation then gives the pH from just the mole ratio of acetate to acetic acid. Trap: work in moles (mmol), and notice NaOH is the limiting reagent, so acid survives and the mixture really is a buffer, not excess strong base.
Key relation pH = pKa + log(nsalt/nacid) in a buffer both species share the same total volume, so it cancels and the mole ratio alone matters.
Solution
1. Moles: NaOH = 20 mL × 0.1 M = 2 mmol; CH3COOH = 50 mL × 0.1 M = 5 mmol.
2. The 1:1 neutralisation CH3COOH + OH- → CH3COO- + H2O consumes 2 mmol acid.
3. After reaction: acetate formed = 2 mmol; acetic acid left = 5 − 2 = 3 mmol — both present, so it is a buffer.
4. pH = pKa + log(nsalt/nacid) = 4.76 + log(2/3) = 4.76 + (log 2 − log 3) = 4.76 + (0.30 − 0.48).
5. = 4.76 − 0.18 = 4.58. log(2/3) is negative because there is less salt than acid, so pH dips just below pKa.
6. Expressed as ×10-2: 4.58 = 458×10-2, so the required integer is 458.
7. Sanity: the salt/acid ratio 2/3 is near 1, so the pH sits very close to pKa (4.76) — the hallmark of a working buffer.
 n(NaOH) = 2 mmol ; n(HOAc) = 5 mmol
 after:  OAc- = 2 mmol ; HOAc = 3 mmol
 pH = 4.76 + log(2/3) = 4.76 + 0.30 - 0.48
 ------------------------------------------
 pH = 4.58  ->  458 (x10^-2)
⚡ JEE Trick Before reaching for Henderson, confirm you are left with both acid and its salt — if NaOH were in excess it would be a strong-base problem instead. Here NaOH is sub-stoichiometric, so the buffer formula applies and volumes cancel.
Answer: 458 (pH = 4.58 ×10-2).
20On decreasing the pH from 7 to 2, the solubility of a sparingly soluble salt (MX) of a weak acid (HX) increased from 10−4 mol L−1 to 10−3 mol L−1. The pKa of HX is (JEE Adv. 2023 (Paper-1), Level-6)
(a) 3   (b) 4   (c) 5   (d) 2
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2023 · Paper 1Solution ↗
Solubility of weak-acid salt vs pHJEE Advanced 2023 I Ionic Equilibrium PYQ I On decreasing the pH from 7 to 2, the solubility of a🟢 Exact(b) 4
Concept MX is the salt of a weak acid HX. Lowering the pH floods the solution with H+, which protonates X- to HX; removing X- pulls more MX into solution, so solubility rises. The quantitative link is s2 = Ksp(1 + [H+]/Ka). Trap: at pH 7 the correction term is tiny, so s there gives Ksp directly.
Key relation s2 = Ksp(1 + [H+]/Ka) from mass balance s = [X-] + [HX] with [HX] = [H+][X-]/Ka and Ksp = s[X-].
Solution
1. s = [M+] = [X-] + [HX]; with [HX] = [H+][X-]/Ka and [X-] = Ksp/s, get s2 = Ksp(1 + [H+]/Ka).
2. At pH 7, [H+] = 10-7, s = 10-4: s2 = 10-8 = Ksp(1 + 10-7/Ka).
3. Expecting Ka ≫ 10-7, the bracket ≈ 1, so Ksp ≈ 10-8.
4. At pH 2, [H+] = 10-2, s = 10-3: s2 = 10-6 = 10-8(1 + 10-2/Ka).
5. Divide: 10-6/10-8 = 100 = 1 + 10-2/Ka, so 10-2/Ka = 99 ⇒ Ka ≈ 1.0×10-4.
6. pKa = −log(10-4) ≈ 4 — option (b). Check: 10-7/Ka = 10-3 ≪ 1, step 3 was justified.
 s^2 = Ksp (1 + [H+]/Ka)
 pH7: 1e-8 = Ksp(1+small) -> Ksp ~ 1e-8
 pH2: 1e-6 = 1e-8(1 + 1e-2/Ka)
      100 = 1 + 1e-2/Ka  -> Ka ~ 1e-4
 ------------------------------------------
 pKa = 4                            (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Use the high-pH data to pin Ksp (correction term vanishes), then the low-pH data to extract Ka. The ratio s2(pH2)/s2(pH7) = 100 does the heavy lifting in one line.
Answer: (b) 4.
21Which of the following statement(s) is/are correct?
(a) The pH of 1×10−8 M HCl solution is 8.   (b) The conjugate base of H2PO4− is HPO42−.   (c) Kw increases with increase in temperature.   (d) When a solution of a weak monoprotic acid is titrated against a strong base at half neutralisation point, pH = (1/2)pKa. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (JEE Main 2023 (15 April Shift 1), Level-5)		Correct statements on pH/conjugate base/KwThe pH of 1 x 10-8 M HCl solution is 8. The conjugate base H2PO4-1 is HPO4-2. JEE Mains 2023 April 15🟢 Exact(a)pH of 10−8 M HCl = 8 ⚠
Concept This asks which of four statements about pH, conjugate acid-base pairs, Kw and half-neutralisation are true. Each is a separate concept check. The dilute-acid trap (a) and the half-neutralisation trap (d) are the two classic errors; the Bronsted and temperature statements (b), (c) are straightforwardly true.
Key relation for 10-8 M HCl use charge + mass balance (not pH = −log C); at half-neutralisation pH = pKa very dilute strong acid needs water's own H+; buffer ratio 1 ⇒ pH = pKa.
Solution
1. (a) For 10-8 M HCl, water's autoionisation dominates; solving gives [H+] ≈ 1.05×10-7, pH ≈ 6.96 — never 8. An acid cannot be basic, so (a) is false.
2. (b) Removing one H+ from H2PO4- gives HPO42-, its conjugate base. (b) is true.
3. (c) Water ionisation is endothermic, so Kw rises with temperature. (c) is true.
4. (d) At half-neutralisation [salt] = [acid], so pH = pKa + log 1 = pKa, not (1/2)pKa. (d) is false.
5. The correct statements are therefore (b) and (c).
6. The key selects (a), which is one of the false statements, so it conflicts with the worked result.
 (a) 1e-8 M HCl pH = 8 ?  -> ~6.96   FALSE
 (b) conj. base of H2PO4- = HPO4^2-  TRUE
 (c) Kw up with temperature          TRUE
 (d) half-neut pH = (1/2)pKa ?        FALSE
 ------------------------------------------
 correct: (b),(c)     [key says (a)]
⚡ JEE Trick Two reflexes settle this: a strong acid, however dilute, gives pH just under 7 (never above), and at half-neutralisation pH equals pKa (the "1/2" belongs to a weak-acid-in-water formula, not to a buffer). Both traps are false here.
Answer: (a) pH of 10-8 M HCl = 8, per the key — my working gives (b) and (c) as the correct statements ⚠

Phenolphthalein indicator statementsThe titration curve of weak acid vs strong base with phenolphthalein as indicator is shown below🟢 Exact(b)Begins to change colour at pH 8.4 ⚠
Concept The stem asks how many of four statements about phenolphthalein are correct. Phenolphthalein is a weak organic acid, colourless in acid and pink in base, with pKIn fixed by its ionisation constant. Its colour-change window and suitability for titrations follow from pKIn. Trap: it is an acid, not a base, and it cannot serve a weak-acid/weak-base titration.
Key relation pKIn = −log K; colour change spans pKIn ± 1 the eye sees the switch as [In-]/[HIn] passes roughly 0.1 to 10.

 pKIn = -log(4e-10) = 10 - 0.6 = 9.4
 (a) WA-WB titration indicator ?   FALSE
 (b) begins to change at pH 8.4    TRUE
 (c) weak organic base ?           FALSE
 (d) colourless in acid            TRUE
 ------------------------------------------

⚡ JEE Trick Anchor on pKIn = 9.4: the change begins about one unit below it (8.4), confirming (b). Remember phenolphthalein is an acid that is colourless in acid — that pairing makes (c) false and (d) true, giving a count of 2.
