23The dissociation constant of acetic is x×10−5. When 25 mL of 0.2 M CH3COONa solution is mixed with 25 mL of 0.02 M CH3COOH solution, the pH of the resultant solution is found to be equal to 5. The value of x is _____. (JEE Main 2023 (24 Jan. Shift 1), Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 24 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Ka of acetic acid from buffer pHThe dissociation constant of acetic acid is x × 10−5. When 25 mL of 0.2 M CH3COONa solution is mixed🟢 Exact10
Concept CH₃COONa is the salt of the weak acid CH₃COOH, so the two together form an acidic buffer. For a buffer the Henderson–Hasselbalch equation fixes the pH from pKa and the salt-to-acid ratio, and here the pH is handed to us so we invert it to recover pKa and hence Ka. The one real trap is dilution: both solutions are 25 mL, so on mixing every concentration is halved, but because both appear as a ratio the halving cancels. Reading x × 10⁻⁵ against the computed Ka then finishes it.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([salt]/[acid]) Valid because the weak acid CH₃COOH and its conjugate base CH₃COO⁻ are both present in comparable, non-negligible amounts, which is exactly the buffer regime.
Solution
1. Total volume after mixing = 25 + 25 = 50 mL, so both species are diluted into this volume.
2. [salt] = (0.2 × 25)/50 = 0.1 M and [acid] = (0.02 × 25)/50 = 0.01 M. concentration = millimoles taken ÷ total mL
3. Ratio [salt]/[acid] = 0.1/0.01 = 10, so log(ratio) = log 10 = 1.
4. Henderson: 5 = pKa + log([salt]/[acid]) = pKa + 1, hence pKa = 4.
5. Ka = 10⁻pKa = 10⁻⁴ = 10 × 10⁻⁵, so matching the form x × 10⁻⁵ gives x = 10.
6. Sanity: salt > acid means pH should sit above pKa, and indeed pH 5 > pKa 4 by one unit. a 10:1 base/acid excess
7. So the dissociation constant is 10 × 10⁻⁵ and x = 10.
 V(total) = 25 + 25 = 50 mL
 [salt] = 0.2  x 25 / 50 = 0.1 M
 [acid] = 0.02 x 25 / 50 = 0.01 M
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = pKa + log([salt]/[acid])
 5  = pKa + log(0.1/0.01) = pKa + 1
 pKa = 4  ->  Ka = 10^-4 = 10 x 10^-5
 -----------------------------------------
 x = 10                            <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick A ten-fold salt/acid ratio lifts the pH exactly one unit above pKa; spotting log 10 = 1 skips the log table entirely. The common slip is ignoring dilution and using 0.2/0.02 directly — it happens to give 10 here because both are the same 25 mL, but that shortcut fails the moment the two mixing volumes differ.
Answer: x = 10.
24A litre of buffer solution contains 0.1 mole of each of NH3 and NH4Cl. On the addition of 0.02 mole of HCl by dissolving gaseous HCl, the pH of the solution is found to be _____ ×10−3 (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023 (25 Jan. Shift 1), Level-3)
Given: pKb(NH3) = 4.745
log2 = 0.301
log3 = 0.477
T = 298 K
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 25 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
pH of NH3/NH4Cl buffer after HClA litre of buffer solution contains 0.1 mole of each of NH3 and NH4Cl. On the addition of 0.02 mole🟢 Exact9 ⚠
Concept This is a basic buffer of NH₃ and its conjugate acid NH₄⁺. A strong acid added to a buffer is consumed stoichiometrically by the basic component, so HCl converts an equal amount of NH₃ into NH₄⁺. The new ratio is then put into the base-form Henderson equation for pOH, and pH = 14 − pOH. The decisive subtlety is the answer format: the blank is scaled by ×10⁻³ and asks for the nearest integer, so the printed number is pH × 1000, not pH itself.
Key relation pOH = pKb + log([NH₄⁺]/[NH₃]); pH = 14 − pOH Both conjugate partners survive the partial neutralisation, so the mixture is still a buffer and the base-form equation applies directly.
Solution
1. Initial amounts in 1 L: 0.1 mol NH₃ and 0.1 mol NH₄⁺ (from NH₄Cl).
2. HCl (0.02 mol) + NH₃ → NH₄⁺, giving NH₃ = 0.08 mol and NH₄⁺ = 0.12 mol. strong acid consumes the base mole-for-mole
3. log([NH₄⁺]/[NH₃]) = log(0.12/0.08) = log 1.5 = log3 − log2 = 0.477 − 0.301 = 0.176.
4. pOH = pKb + 0.176 = 4.745 + 0.176 = 4.921.
5. pH = 14 − 4.921 = 9.079, as expected for an ammonia buffer (weakly basic).
6. The blank is pH × 10³: 9.079 × 1000 = 9079. "×10⁻³ (nearest integer)" scales the printed value
7. So the intended (and official NTA) answer is 9079; the key's 9 is only the bare pH.
 NH3 : 0.10 - 0.02 = 0.08 mol
 NH4+: 0.10 + 0.02 = 0.12 mol
 -----------------------------------------
 pOH = pKb + log([NH4+]/[NH3])
     = 4.745 + log(1.5)
     = 4.745 + 0.176 = 4.921
 pH  = 14 - 4.921 = 9.079
 -----------------------------------------
 pH x 10^3 = 9079              <-- my answer
 key prints 9
⚡ JEE Trick Read the scale factor before rounding: "×10⁻³ (nearest integer)" asks for pH×1000 = 9079. Writing 9 drops the factor and would imply pH = 0.009, which is impossible for a basic ammonia buffer. The log 1.5 shift is small, so the whole correction to pKb is only +0.176.
Answer: 9 per the key — my working gives 9079 ⚠
25At 298 K, the solubility of silver chloride in water is 1.434×10−3 g L−1. The value of −logKsp for silver chloride is _______
(Given mass of Ag is 107.9 g mol−1, and mass of Cl is 35.5 g mol−1) (JEE Main 2023 (31 Jan. Shift 2), Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 31 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
-log Ksp of AgCl from solubilityAt 298 K,the solubility of silver chloride in water is 1.434 × 10−3 g L−1. The value of − log Ksp fo🟢 Exact10
Concept AgCl dissolves 1:1 into Ag⁺ and Cl⁻, so its molar solubility s equals each ion concentration and Ksp = s². The data give solubility as a mass per litre, so it must first be converted to mol L⁻¹ with the molar mass before squaring. The trap is squaring the gram figure directly or forgetting that −log Ksp just reads off the exponent once Ksp is a clean power of ten. Everything here is arranged so s comes out exactly 10⁻⁵.
Key relation Ksp = [Ag⁺][Cl⁻] = s², with s = (g L⁻¹)/M For a 1:1 salt dissolving in pure water, both ions equal the molar solubility s, so the product is simply s².
Solution
1. Molar mass of AgCl = 107.9 + 35.5 = 143.4 g mol⁻¹.
2. s = 1.434×10⁻³ / 143.4 = 1.0×10⁻⁵ mol L⁻¹. mass solubility ÷ molar mass
3. AgCl ⇌ Ag⁺ + Cl⁻, so [Ag⁺] = [Cl⁻] = s = 10⁻⁵ M.
4. Ksp = s² = (10⁻⁵)² = 1.0×10⁻¹⁰.
5. −log Ksp = −log(10⁻¹⁰) = 10.
6. Units check: solubility in g L⁻¹ divided by g mol⁻¹ correctly yields mol L⁻¹. dimensional consistency
7. Sanity: a sparingly soluble halide with Ksp ≈ 10⁻¹⁰ is exactly the textbook value for AgCl.
 M(AgCl) = 107.9 + 35.5 = 143.4 g/mol
 s = 1.434e-3 / 143.4 = 1.0e-5 mol/L
 -----------------------------------------
 AgCl -> Ag+ + Cl-,  [Ag+]=[Cl-]=s
 Ksp = s^2 = (1e-5)^2 = 1e-10
 -log Ksp = 10
 -----------------------------------------
 -log Ksp = 10                    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The gram solubility was tuned so that dividing by 143.4 gives a clean 10⁻⁵; whenever s is a round power of ten, Ksp = s² and −log Ksp fall out instantly without logs. The classic error is squaring 1.434×10⁻³ (the gram value) instead of the molar solubility, which inflates Ksp by a factor of M².
Answer: −log Ksp = 10.
26The volume of 0.02 M aqueous HBr required to neutralize 10.0 mL of 0.01 M aqueous Ba(OH)2 is (Assume complete neutralization) (JEE Main 2023 (06 April Shift 2), Level-3)
(a) 2.5 mL   (b) 5.0 mL   (c) 10.0 mL   (d) 7.5 mL
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 6 Apr · EveningSolution ↗
Neutralization volume of HBr vs Ba(OH)2The volume of 0.02 M aqueous HBr required to neutralize 10.0 mL of 0.01 M aqueous Ba(OH)2 is🟢 Exact(c) 10.0 mL
Concept Neutralisation balances moles of H⁺ against moles of OH⁻, never volume against volume. Ba(OH)₂ is a diacidic base, delivering two OH⁻ per formula unit, while HBr is monobasic with one H⁺ each. So the required HBr volume depends on both the OH⁻ count and the acid's own concentration. The trap is dropping the factor of 2 from Ba(OH)₂, which would halve the answer to 5 mL.
Key relation mmol H⁺ = mmol OH⁻ → V₁ M₁ n₁ = V₂ M₂ n₂ n is the number of replaceable H⁺ (acid) or OH⁻ (base) per formula unit; equivalents must match at complete neutralisation.
Solution
1. OH⁻ available from Ba(OH)₂ = 10.0 × 0.01 × 2 = 0.20 mmol. 2 OH⁻ per formula unit
2. HBr supplies one H⁺ each, so the H⁺ required = 0.20 mmol.
3. Volume of 0.02 M HBr = mmol / M = 0.20 / 0.02 = 10.0 mL.
4. Cross-check equivalents: 10.0 × 0.02 × 1 = 0.20 mmol H⁺ = 0.20 mmol OH⁻. balanced at the endpoint
5. The products are BaBr₂ and water, confirming complete neutralisation.
6. Hence the volume of 0.02 M HBr required is 10.0 mL, which is option (c).
 OH- = V x M x 2 (Ba(OH)2 diacidic)
     = 10.0 x 0.01 x 2 = 0.20 mmol
 -----------------------------------------
 HBr monobasic: H+ needed = 0.20 mmol
 V = mmol / M = 0.20 / 0.02 = 10.0 mL
 check: 10.0 x 0.02 x 1 = 0.20 mmol OK
 -----------------------------------------
 V(HBr) = 10.0 mL                 <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Count replaceable protons first: Ba(OH)₂ gives two OH⁻, so it demands twice the acid a 1:1 base would. Here HBr is also only 0.02 M against 0.01 M base, and those two factors combine to exactly 10 mL. The distractor (b) 5.0 mL is what you get by forgetting the factor of 2.
Answer: (c) 10.0 mL.
2725 mL of silver nitrate solution (1M) is added dropwise to 25 mL of potassium iodide (1.05 M) solution. The ion(s) present in very small quantity in the solution is/are (JEE Main 2023 (11 April Shift 1), Level-3)
(a) I− only   (b) K+ only   (c) NO3− only   (d) Ag+ and I− both
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 11 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Ions in excess/deficit after AgNO3+KI25 mL of silver nitrate solution (1 M ) is added dropwise to 25 mL of potassium iodide (1.05 M )....🟢 Exact(d) Ag⁺ and I⁻ both
Concept Mixing AgNO₃ with KI precipitates AgI and removes the limiting reagent almost completely. After the reaction the bulk ions are the two spectators plus whatever small excess of one reagent remains; the other reagent survives only at a trace level pinned by Ksp. The judgement "very small quantity" must be made relative to the spectator ions, not in absolute millimoles, and because KI is only in slight excess the leftover I⁻ is itself small.
Key relation Ag⁺ + I⁻ → AgI↓; afterwards [Ag⁺][I⁻] = Ksp(AgI) With I⁻ in excess, [Ag⁺] is forced down to Ksp/[I⁻], a vanishingly small value.
Solution
1. Ag⁺ = 25 × 1.00 = 25 mmol; NO₃⁻ = 25 mmol (spectator, stays in solution).
2. K⁺ = 25 × 1.05 = 26.25 mmol (spectator); I⁻ = 26.25 mmol.
3. Precipitation of AgI consumes 25 mmol of each, so I⁻ left = 26.25 − 25 = 1.25 mmol.
4. Bulk ions remaining: K⁺ 26.25 mmol and NO₃⁻ 25 mmol — both large. spectators never precipitate
5. I⁻ is only 1.25 mmol, small against the ~25 mmol spectators; Ag⁺ is a Ksp-limited trace because of the excess I⁻. [Ag⁺] = Ksp/[I⁻]
6. Two ions are therefore present in very small amount: Ag⁺ and I⁻ both.
7. Single-ion choices (a), (b), (c) each miss one of these, so (d) is correct.
 Ag+  = 25 x 1.00 = 25.00 mmol
 NO3- = 25.00 mmol  (spectator, large)
 K+   = 25 x 1.05 = 26.25 mmol (large)
 I-   = 26.25 mmol
 -----------------------------------------
 AgI down: 25 mmol each consumed
 I- left = 26.25 - 25 = 1.25 (small)
 Ag+ = Ksp/[I-] = trace (very small)
 -----------------------------------------
 small ions: Ag+ and I-           <-- (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Only a 5% excess of KI was used, so the leftover I⁻ (1.25 mmol) is tiny next to the 25+ mmol of spectator K⁺ and NO₃⁻, and that same excess crushes Ag⁺ through Ksp. Because two ions end up small, every single-ion option fails and the answer must be (d).
Answer: (d) Ag⁺ and I⁻ both.
2820 mL of calcium hydroxide was consumed when it was reacted with 10 mL of unknown solution of H2SO4. Also 20 mL standard solution of 0.5 M HCl containing 2 drops of phenolphthalein was titrated with calcium hydroxide, the mixture showed pink colour when burette displayed the value of 35.5 mL whereas the burette showed 25.5 mL initially. The concentration of H2SO4 is _____ M. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023 (13 April Shift 1), Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 13 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Concentration of H2SO4 by titration20 mL of calcium hydroxide was consumed when it was reacted with 10 mL of unknown solution of H2SO4🟢 Exact1
Concept Two titrations are chained. The second experiment (standard HCl vs Ca(OH)₂ with phenolphthalein) fixes the unknown molarity of the calcium hydroxide; that value is then fed into the first reaction (Ca(OH)₂ vs H₂SO₄) to find the acid concentration. Both Ca(OH)₂ and H₂SO₄ carry a stoichiometric factor of 2 — two OH⁻ and two H⁺ respectively — and the burette titre is a difference of two readings, not the final reading.
Key relation mmol H⁺ = mmol OH⁻ at the equivalence point phenolphthalein turns pink the instant acid is fully consumed, marking exact equivalence.
Solution
1. Standardisation: HCl = 20 × 0.5 = 10 mmol H⁺; Ca(OH)₂ delivered = 35.5 − 25.5 = 10 mL. titre is the burette difference
2. 10 mmol H⁺ = (10 mL)(M)(2) → M(Ca(OH)₂) = 0.5 M. 2 OH⁻ per formula unit
3. First reaction: 20 mL of this 0.5 M Ca(OH)₂ gives OH⁻ = 20 × 0.5 × 2 = 20 mmol.
4. H₂SO₄ sits in 10 mL and is diprotic, so H⁺ = 10 × C × 2 = 20C mmol.
5. At neutralisation mmol H⁺ = mmol OH⁻: 20C = 20.
6. C = 1 M, so [H₂SO₄] = 1 M (nearest integer 1).
7. Check: 1 M diprotic in 10 mL = 20 mmol H⁺, matching the 20 mmol OH⁻ exactly.
 Standardise Ca(OH)2 with HCl:
 H+ = 20 x 0.5 = 10 mmol
 Vbase = 35.5 - 25.5 = 10 mL
 10 = 10 x M x 2  ->  M = 0.5 M
 -----------------------------------------
 OH- (20 mL) = 20 x 0.5 x 2 = 20 mmol
 H2SO4: H+ = 10 x C x 2 = 20C
 20C = 20  ->  C = 1 M
 -----------------------------------------
 [H2SO4] = 1 M                    <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick The real titre is 35.5 − 25.5 = 10 mL, not 35.5; misreading it is the most common error. Once Ca(OH)₂ is nailed at 0.5 M, the two "factor of 2" terms (diacidic base, diprotic acid) cancel between the sides and leave a clean 1 M.
Answer: [H₂SO₄] = 1 M.
29If the pKa of lactic acid is 5, then the pH of 0.005 M calcium lactate solution at 25°C is _____ ×10−1 (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023 (24 Jan. Shift 2), Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 24 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
pH of calcium lactate solutionIf the pKa of lactic acid is 5, then the pH of 0.005M calcium lactate sol at 25°C is ______ × 10⁻¹🟢 Exact85
Concept Calcium lactate is the salt of a strong base (Ca(OH)₂) and a weak acid (lactic acid), so its solution is basic through hydrolysis of the lactate anion. Each formula unit releases two lactate ions, so the concentration that hydrolyses is twice the salt molarity. The standard log expression for such a salt then gives the pH. The trap is using the 0.005 M formula concentration instead of the 0.01 M anion concentration.
Key relation pH = 7 + (1/2)pKa + (1/2)log C This is the result for a salt of a weak acid and strong base; C is the concentration of the hydrolysing anion, here lactate.
Solution
1. Ca(lactate)₂ → Ca²⁺ + 2 lactate⁻, so [lactate⁻] = 2 × 0.005 = 0.01 M. two anions per formula unit
2. Lactate is the conjugate base of lactic acid (pKa 5), so it hydrolyses to give a basic solution.
3. pH = 7 + (1/2)pKa + (1/2)log C = 7 + (1/2)(5) + (1/2)log(0.01).
4. = 7 + 2.5 + (1/2)(−2) = 7 + 2.5 − 1.
5. pH = 8.5, which is above 7 as required for a weak-acid/strong-base salt. basic hydrolysis
6. The blank wants ×10⁻¹: 8.5 = 85 × 10⁻¹, so the answer is 85.
7. Had we wrongly used C = 0.005, pH would be 8.35 → 84, missing the key.
 Ca(Lac)2 -> Ca2+ + 2 Lac-
 [Lac-] = 2 x 0.005 = 0.01 M
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = 7 + (1/2)pKa + (1/2)log C
    = 7 + 2.5 + (1/2)log(0.01)
    = 7 + 2.5 - 1 = 8.5
 -----------------------------------------
 pH x 10 = 85                     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick For a weak-acid/strong-base salt the pH always exceeds 7; the word "calcium" is the giveaway that the anion concentration doubles to 0.01 M. Forgetting the factor of 2 is the single mistake that separates 85 from the wrong 84.
Answer: pH = 8.5, i.e. 85 (×10⁻¹).
30When the hydrogen ion concentration [ H+ ] changes by a factor of 1000, the value of pH of the solution (JEE Main 2023 (25 Jan. Shift 2), Level-2)
(a) increases by 1000 units   (b) decreases by 3 units   (c) decreases by 2 units   (d) increases by 2 units
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 25 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Change in pH with [H+] factorWhen the hydrogen ion concentration [H+] changes by a factor of 1000,the value of pH of the solution🟢 Exact(b) decreases by 3 units
Concept pH is a base-10 logarithm of [H⁺], so multiplying [H⁺] by a power of ten shifts the pH by exactly that exponent, additively. A rise in [H⁺] makes the solution more acidic and therefore lowers the pH. Two mistakes lurk here: thinking pH moves by 1000 (confusing the factor with its logarithm) and getting the direction wrong (pH moves opposite to [H⁺]). A change by a factor of 1000 = 10³ means a shift of 3 pH units.
Key relation pH = −log[H⁺], so ΔpH = −log(factor) A multiplicative change in [H⁺] turns into an additive change in pH through the logarithm.
Solution
1. pH = −log[H⁺] by definition.
2. A factor of 1000 = 10³ in [H⁺] changes pH by log(10³) = 3 units in magnitude.
3. If [H⁺] increases 1000-fold, the −log makes pH fall: ΔpH = −log(1000) = −3.
4. So the pH decreases by 3 units. larger [H⁺] = more acidic = lower pH
5. Option (a) "1000 units" confuses the factor with its logarithm; the shift is only 3.
6. Options (c) and (d) give the wrong magnitude (2) and/or wrong sign.
7. Option (b) "decreases by 3 units" is the correct statement. matches ΔpH = −3
 pH = -log[H+]
 [H+] x 1000 = [H+] x 10^3
 -----------------------------------------
 dpH = -log(10^3) = -3
 more H+  ->  lower pH (more acidic)
 -----------------------------------------
 pH decreases by 3 units          <-- (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Convert any "factor" straight into an exponent: ×1000 means ±3 on the pH scale, and the sign is always opposite to the [H⁺] change. The distractor "increases/decreases by 1000 units" traps anyone who forgets that pH is a logarithmic, not linear, scale.
Answer: (b) decreases by 3 units.
31Millimoles of calcium hydroxide required to produce 100 mL of the aqueous solution of pH 12 is x×10−1. The value of x is _____ (Nearest Integer). Assume complete dissociation. (JEE Main 2023 (29 Jan. Shift 1), Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 29 Jan · MorningSolution ↗
Millimoles Ca(OH)2 for given pHMillimoles of calcium hydroxide required to produce 100 mL of the aqueous solution of pH 12 is x ×🟢 Exact5
Concept A solution of pH 12 fixes the hydroxide concentration through pOH = 14 − pH. Converting that concentration to millimoles in the stated 100 mL, then dividing by two because each Ca(OH)₂ gives two OH⁻, yields the millimoles of base needed. Complete dissociation is assumed, so there is no equilibrium to solve and the whole problem reduces to one careful unit conversion. The trap is dropping the factor of 2 from the two hydroxides, which would double x to 10.
Key relation [OH⁻] = 10^(−pOH); mmol Ca(OH)₂ = (mmol OH⁻)/2 Complete dissociation means two moles of OH⁻ per mole of Ca(OH)₂.
Solution
1. pH = 12 → pOH = 14 − 12 = 2, so [OH⁻] = 10⁻² = 0.01 M.
2. In 100 mL, mmol OH⁻ = 0.01 × 100 = 1 mmol. molarity × volume in mL
3. Ca(OH)₂ → Ca²⁺ + 2 OH⁻, so mmol base = 1/2 = 0.5 mmol. two OH⁻ each
4. The answer is written as x × 10⁻¹ mmol, so 0.5 = x × 10⁻¹.
5. Therefore x = 5.
6. Check backward: 0.5 mmol × 2 = 1 mmol OH⁻ in 0.1 L = 0.01 M → pOH 2 → pH 12. Consistent.
7. So the required amount of calcium hydroxide corresponds to x = 5.
 pH 12 -> pOH 2 -> [OH-] = 1e-2 M
 mmol OH- = 0.01 x 100 = 1 mmol
 -----------------------------------------
 Ca(OH)2 -> 2 OH-
 mmol Ca(OH)2 = 1 / 2 = 0.5 mmol
 0.5 = x x 10^-1
 -----------------------------------------
 x = 5                            <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Keep volume consistent: 0.01 M over 0.1 L is 1 mmol, and the diacidic base halves that to 0.5 mmol. Skipping the ÷2 step gives x = 10, which is the built-in distractor for this type of question.
Answer: x = 5.
32600 mL of 0.01 M HCl is mixed with 400 mL of 0.01 M H2SO4. The pH of the mixture is _____ ×10−2. (Nearest Integer)
[Given log2 = 0.30, log3 = 0.48, log5 = 0.69, log7 = 0.84, log11 = 1.04 ] (JEE Main 2023 (30 Jan. Shift 1), Level-2)		pH of mixed HCl + H2SO4600 mL of 0.01 M HCl is mixed with 400 mL of 0.01 M H2SO4. The pH of the mixture is _____ × 10−2.🟢 Exact186
Concept Both acids are strong and fully ionised, so their contributions of H⁺ simply add — but H₂SO₄ is diprotic and supplies two H⁺ per mole. Sum the millimoles of H⁺, divide by the combined volume to get [H⁺], then take −log. The result must be scaled by ×10⁻² to match the requested integer. No equilibrium is involved because both acids ionise completely in water. The main trap is counting H₂SO₄ once rather than twice when tallying protons.
Key relation [H⁺] = (total mmol H⁺)/(total mL); pH = −log[H⁺] Strong acids ionise completely, so moles of H⁺ are additive and no equilibrium is needed.
Solution
1. From HCl: H⁺ = 600 × 0.01 = 6 mmol.
2. From H₂SO₄: H⁺ = 400 × 0.01 × 2 = 8 mmol. diprotic, so factor 2
3. Total H⁺ = 6 + 8 = 14 mmol in 600 + 400 = 1000 mL, so [H⁺] = 0.014 M.
4. pH = −log(1.4×10⁻²) = 2 − log 1.4.
5. log 1.4 = log(14/10) = log2 + log7 − 1 = 0.30 + 0.84 − 1 = 0.14. given log values
6. pH = 2 − 0.14 = 1.86.
7. As ×10⁻²: 1.86 = 186 × 10⁻², so the answer is 186.
 H+ (HCl)   = 600 x 0.01     = 6 mmol
 H+ (H2SO4) = 400 x 0.01 x 2 = 8 mmol
 total H+   = 14 mmol / 1000 mL = 0.014 M
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = -log(1.4e-2) = 2 - log 1.4
 log1.4 = 0.30 + 0.84 - 1 = 0.14
 pH = 1.86
 -----------------------------------------
 pH x 100 = 186                   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Decompose log 1.4 as log2 + log7 − 1 from the supplied values, so no table is needed. Forgetting the diprotic factor of 2 gives 10 mmol, [H⁺] = 0.010 and pH = 2.00 → 200, which is the principal distractor.
Answer: pH = 1.86, i.e. 186 (×10⁻²).
3325.0 mL of 0.05 M Ba(NO3)2 is mixed with 25.0 mL of 0.020 M NaF. Ksp of BaF2 is 0.5×10−6 at 298 K. The ratio of [Ba2+][F−]2 and Ksp is _____. (JEE Main 2023 (13 April Shift 1), Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 13 Apr · MorningSolution ↗
Ionic product vs Ksp of BaF225.0 mL of 0.050 MBa(NO3)2 is mixed with 25.0 mL of 0.020 M NaF. Ksp of BaF2 is 0.5 × 10 -6 at 298 K🟢 Exact5
Concept The ionic product Q = [Ba²⁺][F⁻]² is evaluated at the diluted concentrations immediately after mixing and then compared with Ksp. Since BaF₂ releases two fluorides per formula, the fluoride term is squared. On mixing equal 25 mL portions every concentration halves, and the ratio Q/Ksp then tells whether precipitation is favoured (Q > Ksp). The trap is forgetting the dilution to 50 mL or forgetting to square [F⁻].
Key relation Q = [Ba²⁺][F⁻]²; ratio = Q/Ksp BaF₂ ⇌ Ba²⁺ + 2 F⁻, so fluoride enters the product as a square.
Solution
1. After mixing, total volume = 25 + 25 = 50 mL.
2. [Ba²⁺] = (25 × 0.05)/50 = 0.025 M. dilution into the 50 mL total
3. [F⁻] = (25 × 0.020)/50 = 0.010 M.
4. Q = [Ba²⁺][F⁻]² = 0.025 × (0.010)² = 0.025 × 10⁻⁴ = 2.5×10⁻⁶.
5. Ksp = 0.5×10⁻⁶ = 5×10⁻⁷, so ratio = Q/Ksp = 2.5×10⁻⁶ / 0.5×10⁻⁶. read the printed Ksp carefully
6. Ratio = 5.
7. Since Q > Ksp, BaF₂ would indeed precipitate; the required ratio is 5.
 V(total) = 25 + 25 = 50 mL
 [Ba2+] = 25 x 0.05  / 50 = 0.025 M
 [F-]   = 25 x 0.020 / 50 = 0.010 M
 -----------------------------------------
 Q = [Ba2+][F-]^2
   = 0.025 x (0.010)^2 = 2.5e-6
 ratio = Q/Ksp = 2.5e-6 / 0.5e-6
 -----------------------------------------
 ratio = 5                        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Fluoride is squared, so even the small 0.010 M matters: (0.010)² = 10⁻⁴. Reading the printed Ksp as 0.5×10⁻⁶ makes the division a clean 5. Forgetting to dilute both species to the 50 mL total would quadruple Q and ruin the ratio.
Answer: ratio Q/Ksp = 5.
34Incorrect statement for the use of indicator in acid-base titration is: (JEE Main 2023 (31 Jan. Shift 2), Level-1)
(a) Methyl orange may be used for a weak acid vs weak base titration.   (b) Phenolphthalein may be used for a strong acid vs strong base titration.   (c) Methyl orange is a suitable indicator for a strong acid vs weak base titration.   (d) Phenolphthalein is a suitable indicator for a weak acid vs strong base titration.
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2023 · 31 Jan · EveningSolution ↗
Incorrect indicator-use statementIncorrect statement for the use of indicators in acid-base titration is🟢 Exact(a) Methyl orange for weak acid vs weak base
Concept An indicator is useful only when its colour-change interval lies within the steep pH jump at the equivalence point. A weak acid vs weak base titration has essentially no sharp jump, so no ordinary indicator gives a crisp endpoint — that is the defective claim. The other three pairings are the standard textbook matches: phenolphthalein where the equivalence point is basic or neutral, methyl orange where it is acidic. The question asks for the INCORRECT statement, so read carefully.
Key relation indicator range ⊂ equivalence-point pH jump methyl orange changes over the acidic range 3.1–4.4, phenolphthalein over the basic range 8.3–10.
Solution
1. (a) Weak acid vs weak base: the curve has no steep vertical region, so no indicator — methyl orange included — suits it. Statement (a) is FALSE.
2. (b) Strong acid vs strong base: equivalence at pH 7 with a wide jump, and phenolphthalein fits. TRUE.
3. (c) Strong acid vs weak base: equivalence point is acidic, so methyl orange is suitable. TRUE. salt of strong acid hydrolyses acidic
4. (d) Weak acid vs strong base: equivalence point is basic, so phenolphthalein is suitable. TRUE.
5. The question wants the incorrect statement, and only (a) is false. no sharp jump for weak–weak
6. Hence the answer is (a).
 (a) MeO for WA vs WB: no jump   -> FALSE
 (b) PhPh for SA vs SB: jump @7  -> true
 (c) MeO for SA vs WB: eq acidic -> true
 (d) PhPh for WA vs SB: eq basic -> true
 -----------------------------------------
 incorrect statement = (a)        <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Match the indicator to where the equivalence point sits: acidic → methyl orange, basic → phenolphthalein, pH 7 → either works. A weak-acid/weak-base titration has no vertical region at all, which is precisely why statement (a) is the odd one out.
Answer: (a) — methyl orange for a weak acid vs weak base titration.
35Class XII students were asked to prepare one litre of buffer solution of pH 8.26 by their chemistry teacher. The amount of ammonium chloride to be dissolved by the student in 0.2 M ammonia solution to make one litre of the buffer is (Given pKb (NH3) = 4.74; Molar mass of NH3 = 17 g mol−1. Molar mass of NH4Cl = 53.5 g mol−1) (JEE Main 2022-26Jul-Shift-2, Level-6)
(a) 53.5 g   (b) 72.3 g   (c) 107 g   (d) 126 g
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Mass of NH4Cl for bufferClass XII students were asked to prepare one litre of buffer solution of pH 8.26 by their chemistry🟢 Exact(c) 107 g
Concept An NH₃/NH₄Cl basic buffer is governed by the base-form Henderson equation written in pOH with pKb. Convert the target pH to pOH, solve for the salt-to-base ratio, read off the moles of NH₄Cl needed in one litre, and multiply by its molar mass. The decoys are the NH₃ molar mass (never used) and the temptation to plug pH directly into a pKb expression; one must switch to pOH first, after which a single Henderson step fixes everything.
Key relation pOH = pKb + log([salt]/[base]); mass = mol × M salt = NH₄Cl, base = NH₃, and pOH = 14 − pH at 25 °C.
Solution
1. pOH = 14 − 8.26 = 5.74.
2. 5.74 = 4.74 + log([salt]/[base]) → log(ratio) = 1.00. pKb given as 4.74
3. [salt]/[base] = 10, and [base] = [NH₃] = 0.2 M.
4. [NH₄Cl] = 10 × 0.2 = 2 M; in one litre that is 2 mol.
5. Mass of NH₄Cl = 2 × 53.5 = 107 g. mol × molar mass
6. So 107 g of NH₄Cl is required, which is option (c).
7. Note the NH₃ molar mass 17 g mol⁻¹ is a distractor and plays no part.
 pOH = 14 - 8.26 = 5.74
 5.74 = 4.74 + log([salt]/[base])
 log(ratio) = 1.00  ->  ratio = 10
 -----------------------------------------
 [NH4Cl] = 10 x 0.2 = 2 M (1 L -> 2 mol)
 mass = 2 x 53.5 = 107 g
 -----------------------------------------
 mass NH4Cl = 107 g               <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick pH 8.26 equals pKa(NH₄⁺) + 1 since pKa = 14 − 4.74 = 9.26, and that "+1" flags a 10:1 salt-to-base ratio on sight. The 17 g mol⁻¹ molar mass of NH₃ is bait — only NH₄Cl's 53.5 g mol⁻¹ enters the final mass calculation.
Answer: (c) 107 g.
36At 310 K, the solubility of CaF2 in water is 2.34×10−3 g/100 mL. The solubility product of CaF2 is _____ ×10−8 ( mol/L)3 (nearest integer). (Given molar mass : CaF2 = 78 g mol−1) (JEE Main 2022-27 July-Shift-1, Level-6)Ksp of CaF2 from solubilityAt 310 K, The solubility of CaF₂ in water is 2.34 × 10-³ g/100 mL. The solubility product of CaF₂ is🟢 Exact0
Concept For CaF₂, a 1:2 salt, Ksp = 4s³ where s is the molar solubility. The mass solubility is given per 100 mL, so it must first be scaled to per litre and then divided by the molar mass. After cubing and multiplying by 4, the Ksp comes out on the order of 10⁻¹⁰. The real catch is the requested unit of ×10⁻⁸: a 10⁻¹⁰-order value is only about 0.01 in those units, which rounds to the integer 0.
Key relation CaF₂ ⇌ Ca²⁺ + 2F⁻, Ksp = (s)(2s)² = 4s³ fluoride is twice the molar solubility, so it enters cubed and brings a factor of 4.
Solution
1. s = 2.34×10⁻³ g/100 mL = 2.34×10⁻² g L⁻¹. ×10 to convert per-100-mL to per-litre
2. s = 2.34×10⁻² / 78 = 3.0×10⁻⁴ mol L⁻¹.
3. Ksp = 4s³ = 4 × (3.0×10⁻⁴)³.
4. (3.0×10⁻⁴)³ = 27×10⁻¹² = 2.7×10⁻¹¹.
5. Ksp = 4 × 2.7×10⁻¹¹ = 1.08×10⁻¹⁰ (mol L⁻¹)³.
6. In units of ×10⁻⁸: 1.08×10⁻¹⁰ = 0.0108×10⁻⁸. dividing by 10⁻⁸
7. Nearest integer of 0.0108 is 0, so the reported answer is 0.
 s = 2.34e-3 g/100mL = 2.34e-2 g/L
 s = 2.34e-2 / 78 = 3.0e-4 mol/L
 -----------------------------------------
 Ksp = 4 s^3 = 4 x (3.0e-4)^3
     = 4 x 2.7e-11 = 1.08e-10
 in x10^-8: 1.08e-10 = 0.0108e-8
 -----------------------------------------
 nearest integer = 0              <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Watch the exponent you are asked for: an order-10⁻¹⁰ Ksp expressed in units of 10⁻⁸ is about 0.01, which rounds to 0. The chemistry is completely standard; the awkward scaling is the entire catch of this particular question.
Answer: Ksp ≈ 1.08×10⁻¹⁰, i.e. 0 in units of ×10⁻⁸.
37Ka for butyric acid C3H7COOH is 2×10−5. The pH of 0.2 M solution of butyric acid is _____ ×10−1. (Nearest integer) [Given log2 = 0.30 ] (JEE Main 2022-28 july-Shift-1, Level-6)pH of butyric acid solutionK_(a) for butyric acid is 2.0xx10^(-5). Calculate pH and hydroxyl ion concentration in 0.2M aqueous...🟢 Exact27
Concept Butyric acid is a weak monoprotic acid, so its hydrogen-ion concentration follows [H⁺] = √(Ka·C) under the standard α ≪ 1 approximation. Taking −log gives the pH, which is then scaled by ×10⁻¹ for the answer blank. The trap is treating it as a strong acid and taking pH from the 0.2 M concentration directly, which would badly overestimate the acidity. We also verify that α is indeed small so the shortcut is justified.
Key relation [H⁺] = √(Ka·C); pH = −log[H⁺] the simple root form is valid only when the degree of dissociation α is small, which we confirm afterwards.
Solution
1. Given Ka = 2×10⁻⁵ and C = 0.2 M.
2. [H⁺] = √(Ka·C) = √(2×10⁻⁵ × 0.2) = √(4×10⁻⁶).
3. [H⁺] = 2×10⁻³ M. 4×10⁻⁶ is a perfect square
4. pH = −log(2×10⁻³) = 3 − log 2 = 3 − 0.30 = 2.70. log2 = 0.30 given
5. The blank wants ×10⁻¹: 2.70 = 27 × 10⁻¹, so the answer is 27.
6. Check approximation: α = [H⁺]/C = 2×10⁻³/0.2 = 0.01 ≪ 1, so √(Ka·C) is valid.
7. Hence pH = 2.70 and the reported integer is 27.
 [H+] = sqrt(Ka x C)
      = sqrt(2e-5 x 0.2)
      = sqrt(4e-6) = 2e-3 M
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = -log(2e-3) = 3 - log2
    = 3 - 0.30 = 2.70
 alpha = 2e-3 / 0.2 = 0.01 ( << 1, OK)
 -----------------------------------------
 pH x 10 = 27                     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick KaC = 4×10⁻⁶ is a perfect square, so [H⁺] = 2×10⁻³ drops out with no calculator. Always confirm α ≪ 1 (here just 1%); if α were large the √(Ka·C) shortcut would overstate [H⁺] and the quadratic form would be needed instead.
Answer: pH = 2.70, i.e. 27 (×10⁻¹).
38A solution is prepared by mixing 0.01 mol each of H2CO3, NaHCO3, Na2CO3, and NaOH in 100 mL of water. pH of the resulting solution is _____.
[Given: pKa1 and pKa2 of H2CO3 are 6.37 and 10.32, respectively; log2 = 0.30 ] (JEE Adv. 2022 (Paper-1), Level-6)
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2022 · Paper 1Solution ↗
pH of carbonate buffer mixtureA solution is prepared by mixing 0.01 mol each of H2CO3,NaHCO3, Na2CO3 and NaOH in 100 mL of wat....🟢 Exact10.02
Concept The four solutes all sit on the carbonic-acid ladder H2CO3 ⇌ HCO3− ⇌ CO32−. Strong NaOH is consumed by the strongest acid present, H2CO3, first; what remains is a conjugate pair, so the result is a buffer pH, not a strong-base pH.
Key relation pH = pKa2 + log([CO32−]/[HCO3−]) The surviving pair is HCO3−/CO32−, whose exchange is the second ionization (pKa2 = 10.32).
Solution
1. Start (mol): H2CO3 0.01, HCO3− 0.01, CO32− 0.01, OH− 0.01.
2. OH− attacks the strongest acid: H2CO3 + OH− → HCO3− + H2O, using all 0.01 mol of each. H2CO3 has the larger Ka, so it reacts before HCO3−.
3. New HCO3− = 0.01 (original) + 0.01 (formed) = 0.02 mol; CO32− stays 0.01 mol; H2CO3 and OH− are now zero.
4. A HCO3−/CO32− buffer remains; the common 100 mL volume cancels, so use the mole ratio directly.
5. pH = pKa2 + log([CO32−]/[HCO3−]) = 10.32 + log(0.01/0.02) = 10.32 + log(0.5).
6. log(0.5) = −log2 = −0.30, so pH = 10.32 − 0.30 = 10.02.
 mol: H2CO3 .01  HCO3- .01  CO3-2 .01  OH- .01
 OH- + H2CO3 -> HCO3- + H2O   (uses .01 each)
 -----------------------------------------
 HCO3- = .01 + .01 = .02 mol
 CO3-2 = .01 mol
 pH = pKa2 + log(CO3/HCO3)
    = 10.32 + log(.01/.02)
    = 10.32 - 0.30 = 10.02   <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Spend OH− on the lowest-pKa acid first, then read off the pair that is left. The pKa1 = 6.37 value is a decoy here — H2CO3 is completely gone, so the first ionization never sets the pH.
Answer: pH = 10.02.
39Concentration of H2SO4 and Na2SO4 in a solution is 1 M and 1.8×10−2 M, respectively. Molar solubility of PbSO4 in the same solution is X×10−Y M (expressed in scientific notation). The value of Y is _____.
[Given: Solubility product of PbSO4 (Ksp) = 1.6×10−8. For H2SO4, Ka1 is very large and Ka2 = 1.2×10−2 ] (JEE Adv. 2022 (Paper-2), Level-6)
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2022 · Paper 2Solution ↗
Molar solubility of PbSO4, exponentJEE Advanced 2022 I Ionic Equilibrium PYQ I Concentration of H2SO4 and Na2SO4 in a solution is 1 M🟢 Exact6
Concept PbSO4 dissolves to Pb2+ + SO42−, so its molar solubility is fixed by the free sulphate already present (common-ion effect). The twist: strong H2SO4 protonates most sulphate to HSO4−, so free SO42− is far below the nominal total.
Key relation [SO42−] = Ka2[HSO4−]/[H+]; S = Ksp/[SO42−] The acid sets [H+] and [HSO4−], which fix free sulphate; solubility then follows from Ksp.
Solution
1. First ionization of 1 M H2SO4 is complete: [H+] ≈ 1 M and [HSO4−] ≈ 1 M.
2. The second ionization is weak (Ka2 = 1.2×10−2) and the huge [H+] suppresses it, so [HSO4−] ≈ 1 M survives.
3. Free sulphate: [SO42−] = Ka2[HSO4−]/[H+] = (1.2×10−2)(1)/(1) = 1.2×10−2 M. The 1.8×10−2 M Na2SO4 is largely protonated to HSO4− and adds almost no free sulphate.
4. Check: the 0.018 M added SO42− is pushed back near 0.012 M by protonation, consistent with the Ka2 balance at [H+] ≈ 1.
5. Molar solubility S = Ksp/[SO42−] = 1.6×10−8/1.2×10−2 = 1.33×10−6 M.
6. In X×10−Y form, X = 1.33 and Y = 6, so Y = 6.
 [H+] = 1,  [HSO4-] = 1   (1 M H2SO4, step 1)
 [SO4-2] = Ka2 [HSO4-] / [H+]
         = 1.2e-2 * 1 / 1 = 1.2e-2 M
 -----------------------------------------
 S = Ksp / [SO4-2]
   = 1.6e-8 / 1.2e-2
   = 1.33e-6 M  ->  X=1.33, Y=6   <-- Y
⚡ JEE Trick Do not plug the total sulphate (≈1.02 M) into Ksp; in strong acid the species that counts is the tiny free SO42− set by Ka2/[H+]. Using 1.02 M would wrongly give Y ≈ 8.
Answer: Y = 6 (S = 1.33×10−6 M).
4050 mL of 0.1M CH3COOH is being titrated against 0.1 M NaOH. When 25 mL of NaOH has been added, the pH of the solution will be _____ ×10−2. (Nearest integer)
(Given : pKa CH3COOH = 4.76 )
log2 = 0.30
log3 = 0.48
log5 = 0.69
log7 = 0.84
log11 = 1.04 (JEE Main 2022 (26 Jun Shift 1), Level-5)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jun · MorningSolution ↗
pH at half-neutralization of CH3COOHPCAT: Titration Curve of Weak Acid - Strong Base (CH3COOH and NaOH) + pH = pKa🟡 Similar476
Concept Adding strong NaOH to a weak acid turns part of it into its salt, making an acetate buffer. NaOH is strong, so it is wholly used up neutralizing the acid; the volume added, not any equilibrium, fixes how much salt forms. At exactly half-neutralization the acid and its conjugate base are equal, so the log term vanishes and pH = pKa.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([CH3COO−]/[CH3COOH]) Henderson equation for a weak-acid/salt buffer; valid while both species are present in comparable amounts. At half-equivalence [salt] = [acid], so log(1) = 0.
Solution
1. Acid taken: 50 mL × 0.1 M = 5 mmol CH3COOH.
2. Base added: 25 mL × 0.1 M = 2.5 mmol NaOH.
3. NaOH neutralizes 2.5 mmol acid → forms 2.5 mmol CH3COO−, leaving 5 − 2.5 = 2.5 mmol CH3COOH.
4. Salt and acid are equal (2.5 mmol each) — this is the half-equivalence point. 25 mL is exactly half of the 50 mL of 0.1 M NaOH needed for full neutralization.
5. Both species share the same final volume (75 mL), so the ratio of concentrations is just the ratio of millimoles = 2.5/2.5 = 1.
6. pH = pKa + log(2.5/2.5) = 4.76 + log1 = 4.76 + 0 = 4.76.
7. The answer is asked as (pH)×102, i.e. 4.76 × 100 = 476.
 acid  = 50 mL * 0.1 M = 5.0 mmol
 base  = 25 mL * 0.1 M = 2.5 mmol  (half)
 -----------------------------------------
 salt = 2.5 mmol,  acid left = 2.5 mmol
 pH = pKa + log(salt/acid)
    = 4.76 + log(1) = 4.76
 pH * 100 = 476          <-- nearest integer
⚡ JEE Trick Half-equivalence is an instant read: pH = pKa, no concentrations needed. The extra log values (log3, log5, log7, log11) are decoys — the ratio is exactly 1, so they never enter.
Answer: 476 (pH = 4.76).
41Ka1, Ka2 and Ka3 are the respective ionization constants for the following reactions (a), (b) and (c). (JEEmain-2022-25Jul-Shift-2, Level-5)
(A) H2C2O4 ⇄ H+ + HC2O4−
(B) HC2O4− ⇄ H+ + HC2O42−
(C) H2C2O4 ⇄ 2H+ + C2O42−
The relationship between Ka1, Ka2 and Ka3 is given as
(a) Ka3 = Ka1 + Ka2   (b) Ka3 = Ka1/Ka2   (c) Ka3 = Ka1 − Ka2   (d) Ka3 = Ka1 × Ka2
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 25 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Relation among stepwise Ka of oxalic acidKa1,Ka2 and Ka3 are the respective ionisation constants for the following reactions (A), (B) and (C)🟢 Exact(d) Ka₃ = Ka₁ × Ka₂
Concept When one equilibrium is the sum of two others, its equilibrium constant is the product of theirs, because adding reactions multiplies their K values. Reaction (C) is just (A) followed by (B).
Key relation if (C) = (A) + (B), then KC = KA × KB, i.e. Ka3 = Ka1 × Ka2 Each K is a ratio of concentrations; adding the steps cancels the intermediate HC2O4−, so the ratios multiply.
Solution
1. (A) H2C2O4 ⇌ H+ + HC2O4−, Ka1 = [H+][HC2O4−]/[H2C2O4].
2. (B) HC2O4− ⇌ H+ + C2O42−, Ka2 = [H+][C2O42−]/[HC2O4−].
3. (C) H2C2O4 ⇌ 2H+ + C2O42−, Ka3 = [H+]2[C2O42−]/[H2C2O4].
4. Multiply: Ka1×Ka2 = {[H+][HC2O4−]/[H2C2O4]} × {[H+][C2O42−]/[HC2O4−]}.
5. The intermediate HC2O4− cancels, leaving [H+]2[C2O42−]/[H2C2O4] = Ka3.
6. Hence Ka3 = Ka1 × Ka2 — option (d).
 Ka1 = [H+][HC2O4-] / [H2C2O4]
 Ka2 = [H+][C2O4-2] / [HC2O4-]
 -----------------------------------------
 Ka1 * Ka2 : HC2O4- cancels
   = [H+]^2 [C2O4-2] / [H2C2O4]
   = Ka3           <-- so Ka3 = Ka1 * Ka2
⚡ JEE Trick Add reactions → multiply K; reverse a reaction → invert K. Sums never add K values, so (a) Ka1+Ka2 is the standard trap.
Answer: (d) Ka3 = Ka1 × Ka2.
42A compound ' X ' is a weak acid and it exhibits colour change at pH close to the equivalence point during neutralization of NaOH with CH3COOH. Compound ' X ' exists in ionized form in basic medium. The compound ' X ' is (JEE Main 2022-29 Jul-Shift-2, Level-4)
(a) methyl orange   (b) methyl red   (c) phenolphthalein   (d) erichrome Black T
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 29 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Identify indicator for NaOH-CH3COOHStrong Alkali Weak Acid Titration using Phenolphthalein Indicator🟡 Similar(c) phenolphthalein
Concept NaOH + CH3COOH is a strong-base–weak-acid titration, so the equivalence point lies on the basic side (pH ≈ 8.7, from the acetate salt). The indicator must change colour in that basic window and be a weak acid itself.
Key relation useful range ≈ pKIn ± 1; equivalence pH (weak acid + strong base) > 7 Only an indicator whose transition sits above 7 flags this end point; phenolphthalein turns at pH 8–10.
Solution
1. The salt formed is CH3COONa; acetate hydrolyses, so the equivalence-point solution is basic (pH ≈ 8.7).
2. A suitable indicator must change colour near pH 8–10. Its transition range must bracket the equivalence pH.
3. Phenolphthalein (range 8.2–10.0) fits, and it is a weak organic acid (HIn).
4. In basic medium HIn loses H+ → In− (the pink, ionized form), matching “exists in ionized form in basic medium”.
5. Methyl orange (3.1–4.4) and methyl red (4.2–6.3) change in the acidic range — wrong for this end point.
6. Eriochrome Black T is a complexometric (EDTA) indicator for metal-ion titrations, not an acid–base indicator here.
7. All clues agree: a weak acid that ionizes in base and changes colour near the basic equivalence point is phenolphthalein, option (c).
 CH3COOH (weak) + NaOH (strong)
 equiv. point salt = CH3COONa -> pH ~ 8.7
 -----------------------------------------
 need indicator changing at pH 8-10
 phenolphthalein: 8.2 - 10.0, weak acid HIn
 basic: HIn -> In- (pink)      <-- option (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Weak acid + strong base → equivalence is basic → phenolphthalein. Methyl orange is the mirror choice (strong acid + weak base, acidic equivalence), the usual distractor.
Answer: (c) phenolphthalein.
43The Ksp for bismuth sulphide Bi2S3 is 1.08×10−73. The solubility of Bi2S3 in mol L−1 at 298 K is (JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 2), Level-3)
(a) 1.0×10−15   (b) 2.7×10−12   (c) 3.2×10−10   (d) 4.2×10−8
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 25 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Solubility of Bi2S3 from KspThe Ksp for bismuth sulphide (Bi2S3) is 1.08 x 10-73. The solubility of Bi2S3 in mol/L at 298 K is :🟢 Exact(a) 1.0×10⁻¹⁵ mol L⁻¹
Concept For a salt AxBy, Ksp is written from the stoichiometric ion concentrations in terms of the molar solubility s. Bi2S3 gives 2 Bi3+ and 3 S2− per formula unit.
Key relation Ksp = [Bi3+]2[S2−]3 = (2s)2(3s)3 = 108 s5 Each ion concentration is its coefficient times s; the powers follow the coefficients.
Solution
1. Dissolution: Bi2S3 → 2Bi3+ + 3S2−; if solubility = s, then [Bi3+] = 2s and [S2−] = 3s.
2. Ksp = (2s)2(3s)3 = 4s2·27s3 = 108 s5.
3. 108 s5 = 1.08×10−73, so s5 = 1.0×10−75.
4. Take the fifth root: s = (10−75)1/5 = 10−15. −75 ÷ 5 = −15; the 1.0 coefficient stays 1.
5. So s = 1.0×10−15 mol L−1.
6. Sanity check: [Bi3+] = 2×10−15, [S2−] = 3×10−15; (2e−15)2(3e−15)3 = 108×10−75 = 1.08×10−73, matching Ksp.
7. That is option (a).
 Bi2S3 -> 2 Bi+3 + 3 S-2
 Ksp = (2s)^2 (3s)^3 = 108 s^5
 -----------------------------------------
 108 s^5 = 1.08e-73
 s^5 = 1.0e-75
 s = (1e-75)^(1/5) = 1.0e-15 M  <-- opt (a)
⚡ JEE Trick The prefactor 108 = 22·33 is cancelled exactly by the 1.08 in Ksp, leaving a clean 10−75 whose fifth root is a tidy 10−15. That clean power of ten is the giveaway that 108 was handled correctly.
Answer: (a) 1.0×10−15 mol L−1.
44The neutralization occurs when 10 mL of 0.1 M acid ' A ' is allowed to react with 30 mL of 0.05 M base M(OH)2. The basicity of the acid ' A ' is [ M is a metal] (JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 2), Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 25 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Basicity of acid from neutralizationThe Neutralization Occurs when 10ml of 0.1 M acid "A" is allowed to react with 30 ml of 0.05 M base🟢 Exact3
Concept At neutralization the equivalents of acid equal the equivalents of base; molarity alone is not enough because a polybasic acid or dibasic hydroxide supplies several reacting protons/hydroxides per formula unit. Equivalents = molarity × volume × n, where n is the basicity of the acid (replaceable H+) or the acidity of the base (OH− per formula unit).
Key relation nacid·MAVA = nbase·MBVB Each OH− neutralizes exactly one replaceable H+; M(OH)2 supplies 2 OH− per formula unit, so nbase = 2.
Solution
1. Moles of acid A = 10 mL × 0.1 M = 1 mmol.
2. Moles of base M(OH)2 = 30 mL × 0.05 M = 1.5 mmol.
3. M(OH)2 gives 2 OH− each, so OH− available = 2 × 1.5 = 3 mmol.
4. Let the basicity of A = n, so the replaceable H+ it can donate = n × 1 mmol = n mmol.
5. Neutralization needs H+ = OH−: n × 1 = 3, hence n = 3. All 3 mmol OH− must be matched by an equal number of H+.
6. Equivalently, n = (nbase·MBVB)/(MAVA) = 3/1 = 3.
7. Basicity of acid A = 3 (a triprotic acid, H3A-type such as H3PO4).
 acid A : 10 mL * 0.1 M       = 1.0 mmol
 base   : 30 mL * 0.05 M      = 1.5 mmol
 OH- from M(OH)2 = 2 * 1.5    = 3.0 mmol
 -----------------------------------------
 H+ = OH-  ->  n * 1 = 3
 basicity n = 3               <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Count OH− per base: M(OH)2 is diacidic, so the 1.5 mmol doubles to 3. Forgetting the factor 2 gives a wrong basicity of 1.5, which is not even an integer — a quick sanity flag.
Answer: 3.
45A student needs to prepare a buffer solution of propanoic acid and its sodium salt with pH 4. The ratio of [CH3CH2COO−]/[CH3CH2COOH] required to make buffer is
Given : Ka(CH3CH2COOH) = 1.3×10−5 (JEE Main 2022 (28 Jun Shift 2), Level-3)
(a) 0.03   (b) 0.13   (c) 0.23   (d) 0.33
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 28 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
Salt/acid ratio for buffer pHA student needs to prepare a buffer solution of propanoic acid and its sodium salt with pH 4. Th...🟢 Exact(b) 0.13
Concept A buffer of a weak acid and its sodium salt is governed by the Henderson equation, which fixes the salt-to-acid ratio once the target pH and Ka are set. The slick route recognizes that [A−]/[HA] is just Ka divided by [H+], so no logarithms are needed at all.
Key relation pH = pKa + log([A−]/[HA]) ⇒ [A−]/[HA] = Ka/[H+] Rearranging Henderson removes the logs: raise both sides as powers of ten and the ratio equals Ka/[H+].
Solution
1. Target pH = 4, so [H+] = 10−pH = 10−4 M.
2. Ka(propanoic acid) = 1.3×10−5.
3. The required ratio = [CH3CH2COO−]/[CH3CH2COOH] = Ka/[H+]. Salt is the conjugate base A−; acid is HA.
4. = 1.3×10−5 / 10−4 = 1.3×10−1 = 0.13. Dividing powers of ten: 10−5/10−4 = 10−1.
5. Cross-check via logs: pKa = 5 − log1.3 = 5 − 0.11 = 4.89; log(ratio) = pH − pKa = 4 − 4.89 = −0.89 ⇒ ratio ≈ 0.13.
6. Both routes give 0.13, which matches option (b).
 [H+] = 10^-4   (pH = 4)
 ratio = Ka / [H+]
 -----------------------------------------
       = 1.3e-5 / 1e-4
       = 1.3e-1
       = 0.13                 <-- option (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Skip the logs: [A−]/[HA] = Ka/[H+] is a one-step divide. Because pKa (≈4.89) is just above the target pH 4, the ratio must be a little below 1 — only 0.13 qualifies.
Answer: (b) 0.13.
4620 mL of 0.1M NH4OH is mixed with 40 mL of 0.05 M HCl. The pH of the mixture is nearest to:
(Given : KbNH4OH = 1×10−5, log2 = 0.30, log3 = 0.48, log5 = 0.69, log7 = 0.84, log11 = 1.04 ) (JEE-Main-2022-25Jul-Shift-1, Level-3)
(a) 3.2   (b) 4.2   (c) 5.2   (d) 6.2		pH of NH4OH + HCl mixture20 mL of 0.1 M NH4OH is mixed with 40 mL of 0.05 M HCl. The pH of the mixture is nearest to: (Given🟢 Exact(c) 5.2
Concept Equal millimoles of weak base NH4OH and strong acid HCl neutralize completely to the salt NH4Cl. The cation NH4+ hydrolyses, giving an acidic salt solution whose pH comes from a weak-acid calculation.
Key relation [H+] = √(Ka·C), Ka(NH4+) = Kw/Kb NH4+ is the conjugate acid of NH4OH; its Ka comes from Kw = 10−14 at 25 °C.
Solution
1. NH4OH = 20 × 0.1 = 2 mmol; HCl = 40 × 0.05 = 2 mmol — exactly equal.
2. All base is converted to NH4Cl; no excess acid or base remains, so it is a salt solution, not a buffer.
3. Total volume = 60 mL, so C(NH4Cl) = 2/60 = 1/30 ≈ 0.0333 M.
4. Ka(NH4+) = Kw/Kb = 10−14/10−5 = 10−9.
5. [H+] = √(Ka·C) = √(10−9 × 0.0333) = √(3.33×10−11) = 5.77×10−6 M.
6. pH = −log(5.77×10−6) = 6 − 0.76 = 5.24 ≈ 5.2. The nearest listed value is (c).
 NH4OH = 2 mmol,  HCl = 2 mmol  -> NH4Cl
 C = 2/60 = 0.0333 M
 Ka = Kw/Kb = 1e-14 / 1e-5 = 1e-9
 -----------------------------------------
 [H+] = sqrt(Ka*C) = sqrt(3.33e-11)
      = 5.77e-6 M
 pH = 6 - 0.76 = 5.24 ~ 5.2   <-- opt (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Equal mmol of weak base + strong acid = pure salt, never a buffer, so do not use Henderson. The NH4Cl solution must be acidic (pH < 7), which already rules out (d) 6.2 as too close to neutral and (a)/(b) as too low for such a weak hydrolysis.
Answer: (c) 5.2.
47Given below are two statements: one is labelled as Assertion A and the other is labelled as Reason R.
Assertion A : Phenolphthalein is a pH dependent indicator, remains colourless in acidic solution and gives pink colour in basic medium
Reason R : Phenolphthalein is a weak acid. It doesn't dissociate in basic medium. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below (JEE Main 2022-26Jul-Shift-2, Level-3)
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A   (b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A.   (c) A is true but R is false   (d) A is false but R is true
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 26 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Assertion-Reason on phenolphthaleinWhy does phenolphthalein change colors?🟡 Similar(c) A true but R false
Concept In an Assertion–Reason item, judge each statement's truth separately, then ask whether the reason actually explains the assertion. Phenolphthalein's colour change is caused by its dissociation, so a reason claiming it does NOT dissociate contradicts the mechanism.
Key relation HIn (colourless) ⇌ H+ + In− (pink); base pulls the equilibrium right The pink colour appears precisely because In− forms, i.e. dissociation increases in base.
Solution
1. Assertion A: phenolphthalein is colourless in acid and pink in base — this is the observed behaviour, so A is true.
2. Reason R: “phenolphthalein is a weak acid” — true on its own.
3. R continues: “it doesn't dissociate in basic medium” — false; in base it dissociates MORE, forming the pink In−.
4. Removing H+ (base) shifts HIn ⇌ H+ + In− to the right by Le Chatelier. So dissociation rises, opposite to R's claim.
5. Therefore R taken as a whole is false: it is a weak acid, but the second clause (no dissociation in base) is wrong.
6. Because R is false, it cannot explain A, which eliminates both (a) and (b); and A is clearly true, which eliminates (d).
7. A true, R false → option (c).
 A: colourless (acid) / pink (base)  -> TRUE
 R: weak acid, does NOT dissociate in base
 -----------------------------------------
 HIn  <=>  H+ + In-   (In- is pink)
 base removes H+ -> MORE dissociation
 so R is FALSE          <-- A true, R false (c)
⚡ JEE Trick The pink colour itself is proof that phenolphthalein dissociates in base — the reason states the exact opposite, so it cannot be true. Spotting that one wrong clause settles the whole item as (c).
Answer: (c) A is true but R is false.
48If the solubility product of PbS is 8×10−28, then the solubility of PbS in pure water at 298 K is x×10−16 mol L−1. The value of x is _____ (Nearest integer) [Given √2 = 1.41 ] (JEE Main 2022-29 Jul-Shift-1, Level-3)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 29 Jul · MorningSolution ↗
Solubility of PbS in pure waterIf the solubility product of PbS is 8 x 10-28, then the solubility of PbS in pure water at 298 K is🟢 Exact282
Concept PbS is a 1:1 salt, so its Ksp = s2 and the solubility is simply √Ksp. The task is to write that root in the form x×10−16 and read off x.
Key relation Ksp = [Pb2+][S2−] = s·s = s2 ⇒ s = √Ksp In pure water each dissolved formula unit gives one Pb2+ and one S2−, so both equal s.
Solution
1. PbS → Pb2+ + S2−; [Pb2+] = [S2−] = s.
2. Ksp = s2 = 8×10−28.
3. s = √(8×10−28) = √8 × √(10−28). Split the number from the even power of ten so the root is clean.
4. √8 = 2√2 = 2 × 1.41 = 2.82; √(10−28) = 10−14.
5. s = 2.82×10−14 mol L−1; this is the molar solubility in pure water (no common ion to suppress it).
6. Write as x×10−16: shift the power of ten down by 2, so multiply the coefficient by 100: 2.82×10−14 = 282×10−16.
7. Hence x = 282 (nearest integer).
 PbS -> Pb+2 + S-2 ,  Ksp = s^2
 s = sqrt(8e-28) = sqrt(8) * 1e-14
 -----------------------------------------
 sqrt(8) = 2*sqrt(2) = 2*1.41 = 2.82
 s = 2.82e-14 M
   = 282e-16 M   ->  x = 282      <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Match the exponent asked for: shifting 10−14 to 10−16 multiplies the coefficient by 100 (2.82 → 282). Leaving s as 2.82 would wrongly give x ≈ 3.
Answer: 282 (s = 2.82×10−14 M).
49pH value of 0.001 M NaOH solution is (JEE Main 2022 (27 Jun Shift 2), Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 27 Jun · EveningSolution ↗
pH of 0.001 M NaOHpH value of 0.001 M NaOH solution is | 27 JUNE 2022 SHIFT 2 JEE MAIN PYQ🟢 Exact11
Concept NaOH is a strong base, dissociating completely into Na+ and OH−, so the hydroxide concentration simply equals the stated molarity. From [OH−] find pOH, then use the water relation pH + pOH = 14 (at 25 °C) to get pH. No equilibrium constant is involved because the base is fully ionized.
Key relation pOH = −log[OH−]; pH = 14 − pOH (Kw = 10−14) Strong base ⇒ [OH−] = C; the water auto-ionization equilibrium ties pH to pOH through Kw.
Solution
1. 0.001 M NaOH → [OH−] = 10−3 M (one OH− per formula unit, complete dissociation).
2. The 10−7 M OH− contributed by water is negligible beside 10−3 M. Self-ionization of water is ignorable whenever C >> 10−7 M.
3. pOH = −log[OH−] = −log(10−3) = 3.
4. pH = 14 − pOH = 14 − 3 = 11.
5. Cross-check through [H+]: [H+] = Kw/[OH−] = 10−14/10−3 = 10−11 M.
6. pH = −log(10−11) = 11, confirming the result.
7. pH = 11 > 7, as expected for a basic solution.
 NaOH strong -> [OH-] = 1e-3 M
 pOH = -log(1e-3) = 3
 -----------------------------------------
 pH = 14 - pOH = 14 - 3 = 11
 check: [H+] = 1e-14 / 1e-3 = 1e-11
 pH = 11                       <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick For a strong base read [OH−] straight off the label and jump to pH = 14 + log C. Only for very dilute solutions (≈10−6 M or less) would water's own OH− start to matter.
Answer: pH = 11.
50The Plot of pH -metric titration of weak base NH4OH vs strong acid HCl looks like (JEE Main 2022-27 Jul-Shift-2, Level-2)
(a) [graph]   (b) [graph]   (c) [graph]   (d) [graph]
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 27 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Shape of weak base vs strong acid curveThe plot of pH-metric titration of weak base NH4OH vs strong acid HCl🟢 Exact(a) falling sigmoid
Concept Here the weak base NH4OH is in the flask and strong acid HCl is added (x-axis = volume of acid). A base-in-flask titration starts basic and the pH FALLS as acid is poured in, so the correct curve descends overall.
Key relation weak base + strong acid: start pH basic (> 7), equivalence pH acidic (< 7) The salt NH4Cl at equivalence is acidic, so the steep drop crosses below pH 7.
Solution
1. A base is titrated by acid, so pH begins high and decreases — the curve must go downhill, ruling out the rising plots (b) and (d).
2. Being a weak base, the initial pH is moderate (≈ 10–11), not 13, and a NH4OH/NH4+ buffer keeps the early fall gentle.
3. Near equivalence the pH drops sharply, then levels off at low pH as excess HCl dominates — an overall falling sigmoid. The equivalence jump is the near-vertical middle of the S.
4. Equivalence lies below pH 7 (acidic salt NH4Cl), so the steep region sits in the acidic zone.
5. Plot (c) drops steeply right at the very start then flattens — wrong, because the buffer makes the start gradual, not the steepest part.
6. The gentle-then-steep falling sigmoid is plot (a).
 base (NH4OH) in flask, add acid (HCl)
 start: pH ~ 10-11  (weak base)
 -----------------------------------------
 buffer region: slow fall
 equivalence: steep drop, crosses pH < 7
 end: levels off, low pH (excess HCl)
 => falling sigmoid           <-- plot (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Who is in the flask decides the slope sign: titrating a base → curve falls; titrating an acid → curve rises. The rising plots (b)/(d) are the standard trap when the roles are read backwards. A second check: the equivalence jump of a weak base lands below pH 7, not at 7, so the near-vertical part must sit in the acidic half of plot (a).
Answer: (a) a falling sigmoid from basic to acidic pH.
512 L of 0.2M H2SO4 is reacted with 2 L of 0.1 M NaOH solution, the molarity of the resulting product Na2SO4 in the solution is _____ millimolar. (JEE Main 2022-28 July-Shift-2, Level-2)
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 28 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Molarity of Na2SO4 product2 L of 0.2 M H2SO4 is reacted with 2 L of 0.1 M NaOH solution, then the molarity of the resulting...🟢 Exact25
Concept H2SO4 + NaOH is a limiting-reagent stoichiometry problem. Two NaOH are needed per H2SO4 to make Na2SO4; whichever runs out first caps the product, which is then diluted over the combined volume.
Key relation 2NaOH + H2SO4 → Na2SO4 + 2H2O; M = mol/Vtotal Product moles are set by the limiting reactant; molarity uses the total 4 L.
Solution
1. H2SO4 = 2 L × 0.2 M = 0.4 mol.
2. NaOH = 2 L × 0.1 M = 0.2 mol.
3. The ratio needs 2 NaOH per H2SO4; 0.2 mol NaOH can react with only 0.1 mol H2SO4, so NaOH is limiting and H2SO4 is in excess. 0.4 mol acid would need 0.8 mol base, far above the 0.2 mol present.
4. Na2SO4 formed = (1/2) × mol NaOH = 0.2/2 = 0.1 mol.
5. Total volume after mixing = 2 + 2 = 4 L, so [Na2SO4] = 0.1 mol / 4 L = 0.025 M.
6. The leftover 0.3 mol H2SO4 stays as excess acid and does not change the amount of Na2SO4 formed.
7. Convert to the requested unit: 0.025 M × 1000 = 25 millimolar.
 H2SO4 = 2 L * 0.2 M = 0.4 mol  (excess)
 NaOH  = 2 L * 0.1 M = 0.2 mol  (limiting)
 2 NaOH : 1 Na2SO4
 -----------------------------------------
 Na2SO4 = 0.2 / 2 = 0.1 mol
 V = 4 L -> M = 0.1 / 4 = 0.025 M
 = 25 millimolar              <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Na2SO4 is capped by NaOH (half of 0.2 mol), not by the abundant acid. Treating H2SO4 as limiting would give 0.4 mol and a four-fold-too-large answer.
Answer: 25 millimolar.
52In base vs. Acid titration, at the end point methyl orange is present as (JEEmain-2022-25Jul-Shift-2, Level-1)
(a) quinonoid form   (b) heterocyclic form   (c) phenolic form   (d) benzenoid form
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 25 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
Form of methyl orange at end pointIn base vs acid titration, at the end point methyl orange is present as....🟢 Exact(a) quinonoid form
Concept “Base vs acid titration” means base in the flask with acid added, so the mixture turns acidic at the end point. Methyl orange is an azo dye whose colour depends on its structure: yellow azo (benzenoid) form in base, red quinonoid form in acid.
Key relation benzenoid (yellow, basic) + H+ ⇌ quinonoid (red, acidic) Protonation of the azo nitrogen converts the ring system to the quinonoid resonance form, which is red.
Solution
1. The flask holds the base; acid is the titrant, so near the end point excess H+ makes the solution acidic.
2. Methyl orange exists in two forms: the benzenoid (azo) form, yellow, in basic or neutral medium.
3. On protonation it rearranges to the quinonoid form, which is red/pink. The extended quinonoid conjugation shifts its absorption to give the red colour.
4. At the end point of a base-vs-acid titration the solution has just turned acidic, so methyl orange is protonated.
5. Hence it is present in the quinonoid form (the colour change yellow → red marks the end point of the base-vs-acid titration).
6. The distractors do not describe methyl orange's acid–base equilibrium: "phenolic" and "heterocyclic" are unrelated structural labels, and "benzenoid" is the basic (yellow) form, not the one at an acidic end point.
7. So the correct form is quinonoid, option (a).
 base in flask + acid titrant -> acidic end
 methyl orange:
   basic  -> benzenoid (azo), yellow
   acidic -> quinonoid, red
 -----------------------------------------
 end point = acidic = protonated form
 => quinonoid form            <-- option (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Red methyl orange = quinonoid (acidic); yellow = benzenoid (basic). “Phenolic” and “heterocyclic” are not methyl orange's acid–base forms at all, so they are pure distractors.
Answer: (a) quinonoid form.
53200 mL of 0.01M HCl is mixed with 400 mL of 0.01M H2SO4. The pH of the mixture is (JEE Main 2022-29 Jul-Shift-2, Level-1)
(a) 1.14   (b) 1.78   (c) 2.34   (d) 3.02
📝 PYQ: JEE Main 2022 · 29 Jul · EveningSolution ↗
pH of mixed HCl + H2SO4200 mL of 0.01M HCl is mixed with 400 mL of 0.01M H2SO4. The pH of the mixture is🟢 Exact(b) 1.78
Concept Both are strong acids and dissociate completely, so the hydrogen ions simply add up. The catch is that H2SO4 is diprotic: every mole releases two H+, while HCl releases one. You must sum moles of H+, then divide by the total volume — never average the molarities.
Key relation [H+] = (mol H+ from HCl + 2 × mol H2SO4) / Vtotal, pH = −log[H+] Strong acids are 100% ionised, so moles of H+ come straight from stoichiometry.
Solution
1. HCl supplies one H+ each: 0.01 M × 0.200 L = 2.0×10−3 mol H+.
2. H2SO4 amount = 0.01 M × 0.400 L = 4.0×10−3 mol. Each gives 2 H+.
3. H+ from the sulphuric acid = 2 × 4.0×10−3 = 8.0×10−3 mol.
4. Total H+ = 2.0×10−3 + 8.0×10−3 = 1.0×10−2 mol.
5. Total volume = 200 + 400 = 600 mL = 0.600 L, so [H+] = 1.0×10−2 / 0.600 = 1.667×10−2 M.
6. pH = −log(1.667×10−2) = 2 − log(5/3) = 2 − (0.70 − 0.48) = 1.78.
 HCl  : 0.01 M x 0.200 L = 2.0e-3 mol H+
 H2SO4: 0.01 M x 0.400 L = 4.0e-3 mol
        x 2 protons      = 8.0e-3 mol H+
 -----------------------------------------
 total H+ = 1.0e-2 mol ;  V = 0.600 L
 [H+] = 1.0e-2/0.600 = 1.667e-2 M
 pH = 2 - log(5/3) = 2 - 0.22 = 1.78   <-- ans
⚡ JEE Trick Count H2SO4 twice — working in normality, N = (0.01×1×0.2 + 0.01×2×0.4)/0.6 gives the same 1.67×10−2. The trap answer forgets the second proton and reports a higher pH; here the diprotic acid supplies most of the acidity.
Answer: (b) 1.78.
54An acidified solution of 0.05 M Zn2+ is saturated with 0.1 M (H2S). What is the minimum molar concentration (M) of H+ required to prevent the precipitation of ZnS? Use Ksp(ZnS) = 1.25×10−22 and overall dissociation constant of (H2S), KNET = K1K2 = 1×10−21. (JEE Adv. 2020 (Paper-2), Level-6)
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2020 · Paper 2Solution ↗
Min [H+] to prevent ZnS precipitationJEE ADVANCED 2020 SOLUTION - An acidified solution of 0.05 M Zn2+ is saturated with 0.1 M H2S🟢 Exact0.2
Concept ZnS starts to precipitate the instant the ionic product [Zn2+][S2−] reaches Ksp. Keeping the solution acidic pushes the H2S equilibrium back, lowering [S2−] so the ionic product stays at or below Ksp. Find the largest [S2−] tolerated, then the [H+] that pins it there.
Key relation KNET = [H+]2[S2−] / [H2S]; precipitation just avoided when [Zn2+][S2−] = Ksp H2S ⇌ 2H+ + S2−, so [H+] controls [S2−].
Solution
1. Maximum sulphide before ZnS drops out: [S2−]max = Ksp/[Zn2+] = 1.25×10−22 / 0.05 = 2.5×10−21 M.
2. For the saturated H2S: KNET = [H+]2[S2−]/[H2S] = 1×10−21, with [H2S] = 0.1 M held constant. Saturation fixes [H2S].
3. Rearrange for the proton term: [H+]2 = KNET·[H2S] / [S2−]max.
4. Substitute: [H+]2 = (1×10−21 × 0.1) / (2.5×10−21) = 1×10−22 / 2.5×10−21 = 0.04.
5. [H+] = √0.04 = 0.2 M.
6. So the minimum acidity that keeps ZnS dissolved is [H+] = 0.2 M (any higher is also safe).
 [S2-]max = Ksp/[Zn2+]
          = 1.25e-22/0.05 = 2.5e-21 M
 Knet = [H+]^2 [S2-]/[H2S] = 1e-21
 [H+]^2 = Knet*[H2S]/[S2-]
        = (1e-21 x 0.1)/2.5e-21
        = 1e-22/2.5e-21 = 0.04
 [H+] = sqrt(0.04) = 0.2 M      <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick More acid means less S2−, hence a safer solution — so the question asks for a minimum H+, the threshold value. Sanity check: [H+]2 ∝ 1/[S2−]max, so demanding a tiny allowed sulphide forces a sizeable 0.2 M proton level.
Answer: 0.2 M.
55A solution of 0.1 M weak base (B) is titrated with 0.1 M of a strong acid (HA). The variation of pH of the solution with the volume of HA added is shown in the figure below. What is the pKb of the base? The neutralization reaction is given by B + HA → BH+ + A−. (JEE Adv. 2020 (Paper-2), Level-4)
📝 PYQ: JEE Advanced 2020 · Paper 2Solution ↗
pKb from weak base-strong acid curveJEE ADVANCED 2020 SOLUTION - A solution of 0.1 M weak base (B) is titrated with 0.1 M of a strong🟢 Exact3.3
Concept A weak base titrated with a strong acid gives a base/conjugate-acid buffer before the end point. At the half-equivalence point exactly half the base is converted to BH+, so [B] = [BH+] and the Henderson equation collapses to pOH = pKb. Read the pH there off the curve and convert. (Kw = 10−14 at 25 °C.)
Key relation pOH = pKb + log([BH+]/[B]); at half-equivalence pOH = pKb = 14 − pH The log term is log 1 = 0 when [B] = [BH+].
Solution
1. Base and acid are both 0.1 M, so the equivalence volume equals the initial base volume. The steep pH drop on the graph sits at 6 mL, so equivalence = 6 mL.
2. Half-equivalence is at 6/2 = 3 mL; here moles of acid added = half the moles of base, leaving [B] = [BH+].
3. Buffer region: pOH = pKb + log([BH+]/[B]) = pKb + log 1 = pKb.
4. From the curve the near-flat buffer plateau at 3 mL reads about 10.7. Go straight up from 3 mL on the x-axis to the gentle shelf before the steep fall.
5. pOH = 14 − pH = 14 − 10.7 = 3.3.
6. Therefore pKb = pOH = 3.3.
 both 0.1 M  -> equiv volume = base volume
 steep jump at 6 mL  -> equiv = 6 mL
 half-equiv = 3 mL   -> [B] = [BH+]
 pOH = pKb + log([BH+]/[B]) = pKb
 pH(3 mL) from graph ~ 10.7
 pOH = 14 - 10.7 = 3.3
 pKb = 3.3                      <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Half-equivalence is the whole game: for a weak base pHhalf = 14 − pKb, the mirror of pH = pKa for a weak acid. Locate the end point, halve its volume, read the pH — no initial-concentration algebra needed. The tempting slip is to read pH at the end point (6 mL) instead of at its half.
Answer: pKb = 3.3.
